Early Ethnography in the American Arctic




This book offers a portrait of early ethnographic work in the American
Arctic, with a focus on understanding the mutual constitution of the Inuit
and their early ethnographers. It draws mainly on a rich repository of written
testimonies from the early 20th century, the ‘great ethnographic period’,
when new scholarly interest in the region took off. Supplementing the
movements and observations of whalers, traders, and missionaries, the early
chroniclers offered new knowledge of Inuit life. Although their descriptions
of the Inuit bear the marks of their time, the texts have left a deep mark
on later developments and contributed to a long-​lasting view of human life
in the Arctic. The chapters show the infiltration of lives and landscapes, of
thoughts and materials, of Inuit and ethnographers. The book will be rele-
vant to anthropologists as well as historians, geographers, and others with an
interest in the Arctic region and indigenous studies.

Kirsten Hastrup is Professor Emeritus at the Department of Anthropology,
University of Copenhagen, Denmark.

Acknowledgements




This book is based on early ethnographic work in Arctic America, as it came
into the purview of people from neighbouring regions, or sailors and scholars
from other continents. Apart from extensive reading of early 20th-​century
ethnographic works on people living in the American Arctic, the book also
draws on writings by earlier explorers and travellers in the region from the
18th century onwards. The authors of these books may not have called them-
selves ethnographers, yet their writings show a keen interest in understanding
the lives of others, and their work must be credited.
   The centenary of Knud Rasmussen’s Fifth Thule Expedition (1921–​1924),
moving along the entire Arctic American coast from east to west, mapping
Inuit life and lore was a major inspiration for this book. Another spur came
from the people of Avanersuaq (Northwest Greenland), where I have made
recurrent fieldwork since 2007, studying the life and history of people living
there. They were dubbed Polar Eskimo in the early anthropological literature,
dating back to Knud Rasmussen’s first expedition to the High Arctic region
in 1902–​1904. Today they are known as Inughuit, their own name. Present
day Avanersuaq is home to some 750 people, physically separated from their
Greenlandic neighbours in the south by almost a thousand kilometres of
uninhabited coastal lands. Earlier, they were connected to the Americas, so
clearly present across the Smith Sound, facilitating several waves of immigra-
tion from the American continent to Greenland, the first taking place some
4,500 years ago. Conversing with the Inughuit (on and off) for many years
has been essential to my understanding of what it means to live as hunters in
the High Arctic.
   This book benefitted from a fellowship at the Institut d’Études Avancées de
Paris (France), with the financial support of the French State managed by the
Agence Nationale de la Recherche, programme “Investissements d’avenir,”
(ANR-​11-​LABX-​0027-​01 Labex Rfiea+​). For five months in 2016–​2017,
I was enfolded in the highly productive ambience of the Institute. A particular
vote of thanks is owed to the then Directrice, Professor Gretty Mirdal, whose
high level of ambition was seconded by an unusual kindness and personal
engagement with the group.
                                                      Acknowledgements vii

   The years that have passed since then are many, and I must thank my
colleagues at the Institute of Anthropology, University of Copenhagen, for
their friendliness and humour. Warm thanks also to the Head of Department,
Bjarke Oxlund, for offering me an office even after formal retirement. In the
last phase, four readers commented on the draft, each adding their invalu-
able points to the work. They are Gísli Pálsson, Ayo Wahlberg, Adam Kuper,
and Frida Hastrup. With such colleagues one is privileged, and they have my
heartfelt thanks. When dealing with an ethnographic material that is nei-
ther history nor not-​history, the outsiders’ appraisal is essential. Thanks are
owed also to the anonymous reviewers, whose observations and suggestions
were helpful. While writing this book, debts have also accrued to colleagues,
family, friends, and institutions –​contributing to the formation of the work
in different ways and reminding me about everyday pleasures.
   In the chapters to follow, the notions of ‘anthropology’ and ‘ethnography’
are used with a rather loose hand. Ethnography is an older term, emer-
ging in the 19th century, when travellers, naturalists, and others began to
note down how people lived, hunted, and moved in the terrain. They were
forerunners of later, trained folklorists cum ethnographers, with a more dis-
tinct calling. Anthropology emerged as a comprehensive scholarly term in
early 20th century; the obvious interest in ‘other people’ also included phys-
ical anthropology, a branch of anthropology focussing on measuring human
bones, featuring briefly also in this work. As the terms are used in the early
descriptions and modern practice –​and differ between authors, they are used
in a relatively free fashion in the book.
   The editor and staff of Routledge are thanked for their kindness and pro-
fessional skill, making the process of reworking the text a pleasure.
Prologue: The Great Ethnographic Period
in the American Arctic




The rich archive of early exploration and ethnographic interest in the
American Arctic shows how the Inuit became known to the larger world
and described in a new vocabulary. While the ethnographers were primed
by earlier reports, the scholarly interest in the Inuit of the American Arctic
resulted in writings that served as a bottom line for ethnographic knowledge
until relatively recently. Before ethnography was invented, sailors, trappers,
traders, and missionaries had arrived with each their interest in the northern
landscapes.
   The ethnographers at the centre of this book found their way into Inuit
lands from late 19th century onwards and were impressed by the specialized
technologies and wide-​    ranging movements by which the Inuit not only
survived but also met. Their territory stretched from Greenland and Labrador
in the eastern regions to Alaska and the Bering Strait on the western coast.
Within this area, the Inuit groups had much in common but also a great deal
of variation; they shared the language, if some dialects became less intelli-
gible with larger distances, and their technologies and travel-​patterns would
shift from region to region (Friesen 2022: 18). Still, they were in many ways
one people, if living and moving in a region that posed different challenges.
   The American Arctic, setting the scene of this book, was formatted by
geological, geographic, biological, human, and other long-​term agents in the
region, but it befell the early ethnographers, eagerly moving into an unknown
terrain, to write the early Inuit history (see Krupnik 2016). The ethnographic
pioneers who met and lived with the Inuit, wrote down their stories, and
described their ways of living as hunters in the Arctic, were often humbled by
the knowledge and strength of these people who braved the arid landscapes
and the limited game.
   The early ethnographers left a rich repository of testimonies to their
meetings with unknown people and strange landscapes. Sometimes they
were aided by old reports written by passing sailors, or notes left by traders
and trappers who had gone before them. Invariably, the pioneers evoked a
world that was perceived as new, if not unknown in the strict sense of the
term. Discovery transformed into a more distinct, scholarly ethnography in
                                                                   Prologue ix

late 19th century, also in the Arctic where Franz Boas was a leading ethnog-
rapher (Boas [1888] 1964, 1901, 1907). His work spearheaded the so-​called
great ethnographic period in the Arctic from c. 1900 until c. 1930 (Damas
1984: 4).
   The history of discovery and description bends and stretches in many
directions, in both time and space, in the Arctic and elsewhere. Clearly,
Christopher Columbus’ “1492 encounter with the Americas marks a water-
shed in the history of Arctic exploration, in that it opened a new set of
frontiers for Europeans to ‘discover’ and provided new incentives to spur
these discoveries on” (McCannon 2018: 21). The early Arctic exploration,
in the 1500s and 1600s, was partly motivated by economic and partly by
less distinct colonial interests (ibid.). New interests in possible sea routes and
big marine game spurred sailors and traders to fan out in the Arctic waters.
In the process, they met the indigenous inhabitants –​at least on the coasts.
Later arrivals in the 1700s and 1800s took interest also in the people and
animals on land, if still with their own agendas.
   The growing ethnographic literature in late 19th and early 20th century
shifted the scholarly interest from observation, tied up with an attempt to
demonstrate the laws of evolution from simplicity to complexity, to learning –​
that is to understand the complex whole of people, languages, landscapes,
dwellings, animals, materials, and dreams. The massive ethnographic work
from ca. 1900 to ca. 1930, reflects a veritable intellectual conversion from a
dominant natural history, searching for general laws, to an emerging anthro-
pology –​supporting multiple ways of knowing through direct engagement
with both people and their landscapes.
   The chapters to follow are not historically organized; they have each their
theme, reaching backwards and forwards in time. The unfolding histories
of the ethnographers’ meetings with new people, their unearthing the past,
their reporting to funding institutions, and their hardships in the cold give
a strong sense of will and effort, stretching across large regions within and
beyond Arctic America as such. Naturally, Inuit life was already part of a
larger American history, but it became of new interest in the wake of new
arrivals. Boundaries and ‘tribes’ were created, all while people moved from
place to place and hardly had a name –​beyond their favourite game and their
extensive movements in the landscape.
   The centenary of the Fifth Thule Expedition (1921–​1924) headed by
the Danish ethnographer Knud Rasmussen (1879–​1933), offers an oppor-
tune moment to look back on early perceptions of the Arctic region and its
inhabitants. The expedition crossed Arctic America from east to west, from
Greenland to Alaska, mainly by dog sledge but also by boat along the coast,
where sledging was impossible. At the time, not all the Arctic communities
had yet been visited, let alone described by outsiders; few would be totally
unknown, however. Whalers, traders, missionaries, naturalists, explorers,
or, indeed, neighbouring Native Americans would have touched upon the
x Prologue

Inuit along rivers or on the Arctic coasts. The ethnographic field expanded
through meetings with new people as well as new landscapes, materials, and
animals.
   Before anthropologists arrived with their distinct calling and ambition,
foreigners with commercial or clerical interests had already left a mark. If the
ethnographers were not the first to meet the Inuit, their singular achievement
was to carefully document their ways of life, to collect their stories, poems,
and artefacts and to store them for posteriority. This was based on a direct,
long-​term engagement with the Inuit, their landscape and its seasonality,
their hunting practices, and ingenuous technologies. The early ethnographers
instantiated a kind of knowing that pushed back the sense of the Arctic as
unknowable and of its inhabitants as others by default.
   Most Inuit were still itinerant in early 20th century, especially in the
Eastern and Central Arctic, where they depended on the seasonal migrations
of animals and the shifting conditions of the sea-​ice. Their life was precar-
ious, and a careful reading of the landscape, sea, and animals was needed.
While generally mastering this, sometimes whole communities would
succumb to hunger, due to unfavourable weathers, blocked passages, or
disappearing game –​calamities that were often connected. This transpires
in the writings of pioneer ethnographers, whose observations were based
in personal encounters and experiences of the Arctic, also showing how the
region became absorbed into the world of scholarship, and ‘edited’ in the
process.
   In a recent volume on The Inuit World, it is suggested that the “Inuit worlds
we know today began to take shape in the 19th century” (Stern 2022: 2).
This underscores the significance of early ethnographic work among the
Inuit, who were not isolated before Europeans arrived, but who still had to
be ‘written’. People moved and met, as will be seen in the following chapters
focusing on relations rather than tribes. The extensive references to the early
ethnographic (and other) works in the chapters to follow show how know-
ledge of the Inuit expanded. Each chapter has its own theme and time frame,
making the larger result a kind of memoir. It refers to a time when (old)
Europeans and (new) Americans first met the impressive Inuit inhabitants of
the Arctic and described them in vocabularies that were often made along
the way. The coastal Alaskan groups had been known outside of the region
for some time, due to the presence of whalers and explorers with access to
Alaska from the Pacific and the Bering Strait, while the Central and Eastern
Inuit were less known by outsiders –​except, perhaps, the Inuit in Labrador,
having been under Moravian charge since the 18th century.
   We shall meet many of the pioneering ethnographers in the following
chapters and listen to their observations of the Inuit from each of their
vantage point. What unites them is their direct engagement with the land-
scape, moving along with the Inuit and their living like them. Thus, they
parted company from earlier explorers who were mostly bound to their ships
(some of them sinking) and to their canned food from Europe (some of it
                                                                  Prologue xi

poisonous). They were soon to be known as anthropologists –​a term that
pointed to an emerging discipline, still known by that term a hundred years
hence. Both terms will be used rather freely in this book.
    The early ethnographers, trained or untrained, published rich and often
moving descriptions, but these are neither proof of Inuit ways of life nor the
truth about them. Like historical texts, ethnographic writings may be seen
as a “reconstruction of the relationship (about which we know so little)
between individual lives and the contexts in which they unfold” (Ginzburg
1994: 301). In post-​positivist 21st century we know that the anthropo-
logical object is always emergent, the result of a particular intervention
and analytical scope (Hastrup 2004: 468). This also applies to early ethno-
graphic work in the Arctic, however much of the reports were cast as direct
documentation, setting us free to rethink and reframe early observations in
multiple vocabularies, being part of a long anthropological history. Looking
back is not a historical exercise; it is always contemporary, the richer for
its time depth.
    The chapters to follow move rather freely between periods and places, cap-
turing the volatility of social, historical, and regional developments. At the
centre of the work stands the great ethnographic period, introduced above,
taking off around 1900 and culminating with the Fifth Thule Expedition.
Boas’ early work in Baffin Land (1983–​1984) was a precursor to this period
as were some important contributions by others in Labrador and Alaska.
David Damas, who edited an impressive volume on the Arctic, suggests that
after the great period, “there was a virtual hiatus in ethnological inquiry
among the Eskimos for about 30 years, though results from earlier research
continued to be published” (Damas 1984: 4). Consequently, the results of
the great ethnographic period continued to dominate the perception of the
Inuit for a long time, all while the world changed.
    The Fifth Thule Expedition was not a conventional anthropological exer-
cise, implying a long-​time co-​habitation with a particular community on
the fringe of the modern world. Rasmussen was constantly on the move,
partly following the trails of itinerant hunters, partly his own impulse to visit
any Inuit population within reach. This obviously affected his ethnographic
work, that may not always have been up to scholarly expectations in the
1920s. Rasmussen had no university degree, but his energy and his flair for
Inuit life and language largely made up for this at a time when ethnography
was still mainly descriptive. Concerning language, narratives, and poetry he
offered a new understanding of Inuit worldviews as they were transmitted
to him in various genres. Adding to this he unpacked the relations between
the Inuit groups and between them and the newcomers to the region, be they
whalers, traders, clergymen, or others (Hastrup 2016). His exceptional con-
tribution was to insist on visiting all Inuit communities from east to west,
and to write down their tales and poetry, even if he sometimes had to bar-
gain for it. It is clear from Rasmussen’s writings –​including his diaries –​that
he found the situation of the inhabitants gradually degrading as he and his
xii Prologue

companions moved west. He found them too implicated in the modern world
and lost some of his optimism and energy.
    It is tempting to look at the Tristes Tropiques met by Claude Lévi-​Strauss
in central Brazil in the 1930s and described in his book by that name (Lévi-​
Strauss 1955). For Lévi-​Strauss the tristesse was related to the disenchant-
ment of meeting the footprints of Europeans all over the place, even in the
deep forest. The New World was hardly new. Not only had it been inhabited
for millennia by people gradually descending from North America, leaving
traces of several ‘high’ cultures; it had also been visited and inhabited by
Europeans, including missionaries, since the 16th century. There were still
vast stretches with sparsely documented indigenous groups in the uncharted
landscapes of forests and plains, but even here there were marks of Old-​
World dreams.
    The landscape of the sad tropes of South America, was one of trees and
rivers and stunted vision; the Arctic landscape had a different tenor. The
high latitudes were not only cold (as are mountain tops in South America)
they were also marked by a particular ecosystem of ‘low heat energy’ and an
absence of trees (Freeman 1984). Comparing the bare and open American
Arctic with the hidden South American interior enables us to see the Arctic
ethnographies in a clearer light, and not least in a larger global perspec-
tive, preventing Arctic anthropology to fold in on itself. As self-​taught eth-
nographer, Rasmussen certainly differed from Lévi-​Strauss, but they shared
the practice of mobile fieldwork, as well as impatience and intellectual
uncertainty.
    Whither the world? The Nambikwara in Brazil and the Caribou Inuit in
the Arctic (and other groups), were equally perceived by their ethnographers
as implicated in the modern world, however faint the imprint seemed at first.
It is a decidedly pessimist anthropology in both cases. In an essay on Lévi-​
Strauss, Susan Sontag suggests how he, while “lamenting among the shadows,
struggling to distinguish the archaic from the pseudo-​archaic, he acts out a
heroic, diligent, and complex modern pessimism” (Sontag 1970: 196). The
image of intellectual estrangement in the field points to a tension between
subject and object, with which anthropologists must live.
    The landscape works upon the ethnographers, be it in the deep and dark
forest or in the treeless, icy wilderness. While there are of course different
“physiographic provinces” that can be identified in the Arctic –​high moun-
tains, some covered by glacial ice, sedimentary plain, exposed bedrock, and
lowlands –​there is also “a curious sameness to the landscape” everywhere
but in the mountainous regions (Stager and McSkimming 1984: 33). This is
largely due to the prehistoric movement of massive continental glaciers that
once rounded the surfaces of the land. In the Arctic, the sameness of land-
scape “is made more complete by the absence of trees or other tall vegeta-
tion, as well as the winterlong blanket of snow and ice” (ibid.). Behind it we
find a deep, continental history reaching back to the earliest times.
                                                                Prologue xiii

   Inuit landscapes have changed since the early part of the 20th century, as
has ethnography –​now absorbed into a professional anthropology of intel-
lectual standing. It still pays to ask what we should mean by ‘ethnography’
a hundred years later, given the climatic, political, social, and intellectual
changes we are facing. Ethnography never stands untouched by history, it is
history –​not from past to present, but the other way round. The same applies
to names; the Inuit were once known as Eskimos (by outsiders), until it was
decided at the Inuit Circumpolar Conference in 1977 that the proper name
was to be Inuit. The names diversify now, as the communities become more
sedentary and less alike, even while still summarized under the umbrella of
The Inuit World (Stern 2022). Like people, names travel and history shifts –​
as do allegiances within larger political entities. Every ethnographic observa-
tion is provisional, as human life cannot be separated from landscapes and
histories that are always in the making.
   The great ethnographic period in the American Arctic unfolded a cen-
tury ago, but is assessed from the present, obviously. Rather than offering
an inventory of ethnographies, tribes, writings, artefacts, and stories, we
shall revisit all of them from different analytical perspectives. This allows
us to see Inuit life as consisting of individuals, narratives, and sensations –​
fragments of a past that are still with us. Even while the early ethnographers
did their best to write down Inuit stories exactly as they were told, they still
reflected a particular moment and a singular conversation. In the process the
Inuit became characters in their own story, as written by others. Keeping the
world open to many sensations makes the ethnographer realize that his or
her writing is always rooted in personal and situated perceptions. ‘Worlds’
are never fixed; they are experienced at a certain time –​if often written in
another. There is the more reason to analyse the turning points, including the
resolve of humans to share their lives, dreams, and worries with outsiders,
who happen to visit them.
   The present book is yet another fragment of a long history of anthropo-
logical interest in Arctic America, based on snapshots from the vast archive
of ethnographic work, neither showing how it was, nor how it is, but how it
may be seen. The region is in some sense unfathomable, even though under-
written by professional ethnographers since their arrival in the 1880s (with
Boas). Their writings feature prominently in the following chapters, where
we are also in company with explorers, traders, clerics, mounted police, and
not least Inuit –​whose movements, voices, and hunting practices are spun
together in the unpacking of features that made the lived Arctic.
   The question of otherness implicitly permeates the pages of this book. Who
were ‘the others’ when the pioneering travellers and explorers from the 19th
century onwards found their way into Inuit lands? How could professional
anthropologists describe the Arctic people without becoming implicated? As
the newcomers engaged with long-​time inhabitants, their vision changed and
a new world emerged in the uncharted landscape that had neither a definite
xiv Prologue

depth of time, nor an easily demarcated population that could be definitively
boxed into a certain scheme. The world we, humans, live with is always
marked by unconformities and multiple time scales, as Hugh Raffles has
recently, and convincingly argued (Raffles 2020). Where does the story start
in any landscape, always deeply marked by many pasts?
   The Tristes Arctiques, introduced above, is not a token of misery
or a sense of loss, but a reminder of the need to face the power of lan-
guage and the making of ethnographic subjects, individually and collect-
ively. Anthropologists must beware of their documentary propensity when
presenting the results of fieldwork as if finite. It may be an important contri-
bution, but it remains a fleeting story –​reflecting a particular moment and
a distinct way of thinking, connecting rather than separating humans across
the globe, and through the ages.
   All knowledge is interstitial –​not only so in time, but also in lived life,
always affected by meetings, conversations, co-​habitation, and co-​travelling
parties –​seeking to summarize what can never be fixed. The following
chapters may be seen as a meta-​travelogue, offering a glimpse of the deep
infiltration of lives and landscapes, of Inuit and ethnographers –​and of past
and present thinking. A key issue is to acknowledge the formative role played
by the Inuit in the making of ethnographic subjects. Finally, we must rec-
ognize that anthropological work is always double-​sided; the ‘others’ are
constituted through outside observations –​and it goes both ways.
