1	The New World
       Anthropologists in the Wilderness




The early anthropologists in the Arctic showed both trepidation and wonder
when meeting the natives of the region who had been almost invisible to the
modern gaze until the 18th century. Yet, their being unknown was never the
same as being unknowable, and the scholars found the Inuit to be as access-
ible (or inaccessible) as were their landscapes –​however wild they seemed
at first.
    This chapter takes off in the relation between human life and its nat-
ural environment –​or, in the words of Franz Boas, “the mutual influence
of the earth and its inhabitants upon each other” (Boas 1887: 140) cannot
be overestimated. More shall be said about this later, here we shall just take
Boas’ statement as a point of entry into the field of Arctic anthropology,
to which he himself came by way of geography (and physics), and within
which he was one of the founding fathers. For a generation or more, he
was American Anthropology (Powell 2015: 28). The idea of ethnographic
fieldwork, as living with and to some degree as the people studied, was a
side effect of his early geographical expedition to an Arctic environment that
would never allow him to travel or live on his own. His original idea to
focus on the mutual causality between nature and social life was gradually
shattered.
    Looking back, he wrote in 1938:

  I decided to make a journey to the Arctic for the purpose of adding to
  our knowledge of unknown regions and of helping me to understand
  the reaction of the human mind to natural environment. A year spent
  as an Eskimo, among Eskimos had a profound influence upon the devel-
  opment of my views, not immediately, but because it led me away from
  former interests and toward the desire to understand what determines
  the behavior of human beings. The first result of my attempts to explain
  human behavior as a result of geographical environment was a thorough
  disappointment.
                                                         (Boas 1938: 202)


                                           DOI: 10.4324/9781003374145-1
2 The New World

Boas worked at a time when most Inuit groups had already been in touch with
other Americans or Europeans, be they explorers, whalers, traders, trappers,
or would-​be settlers. Yet, somehow, they remained categorically apart from the
newcomers, according to the travellers. The paradox of being both implicated
and apart from the rest of the world is a general feature of social life, wherever
it unfolds. While neither the oneness of humanity nor of the globe is in doubt,
people still live differently in the world and within different landscapes. This is
what anthropologists have studied since its inception, and still do, if now on
a very different geopolitical background. When the discipline emerged in late
19th century there were still communities living beyond the world of books,
and anthropologists saw themselves as their chroniclers. They would read and
compare exploration narratives, colonial reports, missionary archives, and
learned treatises, and draw some general conclusions about people. Around
1900, the distinct method of fieldwork was established, which was to become
the hallmark of the new, professional anthropology. It was a time when proper
ethnographic subjects emerged (Hastrup 2013).
    Evidently, fieldwork has changed since then, as has anthropological
thinking. Yet here we shall stay with the beginnings of what became a
particular Arctic anthropology, within an increasing European interest in
the New World, beckoning anthropologists to engage with the two ‘new’
continents, North and South America. What drew them was precisely the
sense of newness –​even after centuries of contact and colonization –​at least
on the fringe of the woods and the sea. What was also new, was anthro-
pology itself –​an emerging scholarly discipline offering solid knowledge
about the world of ‘others’ beyond the colonial gaze. Added to this was an
increasing commercial traffic by ship across the Atlantic that made it easier
to leave Europe behind.


Another Continent
Boas is acknowledged as the founder of American anthropology, even if
many others contributed. In the Arctic field, Boas was unquestionably the
spearhead (Krupnik 2016a). For him and his contemporaries, anthropo-
logical fieldwork was mainly designed to describe the language and culture
of individual groups before they were completely swallowed up by modern
American ways of life. While Boas did not continue his initial ethnographic
work in the Arctic, others would soon take over and herald a time of great
expeditions to Inuit lands. Among them were Vilhjalmur Stefansson’s
Canadian Arctic Expeditions (1908–​      1912 and 1913–​  1918) and Knud
Rasmussen’s Fifth Thule Expedition (1921–​1924), to mention just their most
spectacular contributions to the geographical and intellectual extension of
the Arctic.
   In the present chapter we shall also reach out to South America pursuing
the dialogue with Claude Lévi-​Strauss, introduced in the Prelude. His work
may seem wide apart from Boas’ vision of straightforward ethnographic
documentation, yet the two of them met in New York in the early 1940s,
                                                            The New World 3

and Boas was to play some role in Lévi-​Strauss’ intellectual development.
They shared an interest in mythological details, among other things, and
both were new to America –​Boas as a long-​time immigrant from Germany,
Lévi-​Strauss a recently arrived refugee from Europe at war. Both were deeply
affected by their American ethnographic experiences, one in the North, the
other in the South. Both were concerned about the world and about racial
prejudice and took their discipline seriously as more than simple documenta-
tion. Lévi-​Strauss, in a presentation from 1986 in Japan, says that although
the anthropologist seeks the bizarre in singular communities, the larger ambi-
tion of the discipline concentrates on “the human phenomenon” as part on
the larger natural phenomenon (Lévi-​Strauss 2011: 18).
   Lévi-​Strauss’ early fieldwork took place in Brazil in three distinct
campaigns into the interior. He stressed that this method not only gave access
to knowledge about particular tribes, it also contributed to a general know-
ledge of human development that was not to be reserved for historians. The
farther away the examples were from our own world, the more profound the
gains of ethnography.

  They show us that it is about a phenomenon, having the character of a
  very small and particular community, but because it studies very simple
  societies, it also shows what is a characteristic phenomenon of humanity
  in its very essence, that it is only to the degree to which all of the different
  centres of human culture are in contact with each other that human and
  social progress can be found; only isolated societies are inert societies, and
  only societies in contact progress.
                                (Lévi-​Strauss [1937] 2016: 62; author’s transl.)

In other words, to find the deeper truth about humankind, one must some-
times suspend the complex and deeply entangled histories of modern soci-
eties and look behind the curtain, as it were, to find the essence of human life.
In low-​density communities, the social phenomena present themselves much
more directly to the researchers’ gaze (ibid.: 44). This reflects a view also of
the small communities in the New World being on the outside of history,
and therefore barely readable and calling for the ethnographer’s presence.
An implication is that when we study people in a particular field, they are
already part of a comprehensive world of both ‘us’ and ‘them’ –​implicitly
dissolving the idea of radical otherness.
   With such claim, time had come to decolonize the anthropological mind
and to get past ancient visions of the charming children of the North, as
expressed by Fridtjof Nansen, the Norwegian polar explorer and national
hero. In a preface to Diamond Jenness’ ethnographic work with the
Canadian Arctic Expedition in the Coronation Gulf Region, Nansen writes
the following:

  A charming people of happy children, not yet stung by the venom of our
  culture, not burdened by the intricate problems and the acid dissatisfaction
4 The New World

   of our society. Starving sometimes perhaps when food is scarce (but that
   does not last long); and when food is plentiful life is laughing happiness
   with no sorrows of the past and none for the morrow. They live hap-
   pily under extreme conditions. During ages they have learnt to master
   the severe forces of nature, and no other people can take their place and
   develop the possibilities of those northern regions. But contact with our
   civilization, when not carefully guarded as in Greenland, will upset the
   whole system of their life and community, and they must sink.
      The greedy white men wish to get hold of their furs; but what do we give
   them instead? Our products, some of which are of very doubtful value to
   them, indeed, and then our ghastly, insidious diseases, and often our bad
   customs and morals.
      They had fitted their life into the conditions of their surrounding; with
   their old weapons taxing the game sufficiently to satisfy amply their
   requirements without reducing the stock. Then we bring them rifles, nay,
   even magazine rifles, offering to buy skins. The slaughter begins; the game,
   formerly so abundant, will in a few years be more or less exterminated,
   and the main condition that made their life possible be gone.
      What a sad guilt we white men have towards native races! But these
   people live in a land that will be of no value to us without them.
                                  (Nansen 1928; in Jenness [1928] 1959: v–​vi)

Nansen wrote this in 1928, when Jenness’ book was first published. Nansen
was already acquainted with Rasmussen’s early publications from the Fifth
Thule expedition. What is more, Nansen himself had written a book on
Eskimo Life (1891) based on a long sojourn on the west-​coast of Greenland,
after having crossed the inland ice on skies with a small group of people
including two Saami. In 1897, he had published two volumes on his daring
three-​year exploration of the Arctic Sea (1893–​1896; Nansen 1897); to this
he added a major review of Arctic exploration through the ages (Nansen
1911). He knew the Arctic very well, as his lamenting of the fate of the Inuit
proves. Nansen had received the Nobel Peace Prize in 1922 for his humani-
tarian work as the first High Commissioner for Refugees, appointed by the
League of Nations. He also was a very prominent Arctic naturalist; his words
mattered.
    Jenness, who had been invited to take part in the Canadian Arctic
Expedition in 1913 and remained in the region for several years, saw his
local hosts in a different manner, more eye-​to-​eye, and with less interest in the
other groups present in the region, be they traders, sailors, police, or other.
Something had happened in the years between Jenness’ actual experience and
Nansen’s outburst in his preface of 1928. The modern world had definitively
infiltrated even the most distant Arctic regions and the last unknowns among
its inhabitants; these people had already become history, as it were. This is
where the Tristes Arctiques begin to make itself felt.
                                                            The New World 5

    While there are almost unimaginable differences between the dense trop-
ical forests in Brazil, where Lévi-​Strauss sojourned, and the ice-​clad Arctic
landscapes, I have had a lingering sense that they somehow mirrored each
other, not least in their being home to people that were cast as ultimate others
until recently. This feature of ‘radical alterity’ (e.g., Taussig 1993) is part of
what we shall explore in this book. It is very much an outsider’s perspective,
and one that for a long time froze the images of the ‘the others’. The alleged
tristesse likewise is orchestrated by outsiders, moving into the region and
meeting people that were impressive, yet also seemed doomed.
    When first immersing myself in High Arctic Greenland, the field as well
as the literature, it struck me that there were intriguing parallels between
the Arctic world, as it had been transmitted through more than a century,
and the one that Lévi-​Strauss met with in Brazil all these years ago. I also
found a certain likeness between the intellectual challenges gluing to his
time and to ours, as global uncertainties multiply, and all parts of the world
are implicated in each other. The High Arctic landscape is of course vastly
different from the Brazilian interior, but both were strongly marked as fron-
tier zones when they were first studied. Rereading Tristes Tropiques recently,
it struck me how Lévi-​Strauss augurs the more-​than-​human socialities of
which we speak these years as if just invented (e.g., Lien and Pálsson 2021).
    The analogy between the Tropics and the Arctic in this context serves to
qualify the field, within which this book is placed, both as a frontier and as a
particular intellectual landscape. Another important point is to substantiate
Lévi-​Strauss’ implicit recognition that in anthropological practice, there is
no ‘before’ and ‘after’ exploration, no pristine state –​and no ethnographic
fact that is not also already an analysis from a particular point of view.
Commenting on Tristes Tropiques, Lévi-​       Strauss retrospectively suggested
that it pointed to a “certain scientific truth, which is perhaps greater than in
our objective works because what I did was to reintegrate the observer into
the object of his observation” (cited in Wilcken 2010: 202). Here, any vision
of accessing ‘other’ worldviews without contaminating them breaks down.
The implications will be clearer as we move into the Arctic along with the
pioneering anthropologists in the region.
    In both South and North America, the wilderness, be it the wide vistas of
the barren Arctic or the overgrown landscapes hiding the Brazilian interior,
formatted the ethnographers’ thinking. In both cases, the newcomers
were lost if not for words, then certainly for comprehension; simple posi-
tivist documentation invariably gave in to overpowering sensations. The
anthropologists became post-​modern before the fact.


Illegible Landscapes
On his way to the unknown interior of the Canadian Arctic from the eastern
coast, Rasmussen writes about a “flat, wild, desolate country, with little
to guide the stranger” ([1927] 1999: 55). He continues at some length,
6 The New World

describing how everything was white, and how hour after hour they trav-
elled on, never seeming to get any farther, and having a constant feeling of
possibly going wrong.

  But as a matter of fact, it mattered little which direction we took, for from
  the day we left the coast we had realized that no information could be
  gleaned even from one settlement as to the position of another, since the
  various parties were always on the move, taking up their quarters here or
  there according to the movements of the game.
                                                 (Rasmussen [1927] 1999: 55)

In other words, the entanglement of landscapes and lives prohibited ethnog-
raphy at a distance in the manner of Bronislaw Malinowski, who studied
the natives “from the door of my tent” as the trope became (Malinowski
1922). In the Arctic, the native inhabitants were beyond view most of the
time, or they were met on the move, and when found by Rasmussen (or
others) they often had to share their tents and offer up their own stories and
material contributions to get a conversation going. The landscape always
made itself felt. It was different for Lévi-​Strauss, when he landed in the Bay
of Rio in Brazil in 1934 and could not see the landscape and the beauty of its
components at first. As a man of reading, he saw through others’ eyes, and
recalled Columbus who wrote down his first impressions:

  The trees were so high that they seemed to touch the sky; and, if I under-
  stood aright, they never lose their leaves; for they were as fresh and as
  green in November as ours are in the months of May; some were even
  in flower, and others were bearing fruit …And wherever I turned the
  nightingales were singing, accompanied by thousands of other birds of
  one sort or another.
                              (Columbus, quoted in Lévi-​Strauss 1963: 84)

Lévi-​Strauss comments: “That’s America: the continent makes itself felt at
once.” He adds the observation that America is made up by a manifold of
presences, shapes, movements, and patches of light that the newly arrived
will not be able to single out or even see. For the newcomer these shapes do
not stand out in their individuality.

  No: it all strikes him as an entity, unique and all comprehending. What
  surrounded me on every side, what overwhelmed me, was not the inex-
  haustible diversity of people and things, but that one single and redoubt-
  able entity: The New World.
                                                     (Lévi-​Strauss 1963: 84)

Imagine a time when one could still confidently think of the New World in
general; it was new to Lévi-​Strauss, of course, but that is not what he is saying
                                                          The New World 7

here. We also note the slowness of arrival at his time. The sense of distance –​
first by the long journey across the ocean and, second, by the strangeness of
the untamed landscapes, became enfolded into the understanding, as does
the road towards the field in general, be it Tropical or Arctic. Along the
strenuous path towards the interior, the telegraph poles and trading posts,
not to speak of the pots and rifles by which the forest-​dwellers were supplied,
constantly reminded Lévi-​Strauss of the modern world, from which one
could no longer truly escape –​even if one could also not decode or even ‘see’
the landscape for what it was.
    At the northern extremity of the not so New World, in the Arctic, first
arrivals often gave people a sense of a landscape that could not be decoded
in bits. It affected the newcomers’ senses in its entirety as an immeasurable,
empty vastness. Yet, even here there were unexpected camps of people around
the corner, part of the larger world, moving along with the game. Rasmussen
had heard of a famine among the Caribou Inuit, a likely scenario, but when
nearing their camp, he was relieved.

  To my great relief, the famine we had expected to encounter was already
  a thing of the past. In front of the tent lay a pile of dead caribou, so many
  indeed that it was difficult to count them. A month before, the people had
  been on the verge of starvation, but now all was changed. Igjugarjuk at
  once gave orders for an extravagant banquet in our honor, and two large
  caribou were put on to boil in huge zinc cauldrons.
     I had expected to find these people in quite a primitive state, and in
  this respect, was disappointed beyond measure. What we did find was
  the worst kind of tinpot store and canned provision culture; a product
  of trading expeditions to the distant Hudson’s Bay Company’s Stations.
  And when a powerful gramophone struck up, and Caruso’s mighty voice
  rang out from Igjugarjuk’s tent, I felt that we had missed our market, as
  far as the study of these people was concerned. We were about a hun-
  dred years too late. Save for their appearance, which was of pronounced
  Eskimo type, they were more like Indians than Eskimos. Their tents were
  of the pointed Indian pattern, made of caribou skins with a smoke hole
  at the top, and in each, on the left hand side, burned the Uvkak, or tent
  fire. All the women wore colored shawls over their skin dresses, just as the
  Indian women do.
                                                  (Rasmussen [1927] 1999: 63)

Rasmussen adds that the only unadulterated ‘Eskimo element’ he had
to work with was the language. He was pleased to note that his own
Greenlandic was immediately understood and was flattered when the
host, Igjugarjuk, “declared that I was the first white man he had ever seen
who was also an Eskimo” ([1927) 1999: 64). While of course people are
essential for ethnographic work anywhere, in the Arctic at his time the
traversing of unknown landscapes and even finding people was part of the
8 The New World

challenge. Moving in a landscape, where nothing was truly measurable by
one’s ordinary sense, made the open Arctic as indiscernible as the tropical
rainforest.
   Lévi-​Strauss is explicit about his relative pleasure in different landscapes.
He does not like the sea; he feels diminished by the mass of water that robs
him of more than half his universe. What is more, “It seems to me that
the sea destroys the normal variety of the earth” (Lévi-​Strauss 1963: 332),
offering only monotony and sameness. He prefers mountains to the sea,
although admittedly, his feelings did not extend to the high mountains.
Their delights are physical, almost abstract, drawing one’s attention “away
from the splendours of Nature and entirely engrossed by preoccupations
relating rather to mechanics or geometry” (ibid.: 333). The landscape of
lower mountains, preferably with pastures, is much more to his liking. He
surmises: “If the sea presents, in my opinion, a landscape many degrees
below proof, mountains offer, by contrast, a world in a state of intense
concentration” (ibid.: 333–​4). While the sea offered only a lifeless surface,
the climbs, and the shifting, often narrowing vistas offered in the moun-
tains invited him to a kind of conversation. And yet, he eventually had to
admit that,

   although I do not feel that I myself have changed, my love for the moun-
   tains is draining away like a wave running backwards down the sand.
   My thoughts are unchanged, but the mountains have taken leave of me.
   Their unchanging joys mean less and less to me, so long and intently have
   I thought them out.
                                                   (Lévi-​Strauss 1963: 334)

These thoughts are important because they illustrate how ethnographers
appropriated different landscapes in a personal, and emotionally charged (if
mostly checked) language. Moving into the Arctic, we find a comparable sense of
the overpowering nature, in which the newcomers found themselves affected at
a deeply personal level. In the early expeditions it was the very emptiness that hit
the travellers as almost barring any life, as happened for Rasmussen:

   There is scarcely any country on earth that present conditions more severe
   and inclement for man than the most easterly parts of the Northwest
   Passage, for it lies waste and bare of all that is otherwise considered neces-
   sary to life: and yet there the Netsilingmiut for generations have known
   how to wage the struggle for existence, in such a manner that strangers
   coming among them will involuntarily receive the impression that it is a
   people who desire no better hunting grounds than these, the very ones
   where their ancestors developed that special culture which they have faith-
   fully handed down from father to son.
                                                       (Rasmussen 1931: 131–​2)
                                                           The New World 9

Clearly, it is impossible for Rasmussen to keep people out of the landscape –​
and vice versa; the two go together. He makes this very clear, saying that “as
no nation in the world can be seen except on the background of the country
in which its lives, the surrounding natural conditions are reflected in cus-
toms and habits, thoughts and mind” (Rasmussen 1931: 133). Thus, it is no
surprise that the Netsilingmiut has become a hardy people, of many crude
customs “that bear the stamp of primitiveness and stone age” (ibid.). In the
Tropics, the vistas are different, but equally elusive. The forests are mostly
too tangled to be even ‘seen’, and people disappear within them. When
arriving to the Bororo in the dense rainforest, Lévi-​Strauss was overwhelmed
by the very obscurity of the settlement and he reflects back also on others:

  So profound, and yet also so confused, are one’s first impressions of a
  native village whose civilisation has remained relatively intact that it is
  difficult to know in which order to set them down. Among the Kaingang
  –​and the same is true of the Caduveo –​extremes of poverty inspire in the
  traveller an initial weariness and discouragement. But there are societies
  so vividly alive, so faithful to their traditions, that their impact is discon-
  certingly strong, and one cannot tell which of the myriad of threads which
  make up the skein is the one to follow. It was among the Bororo that I first
  encountered a problem of this sort.
                                                       (Lévi-​Strauss 1963: 198)

The problem was to identify the details, for “these houses were not so much
built as knotted together, plaited, woven, embroidered and given a patina by
long use” (ibid.). The houses were an incomprehensible structure in jungle,
which Lévi-​Strauss only understood when he ‘settled in’ in the corner of the
huge hutment, where “I did not so much take in these things as allow myself
to be impregnated by them. Certain details fell into place” (ibid.: 199). While
a long way from Arctic, Lévi-​Strauss’ observation testifies to the power of
unknown landscapes and new materials, with which anthropologists must
deal in any field, North or South.
   Even the most amazing vistas in a particular region, may end up by taking
leave of you, and you are lost in a kind of tristesse, unless of course you find
a new attraction. This happened for Lévi-​Strauss, who relates how, having
crossed, and recrossed the desert-​like savannahs of central Brazil had taught
him to “appreciate anew the luxuriant Nature beloved of the ancients: young
grass, flowers, and the dewy freshness of brakes” (Lévi-​Strauss 1963: 335).
Wherever he goes, the world must be re-​interpreted, and he laments the way
in which Europeans had settled for less than the dense forest, having scaled
down passions to what was still within reach.

  But in that forced march we had forgotten the forest. As dense as our
  cities, it was inhabited by other beings –​beings organized in a society
  which, better than either the high peaks or the sun-​baked flatlands, had
10 The New World

  known to keep us at a distance: a collectivity of trees and plants that
  covered our tracks as soon as we had passed. Often difficult to penetrate,
  the forest demands of those who enter it concessions every bit as weighty,
  if less spectacular, than those exacted by the mountains from the walker.
  Its horizon, less extensive than that of the great mountain ranges, closes
  in on the traveller, isolating him as completely as any of the desert’s empty
  perspectives. A world of grasses, flowers, mushrooms, and insects leads
  there an independent life of its own, to which patience and humility are
  our only passports. A hundred yards from the edge of the forest, and the
  world outside is abolished. One universe gives way to another –​less agree-
  able to look at, but rich in rewards for senses nearer to the spirit: hearing,
  I mean, and smell. Good things one had thought never to experience again
  are restored to one: silence, coolness, peace. In our intimacy with the vege-
  table world, we enjoy these things which the sea can no longer give us and
  for which the mountains exact too high a price.
                                                      (Lévi-​Strauss 1963: 335)

Silence, coolness, and peace can also be experienced in the Arctic, if often
pierced –​not by the delicate song of little birds but by the scary cries of
seagulls or crows, the ever-​present scavengers in the region even during the
polar night, and by the roaring storms that block out all other sound. When
close to human habitation the calm may be broken by the sound of dogs,
without which there would be no community of the kind that we can still
meet in Northwest Greenland (Hastrup 2018). What these ‘other’ landscapes
have in common is not only the overwhelming power to undo the geograph-
ical gaze to which one has been accustomed, but also their collapsing any
sense of historical time.
   Both past and present and Old and New Worlds, are intermingled –​des-
pite the freshness that one first experiences. Everywhere, relations are what
make the world, be they relations between people and their landscape or
between continents. When Lévi-​Strauss sat out to discover the unknowns of
the Tropics, he found a new version of the Old World: “The Tropics are not
so much exotic as out of date. It’s not the vegetation which confirms that
you are ‘really there’, but certain trifling architectural details and the hint
of a way of life which would suggest that you had gone backwards in time
rather than forwards across a great part of the earth’s surface” (Lévi-​Strauss
1963: 91). Implicitly, Lévi-​Strauss acknowledged humans as part of the pic-
ture, and as he went out to map the natives he was met by the modern world
in old clothes.
   Something similar could be claimed in the Arctic; while in many ways
‘apart’ from the modern world, people in the Arctic were already implicated
in it. Implicitly, they were known before the ethnographers wrote them
down, so to speak, and they still are if from a new point in history. The fas-
cination of the North is intact, as is the idea of the Arctic landscape as one,
unique, landscape of living –​not least owing to “the absence of trees or other
                                                         The New World 11

tall vegetation, as well as the winter-​long blanket of snow and ice” (Stager
and McSkimming 1984: 33).
    Lévi-​Strauss’ fascination with the South American continent never ceased,
even if he did not repeat his early fieldworks in the region. In 1998, he was
interviewed by Didier Éribon about his long life in anthropology; the con-
versation turned into a book, where Lévi-​Strauss could, and did not want
to hide his deep sentiments about America. While admitting that he had
become an Americanist by chance –​having been given the opportunity for a
first appointment in São Paulo in Brazil, of which he had only a very vague
notion, it was far from accidental that he remained so, he states. For him, the
first impression of contact with the New World remained ineffaceable; in the
New World everything was on an incommensurable scale compared to that
of the Old World. Adding to this was the stunning nature, both more pris-
tine and more grandiose than elsewhere; nowhere else had Lévi-​Strauss met
with a nature where even the wildest appearances covered the patient work
of humans over centuries, even millennia.

  Finally, and this is possibly the main reason, the study of no other con-
  tinent asks for a similar advantage of the imagination. America was essen-
  tially populated by people hailing from Asia, traversing the lands that
  had emerged at the present place of the Bering Strait. But when? The best
  estimates vary by a margin of 50 million years. And of these repeated
  passages over different epochs, there is no trace. Because of the variations
  of sea-​level, the itineraries are probably lost either in the high moun-
  tains or under the water. And this is not all: America offers a stupefying
  spectacle of very high cultures neighbouring each other, on a very low
  technological and economic level. What is more, these high cultures never
  knew anything but an ephemeral existence: Every one of them was born,
  developed, and disappeared in a matter of centuries; and those that had
  disappeared before the arrival of the Spanish were probably more know-
  ledgeable and more refined than the ones now seen in their decline, but
  nevertheless dazzled them.
                                        (In Lévi-​Strauss and Éribon 1998: 84)

Lévi-​Strauss concludes that in truth, and despite all the accumulated work
over a long time, we still do not understand what made America. There
is an unfulfilled dream of solving the riddle, continuing the 19th-​century
scholars hoping for the next find to offer the solution. “This is what renders
Americanism so captivating” (ibid.: 85).
   When reading Lévi-​Strauss on America the resonance between the Tropics
and the Arctic is striking. While the landscapes may appear totally different,
closed or open, they are comparable at a number of levels, having to do
with their illegibility and the elusiveness of their inhabitants. In both cases,
the European travellers were stunned by the vastness of the terrain and the
minute traces of human life. The travellers were unprepared for what met
12 The New World

them –​whether human or non-​human –​because they could not really place
it. No scale seemed to apply; the worlds were both modern and non-​modern,
and they belonged to both the New and the Old World, from the moment
they were ‘discovered’. The inhabitants evaded the distinction between
modern and non-​modern by definition; this enables us to better understand
why ‘we’ have (also) never been modern (Latour 1993). The savage mind is
at play everywhere (Lévi-​Strauss 1962). This echoes with anthropological
thinking a century hence, where we have acknowledged the intermingling of
human and non-​human worlds –​landscapes, vegetation, animals, and forests
(Kohn 2013; Tsing 2015). It also tallies with the anthropological experience
of working in an interstitial space between a pan-​human being and its sin-
gular materializations.


Being out of Date
As mentioned, one of the anthropologists Lévi-​Strauss met in New York
where he spent most of the time during the Second World War, was Franz
Boas –​the highly profiled pioneer in the study of North American native
peoples. While Boas did his military service (1881–​1882), he drew up a
proposal for a dissertation topic that would take him into uncharted terri-
tories, and for which the German Polar Station might serve as an entry. His
interest in the Arctic was fuelled by the intense German interest in the First
International Polar Year (IPY, 1882–​1883) focussing mainly on the natural
sciences. Boas joined an expedition to the whaling station in Cumberland
Sound where a German Polar Station was set up (Müller-​Wille 1998). This
led to Boas’ first fieldwork among the Central Eskimo, as he called them
(Boas [1888] 1964).
    While Boas had foreseen an independent fieldwork out of the Scientific
Station at Kingu of Baffin Island set up for the IPY, he ended up being based
with a Scottish whaling station in Kikkerton, due to simple (yet as always,
grave) landing problems owing to the sea-​ice. The whaling station turned
out to be an unforeseen gift to Boas and his work over a year (Boas 1884). It
proved indispensable for contact and comfort for both him and his servant,
Wilhelm Weike, and not least for access to the people of the region through
the Scottish stationmaster, James Mutch, who remained a life-​long supplier
of notes and ethnographic artefacts (Harper 2008; Krupnik 2016b). Boas
duly acknowledged Mutch’s contribution in his work, pointing towards an
‘assembling’ of knowledge, which is part and parcel of ethnographic work,
if to different degrees. Boas’ introduction to The Central Eskimo opens with
some geographical information, enabling the reader to better understand the
distribution and migrations of the Inuit tribes who inhabit the country –​and
pointing towards the intimate connection between landscape and social life.

  The mode of life of all the Eskimo tribes of Northeastern America is very
  uniform; therefore it is desirable to make a few general observations
                                                         The New World 13

  on the subject before entering into a detailed description of each tribe.
  All depends upon the distribution of food at the different seasons. The
  migrations or the accessibility of the game compel the natives to move
  their habitations from time to time, and hence the distribution of the
  village depends, to a great extent, upon that of the animals, which supply
  them with food.
     As the inhospitable country does not produce vegetation to an extent
  sufficient to sustain life in its human inhabitants, they are forced to depend
  entirely upon animal food. In Arctic America the abundance of seals found
  in all parts of the sea enables man to withstand the inclemency of climate
  and the sterility of the soil. The skins of seals furnish the material for
  summer garments and for the tent; their flesh is almost the only food, and
  their blubber the indispensable fuel during the long dark winter. Scarcely
  less important is the deer, of whose heavy skin the winter garments are
  made, and these enable the Eskimo to brave the storms and the cold of
  winter.
     That the mode of life of the Eskimo depends wholly on the distribution
  of these animals will therefore be apparent, for, as already observed, they
  regulate their dwelling places in accordance with the migrations of the
  latter from place to place in search of food.
                                                         (Boas [1888] 1964: 11)

Here they are, the Inuit, wandering about for food and clothing in the inhos-
pitable landscape. Clearly, at Boas’ time, the Inuit were already implicated
in the modern world, as were even the small, invisible bands living in the
tropical forest of Brazil. Boas still wanted to understand the essence of
Inuit life, in order to also understand the difference between the tribes, if
such could be identified. He made copious descriptions and noted detailed
differences between the groups but admitted to their being out of date and
possibly no longer justifiable ([1888] 1964: 17). Despite such admission, his
ambition to identify and classify distinctive groups remained. It was part
of what it meant to be an anthropologist at the time, even as tribal life was
revolutionized.
   Boas was certainly not blind to the external influence (since the 17th
century): “The constant contact between the Eskimo and the whalers
has affected a perfect revolution in the trade between the Eskimo tribes”
(ibid.: 60) –​but he remained true to the ethnographic quest, not only
among the Inuit but also more broadly among other Native American
groups, whose languages he and his students would carefully study over
the next decennia. Lévi-​Strauss credits Boas for being among the first to
insist that the laws of language function on the subconscious level, beyond
the control of the speaking subjects; this allows for studying these laws
as objective phenomena, and as social facts (in Lévi-​Strauss and Éribon
1998: 59). In this, Boas was in line with the prominent French linguist,
Ferdinand de Saussure (1916), who was an inspiration for the nascent
14 The New World

structural anthropology, that was to fly high with Lévi-​Strauss in mid-​20th
century (Hastrup 2022).
   Boas made the move from the Old to the New World in person, not only
during fieldwork, but also by leaving his native Germany and settling in
America. He tackled the difference between the two worlds in an interesting
way during his work with several other Native American groups after the
Central Eskimo, making significant observations on the relations between
civilized and primitive man.

  Proud of his wonderful achievements, civilized man looks down on the
  humbler members of mankind. He has conquered the forces of nature and
  compelled them to serve him. He has transformed inhospitable forests into
  fertile fields. The mountain fastnesses are yielding their treasures to his
  demands. The fierce animals which are obstructing his progress are being
  exterminated, while others that are useful to him are made to increase
  thousand-​fold. The waves of the ocean carry him from land to land, and
  towering mountain ranges set him no bounds. His genius has moulded
  inert matter into powerful machines which await a touch of his hand to
  serve his manifold demands.
     With pity he looks down upon those members of the human race
  who have not succeeded in subduing nature; who labor to eke a meagre
  existence out of the products of the wilderness; who hear with trem-
  bling the roar of the wild animals, and see the products of their tolls
  destroyed by them; who remain restricted by ocean, river, or mountains;
  who strive to obtain the necessities of life with the help of few and
  simple instruments.
                                                           (Boas 1911: 1–​2)

Clearly, Boas does not subscribe to the general praise of civilization but takes
time to investigate it and to dismantle the conclusion that the alleged super-
iority of civilization proves that the aptitude for civilization must be higher
among the ‘civilized’. His view of civilization includes people outside of
Europe –​suggesting that the civilizations of Peru and Central America might
well be compared to the ancient civilizations of the Old World.

  The inventions and knowledge of the peoples of the Old World seem to
  have been somewhat more numerous and extended than those of the races
  of the New World, but there can be no doubt that the general status
  of their civilization was nearly equally high. This will suffice for our
  consideration.
    What, then, is the difference between the civilization of the Old World
  and that of the New World? It is essentially a difference in time. The one
  reached a certain stage three thousand or four thousand years sooner than
  the other.
                                                           (Boas 1911: 7–​8)
                                                          The New World 15

A difference in time; this is an attempt towards establishing a sense of basic
equality, between the Old and the New World, if not quite successful. Boas
himself searches for supplementary explanations, one of which is that
civilizations must somewhere have grown by the accretion from more primi-
tive neighbours.

  Furthermore, the devastating influences of diseases which nowadays begin
  to ravage the territories newly opened to the whites were not so strong,
  on account of the permanent contiguity of the people of the Old World,
  who were always in contact with one another. The invasion of America
  and Polynesia, on the other hand, was accompanied by the introduction
  of new diseases among the natives of these countries. The suffering and
  devastation wrought by epidemics which followed the discovery are too
  well known to be described in full.
                                                         (Boas 1911: 11–​2)

Part of the barrenness of the American Arctic experienced by travellers from
outside was owed to the sparse populations; for newcomers, large tracts of
the Arctic landscape looked empty, not simply by being barren, but also by
being devoid of human traces. So did apparently the Amazonian landscape,
seen to be the “last great wilderness” –​a nature untouched by humans. Yet,
it “is now recognized as anthropogenic, a ‘cultural forest’, a product of delib-
erate human activity and knowledge” (Turnbull and Chambers 2014: 157).
    Until devastated by European diseases, people lived and built complex
societies in the Amazonian basin, based on intensive soil and agricultural
practices. When people succumbed to epidemics, their towns, causeways,
and canals disappeared under the weight of the forest, rapidly reclaiming
the land, and hiding the human traces. They were soon forgotten and the
surviving natives, who remained partly hidden from view in the rain forest,
were generally presented as radically different from Europeans. Alexander
von Humboldt, the German naturalist who reported on the South American
landscapes and their native species, made notes on relics of civilizations that
had existed long before European arrival, and whose cities were in some
cases taken over by the Spanish (e.g., Humboldt [1849] 2014: 108). Such
writings had “the potential to contradict dramatically the de-​historicized cele-
bration of primal America and the primitivist view of the Amerindians that
went with it” (Pratt 1992: 131). They did not have much impact until more
recently, however, when the afterlife of colonial destruction was reassessed
(e.g., Gordillo 2014). Before, ‘civilization’ was often attributed to just one
on the parties at the crossroads, as we might suggest also for the Arctic;
the superior nature of native hunting and clothing technologies was highly
praised and adopted by the non-​natives –​all while they often continued to
see the Inuit as uncultured.
    When Boas had taken the full leap from geography to anthropology,
he became an inspiration also to Lévi-​    Strauss. I have cited the issue of
16 The New World

language, but the entire comparative effort struck him as valuable, not least
because the tales and traditions that Boas and his students had collected and
published were a rich repository of material for Lévi-​Strauss’ major work on
Mythologiques I–​IV (1964–​1971). While both in New York, they stroke up
a collegiate friendship despite the difference in age and life-​course, seeking to
straddle the gap between the Old and the New World in each their fashion.
While Boas had moved to America on a permanent basis, Lévi-​Strauss was
there as a temporary refugee from the Nazi occupation of France. They seem
to have developed a regard for each other, if at some distance, although we
only have Lévi-​Strauss’ testimony to this (1984; 1998). Lévi-​Strauss had
drawn on many of Boas’ observations from the North Pacific Coast in his
own major oeuvre and praised the fact that the old publication in German
was now to be published also in English, making it available to new readers.

  This translation of Franz Boas’ first major work is also a brilliant homage
  to the memory of a great ethnologist without whom our knowledge of
  the cultures of the pacific Coast would still be only rudimentary. Like any
  scholar, he sometimes made mistakes in his work, but whenever he had
  the opportunity, he was always the first to correct his previous judgments.
  These instances when he was feeling his way are hardly worth mentioning
  in comparison with the prodigious mass of data he collected and published
  over a period of more than half a century on societies which, through the
  magnificence of their art, the originality of their economic system and the
  richness of their social organization and religious practices, earned them-
  selves a prominent position in the great scheme of humanity.
                                                         (Lévi-​Strauss 1984: 8)

This appraisal reminds me of my early days as a student of anthropology
(in the 1970s) when one had to know almost any group of people, at all
continents –​at least those who had been studied by anthropologists –​and
would be examined in the details of art, myths, and social organization across
the world, as seen through the eyes of various anthropologists. At his time,
Lévi-​Strauss not only noted Boas’ massive contribution to anthropology in
general, but also pondered over his own relation to the material and asked
himself whether Boas saw any part of his work as richer, or grander, than
others.

  I remember one occasion when I was invited to dinner at his Grantwood
  home. I was in a dream in front of the magnificent carved, painted chest
  in his dining room and I said imprudently that, in his life as an ethnolo-
  gist, his sojourns with people capable of creating such a masterpiece must
  have been an extraordinary experience. With his legendary puritanism, he
  answered drily, “They were Indians, like any others,” and I did not dare
  to continue.
                                                       (Lévi-​Strauss 1984: 8)
                                                          The New World 17

Lévi-​Strauss explains this attitude with Boas’ reluctance to allow any kind of
hierarchy into his cultural relativist scheme. The friendship ended abruptly in
late 1942. Lévi-​Strauss recounts how Boas had invited himself, Ruth Benedict,
Ralph Linton, and a couple of other colleagues as well as his daughter for
lunch at the faculty club at Columbia University. The lunch celebrated the
arrival of Paul Rivet, director of the Musée de l’Homme in Paris, now a
refugee from the German occupation, as was Lévi-​Strauss. It was December
and frightfully cold, and Boas arrived from Grantwood in an old fur-​hat
dating back to his first fieldwork among the Baffinland Inuit, some 60 years
earlier (Lévi-​Strauss 1984: 9).

  The meal began gaily; you could tell that Boas was happy to see an old
  friend again and to be surrounded by former students, some of whom had
  followed in his footsteps. The conversation was going along at a good
  pace when suddenly, in mid-​sentence, Boas jerked violently backwards,
  as under the effect of an electric shock, and fell over, taking his chair
  with him. I was situated next to him and hurried to help him up, but he
  remained motionless. Rivet, who had been an army medical officer, tried
  in vain to revive him; he was only able to pronounce him dead …we with-
  drew in silence, grief-​stricken at the loss of the greatest ethnologist of all
  time.
                                                         (Lévi-​Strauss 1984: 9)

Boas remained a major source of inspiration for Lévi-​Strauss; in his eyes Boas
had arrived at fundamental ideas. This included a critique of racism based
upon physical anthropological studies that allowed him to prove that the
perceived ‘racial’ differences were the result of the environment, not genetics
(in Lévi-​Strauss and Éribon 1998: 59). It may be slightly more ambiguous
than that (Pöhl 2008), but here we shall leave Lévi-​Strauss’ testimony alone.


Contact at High Latitudes
With its vastness and inaccessibility, the Arctic region still had its secrets,
when professional anthropology emerged on the scene in late 19th century.
It would be a mistake, however, to believe that people who lived there were
truly unknown, because trappers, traders, and whalers had paved the way
also for explorers who in some cases became ethnographers by default.
While the main interest in this book is the Inuit of Arctic America (broadly
speaking), it is occasionally be extended to other people, equally ‘out of date’
as it were, if certainly an integral part of the modern world.
   Gradually, the ethnographic interest in the Arctic took a distinct pro-
fessional form. In the Arctic, either one immersed oneself into society
with all it took of personal involvement and closeness to the ethnographic
subjects, or one was left out (see e.g., Pálsson 2001; 2003; Hastrup 2013;
2017). One challenge was to be as mobile as the people themselves, and as
18 The New World

these were hunters and lived part of the year in temporary abodes, their
ethnographers had to follow suit. In my own experience from Northwest
Greenland, it is still next to impossible to do anything but tacking along
and forgetting about interviews and life histories; one gets snippets along
the way and gradually one’s knowledge builds up. Anthropological prac-
tice always calls for a degree of falling into step. This was what Rasmussen
mastered so eminently; he became a local at least for some time, by sharing
tents and driving his own dog sledge like a local, and not least acknow-
ledging the hosts for what they were: hosts. The field in which he worked
was wide and barred the option of pitching tent once and for all. This was
part of the general challenge to Arctic anthropology –​being easily cast as a
province of the discipline. Not intellectually, but by its being so markedly
regional in its thinking.
   Clearly, early as well as later anthropologists worked within a framework
of a particular colonial history, a history that was deeply marked by the
landscape and by the fewness of its inhabitants. The current changes in the
Arctic environment still call for substantial analyses of the entanglement of
natural and social processes, and it is essential to remember that there is no
access to any untainted truth out there; we are always accompanied by past
histories and (colonial) encounters and by more recent (ethnographic) inter-
pretations, not to speak of local understandings of landscapes, none of which
are objective or disinterested. While the Arctic is in some ways a special
case (like all cases, by implication), it should not be singularized and placed
beyond comparison. It is both distinct and comparable –​and as elsewhere
the observer becomes part of the object.
   It is appropriate to briefly turn back to Boas and an oft-​cited passage from
his diary when he worked among the Central Eskimo in 1883. Almost des-
pite himself, he had to acknowledge his oneness with the people on whose
services he depended. Boas admits:

  I often ask myself what advantages our “good society” possesses over the
  “savages” and the more I see of their customs, I find that we really have
  no grounds to look down on them contemptuously. Where among us is
  there such hospitality as here? Where are there people who carry out any
  task requested of them so willingly and without grumbling!
                                          (Boas in Müller-​Wille 1998: 159)

It could be interpreted as a colonial attitude towards people whom he could
exploit for his own purposes, but this is tempered by the following (oft-​
quoted) passage:

  I believe that if this trip has a significant impact on me as a thinking
  person, then it is the strengthening of my notion of the relativity of all edu-
  cation and the conviction of how the value of people lies in the guidance
                                                          The New World 19

  close to their heart [Herzensbildung], which I find, or miss here, just as
  home, and thus that all service which a person can render to humanity
  must depend on the furthering of truth, which may be sweet or bitter for
  humanity. Yes, whoever furthers it, whoever widely pursues the search for
  truth, may say that he has not lived in vain!
                                           (Boas in Müller-​Wille 1998: 159)

One must remain humble as regards understanding life in the Arctic, because
this life is always plural and any attempt at singularising it in writing remains
just that: one attempt among others to substantiate the multiplicity of
human life.
    Part of the decolonization of the anthropological mind in the Far North
rests with the recognition of the shared humanity of anthropologists
and their collaborators in the field. Conversation is easy and enriching;
both parties become engaged in each other once they agree to talk in the
first place. As persons we take shape within a particular moral space,
responding to each other’s presence and interest (Taylor 1989). In more
practical terms, we might say that the ethnographer always becomes part
of the observed, as suggested above. This leaves us with a productive ambi-
guity in anthropology, as highlighted by a philosopher in an obituary for
Lévi-​Strauss:

  Anthropology is a very special sort of knowledge: it makes the diversity
  of forms of knowledge the critical instrument to characterize both what
  is distinctive about the kind of knowledge we perceive as obvious (as the
  relation to some external reality) by clarifying its difference with other
  knowledges, and what is at stake for all rational subjects of knowledge in
  this activity. Lévi-​Strauss defined anthropology as this kind of knowledge
  which makes of differences (between forms of knowledge) the unique tool
  to produce particular truths. I know of no other thinker who pushed fur-
  ther than him the concern with decolonizing thinking.
                                                         (Maniglier 2010: 67)

Paradoxically, such decolonization seems difficult in parts of Arctic anthro-
pology, when ‘the others’ were still set apart –​often on their own volition.
This came along with the Arctic anthropological interest, more or less imme-
diately. An early case offered by Diamond Jenness, who in his book The
People of the Twilight (1928), wrote an epilogue, called L’Envoi, where he
laments the fate of the people with whom he had worked some 12 years
earlier as part of the Canadian Arctic Expedition.

  The country has changed since 1916. Even while we said our farewells
  the traders were all heading eastward to the new land where beautiful
  white fox skins were valueless and a fortune could be gained in a night.
20 The New World

  White men have invaded it from every quarter, and the twilight of ignor-
  ance and superstition is yielding to the dawn of greater knowledge. Bows
  and arrows have passed with other weapons into the darkness of the past,
  and a new mechanical age has brought magazine rifles, steel traps, and
  even gasoline engines. The caribou are passing with the bows and arrows;
  of all the herds that once crossed the narrow strait to Victoria Island
  hardly one now reaches the Arctic shore. Strange diseases are making
  their appearance, diseases that the old-​time Eskimo never heard of and for
  which he has no name. The stern laws of civilization have descended on
  the land; no longer is infanticide tolerated or the blood-​feud allowed to
  run its course. “Furs, furs, more furs,” is the white man’s cry. “Without
  furs there is no salvation, no ammunition to shoot the scattered game and
  satisfy your hungry children.” The tribal bands where each man toiled
  for all and shared his food in common are resolving into their constituent
  families, and every family vies with the rest in the race for wealth and
  worldly prosperity.
     Whither will it all lead? Fifty years ago the cyclone swept over the
  Eskimos of the Mackenzie River Delta, and of its two thousand inhabitants
  a scant two hundred survive. Fifty years earlier it struck Baffin Island with
  a similar result. Will history, fifty years hence, record the same fate for this
  twilight land where for two years we carried out our mission? Were we the
  harbingers of a brighter dawn, or only messengers of ill-​omen, portending
  disaster?
                                                  (Jenness [1928] 1959: 246–​8)

The early perception of the stillness of faraway people may still tempt
ethnographers to set ‘their’ people apart from the shared humanity. Arctic
anthropology is still more or less caught up in old tropes of an other world,
having been violated by Europeans. We need to decolonize our own minds
to show how the modern and the un-​modern world are partners in the
Anthropocene. This will allow anthropologists to leave behind the idea of
having to chart and represent a distant and mistreated region, and instead
meet people where they are –​and let the differences tell their own story –​
including the story of anthropological interest, given that the observer cannot
but become part of the observed.
    This is exemplified by Eivind Astrup, accompanying Robert Peary on his
first Arctic travels to Northwest Greenland –​in pursuit of the larger goal
of reaching the North Pole. He eventually succeeded, it appears, but only
because of the Inughuit and their dog sledges (Peary 1910). Astrup wrote his
own book, Blandt Nordpolens Naboer [Among the Neighbours of the North
Pole] in Norwegian, 1895. In the English translation it became With Peary
near the Pole (1898). On people of Northwest Greenland he noted:

  Our small merry brethren in the Arctic regions represent an extremity of
  the human race; an insignificant section of it, who take up the battle of
                                                          The New World 21

  existence in regions which to our eyes offer poor prospects for life’s susten-
  ance, and where icy death would seem to reign supreme.
                                                           (Astrup 1898: 48)

   The virtual un-​liveability of the High Arctic was a persistent trope in the
early reports, but it was never true, obviously. Nevertheless, the depiction of
the precarious lives of the people of the New World kept Old World minds
focused on their peculiarity, by their having lived in the cold for millennia.
Let us hear Fridtjof Nansen, who, in his magnum epos on Northern explor-
ation, says:

  Of all the races of the earth, that of the Eskimo is one that has established
  itself farthest north. His world is that of sea-​ice and cold, for which nature
  has not intended human beings. In his slow, stubborn fight against the
  powers of winter he has learnt better than any other how to turn these to
  account, and in these regions, along the ice-​bound shores, he developed his
  peculiar culture, with its ingenious appliances, long before the beginning
  of history. As men of the white race pushed northward to the “highest
  latitudes” they found traces of this remarkable people, who had already
  been there in times long past; and it is only in the last few decades that
  anyone has succeeded in penetrating further north than the Eskimo, partly
  by learning from him or enlisting his help. In these regions, which are his
  own, his culture was superior to that of the white race, and from no other
  people has the arctic navigator learnt so much.
                                                           (Nansen 1911, II: 66)

Again, this leads us back to Lévi-​Strauss’ discussion of the nature of field-
work and his own experience of wasting his life in the Brazilian interior,
without due acknowledgement. Revealing his own sentiments about ‘the field’
60 years later, Lévi-​Strauss admits that he preferred homework over the field,
and without any pejorative intention he suggests that fieldwork is work for
the ladies, which is supported by the fact that they carry it out so brilliantly.
Maybe, he himself did not have enough patience, he suggests (in Lévi-​Strauss
and Éribon 1998: 66). Assessing fieldwork (in the 1990s) more generally,
he laments the increasing bureaucracy that surrounds it; for example, one
cannot relate a myth without producing a written statement about the lit-
erary ownership of the informant with all the bureaucratic consequences that
this entail. He sees this as a caricature of ethnographic work, and the easy
submission to bureaucracy by the ethnographers unnerves him. He felt that
he had done a lot more fieldwork than his critics allowed him, and in any
case, he had made enough to understand what fieldwork is. This is an indis-
pensable condition for sensibly judging and citing the work by others (in
Lévi-​Strauss and Éribon 1998: 67).
   This is still an important point, in the present age of temporal com-
pression, but certainly also more generally. Who knows when and what is
22 The New World

‘enough’ in anthropology, which is always both more and less than a study
of people. We shall return to that later, here we shall simply state that the
empirical foundation of anthropology does not depend on fixed boundaries
and ethnic categories; to the contrary, its solidity rests on the acknowledge-
ment of relations and networks. When delving into the history of early Arctic
anthropology, we sense how a ‘modern’ anthropological perspective never
really befitted a world where everything was in flux.


Looking Back
The rather extensive quotes from early ethnographies are no proof of my
argument, but a reminder of the basic fact that truth is local. It is difficult to
summarize or generalize at the level of ‘facts’ –​yet at the level of anthropo-
logical thinking, this is what we must do to arrive at anything beyond simple
documentation of a particular moment. The price paid for a coherent picture
is to surpass the idea of documentation and positive knowledge.
    The “modern condition …is one of increasingly fragmented and
sequestered pools of knowing and unknowing, with wisdom and a sense of
the totality in short supply” (Turnbull and Chambers 2014: 153). This applies
to scholarship of all kinds, and it is a built-​in risk in anthropology, where one
works through sojourning and conversing with living people. To contribute
to knowledge, we need to get past the guilt of being anthropologists and
the need to align ourselves with current wisdom. This is supported by Lévi-​
Strauss’ confession about Tristes Tropiques in his conversation with Éribon,
saying that he would never have written that book if he had believed in a
future as an academic (in Lévi-​Strauss and Éribon 1998: 76). He certainly
did become an academic despite his audacious book; and we are grateful that
he wrote it while still uncertain of his path. It is the thinking, as much as the
ethnographic observations that keeps anthropology alive as a repository of
wisdom.
    The acknowledgement of uncertainty of understanding offers itself up
as a gift to the anthropological fieldworker, always in doubt about the
hidden traces and messages on ‘the field’ –​le terrain, as it is in French –​and
about his or her own powers of understanding. After all, like Lévi-​Strauss,
the anthropologist is a “traveller, archaeologist of space, trying in vain to
repiece together the idea of the exotic with the help of a particle here and
a fragment of debris there” (Lévi-​Strauss 1963: 44). Anthropologists are
implicitly backwards looking and inward bound, as it were, even when
writing up a present.
    We ever only get fragments of life at a particular point in time; we cannot,
and should not, pretend that we map entire worlds –​renewing themselves at
every turn in response to larger processes and in some extent to our probing
to get a glimpse of what ‘other lives’ really are. This is where a new history
of Arctic anthropology may be relevant to an understanding of the rapidly
changing, high-​modernist Arctic, that we see today. For this, the decolonizing
                                                         The New World 23

of the anthropological mind is adamant; anthropological knowledge remains
interstitial and fragmented. It was never detached from neither place nor
time, and as we read the works of the founders of Arctic anthropology, we
realize how ‘present’ these still are in our modes of thinking. They remain
part of the long-​time conversation between travellers, scholars, and Inuit –​a
conversation that we are bound to continue, as new ways of “performing the
Arctic Scientific Human” emerge (Powell 2017: 98). This reminds us about
the reciprocal formation of people and places, of scholars and technologies,
ethnographers and landscapes.
   It also reminds us to beware of ethnographic stereotypes that tend to
freeze in our imagination. It is tempting once again to look back to New
World people in South America whom Charles Darwin met, and whom he
had to acknowledge as his ancestors. On the long-​lasting voyage with the
Beagle (1831–​1836), the expedition rounded Terra del Fuego at the southern
tip of the continent, and Darwin observed in his Descent of Man:

  The main conclusion arrived at in this work, namely that man is descended
  from some lowly-​organised form, will, I regret to think, be highly dis-
  tasteful to many persons. But there can hardly be a doubt that we are
  descended from barbarians. The astonishment which I felt on first seeing a
  party of Fuegians on a wild and broken shore will never be forgotten by me,
  for the reflection at once rushed into my mind –​such were our ancestors.
  These men were absolutely naked and bedaubed with paint, their long hair
  was tangled, their mouths frothed with excitement, and their expression
  was wild, startled, distrustful. They possessed hardly any arts, and like
  wild animals lived on what they could catch. He who has seen a savage in
  his native land will not feel much shame, if forced to acknowledge that the
  blood of some more humble creature flows in his veins.
                                                  (Darwin [1871] 2008: 332)

At the southern tip of the New (and very old) World, ‘man’ had for a long
time braved the infelicitous surroundings and kept up with nature; Darwin
concluded that humans “still bears in his bodily frame the indelible stamp of
his lowly origin” (ibid: 333).
   Among the ethnographers in the American Arctic, there was not so
much a sense of lowliness, as of vulnerability. At the end of the Fifth Thule
Expedition in 1924 Rasmussen looked back on his attempt at understanding
and documenting Inuit life and thought, and suggested that “before many
years are past, their religion will be extinct, and the white man will have
conquered all, the country and its people; their thoughts, their visions and
their faith” (Rasmussen [1927] 1999: 386). History is never linear, how-
ever; any forward march relies on stones left from the past. This applied
also to his companions from Thule (Northwest Greenland), Qâvigarsuaq
and Anarulúnguaq, who had been part of the protracted expedition that
had brought them to new lands; the American Arctic was not entirely new
24 The New World

for them, if different from the Greenland they knew. When they arrived in
New York before sailing back to Denmark, Rasmussen notes:

   The Expedition was at an end. The years which to us white men had been
   full of strange happenings and experiences, were just everyday life to our
   two Greenlanders. It was their turn now; their expedition was beginning.
   But as I showed them the marvels of this new world, my thoughts were con-
   stantly returning to the people we had left to the men and women who had
   spoken so simply and yet so powerfully of the greatest and smallest things.
   Hunger and feasting, happiness and adversity, the daily round and the great
   moments of life –​they spoke of all with true and simple feeling. So here;
   face to face with a chaos and confusion of marvels, Anarulúnguaq found
   the very words for all it meant: Nature is great; but man is greater still.
                                             (Rasmussen [1927] 1999: 387–​8)

We shall let these words stand, even though we might not truly agree with the
general claim; ‘man’ may be great, but nature does surpass us –​as we realize
in the age of the Anthropocene. What remains is the feat of the Fifth Thule
Expedition, as not least the accomplishments of the people who had braved
the challenges of the New World for millennia and humanized it in the process.
