2	Late Arrivals
       Strangers in the North




The arrival of humans (and other species) migrating from Siberia at the time
of the Pleistocene/​Holocene transition some 11,000 years ago, constituted
a break in time, marking climatic warming and a relatively rapid retreat of
continental glaciers. As the ice retreated, both animals and humans found
ways to gradually move east along the northern fringe and to populate what
is now known as the American Arctic. Looking back on this era is an inspir-
ation to speculate on ‘lost time’, as we have recently learnt from Hugh Raffles,
unearthing forgotten breaks in the deep of history (2020). Such breaks have
deeply affected the possibility of life at all continents.
   The Inuit that were once strangers to the Arctic region, are no longer so.
Yet, with the current, rapid Anthropocene warming, the region has become
strange even to them. There were always changes, both seasonal and abrupt,
that would challenge human communities in the North, as chronicled by
explorers and anthropologists in the region (e.g., Dodds and Nuttall 2019). As
these changes become increasingly rapid and unpredictable, both inhabitants
and anthropologists would be hard-​pressed to say anything definite about
future life in the Arctic, whether envisioned as better or worse. Certainly, the
landscape of plains and low forests, tundra, and ice, will change as will life
in the region, for people as well as flora and fauna. In fact, the landscape was
never still, however deep frozen it seemed.
   In South America, the early immigrants from the Bering Strait region
who had moved southwards found a multiplicity of landscapes, which
became theirs. There were coastlands, hot or warm, mountains, ice-​covered
or green, dense forests, swelling rivers, and rocky patches –​demanding
diverse strategies, and becoming home to large civilizations, also in South
America. In North America, colonization came later, due to the persistent
ice-​masses along the northern fringe of the continent. There are traces of
inhabitants long before they were encountered by Europeans; however, in
the Bering Strait region this happened in the 18th century, when sailors
went north –​or east, when coming from Russia –​eventually claiming
Alaska. It was sold to the United States in 1867, when the peninsula was


                                            DOI: 10.4324/9781003374145-2
28 Late Arrivals

home to some 18,000 Inuit, occupying the coast and the immediate hin-
terland (Jenness 1962: 5). By the end of the 19th century, rifles and white
colonization had changed the region, including its ancient subsistence
economy. This led to an introduction of semi-​domesticated reindeer, and a
new era began (ibid.: 12).
    On the eastern coast of Arctic America, some tracts were briefly colonized
by Norsemen in the 10th century. They arrived from Iceland via South
Greenland and landed on the northern tip of Newfoundland, where a settle-
ment has been excavated at L’Anse aux Meadows. It was first identified by
the Norwegians Anne Stine Ingstad and Helge Ingstad (Ingstad and Ingstad
2001). The settlement had been small scale and relatively short-​lived, but
even so it testified to the open waters of the warming Middle Ages, luring the
Vikings towards the North Atlantic –​and settling on Atlantic Islands such as
the Orkney and the Faroe Islands, and then Iceland (from 830), and south-​
west Greenland (from 985–​1450). From there, some of them continued to
America. To Europeans, America, including South-​and Central America,
remained generally unknown until late 15th century, when Columbus
arrived, not exactly knowing where he was. Global history took a new turn
from then on.
    Once ships of a certain size were built, European sailors not only went
westwards, but also east-​and south-​eastwards, towards new continents and
islands. In the process, the Pacific Islands and their unknown populations
were ‘discovered’, having navigated the Pacific for ages in their small vessels.
The navigational skills required were new to the European sailors, who lit-
erally did not know where the world ended, or if it was a good place to seek
out. Like the Norsemen, they took their chances and trusted their own skills
of navigation (Turnbull 2003: 131ff).
    People moved, but so did also the Pacific Islands, that emerged or
disappeared as centuries passed by and the seabed changed. They were grad-
ually populated or depopulated by brilliant navigators following the deep
human instinct to move. As the sea changed, some were stuck (Nunn 2007a,
2007b). The European sailors were helped by tentative maps and brilliant
local navigators; they found the Pacific Islands inhabited all the way to
New Zealand, to where they had spread over millennia –​finally reaching
New Zealand around 1000 A D (Turnbull 2003; Nunn et al. 2007). By com-
parison, Australia had been populated from Indonesia and New Guinea,
some 60,000 years earlier. It is an amazing story of human expansion and
courage, and of discovering that the sea was one, while the continents were
many. Among the sailors was Captain James Cook, who ended his life by
being killed by natives on Hawaii in 1779, for reasons that have been heavily
disputed in anthropology. In the present context, Cook is of interest by
being one of the first Europeans to navigate through the Bering Strait (in
1778) and into the Chukchi Sea. Like many later sailors, he searched for a
Northwest Passage. If the Inuit were late arrivals in America, the Europeans
were even later.
                                                            Late Arrivals 29

In Northern Mists
The brief notes on migration waves given above serve as a comparative
measure to what we now know about the New World, and the relatively
late arrival of humans to that region, both South and North, slowly moving
from Siberia towards America. Covered by ice, the Arctic remained devoid
of humans for quite a long time. The discoverers may have been driven by a
vision of fame or riches, but also at least in part by a ‘call of the unknown’,
as suggested by Fridtjof Nansen:

  It is our perpetual yearning to overcome difficulties and dangers, to see
  hidden things, to penetrate into the regions outside our beaten track –​it
  is the call of the unknown –​the longing for the land of Beyond, the divine
  force deeply rooted in the soul of man which drove the first hunters out
  into new regions –​the mainspring perhaps of our greatest actions –​of
  winged human thought knowing no bounds to its freedom.
                                                          (Nansen 1927: 20)

The perpetual longing for the unknown is another instance of the passion
for blankness that had propelled European travellers towards other
continents since the age of the Enlightenment (Bloom 1993). The multi-
tude of expeditions, patronized by kings and emperors in the 18th and 19th
centuries were explicitly aimed at mapping the world, not only in terms
of resources and trade routes but also in terms of the natural order (Pratt
1992: 15ff). Blankness is never absolute; it is always marked by metaphors
and categorical exemplars –​and not least by other people, who trotted the
unknown a long time ago, maybe even lived there, not knowing why they
should be anything special. Anthropologists were always the latest arrivals,
as they worked on top of the efforts of early explorers and settlers, and not
least the people they had come to study. They, too, wanted to explore new
terrains.
   The Arctic explorers entered the scene in the last phase of Enlightenment
expeditions, where the question of the natural order also embraced the
‘other’ people, emerging in the process. A comprehensive descriptive scheme
for plants was designed by Carl von Linné (Linnaeus) to classify all known
and (as yet) unknown species. This was to become the foundation of a vast
taxonomic enterprise that would eventually authorize a particular hierarchy
of varieties also within the human species, as different from wild men and
monsters. The ordering exercise was rather abstract (Pratt 1992: 33), but it
did influence the images of ‘others’ at the edge of the known world. In the
19th century, the emerging ethnography took over the study of ‘culture’ and
of migration routes and links between various populations such as the Inuit.
Exploration and incipient ethnographic work contributed to the lifting of the
mists that had until then glued to the Arctic. Let us pass the word to Nansen,
having set out towards the Northern Mists.
30 Late Arrivals

  In the beginning the world appeared to mankind like a fairy tale; every-
  thing that lay beyond the circle of familiar experience was a shifting cloud-
  land of the fancy, a playground for all the fabled beings of mythology; but
  in the farthest distance, towards the west and the north, was the region of
  darkness and mists, where sea, land and sky were merged into a congealed
  mass –​and at the end of all gaped the immeasurable mouth of the abyss,
  the awful void of space.
     Out of this fairy world, in course of time, the calm and sober lines of the
  northern landscape appeared. With unspeakable labor the eye of man has
  forced its way gradually towards the north, over mountains and forests,
  and tundra, onward through the mists along the vacant shores of the polar
  sea –​the vast stillness, where so much struggle and suffering, so many
  bitter failures, so many proud victories, have vanished without a trace,
  muffled beneath the mantle of snow.
     When our thoughts go back through the ages in a waking dream, an
  endless procession passes before us –​like a single mighty epic of the
  human mind’s power of devotion to an idea, right or wrong –​a proces-
  sion of struggling, frost-​covered figures in heavy clothes, some erect and
  powerful, others weak and bent so that they can scarcely drag themselves
  along before the sledges, many of them emaciated and dying of hunger,
  cold and scurvy; but all looking out before them towards the unknown,
  beyond the sunset, where the goal of their struggle is to be found.
                                                         (Nansen 1911; I: 1–​2)

This is the stuff of myth, the myth of polar conquest. The tenacity of this myth
is significant, as we note that Nansen wrote his epic tale of the northern regions
at the pinnacle of polar exploration, having flourished, and largely won the
battle with the Arctic region. The beginnings date further back, of course,
but around 1900, the Northeastern and the Northwestern Passages had been
traversed (in 1878 and 1906, respectively) and a decade later, the poles had
been reached (the North Pole in 1909; the South Pole in 1912). Failures
had been many before the explorers met with success. Yet, the Arctic regions
had been travelled or settled by many other peoples before the explorers, who
were often credited with the discovery. The Far North may have been misty
when seen from outside, but even so, people lived their daily lives and had done
so for ages; they were not strangers to the hardships of the cold.
    The Northern regions, including the seas, had been a major resource for
both animals and humans –​the former made it possible for the latter to sur-
vive for thousands of years before they were ‘discovered’, on the impulse
of a particular vision held by Europeans, who also gradually had mastered
shipbuilding on a new scale. As Nansen observed when anthropology was
still in its infancy:

  The history of Arctic discovery shows how the development of the human
  race has always been borne along by great illusions. Just as Columbus’s
                                                               Late Arrivals 31

  discovery of the West Indies was due to a gross error of calculation, so
  it was the fabled isle of Brazil that drew Cabot out on his voyage, when
  he found North America. It was the fantastic illusions of open polar seas
  and of passages to the riches of Cathay beyond the ice that drove men
  back there in spite of one failure after another; and little by little the polar
  regions were explored. Every complete devotion to an idea yields some
  profit, even though it be different from that which was expected.
                                                            (Nansen 1911, I: 3)

Although explorers failed to find Cathay, one profit from 19th-​century travels
in the white lands of the North was a new sense of the sublime that was to
intensify the impulse for further travel in the fearful waters of the north.
The sublime itself was part of a received scheme for classifying emotions, as
suggested by Edmund Burke’s Philosophical Enquiry into the Origin of our
Ideas of the Sublime and Beautiful from 1757. In this work, the experience
of terror and wonder went hand in hand:

  Whatever is fitted in any sort to excite the ideas of pain, and danger, that is to
  say, whatever is in any sort terrible, or is conversant about terrible objects,
  or operates in a manner analogous to terror, is a source of the sublime; that
  is, it is productive of the strongest emotion which the mind is capable of
  feeling. I say strongest emotion, because I am satisfied the ideas of pain are
  much more powerful than those which enter on the part of pleasure.
                                                         (Burke [1757] 1998: 36)

Burke qualifies this a little when he says that “when danger or pain press
too nearly, they are incapable of giving any delight, and are simply ter-
rible; but at certain distances, and with certain modifications, they may
be, and they are delightful, as we everyday experience” (Burke [1757]
1998: 36–​7).
   Reading about Arctic exploration in the 19th century was a source of sub-
lime, if vicarious, experience (Riffenburgh 1993: 11ff). It is still somewhat
enticing, most will admit, even when it comes to our own easy travels in the
high north in the 21st century. In mid-​19th century, the ill-​fated Franklin
expedition that left London in 1845 –​on vessels named Erebus and Terror –​
certainly spurred a sensational interest in the dangers of the frozen lands.
Only in 1848 did it begin to occur to the public that something had gone
wrong, and various search expeditions were sent out. Six years later the
remains of some of Franklin’s crew were found, starved and frozen to death
while trying to find an inland route back to civilization. The report of this
find brought disaster too near, and the sublime was transformed to sheer
horror (Riffenburgh 1993: 29ff). Finding compatriots ‘frozen in time’ gave
new impetus both to the sense of terror and to the price of polar passions
(Beattie and Geiger 2004). It is worth noting that in the North, the fate
of Franklin and his crew had long been known to the inhabitants, who
32 Late Arrivals

knew of the ship and of the scattered bones in their lands. On the Fifth
Thule Expedition, Knud Rasmussen not only buried the remains of some of
Franklin’s men, but also noted the local recollection of part of the drama, as
passed down from the parents of the speakers.

  One feature common to all the accounts, which struck me as curious at
  the time, was the comparative indifference of the narrators to the tragic
  element in the story; the point that seemed to interest them most was the
  ignorance that prevailed in those days among their own people as to white
  men generally, and their goods and gear in particular as viewed in the light
  of the narrators’ own superior knowledge. This was drawn upon to the
  utmost as a source of comic relief.
                                             (Rasmussen [1927] 1999: 238)

The storytellers made detailed descriptions of all the people who had been
involved in this, and what they had seen and taken from the ships, and how
they had not known what it was. A boat hanging over the side had been cut
down and smashed on the ice, their having no idea of its value, according to
the local reporter, who continued:

  They found guns, also, on the ship, and not knowing what was the right
  use of these things, they broke away the barrels and used the metal for
  harpoon heads. So ignorant were they indeed, in the matter of guns and
  belonging to guns, that on finding some percussion caps, such as were
  used in those days, they took them for tiny thimbles, and really believed
  that there were dwarfs among the white folk, little people who could use
  percussion caps for thimbles.
                        (Qaqortingneq in Rasmussen [1927] 1999: 239–​40)

At first the Inuit were afraid of going down into the ship, but when they
finally did, they found many dead men, the narrator reported. Even worse,
when they descended into the abandoned ship and found light wanting, they
made holes in the sides, below the water line, making the ship sink –​and
thus they lost all the valuables that were down there (ibid.: 240). As dryly
noted by Margaret Atwood in her poetic essay Concerning Franklin and the
Gallant Crew, such vandalism would have been unlikely, given the riches
that were found down in the ship (Atwood 2004a: 31). In her literary essay
about Franklin, Atwood also refers to a verse-​drama written by Gwendolyn
MacEwen (1987), where Rasmussen is one of the characters, talking about
finding the remains of the expedition:

  So I’ve followed you here
  Like a dozen others, looking for relics
  of your ships, your men.
  Here to this awful monastery
                                                            Late Arrivals 33

  where you, where Crozier died,
  and all the men with you died,
  Seeking a passage from imagination to reality,
  Seeking a passage from land to land by sea.
                         (MacEwen 1987: 42–​3;
                        in Atwood 2004a: 28–​9)

In Rasmussen’s own account, Qaqortingneq told him that “there are many
places in our country here where the bones of these white men may still be
found” (Rasmussen [1927] 1999: 240). Together they went to the east coast
of Adelaide Peninsula and found both bones and scraps of clothes that iden-
tified the men as white. They gathered the poor remains together and paid
the last honours to the dead (ibid.: 241).
    Much later, other traces of the Franklin disaster emerged out of the land-
scape. First, the remains of one and then three more bodies were found
(in 1981, 1984, and 1986); the men appeared to have attempted a south-
ward journey with a small boat filled with provisions, dragging it overland.
The result of a thorough examination of the rather well preserved bodies,
was that they had perished not only due to scurvy but also due to lead
poisoning; this again was owed to the brand-​new mode of canning food
(Beattie and Geiger 2004). Beattie and Geiger’s careful analysis and detailed
account lifted the burden that had glued to Franklin, including him having
lost his judgement. Interestingly, Franklin’s story eventually became a lit-
erary image of the Canadian north, thus making ‘Canada’ distinct (Atwood
2004b).
    The fevers and delusions that had accompanied early expeditions into cen-
tral Africa (Fabian 2000: 3) were replaced with different perils of exposure
in the Arctic. Quite apart from the cold, the pack-​ice, and the blizzards that
took their toll, there were strange visual effects that –​when reported –​made
the public wonder about facts and fantasies, all while confirming the sub-
lime nature of the frozen experiences at a safe distance. Authors of Arctic
travelogues and proto ethnographies were aware of this public interest. The
Canadian-​Icelandic chronicler of life in the Arctic, Vilhjalmur Stefansson,
wrote in his book on The Friendly Arctic:

  Doubtless the average man turns to polar narratives, when he turns to
  them at all, with the desire and expectation of reading about suffering,
  heroic perseverance against formidable odds, and tragedy either actual or
  narrowly averted. Perhaps then, it is partly the law of supply and demand
  that accounts for the general tenor of Arctic books.
                                               (Stefansson [1921] 1943: 278)

In late 19th century, the most powerful image-​maker of unknown lands had
become the press, however, where “explorers, confirming as they did the
heroism, romance and adventure of empire, were a particularly celebrated
34 Late Arrivals

genre” (Riffenburgh 1993: 2). Around 1900, the polar explorers had taken
centre stage in the heroic conquest of blankness.
   The image of the lone explorer (often being in the company of several
other people) was to transform into an image of the lone anthropologist
in late 19th and early 20th century. Among the first to take a systematic
interest in the Arctic people was Franz Boas, whom we met in the previous
chapter. Another interested anthropologist was Marcel Mauss who, together
with Henri Beuchat, published a book on the seasonal variation and social
morphology of the Eskimos in 1906, based mainly on secondary sources
(Mauss [1906] 1967). The Inuit had definitively entered the academic world
as natural inhabitants of the legendary North at the turn of the century.
Beuchat, who was an autodidact with several academic skills took part in the
Canadian Arctic Expedition (1913–​1916) under the leadership of Stefansson;
but he disappeared in the ice, when seeking towards land from the sinking
expedition ship, Karluk, under Captain Bartlett. It had been trapped in the
ice and was gradually destroyed. Some sought to reach land on their own,
including Beuchat, and were never seen again (Barbeau 1916; Stefansson
1921). As Diamond Jenness notes, the first year of the expedition was a dis-
aster; the Karluk

  was crushed in the ice, and several members of our staff, including M.
  Beuchat, perished either on the ice or on Wrangell Island. At the same
  time, the remainder of the expedition found itself doomed to spend its first
  winter on the north coast of Alaska.
                                                          (Jenness 1922: 9)

Here, Jenness did some work with the Inuit near Barrow, but he longed to
move east along the Arctic coast.
   Karluk was the flagship of the government-​sponsored Canadian Arctic
Expedition, and the plan was for it to take a crew of scientists and explorers
from north of the Yukon to Hershel Island, but it never made it, as we heard
above. Caught in the ice, it drifted for five months until the ice perforated the
vessel, and they had to give it up; they were prepared. The captain, Robert
A. Bartlett, had made sure to build igloos and deposit food, smaller boats,
etc. on the ice, so they could survive, even if the Karluk would be crushed
(Bartlett 1914). While Bartlett sought help with a small crew in a tiny boat
(eventually landing them in Siberia), Beuchat and others who had chosen to
seek land on their own perished. The Arctic posed a particular challenge to
explorers, as Nansen saw it.

  But from first to last the history of polar exploration is a single mighty
  manifestation of the power of the unknown over the mind of man, per-
  haps greater and more evident than in any other phase of human life.
  Nowhere else have we won our way more slowly, nowhere else has every
                                                            Late Arrivals 35

  new step cost so much trouble, so many privations and sufferings, and
  certainly nowhere have the resulting discoveries promised fewer material
  advantages –​and nevertheless, new forces have always been found to
  carry the attack further, to stretch once more the limits of the world.
                                                          (Nansen 1911, I: 4)

Looking briefly towards the conquering of the South Pole, we know that
Robert Falcon Scott had brought with him a vast supply of opium and
other medicines, which proved to be of little help, however, in the Antarctic
blizzards and devastating cold (Scott [1923] 2003; Fiennes 2003). In the
polar regions, the whiteness and the vastness accounted for a certain image of
purity, further sustained by the scant populations, who softened the sense of
peril, as did the whaling stations where temporary communities of a motley
composition lived.
     When European explorers came to the Arctic region in the 19th century,
they were the strangers, not the people living there. While there was no
tradition of making totalizing representations of the landscape, European
sailors were often impressed by the ease by which the Inuit were able to
draw and discuss the nature of coastlines and places. One such case is that of
Captain William Edward Parry who was commander of a British expedition
searching for the Northwest Passage in 1822, and who relied extensively on
Inuit knowledge (Bravo 1996). He did not find any passage, but in his diaries,
he related how people on Winter Island, and most prominently a woman
informant, were able to render the landscape “with astonishing precision”
(Parry 1824: 277). Parry generally bowed to the “geographical information
obtained from the Eskimaux …on which I conceived the greatest reliance
may be placed” (ibid.: 489). Other cases of shared geographic reasoning are
known, testifying both to a successful cross-​cultural communication and
to the extensive knowledge of the landscape that the Inuit possessed. Most
astonishing is that Europeans found this knowledge surprising. After all, the
Arctic people survived by their ability to read the landscape properly. This is
different from representing it in two dimensions; yet, some people mastered
even this, though they might not have been exposed to paper and pen before.
There practical geographical knowledge could be drawn out.
     When ethnographic interest replaced, or extended discovery as the cen-
tral tenet of Arctic expeditions, the newcomers soon saw fit to engage with
the Inuit. In the far north-​western corner of Greenland, Robert Peary sat the
tone by relying entirely on local resources, including the people (Peary 1898,
I-​II). Fresh game replaced canned food as the main supply, meaning that the
explorers travelled considerably lighter and remained healthier.
     Catering for the European public’s myth of Polar expedition may have led
a number of travellers to oversell their hardships and not least their lonely
conquest of the wild polar lands. Loneliness often included the foregrounding
of the explorer’s ‘I’ when describing his travels. Even Boas, who did
36 Late Arrivals

acknowledge the contribution of both the Scottish leader of the whaling
station, James Mutch, and his Inuit sledge drivers, notably Signa (and who
even had a personal servant with him), tends to fall back on the first person
singular, when he refers to particular trips in his ethnographic notes. Yet in
the preface to his major works, his companions are duly acknowledged as co-​
creators (Boas 1901, 1907; Krupnik 2016; Harper 2008). By individualizing
themselves, the explorers became invested with a special kind of heroism
by their audience that went beyond the celebration of the individual. As
Riffenburgh has it:

  In an era of imperialism and extreme nationalism, when the state was
  extolled as supreme and the individual was subordinated to, yet made
  to personify, the nation, the men who achieved remarkable feats were
  more than just popular heroes; they were symbols of real and imagined
  nationalist or imperialist cultural greatness. Explorers, confirming as they
  did the heroism, romance and adventure of empire, were a particularly
  celebrated genre. They were pictured as journeying into the blank spaces
  of the globe, where they were confronted by constant challenges and
  danger, both natural and human.
                                                     (Riffenburgh 1993: 1–​2)

The passion for blankness, alluded to by Conrad in Heart of Darkness (1902)
as the place of his dreams –​all while sending his protagonists to the dark
interior of Africa –​was one that the audience could relate to, even if it were
not always actually, substantially, true. Most ethnographers having worked
in the Arctic, probably recognize the pleasure of enticing a home-​audience
by a first-​person narrative about the implicit bravery in the cold –​while
mostly just tagging along with the local experts. In fact, the bravery was and
is always theirs, as was and is their generosity towards the anthropologists,
without which the latter could achieve nothing. Even though modern
ethnographers know and are willing to admit this, they (we) face a challenge
when it comes to writing; the pen remains in the hand of the ethnographer,
even when writing down a conversation.


Chroniclers of American Natives
Significantly, the Arctic seems to have been beyond the border of the romantic
New World, as found on the American plains, and sought out by aristocrats
from Germany and France in post-​revolutionary Europe (Liebersohn 1998).
It was probably stirred by de Tocqueville’s work on Democracy in America
(1835 and 1840), praising the democratic institutions of the New World.
Most interesting in our context is the tribute to the American Indians, with
whom he saw an affinity to European aristocrats. Tocqueville himself was
one of them, riding out hunting with the Native Americans and contributing
to a romantic discourse on the ‘natural democrats’ (Liebersohn 1998: 3). It
                                                           Late Arrivals 37

became confounded with the massive immigrations from Europe and the
making of the (new) Americans. While the aristocratic claim was soon to dis-
appear, an image of American democrats persisted (ibid.: 39ff.).
    The romantic image was transferred to the landscape, including Niagara
Falls and the awesome untouched forests, reminding us about Alexander
von Humboldt who wrote extensively about the waterfalls of the Orinoco,
and waterways more generally (Humboldt [1849] 2014: 117–​            40). The
New World not only offered new landscapes, but also contributed to a new
romantic view of nature. In Liebersohn’s view, the romantic stance was in
sharp contrast to the “pace of movement across the continent as settlers cut
down trees, built roads, and linked fields and houses and towns to the coasts
and the wider world” (Liebersohn 1998: 66). A new literary genre emerged,
on the eve of ‘natural America’, exemplified by the Last of the Mohicans
by James Fennimore Cooper (1826), a book of ‘many messages’ (Libersohn
1998: 75).
    In fact, ‘natural America’ was on the wane, all while still attracting
naturalists from Europe. Humboldt’s travels also led him to North America,
where he visited President Jefferson in the White House, having recently
taken up residence in the emerging city of Washington. In fact, the White
House was still under construction (Wulff 2016: 99). Jefferson was deeply
interested in matters of nature and welcomed Humboldt in his home, where
he kept carpenter’s tools, flowerpots, maps, and charts in his office, as well
as shelves filled with books; “Humboldt was delighted to find his hero ‘living
with the simplicity of a philosopher’ ” (ibid. 101).
    This brief excursion to America south of the Arctic serves to remind us
that borders and historical developments, dreams and political powers are
not given but very much in the hands of people, including immigrants to
the new continent. Among them were also Jesuits, who had first arrived in
America in the 17th century, with wavering influence on the natives (see e.g.,
Parkman 1867). The order had been dissolved in France and the colonies in
1764, but was re-​established in 1814; meanwhile, many Jesuits disembarked
to North America, where teaching was obviously needed in La Nouvelle
France (Canada). The Native Americans might not have agreed to that.
    There were other missionaries in the North. In a highly interesting book,
father Émile Petitot (1838–​1916) from Marseilles, ordained as a priest and
leaving for Canada where he worked as a catholic missionary for 12 years
(1862–​1874) at the mission of Fort Good Hope, has been portrayed in all
his fragility by Pierre Déléage (2020). Fort Good Hope was an outlet of the
Hudson’s Bay Company, situated on the banks of the Mackenzie River, having
its source in the Great Slave Lake. The closest Native Americans were the
Dené –​southern neighbours of the Inuit; like many other tribes their way of
life had adapted to the presence of the Company. They exchanged fur pelts for
guns, tobacco, clothing and alcohol, and some of them would even frequent
the mission Our Lady of Good Hope, a few kilometres from the Fort, where
Petitot (and others) would seek to save them (Déleage 2020: 7). Missionaries
38 Late Arrivals

of diverse denominations were eventually found all over the Arctic, also fur-
ther north among the Inuit, where more Company Forts were eventually
established along rivers and seashores. Petitot had a strong attachment to the
Dené youths, with whom he identified, and whom he missed sorely, when
they went away from the fort on some expedition. He wrote:

  I could not get used to these frequent separations, the trips that made
  these nomadic beings so happy. What a joy for them to go back to live in
  the open air, to exchange the dull chimney corner and floor for the elkskin
  lodge, with green branches scattered on the frozen ground and a large
  fire of blazing pine logs in the middle of the dwelling. They followed the
  reindeer as they wandered; they nourished themselves on the slaughter;
  they made eight, ten, twenty meals a day, if they so wished; they cut down
  patches of forest for firewood to warm themselves; and they enjoyed com-
  plete freedom without any outside interference.
                                              (Petitot in Déléage 2020: 11–​2)

Identifying with the Dené youths, Petitot also learned their language and
seems to have been a fine linguist and scholar; his weakness was his spe-
cial friendships with some of the young boys, which made him remorseful,
knowing that such relations contradicted his calling. To get past the longing
for intimate relations with the Dené youngsters, Petitot asked his “superiors
to allow him to undertake what he considered to be the most assured way
to rehabilitate himself in the eyes of God and men: to become a martyr to
the infidel Inuit” (Déléage 2020: 17). These were of a different kind from the
Dené, not least because they had remained beyond the reach of Christian
missions. Moreover, they were in continuous conflict with the Dené. As it
happened, Petitot’s expeditions to the Inuit ended in spectacular failure. For
him they represented the height of savagery, yet they had fascinated him as a
child, and he sincerely wanted to become their first ethnographer –​as well as
their first missionary. Déléage writes about Petitot:

  His writings reveal the intense emotion he felt when confronted with the
  Inuit, whom he described as “thieves and villains” and as living naked
  inside their homes, forming a polygamous people who “surpassed all
  others in license and immorality, imitating their ancestor through cynicism
  of their vile practices.” From reading travelers’ stories, the priest believed
  he could infer that “the crime of sodomy is permitted and practiced as a
  social institution.”
                                                         (Déléage 2020: 17–​8)

Here is a man who constantly held his mirror up to nature, and somehow
found himself reflected. Often, when travelling with Dené in the region,
Petitot felt vulnerable to Inuit hostility –​making him hope for becoming a
martyr (ibid.: 19–​20). He seems to have become beset with illusions, making
                                                            Late Arrivals 39

a temporary and later a permanent return to France inevitable –​after a
long spell at an asylum in Montreal. The New World, for all its allure and
attractive youngsters, was dangerous for a nervous man, whose legacy –​not
least his massive work on the oral traditions of the Dené and the Inuit –​was
all but forgotten for a long time, until it was revisited and closely studied by
Déléage.
    Early explorers in the Arctic often lived with the natives in a more relaxed
fashion than would later scholars. In High Arctic Greenland, regularly
visited by Robert Peary over almost two decades in the period of 1892–​
1909, seeking out the best way to the North Pole, the relations between the
explorers and the Inughuit were often sexual, as witnessed by the offspring
left behind. Both Robert Peary, Captain Bartlett, and Matthew Henson,
the three most prominent members of the expedition, sired children who
would eventually take their last names (at least temporarily). There was
never any stigma attached to being a “child of a ship,” as I have been told
by inhabitants in the sparsely populated region. Apart from that the High
Arctic region was perceived rather differently by individual explorers. Peary
made an interesting observation, while reading his way during the long
Polar night:

  We did a good deal of reading. I had a very complete arctic library, and
  this was chiefly in demand. The fact that we were living under arctic
  conditions whetted the appetite of my boys for records of Arctic explor-
  ation. All these books were eagerly devoured for the story they contained,
  the adventures they recorded, and the useful hints that we might derive
  from them. Somehow we could not make our ideas of the country, the
  natives, the winter night, the cold, the storms, or the hardships agree at all
  with those of some predecessors who had spent a season not very far from
  McCormick Bay. Viewed in the light of our own experience, some things
  we read seemed to us unjust, particularly in respect of the happy, simple-​
  minded natives, with whom our relations were so friendly and who were
  so helpful to us; some things seemed exaggerated; and some, in spite of
  our willingness to believe, took on an aspect of pure romance.
                                                           (Peary 1898, I: 172)

The wavering between romance and desolation seems to have been a general
feature among newcomers to the Arctic region. Norwegian Eivind Astrup,
who travelled with Peary for his first two campaigns in the 1890s, was clear-​
headed and seems to have had a deeper sense of the ‘polar tribes’ and their
having spread across the North American coast all the way to Greenland.

  Of these Polar tribes the most northern are the Esquimaux, or “innuits”
  as they call themselves.
    From the upper coast of America these restless people have by degrees
  spread over the northern archipelago, and thence even over Smith’s
40 Late Arrivals

  Sound and Baffin’s Bay, to gain a footing in Greenland. A natural curi-
  osity and a longing for the unknown has no doubt caused them to
  press forward, while strong competition in the hunting-​fields farther
  south, and consequent disagreements, have increased their rambling
  propensities.
                                                     (Astrup 1898: 48–​9)

The constitutive moment of professional anthropology around 1900, was
very much a child of enlightenment expeditions and later colonialism. There
were few places on the earth that had not already been reported on by
somebody, and the anthropologists-​to-​be tapped into that resource. Some
parts of the world were more accessible than others, yet the call of the
unknown was not to be stilled. In many ways, Arctic anthropology was
the offspring of natural science, aptly illustrated by Franz Boas’ course
towards anthropology, by way of an interest in seawater. Although he
converted from natural science to anthropology, no doubt his interest in
the quality of seawater prompted Boas to consider new aspects of sensing
and seeing (Stocking [1968] 1982: 142). In many ways this interest became
the backbone of his cultural relativist view upon the peoples of the North
(Helmreich 2011: 134). From the investigation of the influence of envir-
onment on people’s perceptions, his interest gradually changed focus.
Spending a year “among Eskimos had a profound influence upon the devel-
opment of my views, not immediately, but because it led me away from
my former interests and toward the desire to understand the behaviour of
human beings” (Boas 1938: 201).
   His was a fieldwork on ice as much as by the seawater; yet even –​or
maybe particularly –​in its frozen form, the sea still shaped local life. In
his well-​known diaries and letters, mostly addressed to his fiancée, Marie
Krackovizer in New York, he wrote about his life with the ice and the for-
bidding weather. One of his primary aims was to survey the eastern coast of
Baffin Land, and to study its impression upon human perception. Like other
anthropologists, he depended entirely on collaboration with local hunters;
exceptionally, he also had a valet, Wilhelm, with him.

  Wilhelm and I will go north tomorrow morning to survey the coast fur-
  ther. We are coming to the water holes, which are kept open by the
  current and prevent us from going farther. As we were leaving the 3
  Eskimos brought our sledge and dogs down the steep ice-​foot face. At
  low tide the face drops steeply about 15’, so everything has to be let
  down on lines.
    The fog hinders us greatly, but we advanced about 4 miles northward.
  When we came back Ssigna was busy chopping up food for the dogs, i.e.
  sawing up frozen seal meat, then chopping it into smaller pieces with an
  axe. Today it is –​40o with a strong N wind. Itu and Tome were at the most
  northerly polynya and brought back 3 seals, whereas Ssigna has none. (…)
                                                             Late Arrivals 41

     You see, Marie, here I am writing the same things on the pages of my
  journal and to you, since this is the only possibility of getting some infor-
  mation about my daily life to you. The igloo is too cold, so I do not write
  more than is absolutely necessary and if I wait a few days you will not
  learn what I am actually doing. So please accept these brief notes with the
  occasional words addressed specifically to you.
     Now, when we have been in the igloo for about 4 hours, it is warm
  enough to write something, though it is still not up to the freezing point,
  yet I feel quite comfortable. Feelings of what is pleasant and unpleasant
  are really quite relative. At home we would be infinitely sorry for some-
  body in our situation, yet here we are cheerful and in good spirits.
              (Franz Boas, 13 December 1883; in Müller-​Wille 1998: 151–​2)

On the following day, they head further north on a difficult ice foot, where
Boas fell into the water, and where they made camp in –​38oC. “Ssigna built an
igloo and fortunately our meal was ready in 4 ½ hours” (ibid.: 152). What such
passages reveal is the extent to which ethnography in the High North is for-
matted along with the experiences of cold and ice on the edge of the sea and not
least by local hosts and helpers who were essential to anthropological work –​
and to survival. Writing itself was proof of both work and life, and Boas’ letter
diary, addressed to Marie –​which could only be posted at long intervals, when
the explorers visited a trading post –​served as literary proof of his work.
   In between letters, Boas read Kant as an antidote to raw nature, no doubt
also influencing his thinking about rationality and sensory experience.
This points to another feature of travelling in the cold; the explorers and
ethnographers would often bring a certain piece of literature with them into
the field. We heard about Peary’s well-​stocked library above, and we know
for instance, how Diamond Jenness had brought a copy of Homer’s Odyssey
with him on the Canadian Arctic expedition, in which he could find spiritual
comfort (Jenness 1991: xxi).
   In the Arctic, travel and exploration had a distinct flavour not least
owing to the cold. If exploring Central Brazil could be seen as an act of
penetrating a world shrouded in trees, Arctic exploration implied an act of
understanding an unmarked world, perceived as a void. This perspective was
further cemented in modern travel writing, where ‘the rest of the world’ was
described from the vantage point of a Eurocentric ‘planetary consciousness’
(Pratt 1992: 15ff).
   A particularly persistent image in European representations of the Far
North was the ancient image of Ultima Thule, alluded to above, situated
on the edge of the horizon. This image persistently influenced explorers’
descriptions of the Inuit. In the process, the natives were not necessarily
incorporated into the central perspective. As Yi-​Fu Tuan writes: “The Eskimo
live at the Arctic margin of the inhabited world. They did not, however, know
this until they came into contact with a large number of white men. Before the
encounter they saw their habitat not only as the world’s geographical center
42 Late Arrivals

but also its cultural and population center” (Tuan 1990: 34). Paradoxically,
Tuan’s sympathetic attempt at relativizing geographical knowledge may
have bypassed local Inuit understandings. While it is true that people always
experience their world from their standing at its centre, it does not mean that
they have no ideas of their position in a larger geographical order. Yet, we
still find Diamond Jenness, studying people in the Coronation Gulf Region in
Northern Canada, musing over the lack of mapping skills.

  I distributed pencils and paper to several adults, both men and women,
  and asked them to draw sketches of people, houses, caribou, and anything
  else they cared to attempt. The results were amusing, similar to what we
  should expect from a child of six. Even my chart they were unable to
  follow. One man, more intelligent than the rest, seemed to grasp that a
  certain line might represent the coast along which he had recently been
  travelling; but when I asked him to name and point out the positions of
  the capes and streams he had passed on his way, he placed his finger indif-
  ferently on places as far apart as Victoria Island and Great Bear Lake. Yet
  an Eskimo of northern Alaska, who was unable to read and had never
  held a pencil in his hand, once drew me a remarkably accurate outline
  of the Colville River and all its tributaries, beside many vivid sketches of
  camp scenes and the hunting of seal and Caribou.
                                                  (Jenness [1928] 1959: 46–​7)

Jenness concludes that compared to the Alaskan Inuit, the small group at
Coronation Gulf is astonishingly primitive. While longing for the unspoilt
natives, when they were finally found their way of life was lamented, even by
Jenness who otherwise deeply appreciated their way of living. In 1888, Boas
had reported on the Inuit knowledge of the geography of their country in the
following manner:

  They have a very clear conception of all the countries they have seen
  or heard of, knowing the distances by day’s journeys, or, as they say,
  by sleeps, and the directions by the cardinal points. So far as I know,
  all these tribes call true south piningnang, while the other points are
  called according to the weather prevailing while the wind blows from the
  different quarters.
                                                   (Boas [1888] 1964: 235)

   True south invariably points away from the coastal habitat of the Arctic
Eskimo to ‘somewhere else’, to lands beyond local experience, while the
other cardinal directions are part of the everyday sense of weather and wind.
The shared marking of true south is hardly a simple result of the colonial
encounter; it is based in a sense of living at the margins. As discussed by
Vincent Crapanzano, people are constantly concerned with both openness
and closure in their construction of horizons that determine what we experi-
ence and how we interpret it.
                                                            Late Arrivals 43

  When a horizon and whatever lies beyond it are given articulate form,
  they freeze our view of the reality that immediately confronts us –​fatally
  I’d say, were it not for the fact that once the beyond is articulated, a new
  horizon emerges and with it a new beyond.
                                                       (Crapanzano 2004: 2)

The promise of new imaginative horizons still drives anthropologists to the
limits of the world as known –​and as more than geography.


Emerging Representations
A strong feature of most reports and early ethnographies of the Arctic is the
depiction of the first encounter with the natives. Explorers and ethnographers
were equally amazed by their own feat of finding these people, even if they
would often have heard or read about them in other sources –​which they
may inadvertently give away. They often make it, but not everybody had
such luck at their first attempt. There is no doubt that the difficulty of even
arriving have an important impact on our sense of the field; this also applied
to explorers who did not necessarily see themselves as ethnographers but
who may have become so by default –​spending long periods in the company
of ‘other people’ such as the Eskimo. A case in point is provided by Astrup,
who wrote how he and his fellow travellers on the ship, Kite, were looking
out for people, whose aid they badly needed to secure their return to their
winter harbour in time. They were on the track:

  On August 12th we formed a party and made for the island, where we
  found some Esquimaux. The first meeting with them was very singular, as
  we did not understand a word of their language. At last we succeeded, by
  means of signs and grimaces, in making them understand our wishes. A
  family, consisting of husband, wife, and two children, agreed to go with
  us in our boat, and as soon as we had them aboard we shaped our course
  homeward, arriving on August 18th.
                                                         (Astrup 1898: 21)

The family immediately understood their need for help –​for the women to
make the necessary skin garments, and for the men to hunt and fill up meat
depots. More locals came along and a whole new community emerged on the
beach. No fear, and certainly no doubt about the white men’s wishes. Yet,
Astrup still seems to thirst for meeting more people –​not realising how far
communication between all settlements in the region extended, embracing all
the inhabitants in one community.

  It was on the evening of July 23rd, 1891, that in one of Kite’s boats we
  slowly approached the shore somewhere on the south side of Whale
  Sound, having a short time before discovered from the deck of the vessel
  what we supposed to be human dwellings.
44 Late Arrivals

  These dwellings, which soon proved to be a collection of tents and earth-​
  huts, were situated at the end of a low projecting promontory, where we
  succeeded with some difficulty in landing.
     Of the eleven inhabitants of the place, the men immediately came down
  to the beach to meet us, whilst the women and children cautiously kept in
  the rear. A couple of us involuntarily extended our hands towards them in
  friendly greeting; but to our surprise, instead of grasping them they stood
  staring at us, without the slightest idea of what such an advance should
  signify. Soon, however, they saw that we intended well, and then they gave
  us a warm welcome …
     It was evident that they had never been in touch with the civilised world,
  or seen a vessel, which was to their eyes most remarkable.
                                                         (Astrup 1898: 73–​4)

To present readers it is far from evident that they would never have seen a
vessel or been in touch with the civilized world. They, like their neighbours
who had helped guiding Kite’s crew to their wintering place, knew white men
quite well from multiple expeditions and whaling vessels that had preceded
Peary’s campaign since early 19th century, when the Melville Bay gradually
reopened after some centuries of closure due to the Little Ice Age. All the
way through the ‘first’ encounters with the Inuit, the invariably new people,
there are echoes of ancient images of the land of beyond and its strange
people, living more northerly than anybody, where it is hardly possible. This
is obvious also in Rasmussen’s first book, Nye Mennesker (‘New People’,
1905) –​even if he knew better.
   Another sense of ‘firstness’ relates to the unearthing of the prehistoric
Thule Culture; in the Arctic, remains of the past had been covered in earth
and permafrost, twisting the question of discovery in another direction. We
heard how ‘Thule’ found its place on the world map, and one would have
thought that there was nothing more to add to the name. Yet, Thule was still
to make a surprise appearance in archaeology, eventually bestowing the Inuit
with a new past. Among the members of the Fifth Thule Expedition (1921–​
1924), was archaeologist Therkel Mathiassen, who was to place ‘Thule’ in
Inuit prehistory.
   The overall vision of the Fifth Thule Expedition was to trace the migra-
tion routes and links between the various Inuit groups living in the thinly
populated coastal areas of Northern Canada and Greenland. The main
investigations of the expedition took place among the Central Eskimo
who had first been described by Boas (1888). Here, it befell Mathiassen
to undertake the archaeological excavations, tracing back some of the
salient features of these people. Meanwhile, Rasmussen was to collect their
tales and poetry, and ethnographer Kaj Birket-​Smith to report on their
material culture, yet obviously –​given the circumstances and difficulties
of dating anything in this deep-​frozen landscape –​there had to be a con-
siderable overlap between their tasks. Yet, Mathiassen sat out as a staunch
                                                             Late Arrivals 45

archaeologist, describing his method meticulously, and ending his descrip-
tion thus:

  Everywhere the greatest difficulty was the frozen ground; even when the
  summer was at the warmest only two or three centimetres thawed in the
  course of the day, the result being that one was three or four days in
  getting through a layer of 10 cm; as an example I might say that it took
  more than a month to get to the bottom of a house-​ruin that was almost
  levelled to the ground, or to the bottom of a deep refuse heap. But by
  having a large number of ruins and sections in hand at the same time the
  work nevertheless proceeded fairly well.
                                                  (Mathiassen 1927a: 2–​3)

In this passage we get a fine picture of a field science, working through layer
after layer of meaning by a painstaking process. Another striking image is
that of the permafrost preventing serious digging; the cultural remains had
to thaw gradually by being exposed to the midday sun. A new (old) ‘culture’
was thawed out of the ice in a curiously inverse process to the ethnographic
gesture of narrative freezing.
   During the Second Thule Expedition (1916–​1918), its members had briefly
assisted Captain Comer who did some digging in the vicinity of the Thule
Station in Northwest Greenland, and along with ‘Comer’s Midden’, as the
find was called, a certain number of dwellings and tools had been found and
registered. When Mathiassen started digging in the area of Naujan in Canada
he found and recognized similar tools as those from Comer’s Midden; this
had some consequence.

  As the first find of this culture was thus found at Thule by members of the
  Second Thule Expedition and the second find, at Naujan, was made by
  the Fifth Thule Expedition, I consider it warrantable to attach the name
  of Thule to the old Central Eskimo culture as represented by the Naujan
  find.
                                    (Mathiassen 1927a: 89, emphasis original)

Out of the ice –​the Thule Culture, once again. The interesting point in the
present connection relates to the backtracking of the Thule Culture itself. It is
“a highly developed and remarkable Eskimo culture” (Mathiassen 1927b: 6),
which “in many respects, is richer and more developed than that met with
among the present-​day Central Eskimos” (ibid.).
    The Thule Culture was identified by its main tools, of which 35 are
listed and charted. The list is not exhaustive, Mathiassen says, but they are
the most characteristic. The ancient Inuit were equipped with dog sledges,
kayaks, umiaks, blubber-​lamps, igloos, spears, bows, and arrows, salmon-​
spears, ulos, and hand drills; they knew how to make clay-​pots and to hollow
out soapstone for lamps. In addition to the building of igloos they also knew
46 Late Arrivals

how to construct houses with whalebones –​a surplus gain from the hunting
of whale. In short, they were supremely well adapted to life on the coast of
the Polar Sea, and they had a rich symbolic imagery as well, expressed in
decorations of various kinds.
   At the time, for most archaeologists the goal was to label “geographically
and temporally restricted assemblages of prehistoric archaeological material
as cultures” (Trigger 1989: 162). This was also Mathiassen’s objective in
a work that was designed at adding sound archaeological evidence to the
earlier speculations provided by Hans Peter Steensby (1916) on the origin of
the Inuit culture. Mathiassen says:

  A culture is, as we know, a very complicated organism, consisting of a
  large number of elements; it is not a permanent, not an unchangeable
  structure, but is ever subject to the laws of development and adjustment.
  Nevertheless, there are some elements which seem to form an integral part
  of the culture, which seem to be inextricably bound up in it …What we
  have to get hold of is what we might call the representative forms of the
  culture: types which at once seem to have been in general use in the Thule
  culture but not within the whole of Eskimo territory, types which thus
  may be termed a characteristic of the Thule culture.
                                                    (Mathiassen 1927b: 3–​4)

In the Arctic, it proved difficult to decide upon the temporal framework of
the Thule culture; it was next to impossible to date the finds, other than
by geological inference, such as the level of the sea. Mathiassen dated the
Naujan find up to about 1600 A D (ibid.: 7), suggesting that it might have
been even younger in Greenland. The relative dating is owed to the east-
ward migration from the ‘original’ area of the Thule Culture in Canada and
into northern Greenland. Among the tales collected by Knud Rasmussen in
Northwest Greenland there is one concerning the immigration from Canada
within living memory (of the 1860s); he met a few of them and noted their
stories (Rasmussen 1905, 1908). The distinction between prehistory, history,
and ethnography dissolves in the (tentative) identification of migrating Arctic
cultures.
   As it happened, Mathiassen did not find any evidence in support of
Steensby’s palaeo-​Eskimo culture. From his excavation of ten sites in the
region, Mathiassen suggests that while the Thule Culture may not be ‘the
original Eskimo culture’, it is still the oldest culture of that particular
region: “Everywhere we find at the bottom of the refuse heaps and in the
earliest ruins a typical Thule culture, bearing in fact a stronger stamp of
marine animal hunting the deeper we go” (Mathiassen 1927b: 200). In
a popular book based on the two scientific volumes, Mathiassen relates
how archaeology has contributed to establishing the origin and history of
Eskimo culture, and now speaks firmly about the authentic Eskimo culture,
“which has been called the Thule-​culture,” and he goes on to enumerate
                                                            Late Arrivals 47

the typical tools and artefacts –​in the same manner as in the scientific
report (Mathiassen 1929: 163). Already in 1929, the ‘Thule-​culture’ had
taken on a life of its own, if invented only a few years before. The people
living on the edge of the horizon suddenly had not only a dimly perceived
and narrated past but also a prehistory –​emerging full scale from the
frozen fields.
   What happened to the Thule culture was not simply its identification
as a culture among others, but its emergence as the authentic Inuit cul-
ture –​establishing the prototype as a community of hunters, relying on sea
mammals. It was not the oldest among the Inuit cultures, but it was the one
most befitting the ancient image of a people living on the edge of the world.
The caribou hunters were no match for this image, and Mathiassen speaks
rather lowly of them. Authenticity, here, is a token of the general image of
Thule as primordial and ‘other’ than of a lived life.


Reaching the Impossible
Another case of discovery made by late arrivals relates to the North Pole,
and the fight between Robert Peary and Frederick Cook over the claim to
be its rightful discoverer. As suggested by Lisa Bloom (1993), the competi-
tive drive to reach the North Pole seemed framed by nationalism and ideas
of masculinity; both Peary and Cook made claims to it, but only one could
be declared winner in the order of things. Let us hear Cook, who was cer-
tain that he had reached the North Pole in 1908, but only able to publish
his claim a few days before Peary announced his own feat in 1909. The
notes, astronomical observations, instruments, and proofs that Cook had
packed up and left at Etah, for his envoi from the New York Explorers’ Club,
Whitney, to take back on Peary’s ship, never reached America. By his return
to the United States, Whitney observed: “Ten years ago Peary did with the
explorer Sverdrup, who was cruising in Smith Sound, what he had done with
Cook: he refused to bring back any of Sverdrup’s letters and records” (in
Bloom 1993: 31). Discovery needs documentation.
   Cook went to trial and was deemed a fraud, since he could not produce
evidence of having reached the Pole. Meanwhile he had published a narrative
in the National Geographic in 1909, on ‘The Discovery of the North Pole’, in
which one can read the following passage of the event of discovery:

  Slowly but surely we neared the turning point. Good astronomical
  observations were daily procured to fix the advancing stages.
    The ice steadily improved, but still there was a depressing monotony of
  scene, and life had no pleasures, no spiritual recreation, nothing to relieve
  the steady physical drag of chronic fatigue.
    But there comes an end to this as to all things. On April 21 the first
  corrected altitude of the sun gave 89 deg. 59 min. 46. Sec.
    The Pole, therefore, was in sight.
48 Late Arrivals

    We advanced the fourteen seconds, made supplementary observations
  and prepared to stay long enough to permit a double round of observations.
    Etukishook and Ahwelab were told that we had reached the “Neig
  Nail” and they sought to celebrate by an advance of savage joys.
    At last we had pierced the boreal center and the flag had been raised to
  the coveted breezes of the North Pole.
    The day was April 21, 1908. The sun indicated local noon, but time was
  a negative problem, for here all meridian meets.
    …..
    We turned our backs to the pole on April 23 and began the long return
  march.
                                                        (Cook 1909: 892–​6)

In the very same issue of the National Geographic, of October 1909, Peary
also has an entry on ‘The Discovery of the North Pole’, where he published
part of his journal:

  The Pole at last! The prize of three centuries. My dream and goal for
  twenty years! Mine at last! I cannot bring myself to realize it. It all seems
  so simple and commonplace. As Bartlett [the Captain] said when turning
  back, and speaking of his work being in these exclusive regions which no
  mortal has ever penetrated before, “It’s just like every day.”
     Of course I had my sensations that made sleep impossible for hours,
  despite my utter fatigue –​the sensations of a lifetime; but I have no room
  for them here. The first thirty hours at the Pole were spent in taking
  observations; in going some ten miles beyond our camp, and some eight
  miles to the right of it; in taking photographs, planting my flags, depositing
  my records, studying the horizon with my telescope for possible land, and
  searching for a practicable place to make a sounding …
     We had reached the goal, but the return was still before us. It was essen-
  tial that we reach the land before the next spring tide, and we must strain
  every nerve to do this.
                                                          (Peary 1909: 888–​91)

Mine at last! In fact, Peary could also not produce watertight evidence of
having reached the Pole. The photograph of his men and the flag at the
designated point was not enough, but he was vindicated by the National
Geographic –​at the expense of Cook –​and was later buried as the Arlington
National Cemetery wrapped in Stars and Stripes and honoured by legend
as the discoverer of the North Pole. Cook never came back on the scene of
polar exploration, having to suffer defamation until the last weeks of his life.
A recent study of his notebook, what remained of it, supports the suggestion
that he did not make it –​but what does it mean (Bryce 2015). It is interesting
to note the place of technology in the affirmation of discovery; obviously, the
                                                            Late Arrivals 49

North Pole cannot simply be observed, but must be proved by more than just
sight (Bloom 1993: 49). The ice does not give the pole away by itself, just as
the frozen landscapes of Baffin Land did not of themselves produce evidence
for the Thule Culture. In both cases, human perseverance and technology
made discovery ‘happen’ –​if not always easily acknowledged.
   A comparable fight for the pole took place in the Antarctic, where Robert
Scott was beaten by Roald Amundson, by a small but decisive margin, in
their respective quests for the South Pole. Amundsen’s party reached it in
December 1911, while Scott’s team arrived in January 1912 –​only to find
themselves beaten. Worse, Scott’s team perished two months later, just 11
miles from base camp, while Amundsen returned, slighting his adversary and
continuing to do so, even when he learnt about his demise. Fridtjof Nansen
eventually had to sort out the disgrace that fell upon Norway through his
diplomatic connections.
   For most ethnographers, the quest for finding ‘new people’ was not to
prove oneself, but to add to general knowledge. In some cases, the ethnog-
rapher was even bound to turn the question of discovery around and claim
that the subjects in view were well-​known. Stefansson proves this, when
arriving to a village by Prince Albert Sound with 27 dwellings inhabited by
the Kanghirgyuargmiut, and stating that some of the other tribes visited “had
a special interest in that they had never before been seen by white men, either
they or their ancestors; these had an interest of their own in being the one
tribe which have oftener than others been visited by white men” (Stefansson
1921: 279). He elaborates:

  One of the first things I did at the village of the Prince Albert Sound
  people was to inquire about their knowledge of white men. This group
  is not only the most prosperous as well as the most numerous of all the
  Copper Eskimo, but they are also remarkable for the extent of their sea-
  sonal migrations and for their consequently greater amount of informa-
  tion in regard to their own country and other countries and their general
  broad-​mindedness. With them as with us, extensive travels have had their
  important effect upon the mental outlook and the character of the people.
  I found therefore (sic.) that they were familiar not only with the presence
  of the exploring vessels to the west of their country during the time of the
  Franklin Search, and with the location of the Bay of Mercy and the aban-
  donment of M’Clure’s ship there, but they also knew about King William
  Island far to the east, and about the frequent visits of white men to it, and
  they even had information about the distant island of North Devon, as we
  could tell not only by the geographic description they gave of the country,
  but also by their reporting correctly the name of the people who inhabit
  it, the Tununirohirmiut, which is also the name recorded by Dr. Franz
  Boas and others from information gathered in Baffin Land and elsewhere
  in the east.
50 Late Arrivals

   But most remarkable of all was the variety of countries they had seen with
   their own eyes.
                                                     (Stefansson 1921: 284)

Stefansson elaborates on this and ascertains that they really had been all
over the place and had met named parties of white men, who had roamed
in their country. Stefansson’s own feat is to ‘discover’ this most cosmopol-
itan tribe and to prove their claims. It is striking, that it should come as a
surprise that the people knew their country, and that they also had taken
note of foreigners who came visiting and who might be potential trading
partners.
   If scientific discovery is mediated and authenticated by technology and
established procedures, anthropological discovery is mediated by the per-
ception of the anthropologist him-​or herself. A case in point, and my final
example, is delivered by Rasmussen. In 1921, on his Fifth Thule Expedition,
there is a strong echo of his first meeting with the Polar Inuit, as related above.
More than two months after the expedition’s arrival at Danish Island on the
northern fringe of Hudson Bay, “we had not set eyes on a single human being
of the tribes we had come all that way to visit” (Rasmussen 1929: 12). They
set out towards Repulse Bay, where they knew of a Hudson’s Bay Company
trading station. On the way, Rasmussen heard the unmistakable sound of a
gunshot and soon saw a line of dog sledges moving in their direction. When
they met, one man broke away from the rest and threw his arms around
Rasmussen to signal to the dogs that he was a friend.

   So there we stood, laughing and shaking each other, while the dogs, crest-
   fallen, lay down on the ice, as if ashamed at having mistaken a friend for
   an enemy. The first thing that struck me when I had recovered a little was
   that the man understood all I said; and I understood him in turn when he
   spoke.
                                                       (Rasmussen 1929: 13)

   These, then, were the people whom the Greenlanders called Akilinermiut
   (“those who dwell in the lands beyond the great sea”); the people I had
   heard about ever since, as a boy, I had first begun to listen to Eskimo tales.
   I could not have found a more picturesque setting for a first impression.
   Here was a whole caravan out in the midst of the ice, men and women in
   curious dresses of skins, like living illustrations to the Greenland story-​
   tellers’ tales of the terrible “inland folk.” Every stitch of their clothing was
   of caribou skin, fine, short-​haired skins of animals killed at the opening of
   the hunting season.
                                                            (Rasmussen 1929: 14)

What is striking is the parallel between this ‘first meeting’ and the previous
one with the Polar Inuit. The new friends once again became anchored in the
                                                             Late Arrivals 51

tales he had heard in Greenland. He intimates both wildness and familiarity
when describing their clothes in detail and continues:

  Many different impressions passed rapidly through my mind at this
  first meeting, but there was one thing which moved me beyond all else,
  and almost at once made a bond between us, as if we had been old
  acquaintances, and that was the language. True, I had always known that
  the natives here spoke the same tongue, but I had never imagined there
  would be so little difference that we could enter into converse at once
  without the slightest hindrance.
                                                     (Rasmussen 1929: 14)

The expansion on ‘first meetings’ and fights over ‘first arrivals’ in the present
context highlights the persistent trope of discovery, summoning the interest
and fame that adhered to Polar explorers.


Dreams and Definitions
While we have travelled beyond a strictly ethnographic view in this chapter,
we may also have experienced how, in anthropological practice, discovery
and definition always merge. This goes for scientific practice more gener-
ally, and it is deeply integrated with the quest for mapping and naming even
the most volatile terrains, the most slippery animals, and the ever-​moving
populations. World history may itself be read from maps, constructing a
particular mode of territorialisation (Brotton 2014). The challenge in the
Arctic was (and is) that the territories were (and are) more slippery than
elsewhere. Even the sea was no boundary but an extension of the Inuit
social world; and the law of the High Seas is increasingly useless as the
sea ice shifts –​as do the visions of an open Polar Sea, now formally cut
up by geographical measures and national rights to the seabed around the
North Pole.
   The dream of discovery, whether of Poles or people, is part of a dream
to sort out one’s own coordinates. Early anthropologists categorized the
peoples on the earth, mapping tribes, where none had existed before, and
often inventing names for them in the process, mostly with reference to their
place or their resources. The northward push revealed the impossibility of
the enlightenment project of definitive classification –​we can see now. This
is one reason to revisit our own knowledge-​project, not in defiance of our
predecessors but in the interest of the present, exceedingly volatile world,
reshaping itself in the wake of high modern hopes that have failed –​not least
in the Arctic.
   During some 500 years, the late-​arriving Europeans who had stumbled
upon America, pushed both north and south, at first in trickles and later
in great rushes. They gradually mapped and settled in the temperate
regions of America, where the plains could be cultivated. The lands of
52 Late Arrivals

the Far North remained wild and uninviting. This is vividly expressed by
Hugh Brody:

  The European imagination was chilled by the prospect of polar seas and
  terrae incognitae whipped by icy winds. Coastlines on old maps petered
  out in vague uncertainties, in a blurred suggestion of ice-​capped inaccess-
  ibility. There may have been whales, as the Basques had discovered by the
  sixteenth century, and there might have been routes to the Orient, as ten-
  acious seekers for the Northwest Passage insisted. But even if there were
  land beyond the charted margins of the North, there could not be civil-
  ization –​even in rudimentary form –​in regions that were dark, frozen,
  impassable. The North was thought a priori to be poor because it was
  nature unrelieved by the possibility of culture.
                                                            (Brody 2002: 54)

This gives pause for thought, and explain why discovery is never a one-​
sided feat, whether it is of new species, strange lands, or new people. Such
entities never exist on their own; they are part of a long and motley story of
unconformities –​of time, space, and forgotten histories (c.f. Raffles 2020).
Longing for the unknown requires an imagery that can drive one’s thoughts
there in the first place. Whether we are talking about enlightenment naturalists
of the old school or of ethnographers at the turn of the 20th century, their
dreams had already been formatted. In that sense, the inhabitants of the
misty North were most often discovered well before one’s own pioneering
adventures. This also applied to European and American anthropologists
venturing into the vast Arctic landscape in search of the Inuit, who had been
known by whalers, trappers, and traders for a long time –​and not least
familiar with each other. While we may agree on who are the late arrivals,
the question remains: Who are the strangers?
