3	Moving Frontiers
       Life in the Indeterminate Zone




Life in the indeterminate zone was always precarious in the Arctic, even if
the wilderness was increasingly seen as an antidote to urban modernity, the
last resort of the original, liveable world in 19th-​century North America. It
was the ultimate, authentic landscape combining the grandeur of the sublime
with the primitive simplicity of the frontier (Cronon 1995: 80). Such poetic
view of the original landscape was entertained by Americans, who were new
to the continent, and where natives and immigrants would increasingly fight
over the land. The encounter between hopeful settlers from an impoverished
Old World and the Native Americans of the (so-​called) New World with its
vast plains and shadowy woods has become a standard source of shame. As
part of a social memory, images of the past have a bearing on present social
order; conversely, our sense of the present depends on our knowledge of the
past (Connerton 1989: 3).
   Even the Arctic landscape, in the shape of a wilderness of low forest, vast
barren stretches, endless waterways, and unfathomable ice, did not remain
untouched by the Old World-​immigrants for long. It was explored and par-
tially appropriated by whalers and trappers from elsewhere; in their wake
came trading companies that would later make a claim to ownership over
regions that were never owned before. Today the Arctic landscapes are being
explored and exploited both by southerners and northerners not simply with
a view to fox-​skin, whales, or other living resources but to deeper repositories
of minerals, oil, and tar (Nuttall 2010). To this we may add the massive and
never-​ending interest in access to resources dreamt up within new geopolit-
ical horizons (Doel et al. 2014), and the increasing, and troubling interest in
contested regions, such as the Polar continental shelf (Powell 2017). A New
Arctic is gradually established by science, as seen from within a larger Polar
world. These are important elements in the moving frontiers at the centre of
this chapter.
   Looking back to early ethnography, we realize that the landscape was
never still; people were always moving and redefining themselves in practice,
and interests were many. When ‘discovered’, the Inuit were placed in the very
modern dream of untouched natives. Anthropologists saw them as ‘original’,

DOI: 10.4324/9781003374145-3
                                                         Moving Frontiers 57

even though they were deeply marked by the fleeting frontiers that affected
their life. I shall let Vilhjalmur Stefansson express some of what concerns us
in this chapter.

  In the fertile lands of the United States and Canada a saying grew up that
  “the only good Indian is a dead Indian,” because the Indian encumbered
  the land which the farmer needed for cultivation of crops, and the miner
  for his digging and delving. The Indian was in the way and had to go, for
  we could not let mere humanitarianism and justice restrain us from taking
  possession of the valuable lands that the Indian had inherited from his
  ancestors. In the South, economic and humanitarian interests were diamet-
  rically opposed, and the economic had their way. In the North, economic
  and humanitarian interests happened to coincide. The northern land was
  valueless to the farmer, and the country was of value to the trading com-
  panies only in so far as it produced fur; and furs could best be secured
  by perpetuating the Indian and keeping him in possession of the lands,
  because dead men do not set traps. The only good Indian in the North was
  the live Indian who brought in fur to sell. No doubt it is largely the result of
  this economic fact that the Hudson’s Bay Company has always treated the
  Indian so well that is has never been to the Indian’s interest to quarrel with
  the Company, any more than it was to the Company’s interest to quarrel
  with the Indian. And now that civilization, with its diseases, is making
  inroads into the country, and the Indian seems in danger of disappearing,
  it is not only human lives but also dollars and cents that the Company
  sees disappearing before its eyes. When they controlled all the North, they
  handled its problems a great deal more wisely than the Canadian govern-
  ment has done since, although the Canadians have been both wiser and
  cleaner-​handed than the people of the United States. But the Company no
  longer own Canada, and they are powerless to check the evil tendencies
  which they recognize more clearly than any one else.
                                                         (Stefansson 1913: 30)

Stefansson captures the dual character of the frontier here, speaking from
the vantage point of a trading post on the Mackenzie River. The region was
a meeting place between different groups, as we saw when we followed the
Jesuit missionary Émile Petitot to the north (cf. Chapter 2), where the Dene
and the Inuit met. It was also a trading zone in a landscape that was perceived
(by some) as barely liveable. Stefansson observes how modern states had mis-
read the natives and overlooked the converging interests between them and
the new states. True knowledge is hard won in the arctic landscape. Unlike
a tropical rainforest, the barren grounds of the North may have seemed to
offer themselves up for easy mapping; but this was a deception.
   The arctic frontier is both a particular place and an imaginative horizon.
In this chapter we shall seek to unpack what it means to live within what
seems a permanent frontier zone and question the idea of ethnography in the
58 Moving Frontiers

process. As Hugh Brody suggests, the notion of frontier carries a particular
weight in the Arctic:

  The history of the far north is the story of frontiers. Unexplored territory
  and untapped resources captured European imagination and fed visions of
  sudden riches. Region by region, economic sector by economic sector, the
  Subarctic and the Arctic have been celebrated as a new or –​in an idiom
  that lays claim to old romantic connections –​a last frontier.
                                                           (Brody 1987: 191)

The Arctic is still a frontier, a territory concealing untapped resources. It is
not that the region remains unmapped, nor that animals remain uncounted,
or even that mineral resources are unknown, but that something new is
appearing out of the melting landscape, which has not yet quite revealed
itself.


The Frontier as Edge
The frontier is a space of imagined possibilities, and of presumed open
access. Once again, let us look back to the Tropics, the unlikely mirror of
the Arctic. In 19th-​century Brazil, the north-​western part of the hinterlands
had been penetrated only by canoe expeditions along Amazon branches, not
least in pursuit of a favourite stimulant, guarana. It would take six months to
get there, and on their way the expeditions would plant manioc and maize,
which would ripen before they came back and secure fresh provisions on the
return journey. With the steamship and the possibility to get further into the
depths of the Amazonian river system, the early expeditions were relegated
to a heroic and nearly forgotten past (Lévi-​Strauss 1963: 249–​50). In late
19th century, Marshall Rondon headed the construction of a road from Rio
de Janeiro to Cuiaba, the capital of Mato Grosso, where Lévi-​Strauss spent
some time, and where he learnt about the guarana business, and about the
local adversity to the road that threatened the canoe trade monopoly.
   What really intrigued Lévi-​Strauss was the telegraph line that Rondon
had also spearheaded, from Brazil to Bolivia and Peru (1900–​            1906).
Somewhere along the line Rondon met with the warlike Bororo, whom
he allegedly succeeded in pacifying and even persuaded to help him com-
pleting the telegraph line. Following this feat, Rondon was put in charge of
extending the telegraph line from Mato Grosso to the Amazon, in course of
which he found the native Nambikwara. We are in Lévi-​Strauss’ territory
here, these two tribes being very important in his work, and we understand
his fascination by the infrastructure, through which these tribes came into
the purview of newcomers and accessible to himself. The opening up of
this almost mythical territory in the early 20th century had revived hopes
of a golden future, he relates, even as the region as such was still clouded
in shade.
                                                          Moving Frontiers 59

  This state of ignorance, when allied to the still-​recent opening-​up of the
  American Far West and the consequent Gold Rush, led to the craziest
  aspirations among the peoples of the Mato Grosso, and even, too, among
  those along the coast. Once Rondon’s men had laid their telegraph line, a
  great flood of emigrants would invade the territory, exploit its undreamed-​
  of resources, and build there a Brazilian Chicago. A great disappointment
  was in store: like that north-​easterly region in which is the ill-​omened ter-
  ritory described by Euclides da Cunha in Os Sertões, the Serra do Norte
  proved to be a semi-​desert, a savannah as inhospitable as any in America.
  And, what is more, the telegraph line lost all its importance after the
  beginnings, in 1922, or thereabouts, of radio-​telegraphy: these coincided
  with the completing of the line, which became overnight an object of
  merely archaeological interest, veteran of a period in the development of
  science which came to an end just as the line was ready to come into ser-
  vice. It did, however, enjoy its hour of glory when, in 1924, the federal
  government was cut off from all contact with the interior by the insurrec-
  tion of São Paulo. Only by telegraph could Rio remain in contact with
  Cuiaba, by way of Belem and Manaus. Then began the days of decline,
  and those few enthusiasts who had thought to take employment in the
  telegraph service dropped away or lingered on, forgotten. When I reached
  them, it was several years since they had been sent any provision. Nobody
  quite dared to shut the line down; but nobody thought of using it, either.
                                                      (Lévi-​Strauss 1963: 250)

This history of failed dreams, experienced by our traveller only some ten
years after the fact of its failure, was followed by the slow demise of the staff,
who had had such high hopes; “they had neither the courage nor the means
to leave; and so, slowly, one after another, eaten away by sickness, hunger,
and solitude, they were dying off” (Lévi-​Strauss 1963: 251). With them died
the hopes for the region, even while the hostile interiors were pacified. This
elegy of a tropical frontier reminds us that frontier zones provide no guaran-
ties. It is in their nature to promise and to shift imaginaries around, but they
often fail to deliver. This suggests that the frontier is a space in both histor-
ical and territorial suspension; a case in point is the Alaskan Gold Rush, that
swept away the newly attempted development of a reindeer industry in the
late 19th century (Jenness 1962: 13). The whole situation was volatile.

  In 1896 a dozen paddle-​wheeled steamers and launches plied up and down
  the Yukon River carrying hundreds of frenzied miners and their supplies.
  After the rush to the Yukon came the rush to Nome, and before the Nome
  rush had subsided, white miners had spread all over the Alaska Peninsula,
  even the unexplored Brooks Range of mountains that roughly parallels the
  arctic coast. Prospectors wandered into Eskimo settlements, hunted and
  trapped in hereditary Eskimo hunting grounds, and engaged Eskimo men
  to transport their supplies by boat and dog-​sled. The natives themselves
60 Moving Frontiers

  rarely attempted to mine on their own account, and they generally kept
  aloof from the regular mining camps where they were mere hewers of
  wood and drawers of water; but they welcomed every opportunity for
  wage-​employment on tasks with which they were already familiar, and
  on mechanical jobs, such as operating marine and stationary engines, for
  which they possessed a natural skill.
                                                       (Jenness 1962: 13)

We are in no doubt about the emergent frontier zone here, affecting the
landscape as well as its inhabitants. Before the Gold Rush, the conventional
subsistence economy of the Inuit was already faltering, not least due to the
access and excessive use of rifles that had severely reduced the number of
land animals as well as the large sea-​mammals. Bewildered, as Jenness noted
(1962: 11), they bound themselves to the money economy of the whites.

  The new white mining settlements, the flourishing salmon industry in
  Bristol Bay, the multiplying herds of semi-​  domesticated reindeer, and
  the decline in numbers of the whale and walrus –​these had transformed
  the Eskimo economy. Gone were the fleets of commercial whalers that
  had scoured the Atlantic west of the African bulge during the mid-​winter
  months, then in late spring had rounded the Horn and, turning north-
  ward, passed in July through the Bering Strait into the Arctic Ocean.
                                                          (Jenness 1962: 13)

The open seas and the commercial vessels that followed in the wake of the
opening of the Arctic, seemed to close once again. Stefansson bears witness
to this, when he discusses the shifting nature of the Far North, having con-
tinually retreated with the gradual exploration, mapping, immigration,
and even a kind of cultivation in some places. The idea of the Far North
as uninhabitable was certainly receding, thanks in part to anthropologists
like Stefansson, who had shown in person how much the Arctic had to offer
brave frontiersmen.

  The “Far West” is gone. But in the North is a greater frontier than the
  West ever was, stretching across Canada and across Siberia. The commer-
  cial value of the remotest arctic islands will be seen ere we die who are
  now young.
                                                       (Stefansson 1921: 74)

He adds:

  To those of broad outlook it needs no commercial development to jus-
  tify polar exploration, or any honest attempt to widen the bounds of
  knowledge. Though we hope for commercial developments from the
  Canadian Arctic Expedition of 1913 to 1918, we need not await them
                                                        Moving Frontiers 61

  for justification. More than a dozen volumes of scientific results are partly
  written (some of them are printed), and charts of new lands have been
  published as a result of the decision represented by the telegraphic order
  issued at Ottawa when to those of defeatist temperament everything
  looked black: “Pursue expedition as per original plan.”
                                                      (Stefansson 1921: 74)

The power of the telegraph is not missed by the reader of Levi-​Strauss’ elegy
of its Brazilian counterpart. Nor will the merging of anthropological and
commercial dreams have escaped notice, even as they seem out of anthropo-
logical character. Yet, they demonstrate the imaginative nature of the frontier,
having lured explorers and gold-​diggers to any region of possible promise.
Anna Tsing suggests a particular perception of frontier, taking us further
towards its nature.

  A frontier is an edge of space and time: a zone of not yet –​not yet mapped,
  not yet regulated. It is a zone of unmapping: even in its planning, a frontier
  is imagined as unplanned. Frontiers aren’t just discovered at the edge; they
  are projects in making geographical and temporal experience. Frontiers
  make wildness, entangling visions and vines and violence; their wildness
  is both material and imaginative. This wildness reaches backward as well
  as forward in time, bringing old forms of savagery to life in the contem-
  porary landscape. Frontiers energize old fantasies, even as they embody
  their impossibilities.
                                                            (Tsing 2005: 28–​9)

Clearly the frontier is not simply a place of potential gains, mostly in an
outsider’s perspective, it is also a time of a particular quality, between
lost possibilities and still unknown opportunities (Hastrup 2020). It is,
therefore, never solely constituted by exploitative outsiders (even if they
are there), but also by insiders (who have always been there) scanning the
horizon for new kinds of income, without which they are doomed; if they
have the strength, they may pursue other dreams elsewhere, as the mobile
arctic hunters knew.
   The arctic hunters were generally placed between old fantasies and future
impossibilities. They were often portrayed as an ‘extreme case’ of human life,
fighting for survival in regions that “in our eyes, offer very few or even no
conditions for life, and where only cold or death would be thought to reign”
to quote Eivind Astrup sojourning among the Inuit in Northwest Greenland
in the 1890s (Astrup [1895] 1898: 34). The frontier is not only unmapped,
but virtually unliveable, until it is no longer a frontier but a place; this
happened in the American West, colonized by immigrants marginalizing the
natives, even while still admired by romantics. At a slightly different scale,
the inhabitants on the plains and on the coast, struggled for rights of land in
the Canadian North (cf. Chapter 2).
62 Moving Frontiers

    If not directly offending the dreams of the frontiersmen from the south,
the natives at least challenged their sense of ownership and access. This was
related to an implicit sense of proper society being sedentary, and possessions
clearly delineated –​a notion that has a marred anthropology since its incep-
tion (Hastrup and Olwig 2012: 8–​9). The old vision of savagery in the Arctic
was accentuated both by the mobile lifestyle that made people escape the
sedentary way of life, upon which governmental power rested in Europe and
its overseas derivatives, and by the freezing temperatures that glazed over the
vision. Brody writes:

  The gulf between hunter and peasant consciousness that is revealed by so
  many opposing ideas, ideals and social habits is reinforced, in the case of
  the hunters of the far north by climate. In all peasant-​originated systems,
  soil and warmth define a framework of human well-​being. Late frosts and
  early winters are the enemy. Cold is a symbol of lovelessness, poverty and
  fear. A world without summer becomes a world without possibility of
  human existence.
                                                             (Brody 1987: 15)

Moving up and out into the cold made heroes of the explorers from the tem-
perate zone who survived in the elegiac lands. Pushing back the frontier, and
not least pushing it northwards as far as American and European interests
went, implied that local modes of managing nature’s affordances were mis-
takenly perceived as signalling free access. While management might have
been invisible in the vast Arctic landscape where people were few and the
hunting grounds spread out on what seemed a limitless expanse, there were
still old rules of sharing the catch with one’s neighbours whose hunting
luck had failed; it could go the other way some time in the future, as we
know from the early literature on Northwest Greenland (e.g., Kane [1856]
2012: 133). We sense how time plays into the vision of resources, and how
subsistence and survival in this region hinged on a fine balance between the
immediate catch and later prospects. Human life depended on a strategy of
non-​depletion and implied a sense of being permanently suspended in time
or captured in a history unknown. It also required a sophisticated view of
‘wildlife’.
    In the High North, the landscape itself was so volatile, that newcomers
from sedentary climes could not even ‘see’ any local economy. Brody suggests:

  Hunters of the North were judged to have no real or viable economy of
  their own. This self-​serving opinion removed, at an ideological stroke, any
  prospect for legal or moral opposition to European economic expansion
  into places where hunters lived. The possibility of a conflict of economic
  interest between frontiersmen and hunters were hypothesized into irrele-
  vancy. Stereotypes of the savage accompanied the push west, the advance
  of the northern frontier, and all the excited expansion first of European
                                                       Moving Frontiers 63

  and then American interests, which constituted the progressive expropri-
  ation or destruction of the hunters’ terrain.
                                                         (Brody 2002: 55)

It is not only an issue of the past. A significant proportion of Arctic peoples
are still hunters, whalers or caribou herders, or something else for which they
depend on a vast landscape to keep up with their needs. This applies to the
sea no less than the land –​both being ancient repositories of game. Through
ages of modern development and overseas contact the sea had remained free
and borderless –​mare liberum. This has changed; the free ocean has shrunk
considerably due to expansive international interests –​and to the receding
ice, that makes new claims to the Polar Sea possible.


Geography at Sea
The gulf between peasant and hunter on the frontier was transmuted also
into a clash between landlocked and seaborne modes of orientation. The
seas have their own historical geographies, connecting as much as separating
the landmasses and upholding social values (Lambert et al. 2006). The New
World was ‘discovered’ (by Europeans) when Old World technologies had
developed to a point that could carry human cargoes across the Atlantic. The
Viking voyages over Iceland and Greenland to North America in the 10th
century were among the first known and historically documented successes
in this enterprise (cf. Chapter 2). Possibly there may also have been some
connections across the Pacific that have later been obscured (Lévi-​Strauss
1963: 239–​  46ff). The Norwegian explorer, Thor Heyerdahl, sought to
prove this in the 1950s, when he crossed the Pacific on a self-​made replica
of an early Pacific raft (Heyerdahl 1948). Proofs are difficult, yet unknown
travellers may have found ways of their own, now forgotten.
   On the western coasts of America, other points of entry than the well-​
known bridge across the Bering Strait may have contributed to the pre-​
Columbian American culture that made Lévi-​Strauss suggest that the entire
western hemisphere should be seen as one whole (Lévi-​Strauss 1963: 239).
Wholeness is a tricky notion, if we think of the remarkable differences in
the pace of European discovery. “The North Pacific, the Bering Sea, and the
Arctic coasts of Alaska were not discovered by Europeans until the eight-
eenth century, 200 years later than the opposite north-​eastern coasts of
Canada and Greenland” (Collins 1984: 13). Today, there seems to be little
doubt that the Palaeo Inuit as well as the Thule Inuit (who eventually met
each other, and may even have lived together for some time as archaeologists
now suggest), both ultimately hailed from the coasts of the Bering Strait, by
both winding and straight lines.
   The Thule Inuit traversed the North American coast from the Bering Strait
to Greenland in the 11th and 12th century and remained scattered on the
shores along the Polar Sea and the hinterlands. They were mainly hunters
64 Moving Frontiers

of marine mammals and their social life was deeply entangled with the sea,
either open or frozen, while not foreign to reindeer hunting or fishing in
inland rivers. They emerged in the European imagination as sturdy hunters
of the Arctic seas, to the point even that inland dwellers were seen as an
anomaly. An instance of this is provided by Rasmussen who, on the Fifth
Thule Expedition, reflects on his first meeting with the Inuit.

  These, then, were the people whom the Greenlanders called Akilinermiut
  (“those who dwell in the land beyond the great sea”); the people I had
  heard about ever since, as a boy, I had first begun to listen to the Eskimo
  folk tales. I could not have found a more picturesque setting for a first
  impression. Here was a whole caravan out in the midst of the ice, men
  and women in curious dresses of skins, like living illustrations to the
  Greenland story-​tellers’ tales of the terrible “inland folk.” Every stitch
  of their clothing was of caribou skin, fine, short-​haired skins of animals
  killed at the opening of the autumn hunting season. The dresses of the
  women especially rendered them altogether shapeless –​very wide in
  the upper part, with a big fur hood falling from the shoulders down over
  the back, and long loose coat tails coming down over the breeches before
  and behind, edged with white skin. The footwear also was peculiar, the
  actual boots being apparently covered with an outer envelope, commen-
  cing in a long tongue right up on the thigh, and terminating just below the
  calf in a sort of bag; a most comical arrangement, serving as far as could
  be seen no useful purpose whatever.
     The curious fur dresses of the men were as if made for running; they
  were not so long as those of the women, but had the same tail fashioning
  front and back. The tails were either of equal length, divided up the thigh,
  or comparatively short in front with a longer tail behind.
                                                       (Rasmussen 1929: 14)

The interest of this passage adheres as much to the manifest reference to
his grandmother’s tales as to the description of the dresses, and the implicit
connection between Greenland and the Eastern Arctic. It reminds us of Henri
Lefebvre’s notion of spatial history, pointing to ‘space’ as where a histor-
ical plot unfolds, and where it is constituted as a place by the history itself
(Lefebvre 1991: 15; 36–​8; et passim). In the (lived) Arctic, the spatial history
extends to the surrounding seas, certainly part of the spatial constitution.
The first many generations of immigrants to the American Arctic came across
the ice between the Asian and the Arctic continents, while it still lasted, later
by boats or sledges. The ocean was an important agent in the making of the
Arctic frontier.
   The mutuality of space and history implies that with changing
perceptions of the oceanic space, the social relationships emerging from the
sea are also changing. While fishermen, whalers, pirates, and other sailors
from diverse continents freely navigated the oceans and drew out natural
                                                         Moving Frontiers 65

riches from its depth, their movements were closely related to values on
land and became inscribed in the national order of things –​territorially
and economically. A brief glance on the whalers, exploring and exploiting
the Arctic seas in earlier centuries, reveals how their trade was mainly a
response to the European demand for whale oil for modern streetlamps,
and whalebone for women’s corsetry. The whaling crews made up their
own boat-​communities –​forever remembered in Herbert Melville’s famous
narrative of Moby Dick (Melville 1851) in which he referred extensively
to the work of the illustrious whaler and innovator William Scoresby who
had modernized whaling in the Arctic in the 18th century and who had
operated in the Arctic Sea between East Greenland and Svalbard (Scoresby
1823; Bravo 2006).
    Since Melville’s masterpiece saw light, both whaling and sailing have
become circumscribed by national and international regulations, and
fishing today is often literally a kind of farming, where hunting has become
replaced by harvesting. It has been suggested that the ocean has now turned
into a ‘virtual aquarium’, pointing to the emergence of ocean management
regimes, based on enclosed and well-​defined areas (Pálsson 1998). The sea
has become as territorialized as the land, divided by nations, and regulated
by laws of access and harvesting. This contains a paradox between the essen-
tial liquidity of the ocean itself, including its moving inhabitants and the
floating fishing vessels, on the one hand, and the essential stability and terri-
toriality related to national thinking on the other. The result is that the once
so stable boundary between land-​and sea-​socialities is increasingly blurred;
it is not simply an instance of extending a sedentary view to the sea, but also
of incorporating the sea into the society in new ways. Sea ‘farming’ not only
creates new economic niches, but also contributes to a reshaping of the rela-
tionship between nature and society, and to a dissolving of the opposition
between land and water –​as in the case of industrial aquaculture and the
implied domestication of salmon for example, coming with the price of de-​
naturalising their seawater habitat (Lien 2015).
    Boas made his entry upon the scene in the final phase of the whaling
industry and the related dreams of prosperity; urban street lightening had
changed, and women’s fashion become less rigid. The population of whales
seemed likewise to have diminished. Yet, at Boas’ time many whaling stations
along the Northern coasts were still in place and had a major impact on the
conditions of the Inuit in the hinterlands –​who had been drawn towards the
stations, if not to live permanently, then at least to visit from time to time for
provisions. The stations not only attracted the Inuit they also changed them,
as Boas noted.

  The constant contact between the Eskimo and the whalers has effected a
  perfect revolution in the trade between the Eskimo tribes. As the whale
  catch in Cumberland Sound has fallen off during the past fifteen years, a
  remigration of the populations of Davis Strait has occurred, ships visiting
66 Moving Frontiers

  these shores every fall and a regular traffic being kept up. Therefore
  many Oqomiut now travel as far as Qivitung in order to trade there. As
  Nugumiut is still frequently visited by whalers, there is no inducement for
  the inhabitants to leave their country.
     Within a few years the Akuliarmiut also have become amply provided
  with firearms and European products in general by means of the new
  whaling station which has been established in their vicinity.
     As for the Iglulirmiut, the importance of European manufactures at
  Pond Bay makes the trade with that region even more important than
  formerly.
     The Aivillirmiut and the Kinipetu have immediate intercourse with
  the whalers frequenting the western shore of Hudson Bay. Besides, the
  southern tribes trade with the stations of the Hudson’s Bay Company.
     The more western tribes of Boothia and its environs are dependent on
  the mediation of the Aivillirmiut for their supply of goods, as they them-
  selves have no chance of communicating with the whites.
                                                    (Boas [1888]1964: 60–​1)

The double edge of the frontier zone transpires here. It is not only the
European whalers that are on the lookout for new gains; the locals are
equally eager to partake in the quest for new resources. It is no coincidence
that Boas’ ethnographic work is marked by his base at the whaling station
at Kikkerton, even if he made excursions to other settlements. In between,
he lived in the house of James Mutch, in charge of the whaling Station, and
became very dependent upon him. Mutch took all practical matters in hand,
and long after Boas’ departure, he supplied both ethnographic notes and
material objects for his collections, so that Boas could continue his work
without ever returning to the Arctic (Harper 2008). This position influenced
his work so deeply, that it stopped being noticed, just as Rasmussen could
not see the inland dwellers through any other gaze than his own. Boas had
helpers among the locals, not least Signa, his indefatigable sledge driver –​
who was found for him by Mutch, himself being both a whaler and a resident
with a local wife. Boas’ ethnographic work is deeply marked by this frontier
zone (Boas 1901: 4; Harper 2008).
   Arctic whaling was a big issue on the shores of the polar sea. In the dra-
matic arrival of the Canadian Arctic Expedition (1913–​        1916), in which
Diamond Jenness took part, the presence of whalers and stations deeply mark
the descriptions. For the first long haul towards the field, they were on board
the ship, Karluk, and would regularly take note of other vessels, meet up with
whalers, and not least seek temporary refuge at whaling stations, where they
would often find Inuit employed and from whom they could gain important
knowledge. When they had had to leave the Karluk (cf. Chapter 2), they
stayed over at the Cape Smythe whaling and trading station, nursing their
afflictions, having the women make them new footgear, noting down the par-
ticularities of dancing and drumming, and describing the tattoos on women’s
                                                          Moving Frontiers 67

faces, all while writing letters, musing over possible plans, and having new
tents made. The leader of the station, Mr. Brower, accommodates them and
sees to their needs. Jenness, the ever-​observant reporter, notes from his post
at the station:

   I saw an Eskimo today go out over the ice dragging his kayak, which was
   lashed to a small sled. He carried a spear and was going after seals in the
   leads of open water. Later I saw him return empty-​handed. Wilkins took
   his photograph. The Eskimos here are rapidly losing the knowledge of their
   forefathers. They come to Mr. Brower to get him to make umiaks, sleds and
   snowshoes. At present he has two if not more native men in his employ…
      …I spent the morning reading and writing; in the afternoon while the
   store was being cleaned up, I took a walk through the settlement. Some
   (the majority) of the Eskimos I notice are living in houses resembling white
   men’s cottages –​not covered with peat and snow to withstand the winter
   cold. I learned two new cat’s-​cradle figures this afternoon. Mr. Brower and
   one of his Eskimo workmen between them have nearly completed a ski
   to replace the ski of mine that was broken. They used a piece of hickory
   timber, of which a stock is kept to make sled-​runners. The new tent is
   rectangular, with three poles and an inner lining. It is very much larger
   than either of our old tents, and correspondingly heavy. I should guess
   that it weighs about 60 lbs. The lining for the Burberry tent is also nearly
   finished. Wilkins completed his dark-​room lamp –​and ingenious piece of
   work. McConnell spent the day in various odd jobs and in reading. He
   made the buckwheat and cornmeal cakes for breakfast this morning. They
   are made in exactly the same way as slap-​jacks, except that a cupful of
   buckwheat flour or cornmeal is added to the ordinary flour. Mrs. Brower
   is making me a sleeping bag. I am much stronger now than when I arrived;
   after walking for more than an hour I did not feel tired in the least. Mr.
   Stefansson’s side is rather better too. There was an aurora tonight of mod-
   erate brilliancy –​streamers running north and south.
                        (Jenness 1991: 27; the two parts of the quote are from
                               17 October and 18 October 1913, respectively)

What we have here is a small piece of auto-​ethnography from the whaling
station; all members of the Expedition were under strict instructions by the
Canadian Government to keep diaries of their activities for the duration of
their travels. Jenness takes this very literally and offers details of life of many
kinds; Stefansson had a larger view of things –​he was to keep an eye out
for uninhabited islands and claim them for Canada, but he did note some of
Jenness’ achievements in an appendix to The Friendly Arctic:

   Jenness had some very interesting experiences, and obtained a good
   understanding of the language, habits, folk-​lore, and viewpoints on life
   in general, such as can only be obtained by continued intimate relations.
68 Moving Frontiers

  During the winter he supplemented this with intensive studies of the
  winter snow-​house life, and many gramophone records of songs, shaman-
  istic performances, and the like. Finger-​prints of many of the people and
  many of their string games, or cats’-​cradles were recorded.
                                                      (Stefansson 1921: 751)

Jenness was a man of details; traders and whalers were part of the world,
but it was the hallmark of ethnography to describe people in their native
condition. This was difficult as the Inuit had been implicated in long-​
distance trade for some time, if not directly then at least indirectly though
chains of exchange. In other words, their lives bore all the marks of an
undefinable frontier zone, emerging in Jenness’ report of a meeting on
the long march along the coast from west towards east in search of the
Karluk, with Stefansson and two Inuit. He spotted a tent and a platform
on the shore:

  A middle-​aged man, tall and rather dark, came out to meet us. Although
  his features marked him as an Eskimo, the clothes he wore hardly differed
  from our own. He glanced rapidly from one to another, finally fixing his
  gaze on Stefánsson, who, after a moment’s hesitation, greeted him as an
  old acquaintance. His name, we learned, was Arksiatark, and his home lay
  inland in the basin of the Colville River, which he had descended only a
  month before to trap white fox on the coast during the mid-​winter season,
  when the furs of those animals reach their prime. He told us that he had
  mistaken our party for that of his eldest son, who had sailed westwards
  in the family skin-​boat shortly before freeze-​up in order to purchase some
  supplies at Barrow but would doubtless return by sled now that the ice
  had closed and rendered his boat useless.
     Despite his evident disappointment, Arksiatark suggested that we pass
  the night with him; and he helped us to unload the sleds and pitch camp.
  His wife then came forward to greet us, and to invite us to partake of a
  meal in their tent, which was a patchwork of cloth stretched over an oval
  dome formed from willow twigs. We followed her inside, crawling one
  by one under the canvas flap that served as a door, and found ourselves
  in a space 9 feet long by 7 feet wide, but only 5 feet high. The flooring
  consisted of rough boards hewn out of driftwood; a rectangular stove
  fashioned from kerosene tins stood to the right of the door; and, scattered
  at the back of the tent, were bed skins of caribou fur and a few miscel-
  lanies. Evidently most of the family possessions had been stored outside
  on the wooden platform, which rose on four posts high above reach of the
  marauding dogs.
                                                          (Jenness 1957: 11–​3)

Here is a native hunter turned trapper, living on the edge between ancient
materials and a need for modern amenities. Jenness seems ill at ease in this
                                                       Moving Frontiers 69

world in-​between, where proper behaviour is not as obvious as elsewhere.
Taking the example from Stefansson he refuses to wash his face and hands in
the communal basin, and thus also avoids drying them in the far from clean
towel, while the two Inuit companions see no way out of it. The note on the
son’s expedition to Barrow for supplies is no less interesting as a mark of
the frontier-​zone. Trading was an ingrained part of life in many places in the
Arctic, where trading posts had proliferated.


Trading Companies
As noted by Eric Wolf, the Iberian fleets dominated the Atlantic until the
end of the 16th century, curtailing the expansion of other European trade
interests (Wolf 1982: 158). As Iberian powers waned, northern Europeans
began to settle on the American coast, and furs became of interest (among
other items). This was to affect the Native Americans deeply, as they became
embroiled in the trade. The beaver was among the first furry animals to
attract interest, and this quickly tolled on their numbers; a signal feature
of this “was its rapid movement westward as one beaver population after
another was hunted out, and the fur hunters had to push further inland in
search of untapped beaver grounds” (ibid.: 161). Eventually the hunt for fur
spread northwards, and to other animals. It is worth noting Lewis Henry
Morgan’s work on the The American Beaver and His Works (1868), one of
the earliest anthropological analyses of an animal as an agent in the world,
and one that speaks to the multispecies approach to anthropology today
(Feeley-​Harnik 2021). While Morgan’s observations on kinship seem obso-
lete today, his work on the beaver attracts increasing interest.
   Morgan did not overlook the impact of the new European settlers in
America and their cravings, increasingly satisfied by trading companies,
having proliferated since the 17th century. Trading stations were often found
where waterways, coastal or riverine, not only made access possible, but also
made a measure of permanent settlement possible. This was evident at the
Hudson’s Bay Company trading stations, where people gathered and mixed,
and where, effectively, the American natives became enrolled in European
cravings and competitions.
   The Hudson’s Bay Company was central to the opening of the inaccess-
ible Arctic and in maintaining permanent trans-​Atlantic connections for cen-
turies. The crossings may have been seasonal, given the ice-​masses in the
northern waters, but their impact on the American side was permanent. The
Company built its first posts on the shores of Hudson’s and James Bays, on
the north-​eastern coast. Later there would be many offshoots elsewhere also
in inland Canada, like the fort on the Mackenzie River we heard of the in the
previous chapter. Both the ports and the trading routes in the hinterlands had
major impact on the Canadian landscape, as it is known today, as had the
many settlers arriving on the Company vessels. The regular passages across
the sea contributed to a more robust knowledge of the North Atlantic Ocean
70 Moving Frontiers

(Hall 2013). Sea currents, icebergs, and winds were noted in the logbooks
and contributed to a growing body of oceanic knowledge. Setting out from
London, the ships gathered in convoys either at the Orkneys or the Hebrides.
This habit was necessitated both by the weather and by recurrent (terri-
torial) conflicts between the British and the French. The passage to Hudson
Strait from Europe took three to four weeks, while the passage into the Bay
depended entirely upon the ice conditions.
   Once in the Bay, the vessels steered towards the forts, of which the
oldest and biggest was York Factory, established in 1684, primarily as
a fur-​trading post, and remaining the Company’s northern headquarters
until 1873. Through rivers and lakes, and across the Bay itself, native
populations could paddle to the doorstep during summer. This included
the Inuit on the northern shores of the Bay. The Company ships also took
passengers, who, along with European sailors who had wanted to remain
in America, became settlers along Red River in Rupert’s Land. This was
a region claimed by the Hudson’s Bay Company as being outside of the
British colonial regime. It was a time, when both lands and trade rights
were contested by Europeans; the fur trade was a particular source of ani-
mosity, as there were neither fixed boundaries nor rights. A historian offers
the following observations:

  Shortly after the fall of Montreal in 1760, the French fur trade, of the
  St Lawrence passed under British control, and a new era opened in the
  contest for the fur resources of the vast lands lying to the north and west
  of Lake Superior. From bases on the St Lawrence and the Great Lakes,
  British-​financed traders soon penetrated beyond the areas of the Northwest
  previously occupied during the French regime, and precipitated a serious
  decline in the flow of furs to the Hudson’s Bay Company (hereafter HBC)
  settlements on the shores of Hudson Bay. This turn of events forced the
  HBC to abandon its century-​long policy of trading with the Indians on the
  shores of the Bay and to build trading posts in the interior in direct com-
  petition with those of the St. Lawrence traders.
                                                       (Moodie 1987: 360–​1)

The competition led to the gradual establishment of inland trading posts
by the Hudson’s Bay Company, not wanting to lose out to the North West
Company working inland, where settlers and natives mixed. In response to
this, the Hudson’s Bay Company engaged in a determined effort to recu-
perate earlier standing and privilege. Eventually the two companies merged
(in 1821). In 1870, company lands were transferred to the Dominion of
Canada. In the Red River lands this led to an outcry by the Métis buffalo
hunters, who organized their own militia (Hall and Huck 2002).
   In Labrador, the advent of the Hudson’s Bay Company contributed to
some turmoil in the otherwise peaceful region, where Inuit and Moravian
Brethern had kept a fine balance between themselves. When Newfoundland
                                                       Moving Frontiers 71

cod-​fishers, and not least the Company expanded into the region, the balance
was threatened.

  Although the Moravians lacked the authority to prevent such invasion of
  their territory, they succeeded in retaining the bulk of the trade because
  their stores stood open every day of the year except Sundays and church
  holidays, and they themselves lived on the most intimate terms with the
  Eskimos, who regarded them as belonging to the “people.”
                                                          (Jenness 1965: 25)

Eventually, the Labrador Inuit, who had been shielded by the massive sea
ice and by the Moravian brothers, were gradually subsumed by incoming
trappers and fishermen, directly invited to colonize the region (ibid.: 9).
   The trading posts made new landscapes and populations, not least around
the bigger forts that became centres of trade and of cross-​cultural encounters.
Above, I cited Stefansson for saying that the Hudson’s Bay Company no
longer owned Canada (at his time). This was more than a piece of rhetoric.
It has been suggested that in its early years, the Company was comparable
to a sovereign state at a time when “Outside of Europe, sovereignty –​the
ability to operate independently of other legal systems, and to acquire and
rule over subjects –​took a number of surprising forms, irrespective of any
divisions between state and non-​state, colonizer and colonized” (Cavanagh
2011: 25). In colonial North America, where practice was always more vital
than theory, the result seems to have been a layering of multiple sovereignties
on top of each other, also appearing “in the practices of imperial-​sponsored
governments, private companies, and a growing number of small, self-​
governed white settlements (some of these tied strongly to a European state,
some tenuously, and some not at all)” (ibid.: 26). Such sovereignty appears
to have adhered to the Hudson’s Bay Company, having legitimate polit-
ical authority. Its monopoly was based on the understanding that no native
would be allowed to starve within reach of the posts (Neatby 1984: 397). As
Knud Rasmussen noted in the early 1920s, despite their primary mercantile
interests, generally the traders took responsibility for the locals:

  Throughout the North-​West Passage I invariably found the traders on the
  best of terms with the Eskimos near.
     There are wide regions where the Hudson’s Bay Company is the only
  link between the native population and the outer world. The Hudson’s Bay
  Company stands for civilization, and its outposts in these desolate lands
  represent the life and work of men who bear the white man’s burden, the
  white man’s great responsibility.
                                             (Rasmussen [1927] 1999: 299)

Naturally, politics exceeded the local. In the 17th century, when the
Hudson’s Bay Company was first established, it served as a bulwark for the
72 Moving Frontiers

highly contested sovereignty of England, operating by royal charter. The
Company was expected “to erect and build such Castles, Fortifications,
Forts, Garrisons, Colonies or Plantations, Towns or Villages, in any Parts or
Places …as they in their discretion shall think fit and requisite” (in Cavanagh
2011: 29). They were also entitled to recompense themselves for any loss or
damages inflicted upon them by people obstructing their trade, and to defend
themselves against threats from outside. The Royal Charter for incorpor-
ating the Hudson’s Bay Company Granted by His Majesty King Charles the
Second, in the twenty-​second year of his reign, A.D. 1670, underscored their
rights, to:

  free Liberty and Licence, in case they conceived it necessary, to send either
  Ships of War, Men of Ammunition, unto any of their Plantations, Forts,
  Factories, or Places of Trade aforesaid, for the Security and Defence of
  the same, and to choose Commanders and Officers over them, and to give
  them Power and Authority, by Commission under their Common Seal, or
  otherwise, to continue or make Peace or War with any Prince or People
  whatsoever, that are not Christians, in any Places where the said Company
  shall have any Plantations, Forts or Factories, or adjacent thereunto.
                                                      (In Cavanagh 2011: 29)

With the growing tension between the French and the English, the Company
decided to keep relatively neutral, and the French were allowed to estab-
lish a dozen trading posts across the Northwest, as near as ‘within a day’s
paddle’. Having learnt how existing tensions between natives –​‘that are
not Christians’ –​had escalated due to the increasing availability of guns,
the Company saw that another policy was needed concerning the groups
living near the Bay, and proper Treaties were made to secure their peaceful
cohabitation.
    Evidently, the main purpose of Hudson’s Bay Company was trade, but
in addition to the de facto governing and contributing to science by their
naturalist observations and collections, it also had a direct influence of the
wellbeing of the native peoples in the ill-​mapped and sparsely populated nor-
thern regions. This included a concern for their health. In the 18th and 19th
centuries, smallpox epidemics would hit the north, brought over by ships
from Europe and spreading by way of mobile groups; the fatality rates were
immense. In Europe an inoculation method was developed, and it eventually
found its way to the trading posts; by the advent of a major smallpox epi-
demic in 1782 in the region, spreading the Bay, the station masters ended
up choosing quarantine, however, because inoculation could, under less
than professional standards, accelerate the epidemics (Hackett 2004). The
stations went free, but many natives did not.
    Yet, with the de facto invention of a new kind of vaccination (with cowpox),
the Company took responsibility and saw to it that a vaccination programme
was made at all posts in 1837–​1838, even all the way to the MacKenzie area.
                                                        Moving Frontiers 73

The health of the fur hunters was on the Company’s mind, and not only for
commercial reasons, though this would probably also have been considered.
Meanwhile the Company had to face new challenges both in the European
fur market, in French–​British politics, and in increasing competition from the
North West Company, going into the more distant territories in the Arctic
(Stephen 2013). The two companies operated in what was sometimes known
as the Petit Nord (the original Hudson’s Bay Company interest area), and
the Grand Nord, reaching up into the Mackenzie River Basin, of which we
heard of above as a meeting place between the Company and the Dené and
further afield the Inuit (Moodie 1987). The forts soon became significant
melting pots.
   The interest in expanding the boundaries of trade eventually hit the high
north. It had not even been considered liveable during the 17th and 18th
centuries, but became of interest to the Hudson’s Bay Company. The stereo-
types of the North and of the hunters changed in the process of appropriating
the changing landscapes in the Northwest (Brody 2002: 55). In late 18th
and early 19th centuries fur traders had reached the area, earlier seen as too
far from the trading posts to attract their interest. Alexander Mackenzie,
representing the North West Company, travelled into British Columbia in
1793, followed in 1828 by George Simpson, governor of the Hudson’s Bay
Company, and new ways were paved for further trade.
   The two companies merged in 1821; this was precipitated by the war
between the United States and Britain over rights of further westward expan-
sion, which the Americans wanted to launch out of the great lakes. Both
companies were concerned about an upstart American Company, seeking to
capitalize on their rivalry and wanting to get a share in the Northwest. With
the Napoleonic war in Europe, the United States declared war on Britain in
North America; this provided an opportunity to act. The British enrolled the
fur traders in their quest, and before the Americans even knew that a war
was on, their fort at Mackinac Island was seized by “Captain Roberts and
his force of 400 aboriginal warriors and about 180 fur-​traders” (Hall and
Huck 2002: 4).
   The refashioned Company contributed to a new balance between hunters
and traders; they needed each other. The hostile picture of the northern
hunters was moderated, and in the process the picture of the North itself
was remade; in practice it kept being pushed northwards, as more and more
of the inhospitable lands were cultivated by homesteaders and turned into
lush fields (Brody 2002: 56–​7). Fur traders and trade companies played an
important role in opening and getting access to the arctic coastlands. While
forests and fields can be metered out, it was much more difficult for hunting
grounds that remained elusive, quite irrespective of the boundary-​making
efforts of settlers and treaties. They were not living by the same maps.

  Treaties and traplines encircle the Indians with legal and territorial limits.
  At the same time, potential settlement and resources have become the
74 Moving Frontiers

  subjects of a new northern mythology. Harsh as the climate may be, the
  North begins to be characterized as a place of limitless material possibil-
  ities. Gold for the panning, land for the clearing, vistas of infinite expan-
  sion –​these were and are the prospects held out by northern adventurers.
  New visions of this kind represent a shift towards domestic or internal
  expansion. Mercantile colonialism gives way to a more comprehensive
  occupancy of the North. But the white man’s dreams and maps are the
  omens and portents of a future in which Indians are expected to play no
  part. The “North” conveniently moves north.
                                                           (Brody 2002: 115)

In Brody’s words, the old North became the new West –​a new site of limit-
less dreams. When, finally, the Europeans penetrated the boreal forests
and reached the Arctic coastlands beyond the trees, they were found to
be inhabited by Inuit populations, who were not yet well-​known, even if
Labrador Inuit and Baffin Landers north of Hudson Bay had been neighbours
for some time.
   Baffin Land was where Rasmussen met his first inland Inuit in 1921, as
we heard in the previous chapter (Chapter 2). As it happened, both parties
in this ‘first encounter’ were on their way to the Hudson’s Bay Company
trading station in Repulse Bay. In Rasmussen’s case it was mainly a matter
of seeking information about the distribution of the population in the
area between Iglulik and Chesterfield Inlet. He spent a day with the New
People and quickly learnt a lot about the larger population from them. At
this meeting, he was even able to touch upon matters of religion; here he
“very soon ascertained that these people were still entirely primitive in their
views and unaffected by outside influences” (Rasmussen 1929: 16). How
‘unaffected’ anyone in this region was, is debatable. Yet, what matters in
the present context is that it was a ‘first’ contact with the Hudson’s Bay
Company for Rasmussen.

  On the day following this first meeting, we arrived at Repulse Bay while
  it was still daylight, and made the acquaintance here of Captain George
  Cleveland, an old whaling captain now in charge of the Hudson’s Bay
  Company’s trading station here. Captain Cleveland received us with great
  hospitality, and most willingly furnished us with a great deal of informa-
  tion that proved very valuable in arranging our plans of work. We stayed
  here a few days, and made several other acquaintances, including that of
  an old man in particular with whom I was to have further dealings in the
  course of my work later on. His name was Ivaluardjuk; he had a long
  white beard and red, rheumy eyes, worn dim with over many blizzards.
  He was, it appeared, the geographer of his tribe, and was remarkably well
  up in the country and its inhabitants throughout the entire range between
  Ponds Inlet and Chesterfield. When I brought out a pencil and paper, he
  drew, to my astonishment, the whole coastline from Repulse Bay to Ponds
                                                         Moving Frontiers 75

  Inlet, without hesitation, and though the proportions of course could not
  be correct, the map yet gave such good general view that we were now
  able to fix a number of the Eskimo place-​names. This was a very essen-
  tial point for us, as we should have to discuss the various localities with
  natives who knew nothing of the English names given to parts of their ter-
  ritory by Parry and Lyon a hundred years before, while on the other hand
  they had an abundance of names of their own for fjords, lakes, mountains
  and all characteristic features.
                                                   (Rasmussen 1929: 16–​7)

Let this quote stand as it is, reverberating with all sorts of knowledge circu-
lating at the frontier zone –​including new kinds of religious thinking trans-
mitted by missionaries, like the Moravians in Labrador, or like Father Petitot,
introduced in Chapter 2 and whose linguistic skills were extraordinary, while
his feelings were all too excited.
   The Hudson’s Bay Company was established at the same time as the
Royal Society of London for the Improvement of Natural Knowledge that
had sprung from a new empirical leaning in the scholarly community,
spearheaded by Sir Francis Bacon. He had little regard for the speculative
deductions of the scholastics; instead, he “legitimized inquiry thorough
inductive methods of reasoning as a practical means of both increasing power
over nature and improving the quality of human life. For Bacon the essence
of the new science was that it was to be natural, methodical and above all
social” (Zeller 1989: 80). Allegedly, Charles II endorsed the Company partly
because it could expand the bounds of empirical knowledge, for which travel
was important. The officers of the Company readily embraced the knowledge
project at their northern outposts, not least because the expected contribu-
tion to natural historical knowledge would add meaning and prestige to their
marginal lives (ibid.: 81).
   In the 18th and 19th centuries this general empirical interest sent multitudes
of scholars out into the world, in the interest of gaining new knowledge.
Seaborne expeditions, such as Captain James Cook’s, were grand-​            scale
endeavours to map the globe, after the New World had been discovered. The
land-​borne expedition in America of Alexander von Humboldt (1769–​1859)
had made the connections between landscape, vegetation, and animal life
visible. He wrote:

  I was passionately keen on botany and certain aspects of zoology, and
  flattered myself that our researches might add to some new species to those
  already known. However, rather than discovering new, isolated facts I pre-
  ferred linking already known ones together. The discovery of a new genus
  seemed to me far less interesting than an observation on the geographical
  relations of plants, or the migration of social plants, and the heights that
  different plants reach on the peaks of the cordilleras.
                                        (Humboldt [1814–​1824] 1995: 5–​6)
76 Moving Frontiers

Humboldt was to influence natural history deeply, and this was acknowledged.
In the previous chapter, we met him with the American president, in the
newly founded city of Washington on his way home from South America to
Germany. Like him, many Company men actively sought new knowledge and
collected specimens of plants that would become part of collections in Britain.
The marginal Britons around the Hudson’s Bay Company forts wanted to
distinguish themselves through scientific interest (Zeller 1989: 94ff), possibly
affecting the British agreement to organize a proper naturalist expedition
at the behest of the Royal Society and the Royal Geographical Society. The
ambition for the British Arctic Expedition (1875–​1876) was grand, embra-
cing a manifold of interests, from fossils, algae, and butterflies to muskox
and enlisting numerous people, many of whom were to leave their mark on
science (Levere 1993: 264ff). The Company men were enlisted in the field-
work, and the writings multiplied. Science became international.


The Price of Global Connection
Once implicated in global trade, indigenous populations became exposed to
new epidemics –​such as the smallpox epidemic, we heard of above. In the
words of Le Roy Ladurie (1981), the globe had become “unified by disease”
in the 14th to 17th centuries. While germs spread before (and after) this
period, their circulation intensified with the new patterns of trade and later
with the conquest of the Americas, including the Transatlantic trade com-
panies. This unification, or “the creation –​first in Eurasia, and subsequently
in the Atlantic area –​of a ‘common market’ of microbes, passed through a
particularly intense, rapid, dramatic, one might even say apocalyptic phase,
during the period roughly 1300–​1600” (Ladurie 1981: 30).
   Robert McGhee suggests that: “Sixteenth-​to-​18th-​century contact along
the coasts of Greenland, Labrador, Baffin Island, and even Hudson’s Bay may
have been sufficient to allow for repeated transmission of Old World dis-
ease to local Inuit groups” (McGhee 1997: 570). This corroborates Ladurie’s
suggestion, and we know it to be true –​and hard to fight. In an epilogue to
the second edition of People of the Twilight (1959), Jenness expresses his
sorrow over the fate of the Copper Inuit.

  In America, as elsewhere, the kiss of the white man has brought death
  to numberless natives whom isolation had protected from our diseases;
  and the People of the Twilight did not escape. Within ten years of our
  departure from Bernard Harbour one third of them had been swept away
  by influenza, and of the hundred Eskimos gathered around the radio
  station at Coppermine no fewer than twenty-​six suffered from advanced
  tuberculosis.
     Was this inevitable? Had we been more conscious of our responsibilities
  could we have spared them so rude an awakening, so painful an entry into
                                                             Moving Frontiers 77

  what we call the civilized world? Or does fate spin her web remorselessly,
  blind to human hopes and weaknesses, indifferent to human suffering?
  The children of Jennie’s generation who escaped new diseases underwent
  a social and economic revolution. Even as we sailed away, traders entered
  their country seeking fox-​furs, always fox-​furs; and for those pelts useless
  for real clothing they offered rifles, shot-​guns, steel tools and other goods
  that promised to make life so much easier. So the Eskimos abandoned
  their communal seal hunts and scattered in isolated families along the
  coasts in order to trap white foxes during the winter months when the
  fur of that animal reaches its prime. Their disposal loosened the old com-
  munal ties that had held the families together.
                                                     (Jenness [1928] 1959: 249)

Jenness elaborates on the fact that the world of commerce had caught the
Inuit in its mesh, destroyed their self-​sufficiency, and made them slaves of
a new economy. All of this was accompanied by another revolution, he
laments; the atomic age was reaching out for the Arctic, and youngsters
found employment at radar stations and the like. Jenness worried about the
money economy, and asked how one could “teach Eskimos but one step
removed from the stone age to use it for their own and others’ welfare, and
not for increasing human unhappiness?” (ibid.: 249–​50). There is no easy
answer to this question. Both disease and money came with people from
outside, and it is hard to see how isolation could have lasted, or how contact
could have been controlled. This brings us to a new frontier that is opening in
the northern regions, as the North has moved further North, to paraphrase
Brody (cited above).
   While the early encroachments of whalers, trappers, and traders had living
resources as their main target –​be it whale, fox, marten, or beaver –​in the 20th
century a dead resource became more valuable and more strongly contested.
This was related to the increasing state interest in the arctic landscapes,
containing untapped riches of a geological kind. After Second World War,
there was an explicit wish to move and resettle the Inuit in modern built-​up
communities, gradually alienating them from the semi-​nomadic and family-​
based mode of living, all while freeing access to new energy resources and
military installations.
   This was not so different from the early missions in Brazil, whose visions
of saving the tribes from themselves implied a break-​up of their original
settlements. Anthropologists are familiar with the old circular arrangements
of their housings and camps, divided into moieties, and with the men’s house
at the centre. This was a general feature of the Gé (linguistic) group, including
the Bororo, famously studied by Lévi-​Strauss, who noted:

  So vital to the social and religious life of the tribe is this circular lay-​out that
  the Salesian missionaries soon realized that the surest way of converting the
78 Moving Frontiers

  Bororo was to make them abandon their village and move to one in which
  the huts were laid out in parallel rows. They would then be, in every sense,
  disoriented. All feeling for their traditions would desert them, as if their
  social and religious systems (these were inseparable as we shall see) were
  so complex that they could not exist without the schema made visible in
  their ground-​plans and reaffirmed to them in the daily rhythm of their lives.
     To this extent we can absolve the Salesian Fathers: They took infinite
  trouble to understand this difficult cultural structure and to preserve the
  recollection of it. Anyone who works among the Bororo must first master
  what the Fathers have to say about them.
                                                     (Lévi-​Strauss 1963: 204)

Missionaries had worked in the Arctic for some time in an equal attempt
at converting the Inuit. While the Inuit were not forced to rebuild their
abodes, the presence of missionaries and permanent trading stations on the
coast created new centres and gradually changed the patterns of habita-
tion. New towns in the North were built (partly) in response to the break-
down of the fur trade, and the need to open for alternative employment
(Tester et al. 2013). The first Hudson’s Bay Company post in the Arctic
proper had opened in 1911 on Baffin Island, providing an outlet for trading
furs against modern goods. The Company and its activities were well-​
established, semi-​autonomous and state-​appreciated system of exchange
for all parties. The state even relocated a group of Inuit in 1934 to Devon
Island, by a Hudson’s Bay Company post –​to keep the trade alive at least
for some time (ibid.: 18).

  All of this changed with the collapse of the fox-​fur trade following World
  War II. Prices fell from $25 a pelt in 1945 to as low as $3.50 in 1949
  (…). By 1953, they had recovered to only about $12. Inuit could no longer
  complement their lifestyles with the goods made affordable by trapping.
  Confronted with the ravages of tuberculosis, pressure to send their children
  to residential and federal day schools, and the lure of religious holidays and
  celebrations “in town,” Inuit relocated (and in some cases were relocated)
  to Arctic settlements. What followed was a period of debilitating welfare
  relations, broken by the attempt to introduce Inuit to industrial employ-
  ment. These efforts included employment on the Distant Early Warning
  (DEW) Line in 1956–​57 and the opening of the North Rankin Nickel
  Mine in 1957. Further attempts to initiate commercial activity based on
  harvesting of eider down, carving, printmaking, boatbuilding, and fishing
  introduced Inuit to wage employment and settlement living. Mining offered
  new hope for assimilation of Inuit into a modern industrial economy.
                                                        (Tester et al. 2013: 18)

More mines were to follow, and while some Inuit readily embraced the pos-
sibility for a sustained wage income, it was not easy to keep optimistic in
                                                        Moving Frontiers 79

the long run. Some mines proved unprofitable and were soon abandoned,
such as the North Rankin Nickel Mine, closing in 1962. The official report
noted the mine “as being successful although mine closure created (and con-
tinues to create) problems for some Inuit employees” (quoted in Tester et al.
2013: 26). What is the proper measure of success, one wonders? Mines and
their labourers were to find themselves in equally ambiguous situations in the
Canadian Arctic, and the whole modernization project seems wasted in the
region. As late as in the 1980s new mines were created, with news-​media
recalling the High Arctic as a Last Frontier and claiming that the opening of
Cominco Polaris-​led zinc mine in 1982 on Little Cornwallis Island showed
Cominco as an ‘industrial pioneer’ and further stated how “to open a mine
in this remote region is an act of industrial daring and courage” (Green
2013: 38).
   Apart from not solving local social problems but calling for increasingly
highly specialized workers flying in and out –​not even living in the new
towns –​the Inuit workers were left with appalling house conditions as well as
a gradual poisoning of the environment –​their own highly specialized know-
ledge field (Rodon et al. 2013). The mining companies followed the pattern
established by the trade companies, drawing in local labour in their projects,
all while seeking to stifle the frontier (see also Powell 2017). The trading
posts, and later the mines, changed not only the habits and expectations of
the Inuit; they refashioned their landscape –​resettling them in places that
had been empty –​or possibly inhabited only temporarily before (e.g., Wenzel
2008). Before that, and elsewhere, the whalers had created a ‘perfect revolu-
tion’ among the Inuit tribes, as Boas noted –​for better and for worse.
   The European newcomers to the Arctic tended to posit the native hunters
on the margins of the liveable world. This amounted to a categorical
inequality between hunters and farmers –​the latter including explorers and
scientists hailing from sedentary states (Brody 2000: 6–​7). Meanwhile the
Arctic hunters were seeking to place themselves strategically in relation to the
resources, with an eye also to new possibilities offered by the trading posts.
At issue is the difference in numbers, hunters being so very few by com-
parison to old-​world peasants or labourers. It is for anthropologists to show
how mobile lives matter, and how frontiers may reveal the infinite plasticity
and flexibility of human life –​giving hope for the future.


Refashioning the Margins
Returning to the notion of frontier we should take note of Tsing’s suggestion
that the “frontier is not a natural or indigenous category. It is a travelling
theory, a foreign form requiring translation. It arrived with many layers
of previous associations” (Tsing 2005: 31). She quotes Frederick Jackson
Turner’s address on The Significance of the Frontier in American History
([1893] 1994), and its long-​  lasting inspiration to Americans, while also
noting its amazing erasures. Turner described the frontier as “the meeting
80 Moving Frontiers

place between savagery and civilization” ([1893] 1994: 32). As such the
frontier is neither a place nor a process, but “an imaginative project cap-
able of molding both places and processes” (Tsing 2005: 32). Famously, its
transformative power is described like this by Turner himself: “the wilder-
ness masters the colonist …Little by little he masters the wilderness” (Turner
[1893] 1994: 33). This process was found also to embrace the Arctic, if at a
different pace.
   While the ‘frontier’ has many overtones, it is first and foremost an
undecided space of possibilities. Stefansson noted how the Far North was a
shifting term and it makes sense to remind us of the ambiguous nature of the
frontier, as it continues to frame Arctic realities. In 1921, Stefansson wrote:

  The “Far North” is a shifty term. The Romans considered the middle of
  France too frigid ever to support a high civilization. Fifty years ago the
  Arctic was supposed to stretch a long arm down to where now stands
  Winnipeg with its 200,000 people, and it was debated if potatoes could
  successfully be cultivated in that part of Saskatchewan which is now
  known to be nearly if not quite the world’s greatest wheat country. So
  the “Far North” will continue retreating till the Arctic that is unpeopled
  with our race shall have shrunk far within the technical arctic circle as
  laid down by the mathematical astronomer and geodesist. The lands com-
  monly supposed to be covered with ice are even now covered with grass;
  the “eternal silence” of the North exists only in books; the “vast arctic
  deserts and plains where no living thing can flourish” are the abode of fat
  herds of indigenous grazing animals winter and summer –​as you will see
  if you read on in this book.
     The “Far West” is gone. But in the North is a greater frontier than the
  West ever was, stretching across Canada and across Siberia. The commer-
  cial value of the remotest arctic islands will be seen ere we die who are
  now young.
                                                     (Stefansson 1921: 73–​4)

Stefansson himself pushed back the frontier, by mapping new lands and
identifying new resources. Crossing old boundaries, he and other arctic
ethnographers also contributed to the process of unifying what had been
divided, and of levelling whatever differences had earlier caused major
(imaginative) obstacles for truly meeting others. It was a long and turbulent
process, and still unfinished as history generally is. In that sense, the frontier
is still in evidence; it is as ‘throwntogether’ as other places and steeped with
ancient histories and current concerns (Massey 2005). So are the anthropo-
logical fields that we traverse in our search for a knowledge that may become
part of a shared, global reflection on the shrinking landscapes for ever larger
populations of our time.
    In the Arctic, anthropology and the sciences more generally have served to
make the troubling wilderness of the region and its life forms legible; it has
                                                          Moving Frontiers 81

contributed to the establishment of particular ‘truth spots’ even in a highly
mobile environment (Bocking 2013). Wherever we work, anthropological
knowledge remains interstitial; it emerges between the ‘here and now’, and
the ‘there and past’. It remains tentative, even precarious all while creating its
own truth spots –​such as tribal names that were invented and subsequently
fixed. From the moment the Arctic emerged as a frontier zone, it was both
marginal, singular, and a whole onto itself, and already implicated in another
world through multiple material and imaginative relations between people
and their resources.
   When Boas wrote: “As I lived amongst them as one of them, I learned
their habits and ways, I saw their customs referring to birth and death, etc.”
(Boas 1998: 138), this is neither true nor false. He hardly lived ‘as one of
them’ –​we never do as anthropologists, except superficially. It is not only
that most of his encounters went through the whaling station; it is the far
more important point of his reason for being there was different. If the fron-
tier zone always eclipsed the New and the Old World –​levelling differences
through shared interests –​so did anthropology. Since its discovery, the
Arctic region has been constituted as a frontier, an overpowering landscape
of possible resources to be tapped into, lived by, exchanged, or exported.
Its barrenness has been proved wrong, while the indeterminacy of place has
been proved right.

4	Animal Companions
       The Precariousness of the Hunt




Inuit life in Arctic America depended almost exclusively on living off animal
resources. The seasons offered a particular game to fill the pot and supply the
necessary skin, while other means of existence had disappeared with the ice,
or with other natural changes. Fish and fowl might sometimes fill a gap while
waiting for the reindeer or the muskoxen to return, and seals would offer a
main stable during winter in many places. Still, there was no guarantee for
survival, as we know from passing travellers and ethnographers who often
commented upon the provisions left (or lost) at some places. Knud Rasmussen
was one of the travellers observing the situation. Among the Netsilik Inuit
(Netsilingmiut) he had a conversation with Qaqortingneq, who said:

  You have seen us happy this summer; we have been happy, not only
  because we have enjoyed living together with entirely new people in our
  village, who came to us from far away and yet could speak our tongue.
  You also came to us with ammunition for our guns and with iron for our
  ice-​hunting harpoons. And this has increased our happiness at being with
  you. For we Netsilingmiut are like this: Even though we don’t go hanging
  our heads with anxiety over how many snowstorms next winter will have
  in store for us, we feel in fact safer and in better humour when our hunting
  gear and ammunition are as we like to have them. I suppose you have seen
  sufficient of our life here to understand that there is never too much meat.
  Of course, there are times when we kill much more than we can eat on the
  spot, for instance in autumn when the caribou gather in herds to leave our
  country. The same is the case when the trout are many in the rivers near
  the time when the ice spread over the lakes. In times like these we often
  feel we have so much food that we will not be able to eat it all. But who-
