  ever does that forgets the many, many days in winter when we can find
  no food at all; he forgets that the caribou go away from our country and
  that even the seals may disappear or snowstorms prevent us from finding
  their breathing holes. And so the man that is wise never lolls about idle
  when the weather is good; he can never know when bad days may eat up
  his meat caches and drive him and his family into starvation.

                                            DOI: 10.4324/9781003374145-4
86 Animal Companions

    Life is so with us that we are never surprised when we hear that someone
  has starved to death. We are so used to it. It sometimes happens to the best
  of us. They cannot help it, it is not their fault, it is either sila [the weather]
  or perseq [blizzard] or to·nraq [evil spirit, i.e., sickness].
                                         (Rasmussen 1931: 134; his insertions)

This is a vivid picture of the multiplicity of animals that made human life
possible in the Arctic landscape but could never be taken for granted. Being
hunters meant being mobile to a degree that in some parts of the American
and Canadian Arctic it was difficult to speak of distinct tribes, even if
there were notable ethnographic regions, Aleut, Pacific, Alaska, Central
Arctic, Eastern Arctic, Labrador, and Greenland, just to paint a very gen-
eral picture (for an overview, see Damas 1984). People might have felt ‘at
home’ in certain territories and along preferred routes, but they were quite
cosmopolitan, as we heard from Vilhjalmur Stefansson’s ‘discovery’ of a
well-​travelled and highly knowledgeable tribe by Prince Albert Sound (cf.
Chapter 2).
   When we approach the Far North and its inhabitants, human and non-​
human, we are faced with a hybrid geography, in Sarah Whatmore’s term
(2002). Hybridity points to the practicing of space by humans and others
as part of the geography to be charted. “The spatial vernacular of such geo­
graphies is fluid, not flat, unsettling the coordinates of distance and prox-
imity; local and global; inside and outside” (ibid.: 6). Arctic hunters had their
own spatial vernacular reflecting the human–​animal relations at the core of
their lives. They depended on their companion species, as these emerged
in different landscapes and required practices of different scales, from big
whaling to delicate bird-​catching. We cannot cover all animals, and in this
chapter we shall focus on dogs, muskoxen, reindeer, and marine animals,
showing distinct relationships between people, animals, and their shared
landscape.


The Eskimo Dog
A signal animal companion of the Inuit was, and in some places still is, the
dog. In a famous Inughuit myth, known in other variants across the Inuit
region, a woman marries a dog and gives birth to five pairs of twins, each
consisting of a boy and a girl. In their turn, they become the progenitors of
other populations; the first couple of the white people, the second of the
savages, the third of the wolves, the fourth of a supernatural race of giants,
and the last become progenitors of yet another supernatural race, this time
of dwarves (Rasmussen 1908: 15, 85–​6).

  The dogs descended from the wolves, among which some selected spe-
  cimen entered into a new relationship with another species for the first
  time. This meeting probably happened many times across the North,
                                                     Animal Companions 87

  either through a copying or independent realisation or of positive inter-​
  species reciprocity, but it must have happened somewhere for the first time
  and changed everything.
                                                        (Wishart 2018: 1)

Wolves and humans came together in some way or other; the scraps left by
people could have been tempting to the wolves that kept close to humans
for that very reason. In his diary from the Second Thule Expedition in
North Greenland (1916–​1918), Rasmussen related how they were accom-
panied by wolves from camp to camp, keeping their distance but always
close. Sometimes they were too close for comfort, often at moments when the
expedition was most vulnerable. This happened on the return journey, when
an Inughuit participant had disappeared, never to be found again (Rasmussen
1919: 407). While the travellers did not normally fear the wolves, they could
become portents of ill fate.
   By contrast, the dogs that developed from wolves were to become indis-
putable companions. Robert Wishart elaborates on the special relation
between them.

  Wherever and whenever the first realisation of this special relationship
  occurred, the two species of wolves and humans came closer through the
  generations to follow, with each species finding benefit in this new rela-
  tionship. Wolves, in their transformation to dogs, benefitted from human
  provisions and protection, but so did the people benefit from their new
  hunting and protecting companions. The capacity of dogs to transport
  people or goods came later but it is also a crucial part of our northern
  mutual story.
                                                          (Wishart 2018: 1)

The dog had its own place in Inuit mythology, possibly reaching back into
pre-​history, and one could venture the thought that dogs by their labour
and skills have held together the region of the Inuit, prehistoric and historic.
McGhee writes:

  There is no archaeological evidence …to indicate that the Dorset people
  used dogsleds. We can be sure that at least some Dorset groups must have
  had dogs. The pact between humans and dogs was made during the last
  Ice Age, and the two species have been inseparable ever since.
                                                      (McGhee 1996: 145)

   Since the first Palaeo-​Inuit moved from Siberia to North America and
brought dogs with them, “the descendants of these animals would have
continued to earn their keep as hunting companions, camp guards, and
scavengers” (ibid.). From the late Dorset people arriving in Northwest
Greenland around 1000 C E , a couple of centuries before the Thule Inuit,
88 Animal Companions

there are only scant traces of dogs in the form of a proper harness, yet the
dogs may occasionally have been hitched to small sleds, of which there is
some archaeological evidence in the shape of small sledge shoes, by a simple
leather line (Holtved 1944). McGhee argues more generally, that “the scar-
city of finds relating to dogs suggests that most Dorset camps possessed few
of the animals and that they were not an essential element of transportation”
(McGhee 1996: 146). There may have been a pact, but in case of general
food shortage, the dogs were the first to go, as famine-​food (Hill 2018: 99).
It takes a relatively strong hunting economy to feed dogs, eating off the same
resources as people. Even so, it seems unquestionable that dog traction was
part of the Thule way of life, and that it “facilitated the spread of people
eastward, along with a sophisticated toolkit and innovations in watercraft”
(ibid.).
    Dogs and dog sledges were essential for survival in a landscape that
demanded extensive mobility; transport may have varied regionally and his-
torically, but dogs had been part of social life for as long as there were people
in the High North. They were part of a particular assemblage of human,
animal, and material agents, the latter including the sea ice. The assemblage is
never given a priori; with the dogs in particular, their relation to other species
is inherently unstable (Hastrup 2018). They may be co-​hunters, helping their
masters to get meat, or they may themselves become meat, enabling people to
survive a meagre period. They are always there, as pack-​dogs, sledge-​dogs, or
watchdogs; sometimes they are a great nuisance, as when stealing provisions,
and sometimes a source of deep concern, when both people and dogs are
starving, yet they are always necessary.
    In Northwest Greenland, the dog sledge was (and is) indispensable for a
good part of the year, as the Inughuit travel on the sea ice between settlements
and hunting grounds. Erik Holtved, working in the region in the 1930s (and
later) writes:

  The dogs take up a goodly part of the existence of the Polar Eskimos.
  A good dog team is necessary in order to keep up what, according to
  Polar Eskimo conditions, is considered a high standard of living: and
  losing one’s dog team is a very serious matter, which among other things
  entails greater dependency on others. The number of dogs in a team
  varies from time to time, but it is generally between 7 and 15. However,
  a great deal of meat is naturally required for dog food so only the most
  efficient hunters can afford to keep the really big teams. To understand
  the great importance of the dogs, one must keep in mind that the hunting
  trips of the Polar Eskimos often extend over hundreds of kilometres
  from home and may last for months. An existence without dogs is a
  toilsome existence. Nevertheless, times are recalled when the dogs for
  some reason had died, and people had to push the sledges themselves by
  the upstanders.
                                                     (Holtved 1967: 69–​70)
                                                     Animal Companions 89

This is a picture of yesteryear, but it is still a valid observation –​even if the
ice cover is increasingly unstable, and sea-​ice hunting shrinking. The long-​
term historical continuity of Inughuit ways of life, implying a partnership
with dogs, offers an interesting parallel to the dogs of an Amazonian people,
the Runa, with whom Eduardo Kohn worked. Dogs and humans live lives of
their own, Kohn states:

  And yet their lives are also deeply entangled with those of their human
  masters. This entanglement does not just involve the circumscribed con-
  text of the home or the village. It is also the product of the interactions
  that dogs and people have with the biotic world of the forest as well as the
  socio-​political world beyond Ávila through which both species are linked
  by the legacy of a colonial history.
                                                          (Kohn 2013: 135)

We could claim a similar relationship between hunters and dogs in the Arctic,
even as we replace the forest with living ice. In High Arctic Greenland dogs
still play a double role, as servants indispensable for dragging the sledge, and
partners in the polar-​bear hunt; they are the first to sense the bears, and when
close enough the pack is loosened from their braces and sent off to encircle
and keep the bear in place until the hunter can catch up with his gun ready.
This reflects the long history of collaboration that goes back to the end of the
last ice-​age in the north Americas when the ‘pact’ between humans and dogs
was first made, as we heard above (McGhee 1996: 245).
    In all regions of the Arctic, dogs have played an important role as
companions for the Inuit, to judge from the descriptions made by early
ethnographers. I shall render a case from the Copper Inuit, giving a vivid pic-
ture of the division of labour between men and women, children, and dogs,
when it came to the migrations towards new hunting grounds, as noted by
Jenness.

  During migrations the man does all the loading of the sled, his wife handing
  him the household goods through the doorway or through a hole in the
  wall. She helps him, however, to lash up the sled, while the children, if there
  are any, harness the dogs. On the trail the woman pulls ahead of the dogs,
  but the man hauls just in front of the sled, where he can urge on the dogs
  from behind; the son has a place beside his father, the daughter beside her
  mother. In the summer packing, the man generally carries the tent, with his
  tools and weapons on top of it; the woman has her own and her husband’s
  sleeping gear, the lamp, if one is carried, and the cooking pot, while the
  children carry their own sleeping bags and their bows and playthings.
                                                         (Jenness 1922: 87–​8)

Even though the dogs drag the sledge, they are not alone in doing so.
There are not enough of them, and people must help dragging while also
90 Animal Companions

carrying heavy loads on their backs. They are in it together, so to speak,
further substantiated by a certain naming practice among the Copper Inuit.
Throughout the region, children were named after recently deceased kinsmen
or women, and “the same rule applies to dogs also. Aksiatiak called one of
his dogs Itaiyuk after his dead father; later, when his wife bore him a son, he
named him Itaiyuk also” (Jenness 1922: 157). It is not that they are generally
sentimental about the dogs; surplus pups are easily disposed of, and dogs
that steal food from humans or eat skins in times of famine are easily kicked.
Yet, there is no doubt that the welfare, such as it is, of dogs is of concern to
the mobile hunters. In the wake of bouts of the dreaded dog-​disease, people
would have to get new dogs in exchange for something else, but in the early
20th century a regular trade of a small kind took off. The Copper Inuit began
to trade their dogs with the natives around Great Bear Lake, after Stefansson
had introduced them to one another in 1910. Since then, they had regu-
larly visited the region and exchanged their dogs for guns and ammunition
(Jenness 1922: 47). That was a new role for the dogs, being traded to native
tribes around the Slave Lake, and they fared rather badly.

  The thing that impresses a stranger in the country, and one to which a
  person of any feeling or imagination does not soon grow callous, is the
  cruelty and thoughtlessness with which dogs are treated in the north. It
  is a common thing that they are not fed all summer, and some there-
  fore die of starvation, while most of them survive only as living skeletons
  until the approach of fall makes it necessary to feed them up in order
  that they may have some strength for the coming winter. And it is not
  merely that they are never fed –​to show kindness to a dog is an unheard-​
  of-​thing. If he merely passes your tent door, walking along and minding
  his own business, it is good form for you to seize a hatchet or a hammer
  or anything that is near and throw it out to see if you can hit him. If you
  happen to knock an eye out or break a leg, it is considered an excellent
  joke, unless the dog’s particular owner should be near, in which case he is
  offended; not because he feels for the dog, but merely because he thinks
  that showing offense may give him some chance of recovering damages.
  Some of the white men treat the dogs a little better than the average Indian
  or half-​breed, but a dog used to kindness is nevertheless a thing that I do
  not remember seeing. The result is that the poor dogs, who always expect
  a kick, will always receive your approach with a snarl. By buying some of
  these dogs and using them myself, I have found that even after this sort of
  bringing up they quickly become under kind treatment as friendly as our
  house dogs at home.
                                                         (Stefansson 1913: 11)

Dogs brought up among the Inuit are described in various ways by the early
ethnographers –​being rather different between themselves. In certain seasons
and places dogs had a hard time, due to general hardship for all, and would
                                                     Animal Companions 91

be left with a few fishbones for their feed. They shared the plight of their
masters in times of feast as well as times of famine. In his diary from the
Copper Inuit, Jenness mentions the dogs rather frequently, and notes how
little feed they often had to subsist on. He also elaborates on the relationship
between humans and dogs in more general terms, underscoring the complex
companionship:

  They are not cruel to their dogs, though when they do strike them they
  strike hard. In winter they feed them in the houses out of their musk-​
  ox ladles, calling them in one by one and driving each out when it has
  finished, either with the cry “hoq hoq” or with the addition of a blow with
  the snow-​duster. Yet they consider themselves first. After a day’s travel the
  dogs are not fed, for they are tired and do not want to eat, the Eskimos
  say. They feed them the next morning. This practice has one advantage –​it
  saves dog-​food for the day –​but most dog-​drivers would not recommend
  it. Occasionally dogs are muzzled to prevent them barking or howling and
  frightening away caribou, or if quarrelsome one foreleg is looped through
  a cord tied round the neck so that it hobbles on three legs.
                                       (Jenness, 26 May 1915; 1991: 442–​3)

Jenness goes on writing about the pups, to whom the Inuit are not cruel,
even if they sometimes have to scold them; Jenness ends the passage by
ascertaining, that the

  link that binds dog and men is naturally closer than that which binds him
  to seal or caribou, and so he uses the word kia (who) for dogs as well as
  for persons, but huna for other animals which have no individual names.
                                                                     (Ibid.)

This description leaves us with a clear impression of dogs living on the
margins of human society; they are both within and without it. They make
nomadic life easier, even when the Inuit had to carry or drag most of their
gear themselves. In summertime, when sledges were not usable, dogs could
still carry something on their backs. The multiplicity of the dog’s work is suc-
cinctly described by Rasmussen in his extensive work on the Netsilingmiut,
describing how they are not particular about the beatings they give them, and
adding that a dog must never be overfed in wintertime, “for then it loses its
energy and will not use its nose.” He continues:

  And of course it is just in its ability as a hunting dog that its value to the
  little family lies. As a draught animal it might well be dispensed with. But
  a breathing hole hunter without a dog might just as well stay at home
  and suck his thumb. If he has a dog, a hungry one for preference, it will
  lead him over the ice with distended nostrils in order to get the scent of
  something eatable, and it is from wonderfully long distances that it will
92 Animal Companions

   suddenly prick its ears, lift its tail and rush away, not stopping until with
   unfailing certainty and without wavering a moment it has taken its master
   to a breathing hole.
                                                        (Rasmussen 1931: 148)

Before letting the dogs recede into the background –​where they always
lurk in the Arctic –​let us not forget their vigour and sweeping beauty when
dragging the sledge. I refer to a moment of wonder for Rasmussen, when he
was still new to Baffin Land and on a foraging tour with a small team to Lyon
Inlet, after which they went northward on the lookout for a village that was
supposed to be in the vicinity, but proving difficult to locate:

   Suddenly out of the darkness ahead shot a long sledge with the wildest
   team I have ever seen. Fifteen white dogs racing down at full speed, with
   six men on the sledge. They came down on us at such a pace that we felt
   the wind of them as they drew alongside. A little man with a large beard,
   completely covered in ice, leapt out and came towards me, holding out
   his hand white man’s fashion. Then halting, he pointed inland to some
   snow huts. His keen eyes were alight with vitality as he uttered the ringing
   greeting: “Qujangnamik” (thanks to the coming guests).
      This was Aua, the angakoq.
      Observing that my dogs were tired after their day’s run, he invited me to
   change over to his sledge, and quietly, but with authority, told off one of
   the young men in his party to attend to mine. Aua’s dogs gave tongue vio-
   lently, eager to be off again and get home to their meal; and soon we were
   racing away towards the village. A brief dash at breakneck speed, and we
   arrived at the verge of a big lake, where snow huts with gut windows sent
   out a warm glow of welcome.
                                                (Rasmussen [1927] 1999: 21)

There is more to the quote here than the impressive dog team; there is also a
view of Rasmussen’s own personality –​impressionable, and greatly appreci-
ating vitality in both dogs and men. This reminds us that ethnographies have
different authors, writing in different tonalities; there is not one single picture
of people and their companions.


The Muskox Way
In 1910, the Danish archaeologist Hans Peter Steensby suggested that the first
immigration into Northwest Greenland from the Canadian Arctic Islands
took place in the wake of the muskox. Around 4,500 years ago the muskox
crossed the Smith Sound from the Canadian archipelago and continued
along the coast of North Greenland and down along the north-​eastern coast
(Steensby 1910: 400; 1916: 214). Steensby dubbed this the Muskox Way –​
an appealing name for a particular moment in the human–​muskox relation,
                                                   Animal Companions 93

eventually also leading to the ‘discovery’ of the Independence I Culture, being
the earliest traces of humans in Greenland, first identified by archaeologist
Eigil Knuth (1967a, b). He named it after the location of his excavations
near the Independence Fjord; this fjord, again, was named by Robert Peary,
arriving there on Independence Day in 1892, and leaving a note on this in a
cairn (Peary 1898: 349). Incidentally, Peary also noted that their “dog food
consisted of pemmican, eked out by those of their number that we killed,
and a bountiful feast of musk-​ox meat at Independence Bay” (ibid.: 280) –​
just to remind us of the motley of traces that make an ethnographic region,
human, and animal. The Independence Culture has been revisited by several
Danish Archaeologists since then, shedding new light on its beginnings, and
its relatively modest population of humans and muskoxen alike (Grønnow
and Jensen 2003; Jensen and Gotfredsen 2022). There were many muskox
ways beyond the one established in North Greenland (Hastrup 2022).
    The existing population of muskox is native only to the New World (nor-
thern North America and Greenland). Apparently, the animal had a wider
Arctic distribution during the late Pleistocene, after which the geograph-
ical range as well as the genetic diversity decreased –​sometime between the
Last Glacial maximum and the mid-​Holocene, that is roughly within the
interval 18,000–​4,000 YBP (MacPhee et al. 2005; Lent 1999). Fossil records
of muskoxen confirm that they originated in Eurasia but entered North
America in early Pleistocene (MacPhee et al. 2005). Most living species
today owe their characteristics to the adaptation to an environment dating
back to before human interference with it. Here we shall zoom in on the
muskox and its dispersal in the New World, on the backdrop of reduced
mega-​ faunal diversity around the Pleistocene-​    Holocene transition, when
people also wandered into North America from Asia, across the landmass
later known as Beringia, at some point in time forming a vast subcontinent,
allowing animals to migrate –​also southwards on the American continents
(Lent 1999: 18).
    The diversity within the migrating species was gradually reduced to one,
the Ovibos moschatus, slowly moving eastwards, in all probability stimu-
lating the migration of Palaeo-​Eskimos of the Arctic Small Tool tradition to
move in the same direction (Lent 1999: 58). The Muskox Way took shape, all
while the animal’s territory shrunk to the range of the Arctic Archipelago and
Greenland (MacPhee et al. 2005). Archaeological investigations along this
way have yielded evidence of a substantial human exploitation of muskoxen,
possibly leading to (temporary) local depletions; probably there were
never really many muskoxen on which people could prey at this time (Lent
1999: 23). Numbers seemed to shrink further as humans moved in. They
remained in the East, as described by Lyle Dick in his book Muskox Land,
whose title underscores the relationship between human history and arctic
ecology. Ellesmere Island had been named Umingmak Nuna (muskox land)
by Inuit and Inughuit from Greenland, who hunted there (Dick 2001: xxv).
People would follow the muskox to where it took them, and along with the
94 Animal Companions

caribou, the muskox played a vital role in the development of the tundra
ecosystem.
    The 19th-​and 20th-​   century Arctic explorations were prefaced by a
number of expeditions in late 18th century that have been less publicized,
if certainly serving as a take-​off for later, more famous expeditions (Levere
1993). Both the Royal Navy and the Hudson’s Bay Company pushed
British interests northwards, partly in the continuing interest of finding the
Northwest Passage, and partly because The Royal Society in London had
strong interests in scientific exploration as an activity in its own right –​if
often in tandem with commerce and mining adventures, as we heard in the
previous chapter. Mostly, the expeditions went by sea, but after some hints
about a copper mine in the North, the Hudson’s Bay Company staged an
over-​land expedition from the Bay and northwards. The appointed leader
was the naturalist Samuel Hearne, who had a long experience from the Royal
Navy and who had entered service in the HBC, being based at The Prince
of Wales Fort by the mouth of Churchill River. From here, the route to the
Arctic Ocean was unmapped, but there were rumours about great riches
in the Barren Grounds. Hearne was to sort that out in three consecutive
campaigns, finally bringing him and his crew to the Coppermine River.
    Early in his diary, Hearne notes how they proceeded

  toward the North West by West and West North West through low scrubby
  pines, intermixed with some dwarfed larch, which is commonly called
  juniper in Hudson’s Bay. In our road, we frequently saw some tracks of
  deer, and many musk oxen, as they are called here.
                                                (Hearne [1797] 1911: 64)

Finally, they met them live, in large flocks; noting that the bulls were few in
proportion to the cows, his Indian co-​travellers suggested that they killed
each other contending for the females (ibid.: 165). The first unmistake-
able encounter with living muskoxen near Sandstone Rapids resulted in the
following observations of this species that Hearne now knew to be real and
well named:

  Though they are a beast of great magnitude, and apparently of a very
  unwieldy structure, yet they climb the rocks with great ease and agility,
  and are nearly as sure-​footed as a goat; like it too, they will feed on any-
  thing; though they seem fondest of grass, yet in Winter, when that art-
  icle cannot be had in sufficient quantity, they will eat moss, or any other
  herbage they can find, as also the tops of willows and the tender branches
  of the one tree.
                                               (Hearne [1797] 1911: 156–​66)

Hearne described several other animal species, but our concern here is with
the newly discovered muskoxen, and with his observation that they remained
                                                   Animal Companions 95

in the Far North all year round. He found support of this by finding muskox
dung on hills that were denuded of snow, but where there was no trace of
their tracks in the moss; “this was a certain proof that these long ridges of
dung must have been dropped in the snow as the beasts were passing and
repassing over it in the Winter” (Hearne [1797] 1911: 192). This may not
look like a sensational observation, but at the time it was new knowledge,
as was the identification of the Copper Indians’ Bird of Warning, that would
point them towards deer and muskoxen “which, without their assistance in
all probability, they never could have found” (Hearne [1797] 1911: 193).
The need for ‘help’ to find muskoxen indicates that they were hardly abun-
dant. This explains why they were so little known, if soon to become of
interest to people neighbouring Hudson’s Bay Company stations.
   With time and new knowledge of the Arctic filtering through to Europe,
a more distinctly scientific interest emerged, notably in botany and zoology.
With the British Arctic Expedition (1875–​1876) led by George Nares, was
zoologist Henry Feilden whose reports on people, flora, and fauna were
wide-​ranging and detailed and included as substantial appendices to Nares’
report. Feilden had an eye also for the muskox, and made the accurate obser-
vation that,

  the number of musk-​oxen in Grinnell Land is extremely limited, whilst the
  means of subsistence can only supply the wants of a fixed number; con-
  sequently, after an invasion such as ours, when every animal obtainable
  was slaughtered for food, it must take some years to restock the Ground.
                                                  (Feilden 1878: 198–​202)

Numbers are obviously uncertain when it comes to a largely unknown
species in an uncharted land, inhabited by very few people. The muskox lived
in barren and sparsely populated regions, depending on finding its own way
in changing climates, but became increasingly implicated in human politics,
including shifting hunting regulations.
   In the process of accelerating commercial muskox hunting, not least
through the Hudson’s Bay Company, the Chipveyan gradually ‘lost’ their
muskox resource from the 1830s due to overhunting, while also suffering
from a severe decline of the caribou (Burch 1977: 143). Meanwhile, the Inuit
became eager muskox hunters, carrying “their muskox-​hunting activities into
regions still farther distant from their former coastal domain” (ibid.: 144).
As Boas noted:

  Wherever the musk ox is found it is eagerly pursued by the Eskimo.
  Though dogs are of no use in the chase of the nimble deer, they are a great
  help in hunting this animal. When a track is found the dogs are led loose
  and soon overtake the herd. The latter form a circle of defence in which
  they are kept at bay until the hunter approaches. While the dogs continue
  attacking and dodging, the musk oxen try to hit them with their horns and
96 Animal Companions

  do not heed the Eskimo, who assails them at close quarters with a lance to
  which a thong is frequently attached. When an ox is wounded it makes an
  impetuous attack on the hunter, who dodges to one side. The dogs being
  at hand again immediately keep it at bay, thus enabling the hunter to let
  fly another arrow or throw his lance again. Thus, the struggle continues
  until the greater part of the herd is killed. In rare instances an ox dashes
  out of the circle and escapes from the pack.
                                                      (Boas [1888] 1964: 101)

The fluctuation of muskox populations spurred different scientific interpret-
ations and political interventions. The Inuit interest was more consistent, if
not always easily met, making early ethnographers among the Central Inuit
uncertain about the actual place of the muskox in the hunting practices. It
may have gradually assembled in the Eastern Arctic. If an old immigrant
from the East, the muskox was new to the ethnographers among whom it
even briefly became an issue of language politics. Stefansson claims that it is
inappropriate to call ‘him’ muskox; the name “is in a sense libellous of him,
as it is in a sense deceptive to the reader.” He continues:

  I have made no researches to discover who first perpetrated the blunder
  of calling him “musk ox.” It may have been some early English navi-
  gator who was a better sailor than zoölogist and misidentified him with
  the musk deer of Asia. Or possibly he was more of a trader than he was
  a scientist and wanted to lead people to believe that he had discovered a
  new commercial source of the costly musk perfume of our ancestors –​
  a trick with many parallels in early exploration, of which none is more
  interesting than Eric the Red’s frank admission that he named Greenland
  so in order to induce his fellow Norsemen to colonize it.
                                                     (Stefansson 1921: 238)

Stefansson had a lot more to say about the animal, scientifically known as
Ovibos (or sheep-​cow), which is “what he is to the casual view –​a cow (or
bull) with a coat of wool” (ibid.), seeking further support in Otto Sverdrup’s
report of his four-​year sojourn in the eastern part of the Arctic Archipelago,
where he met –​and slaughtered –​a good many ‘Polar cattle’, as he called
them (Sverdrup 1903, I: 35; 160ff).
    National politics was soon to become more important than language pol-
itics, however. Already in a preface to Stefansson’s book (of 1921), the Rt.
Hon. Sir Robert Laird Borden, Prime Minister of Canada, praised the author
for having by his expedition added many thousands of square miles to the ter-
ritory of Canada, and opened a possibility for domestication of the muskox
(Borden in Stefansson 1921: xxiv–​xxv). The many square miles refer to the
Eastern Arctic Archipelago.
    Within the archipelago, Ellesmere Island (and neighbouring islands) had
for a long time served as a refuge for muskox and had once been an important
                                                     Animal Companions 97

threading stone on the Muskox Way towards North Greenland. Yet, it
was little known to the outside world, but Boas heard about Umingmak
Nuna from the Baffin-​Landers, and was convinced that it was, indeed, the
name of Ellesmere Island –​inhabited by people closely related to the North
Greenlanders on the other side of Smith Sound (Boas [1888] 1964: 51–​2).
Indeed, they were related, also in their joint interest in the muskoxen. While
the meagre vegetal resources even in the valleys might easily disappear under
the snow, there were also sheltered inland valleys with an unusual level of
growth for this latitude that might support muskoxen (Dick 2001: 33).
   Politics was later to affect the traffic across Smith Sound, and to challenge
Inughuit access to the resource landscape. Britain had transferred the East
Arctic Islands to the dominion of Canada in 1880, but it took some time
before the borders were politically implemented. Robert Peary’s repeated
excursions from Greenland to Ellesmere Island during his long quest for
reaching the North Pole (1891–​1909), effectively reminded the Canadian
authorities about the East Arctic Islands. Coming from the Greenlandic side
with Inughuit helpers, Peary inadvertently underscored a potential Danish
claim to the region. The Canadians had to act, recalling how Sverdrup had
not only named (and implicitly claimed) the islands with Norwegian names
but even hoisted the flag (see Sverdrup 1904, I & II; Dick 2001; Schledermann
2003). It was not enough to assert sovereignty; it also had to be demonstrated
through occupation, and the Canadian authorities sat out to do just that.
   Part of the concern in early 20th century was also the vehement exploit-
ation of the muskox by the many recent expeditions. The emissary to
Ellesmere Island for the Canadian government estimated that Peary and
other American expeditions alone had taken 800 muskoxen over a gener-
ation; Sverdrup and his crew were presumed to have killed some 200 animals
on Ellesmere Island during their expedition (1898–​1902), while Cook had
taken c. 100 during his polar expedition in 1908 (Dick 2001: 268–​9). This,
it was feared, would have given the Inughuit in Greenland a renewed taste
for muskox, and the Canadian emissary advocated regulations “to prevent
numbers of Eskimo natives of foreign countries exploiting Canadian terri-
tory, and destroying valuable hunting and fishing grounds” (Bernier 1910,
cited in Dick 2001: 270).
   The sense of political emergency was further fuelled by Rasmussen’s plans
for the Fifth Thule Expedition (1921–​1924); the Canadians were alarmed
when hearing about them in 1920, believing that he wanted to establish
trading stations in Canada. He was told not to hunt muskoxen, to which he
replied: “There is no question of our breaking Canadian Game Laws because
we are not coming into Canada but a part farther north. It is not under
Canadian jurisdiction” (Rasmussen, cited in Dick 2001: 274). In his view,
Ellesmere Island had always been a (Greenlandic) Inughuit hunting ground.
The latent conflict continued and in 1921 the Dominion Commissioner of
Parks, J.B. Harkin, pressed on to make Canada curtail Inughuit muskox
hunting, recommending that a Royal Canadian Mounted Police post should
98 Animal Companions

be established on Ellesmere Island for the purpose of stopping the slaughter
and asserting Canadian authority (Harkin, cited in Dick 2001: 274–​5).
   Posts were eventually established in Craig Harbour (1922) and at
Bache Peninsula (1926), ironically calling on the Inughuit from Northwest
Greenland for help with transport and hunting –​as there were no ‘Canadian
Inuit’ in the region at all. Consequently, and once again in the interest of
protecting Canadian sovereignty, the defunct Royal Canadian Mounted
Police (RCMP) detachments on Ellesmere Island were to be re-​established
and ‘native’ settlements created –​first at Craig Harbour along with the
RCMP-​post (1951) and later at Resolute and Grise Fjord (Dick 2001: 426ff).
The latter, meaning ‘Pig Fjord’, had been so named by Sverdrup who found
the noise of walrus sounding like grunting pigs, well known in the Nordic
countries from where he hailed.
   The early explorers not only met with new animals but also increasingly
with new people who relied on muskox for their living. As the Hudson’s
Bay Company became more pointed in its effort to extend its own influ-
ence, it began to map both animals and people, leaving some of the earliest
descriptions of muskox hunters. Ernest Burch made a survey of the material,
with a view to clarify the relations between “one prey species, the muskox,
and one predator species, man” (Burch 1977: 137). From research in the
Company reports and other sources, Burch ascertains that Muskoxen were
relatively abundant on the Canadian mainland west of Hudson Bay in 1689,
when they were first reported by Europeans, and seem to have remained
so through the following century. The southern and western boundaries of
muskox country was some 150 kilometres below the treeline and parallel
to it. The Chipevyan –​some of the Company’s nearest neighbours –​were
muskox hunters, and sometimes supplied the station with meat (as noted
also by Hearne [1795] 1911: 137). The result was that “the muskox became
both a casual and a critical resource” as Ernest S. Burch has it (1977: 142).
The hunters had to navigate within this framework.
   When the Company began to accept hides as trade items, the Chipevyan
up-​scaled the muskox hunt, as did the Inuit hunters along the eastern margin
of the area, gradually decimating the fragile herds. This is the short version
of a long story leading to the decline, first of caribou and next of muskoxen,
the latter often being an emergency resource when the caribou failed (Burch
1977: 142). By mid-​19th century, the remaining muskoxen in the region
south of the treeline had disappeared and further north their range had
shrunk to just a few places along salient rivers. The acquired taste (also for
hides) in Europe had taken its toll; around 1900 there were only four small
and isolated muskox refuges left in the central Canadian Arctic.
   In 1917, as we heard above, the government of Canada placed a complete
ban on muskox hunting, to be enforced by the Royal Canadian Mounted
Police. At the same time, the caribou population started to decline dramati­
cally, and with no emergency resources, hundreds of Inuit starved to death
in a major famine in 1917–​1921 (Burch 1977: 146–​7). Burch claims that
                                                    Animal Companions 99

some of the early investigators, including Birket-​Smith, who took part in the
Fifth Thule Expedition, failed to appreciate the role played by the muskox-​
hunt in the local economies in his report (Birket-​Smith 1929). By contrast,
Diamond Jenness of the Canadian Arctic Expedition (1913–​1916), certainly
appreciated the important relations between hunters and muskoxen on
Banks and Victoria Islands (Jenness 1922). So did Rasmussen, meeting the
hunters in Canada and naming them as (a rather indistinct) Musk Ox People
(Rasmussen [1927: 191ff] 1999: 246ff).
    On Banks Island, where archaeologists have found evidence that
muskoxen have been hunted for at least the last 3,400 years, only a few
were present when Stefansson explored the island in the mid-​1910s (Nagy
2005: 95). Interviews convinced Stefansson that the Copper Inuit living
in the region had overhunted them (Stefansson 1921: 241). It is the more
remarkable that muskox numbers on Banks Island peaked at some 65,000
individuals in 1994, after years of protection, and still being the world’s lar-
gest indigenous population of muskoxen in 2005 (Nagy 2005). In 2014 it
was down to c. 14,000 and decreasing –​as on Eastern Victoria Island, once
equally abundant as Banks Island but down to c. 10,000, in 2015 (Cuyler
et al. 2020: 807). This may be owed to stochastic events, rather than human
interference; humans do not control the muskox, however much they desire
it. It has its own way.


Caribou Trails
The Caribou Inuit are well documented by the early ethnographers in the
region. They were not ‘a tribe’, but a grouping of people around the trails of
the caribou, moving seasonally between the tundra and the coastal region,
and inciting people to hunt them. Like the muskox, the caribou belongs to the
terrestrial ecosystem, including the tundra of the Canadian mainland, the so-​
called Barren Grounds (Freeman 1984: 41). It was a migrating species, and
while a pattern of migration was visible, the caribou was not always reliable.
Sometimes Barren Grounds Caribou would leave the tundra for the forested
taiga regions further south, while others might remain north during winter;
snow conditions played an important role in such caribou movements.

  They avoid low-​lying areas in which deep accumulations of snow occur
  (even though such areas might, in summer, provide the richest vegetation).
  In fact wintering caribou are mainly restricted to those places avoided as
  summer feeding areas, that is, the exposed ridges and slopes and hillsides
  where, due to abrasive wind action, no snow accumulates and little but
  lichen and woody growth is to be found.
                                                        (Freeman 1984: 44)

They would move north towards the coast when the terrain allowed, how-
ever, and here they would be met by ready hunters. It has been noted that
100 Animal Companions

although the Inuit hunters appear to have hunted over large areas, “they make
use of their detailed knowledge of animal behavior to concentrate hunting
on small ‘core’ areas where the likelihood of success is greatly enhanced”
(ibid.: 43). One of the earliest descriptions of a reindeer hunt is owed to
John Ross, whose ship was temporarily ice-​bound in the late 1820s at his
second attempt at finding the North-​West Passage. He took the opportunity
to interview some of the first natives they met about hunting, not only out of
ethnographic interest but also needing provisions for his crew. Having been
informed that there were plenty of muskoxen on the hills to the southward,
and that the reindeer all came this way in April, they gained further infor-
mation on the hunt itself. Their method of hunting, as the locals described
it, consisted mainly in aping the appearance of the animal, by means of two
men, the foremost carrying the head and horns over his own, and thus being
able to gain access within the herd (Ross 1835: 252). Once within the herd,
the hunter could easily kill the caribou.
    Another explorer of renown who spent time in the land of the caribou was
Roald Amundsen, who was equally ice-​locked in the North-​West Passage,
and who eventually was the first to traverse it from east to west. He was not
blind to the importance of reindeer (caribou) for the making of skins and
clothes and described the hunt in ethnographic fashion.

  Now the great season is approaching when the ice on the rivers and lakes
  breaks up and reindeer hunting begins. This is without comparison the
  Eskimo’s most glorious time. He then gorges himself with meat. Reindeer
  begin to come north of the Boothia Isthmus as early as the month of May,
  but it is only now and then that one is killed with the bow and arrow at
  that season. It is only when the ice disappears and the kayak is launched
  that reindeer hunting begins in earnest.
    When the kayak is fitted out for the reindeer chase, the hunter has his
  two reindeer lances ready beside him, fixed in straps in the kayak skin
  so that they may not fall overboard. The reindeer has a fixed track to
  the north for their migration. When passing Nechilli they take such a
  course as to make it easy for the Eskimo to drive them into the water.
  The huntsmen divide into two parties, one with the kayaks and the other
  without. The kayak men station themselves on the bank opposite to the
  one from which the deer are coming. When a herd of reindeer approaches,
  the drivers make a wide circle around them and drive them into the water.
  As soon as the deer are well into it, the kayak men jump into their boats
  and spear one reindeer after another. The animals are towed to the bank
  and taken care of by the huntsmen on shore.
                                                 (Amundsen 1908, I: 326–​7)

Despite inevitable differences from year to year, the caribou seemed reli-
able enough for some groups to bank on them, and to develop collabora-
tive hunting practices allowing the hunters to lure the animals into ever
                                                   Animal Companions 101

narrower stone alleys and shoot them by bows and arrows at the endpoint.
The caribou played a major role for the Copper Inuit who divided their life
between sealing in winter and caribou hunting (and fishing) during summer.
Let us hear Stefansson:

  All tribes of the Copper Eskimo in April and May have their camps on ice
  across which the caribou migrate in thousands on their way north. None
  of these make any use of their opportunities so far as we could learn.
  Especially the Noahonirmiut near Lambert Island and the Kãnhiryuarmiut
  on Prince Albert Sound see endless successions of small bands passing
  their very doors. As mentioned above, there seems no taboo on caribou
  killing or caribou meat at this season, but we were merely told “nobody
  ever hunts caribou on the ice” –​they live in abundance of seal meat at this
  time and blubber gathering is their most important object. It would be
  really foolish for them to spend their time pursuing the skinny migrating
  cows, if they did they would have to face the winter without the store of
  blubber that is their salvation at the time of autumn scarcity.
                                                       (Stefansson 1914: 54)

The seasonality of Inuit life is apparent in all ethnographic descriptions,
differing in other respects; Stefansson dismisses the idea of a taboo on caribou
hunting while seals are still abundant, a taboo that others have suggested.
For Stefansson, pragmatics is always at the forefront of his observations,
and his interest in finding new lands for Canada obviously affected his sense
of place, and of the shifting companionship between humans and animals.
Jenness was more in line with the Inuit ways.

  The caribou or reindeer, for the two are identical, is the camel of the
  Arctic, the animal that transforms a barren waste into a hospitable
  dwelling-​place for man. Domesticated, it transports his possessions and
  supplies him with milk; and tame or wild, it furnishes him with most of
  his needs. My Eskimos had no knowledge of its domestication, but they
  made clothing and tents from its fur, straps and lines from the tanned hide,
  tools and weapons from the bones and antlers, ornaments from the teeth
  and toes, and thread and cord from the sinew of its back and legs. Every
  part of the animal thus has its use; but much was often wasted, especially
  at the end of summer, when the natives had wandered far from their sleds
  and winter sealing-​grounds. They needed its fur for clothing, seven whole
  skins making only a single costume that lasted at the most three years;
  so they slaughtered bulls, cows, and calves, took the hides, the sinew, the
  back fat, and what little meat they could carry, and abandoned the rest
  to the wolves and the foxes. Such indiscriminate destruction might have
  been branded as foolish, even criminal; but when white men have uncon-
  sciously exterminated the buffalo by similar methods, how can we expect
  primitive people like the Eskimos to make regulations for the preservation
102 Animal Companions

  of their game? After all, no regulations were necessary as long as their
  only weapons were bows and arrows, which exacted too light a toll to
  cause any diminution in the herds of caribou that returned to their Arctic
  grazing-​grounds year after year.
                                                     (Jenness 1928: 152–​3)

Bow and arrow was still the main hunting instrument at the time, which
made caribou hunting differ markedly from the big slaughter of buffalos fur-
ther south by natives and whites alike. In the North, a few more Caribou hit
by an arrow did not make a noticeable difference to the stock. The animals
were approached by simple stalking, or by driving bands of deer towards
concealed hunters. The drivers were women and children, pressing the herd
into a V-​shaped fence made of stones, where the hunters would take place in
the pointed end, and where the animals would then be trapped.

  Then they bunch up irresolutely and give the hunters a good opportunity
  to shoot. Members of our party have seen only as many as eleven caribou
  killed in this way by four hunters and half-​a-​dozen women and children.
  It is uncertain how many they get in lucky hauls, none can tell themselves,
  for none can count, but they considered eleven a small catch …
     When deer have been killed in some number, most of the meat is cut
  up and half-​dried, never fully dried, spread out on stones. The blood is
  always taken and used for soups, and the moss contents of the stomachs
  are allowed to ferment a few days in the sun and then eaten. This last dish,
  fresh or fermented, is about the only article of vegetable diet used by these
  Eskimo.
                                                        (Stefansson 1914: 58)

Stefansson is observant but seems always to be removed from the Inuit.
By contrast, Jenness, having gone inland to the lakes with his host family,
noted the spring arrival of ducks, loons, and ptarmigans, and saw how
the caribou were scarce, only wandering singly or in twos and threes –​
searching for the richest pastures (Jenness [1928] 1959: 130). From their
camp at the lake the Inuit drifted out every day in different directions, the
women to fish and the men to hunt. The women were the more successful,
so they only had fish every evening, while the “hungry dogs looked on with
mournful eyes; tied to stones, they dined miserably on bones and broth”;
Jenness continues:

  I hunted, most of this period, to obtain caribou meat for my starving dog-​
  team, but during a fortnight’s steady tramping saw but two young bulls,
  which we consumed entirely in three days. The stony ridges cut to pieces
  my light seal skin boots, intended only for soft snow, and Icehouse, who
  had tried to patch them each evening, finally rebelled. “Stop hunting for a
  time,” she said. “Your boots are wrecked beyond repair, and caribou will
                                                  Animal Companions 103

  be scarce until the warmer weather comes and the mosquitoes drive them
  near our camps. Stop hunting, and I will make you new boots with stouter
  soles for the summer months.”
     It was not only my boots that troubled her. She believed that an evil
  demon, haunting the hills to the westward where I generally made my
  excursions, trapped and destroyed the solitary hunter. In this very dis-
  trict, many years before, it had spirited away her first husband, who had
  vanished without leaving a trace. So when I, too, vanished for sixteen
  hours one day she became greatly alarmed, and entreated Ikpuck to lead
  a searching party. Willy-​nilly, I had to join the fishermen and let my dogs
  go hungry.
                                                 (Jenness [1928] 1951: 131–​2)

Jenness certainly lived with his hosts. It so happened that the fish began to
bite less freely, and some of the men began wandering farther afield in search
for caribou. One day, Ikpuck shot a caribou eight miles away, and instead
of carrying it home, he covered it with snow and advised the entire group
to move camp in that direction. Everybody welcomed the idea of shifting
the ground around a bit, and began packing, hiding the dried fish that had
been assembled in stone cairns, while the empty sleds were turned upside
down on stone pillars and topped up with the stone lamps and other things
that were too heavy to carry (Jenness [1928] 1951: 132–​3). The introduc-
tion of guns was instrumental in the reshuffling of both people and animals.
Rasmussen explicitly laments the introduction of firearms on the Barren
Grounds, suggesting that the number of people (known as Padlermiut) had
diminished dramatically over a generation, from some 600 people to barely
100; this was owed to the firearms that had affected Caribou, making them
avoid their old routes and crossings, and making social life more difficult
(Rasmussen [1927] 1999: 67). He elaborates on this in the Report of the
Fifth Thule Expedition:

  In these parts of the Barren Grounds there are enormous numbers of
  caribou, which follow their old migration paths, and cross rivers and lakes
  at quite certain places. As long as the Eskimos only used bow and arrow,
  their kayaks and their special kayak lance, the animals were not scared to
  the extent as they are now, when the hunters shoot away at the animals
  opposite the crossing places with long-​range magazine rifles. When the
  caribou have experienced this disturbance for a few years they take other
  paths, whereas the Eskimos remain at the same villages, and this causes
  the catastrophe. They think that there are no caribou, whereas the truth is
  that the caribou have merely chosen another road.
                                                    (Rasmussen 1930: 36–​7)

In Alaska, and a generation before, the Inuit had also lost many of their old
sources of income and struggled to reform their economy. The creator of a
104 Animal Companions

well-​organized, operating school system, Dr. Sheldon Jackson “recommended
the introduction of semi-​domesticated reindeer from neighbouring Siberia to
ward off the impending crisis” (Jenness 1962: 12).

  At first, Congress demurred and refused to vote him the necessary funds;
  but he appealed to the public, and with the money it contributed bought
  16 deer in 1891 and 171 in 1892, which the captain of the revenue steamer
  transported across the Bering Sea to Teller in Port Clarence. The success of
  these two first shipments fired the imagination of the public, which forced
  Congress to finance other purchases until by 1902 the Bureau of Education
  had bought over 1,280 animals. Importation ceased in 1902, but within
  eight years of that date the 1,280 reindeer had increased to 27,325, grouped
  into numerous herds that ranged from Point Barrow on the arctic coast to
  Ugashik within forty miles of the Guld of Alaska, and from the promon-
  tory of Wales facing Siberia to Tanana far up the Yukon River.
                                                            (Jenness 1962: 12)

The economic value was immense, but best of all it secured the economy of
some 3,500 Inuit directly, and possibly as many indirectly, Jenness notes,
adding how “one man’s vision swiftly translated into action gave northern
Alaska a new food and clothing resource, one which dissipated the spectre
of starvation that had been haunting the region for a decade” (Jenness
1962: 12). He gives the word to the governor of Alaska (1939–​1952), Ernest
Gruening, who “did not exaggerate one iota,” when he wrote that “the most
important single contribution made to the natives –​or at least to part of
them –​in the first half-​century of United States rule was the importation of
reindeer, which, during the period when their natural food supply had been
greatly impaired, saved thousands of Eskimos from death by starvation”
(Gruening 1954: 397; and in Jenness 1962: 12).
   Famine was a recurrent issue in Arctic America in the early decades of
20th century, during ‘the great ethnographic period’, as we can see from
the many works and reports written. It was partly owed to new political
systems and shifting trading patterns, and partly to an increasing pressure
on the natural resources. People began to gather at the ports or stations
along the coast and leave their ancient ways of living –​lamented by some of
the chroniclers, but also urging them to document the life and traditions of
the ‘original’ inhabitants. Rasmussen inadvertently gives vent to this in the
description of his arrival in Alaska on the Fifth Thule Expedition and saw a
flat tundra, stretching away towards Point Barrow with only small, scattered
Inuit encampments along the way, often visited by white men. Reaching
Point Barrow, Rasmussen found a population of some 250 natives and a few
white men. He admits:

  I had not expected to find anything of interest on this part of my journey,
  and really conceived my collections at an end on leaving Canada; I
  soon found that this was not the case. Men and women here were less
                                                   Animal Companions 105

  sophisticated than those of the Mackenzie Delta, and there was a store of
  folklore and mythology, ready to hand. I decided therefore to take advan-
  tage of the opportunity, and make some stay here, despite the advice of
  experts who declared that if I did not push at once, I should have to wait
  until August and go on by the sea. We were just at the most exciting part
  of the annual whaling season.
                                          (Rasmussen [1927] 1999: 305–​6)

We shall return to the whales below, here we shall just take note of Rasmussen’s
general impression of a well-​run state since the United States had bought the
whole territory from Russia.

  In 1890, the Bureau of Education set to work to improve the conditions
  of the native population, and now, after 35 years, we find them indus-
  trious, ambitious and independent, a wonderful testimony to the value of
  systematic educational methods. A point of great importance in material
  respects was the introduction of tame reindeer from Siberia. Dr. Jackson,
  the Alaskan Eskimos’ greatest benefactor, succeeded in getting some 1280
  animals brought over, and there are now close on half a million, with
  every prospect of running into millions before long.
                                             (Rasmussen [1927] 1999: 307)

Times were changing in Alaska, marking both people and animals deeply;
times always change, be it through changing landscapes, ambitious people,
or new political realities. Yet, something strangely seems to adhere to life in
the Arctic, even while it changes. While families are less mobile than before,
many still depend on living resources and their seasonal migration for sur-
vival –​and not least on their ability to anticipate animal movement (Nuttall
2010; Hastrup 2013b).
   It is a complexity we may see also in the stronghold of the reindeer as
a rich resource for people in the entire Arctic region, from Greenland to
Alaska; sometimes it did not show up at the right time and left people
hungry. Still, the reindeer was not only meat for consumption and feasting
on blood, fillets, tongues, marrow, it was also skins for tents and clothing,
sinews for strings, and much more. There is a reason why it was coveted also
by expeditioners, as well as a reason for its being a stable target for hunters.


Companions of the Sea
Hunting marine mammals along the coast of the Arctic Sea has been pre-
dominant among the Inuit since prehistoric times, even if supplemented by
other animals, when available. Neither the rocky coasts nor the vast tundra
could provide the inhabitants with enough to eat without seasonal migration
along the coast, or between the coast and the tundra, or river deltas. People
would group around resources, or sometimes go their own way, if there was
nothing to share in the immediate vicinity.
106 Animal Companions

   For a long time, whales defined the region both from within and without,
in the sense that the last migration of Thule Inuit from the Bering Strait to
Greenland (c. 1200), was made partly by umiak, the communal whaling boat.
The migrating people were whale hunters, having lived along the Arctic Sea;
like the dog sledge, the umiak was emblematic to the latest migrants from the
Western to the Eastern Arctic. Whales were not the only sea mammal of note;
people would also hunt walrus, polar bear, and more than anything they
would depend on seals. This was expressed in the tale of Nerrivik, Sedna,
or the Mother of the Sea –​different names for a woman, who orchestrated
the movement of sea animals from the bottom of the sea and decided when
and whether they should give themselves up. Rasmussen, having just met the
angakok Aua at Igloolik, asked him to tell what he knew about her. He said:

  There was once a girl who refused all offers of marriage, until at last
  she was enticed away by a petrel disguised as a handsome young man.
  After living with him for some time, she was rescued by her father, but
  the petrel, setting out in pursuit, raised a violent storm, and the father, in
  terror, threw the girl overboard to lighten the boat. She clung to the side,
  and he chopped off, first the tips of her fingers, then the other joints, and
  finally the wrists. And the joints turned into seal and walrus as they fell
  into the sea. But the girl sank to the bottom, and lives there now, and rules
  over all the creatures of the sea.
                                               (Rasmussen [1927] 1999: 27–​8)

This tale is told in many versions; but the Mother of the Sea always had to be
kept happy by receiving some recognition. As for the whales, they had been a
forceful player in Inuit lives, from the first immigrants from the Bering Strait
to the 20th century. They were not a cheap resource however:

  Whaling ships churned the waters of the Beaufort Sea until about 1906,
  when the price of baleen fell to 40 cents and less, and the pursuit of the
  bowhead ceased to be profitable. By that date not only had the number of
  whales and caribou gravely diminished, but the number of Eskimo also.
  A little earlier influenza and other diseases introduced by the whalers had
  produced a similar diminution in the population of the Eastern Arctic
  (Boas 1888, p. 426) but there, for some reason which is not yet clear, the
  whaling captains had carried only limited stocks of intoxicating liquor,
  and had restricted its consumption very largely to their own crews. In the
  Western Arctic, on the other hand, they not only distributed liquor to the
  Eskimos with full hands, but taught them how to make it by distilling
  molasses or potatoes from one 5-​gallon coal-​oil can to another.
                                                           (Jenness 1964: 14)

Alaska seems to have suffered from the low morale of the whalers, who were
soon to withdraw, as the whales were overhunted. The development in the
                                                    Animal Companions 107

Western Arctic spearheaded the massive changes that affected all Inuit. The
geographical position of Alaska made it special in the transformation of Inuit
lives. The changes were so rapid that the aboriginal North Alaskan Eskimo
culture could only be known through the memory of older living informants
(Spencer 1959: 1). Rasmussen writes:

  In Alaska, natural conditions and animal life have necessitated a devel-
  opment of industry on two definite lines: the caribou hunters on the one
  hand, the whalers on the other. Hunting by sea had its definite seasons,
  precluding any very nomadic form of life, while the immense areas through
  which the caribou had to be followed on the other hand made it impos-
  sible to keep to the coast in the whaling season. Consequently, some of the
  natives sailed up the rivers and settled more or less inland, though coming
  down once a year to the coast for sealskin and blubber, bringing caribou
  skin and furs in exchange.
     Point Barrow has always been one of the main centres for the Eskimo
  whaling industry. The whales begin to arrive in numbers about the begin-
  ning of April, and continue to come until the first week in June; the whaling
  was carried out from skin boats at the edge of the firm winter ice.
                                           (Rasmussen [1927] 1999: 309–​10)

He elaborates further on both the ice and the whales, praising the whalers for
their harpooning skills, and gradually merging present and past achievements.
There was more to Arctic hunting than whales and reindeer, of course, as we
can see from the ‘review’ of the affordances in a world caught between past
and present. Jenness writes in his diary of 11 December 1913:

  This evening Aksiatak drew a sketch of part of the Colville River, and he
  and Aluk showed me where they fished and hunted. Afterwards he gave
  me the map. Caribou, he says, are more numerous than they used to be
  in some parts of the Colville [River Valley]. There are plenty of red and
  blue foxes, and some black ones are met with further up. Ptarmigan are
  numerous, also squirrels, but there are no muskrats. Moose are seen fur-
  ther to the south, but black bears are occasionally found in the portion
  of the river he sketched. The river and all the lakes are full of fish, chiefly
  anakliq [whitefish], which in one lake grow to a great size and are very fat.
  One lake, however, contains five or six different kinds of fish, though the
  land around seems no different save that the willows are especially thick
  here. Some Eskimos remain on the river all the winter, obtaining their
  flour from the traders in summer. They do not use much flour, however,
  but live almost exclusively on fish.
                                                           (Jenness 1991: 81)

In the Central Arctic, whaling was less important than was walrus hunting,
being of major value to people during winter, when game was sparse. It
108 Animal Companions

often implied a long wait on the ice, but the time was never empty as we
gather from Rasmussen’s report on his own experience with Aua. Something
happened all the time, and a walrus might be seen in an opening gap where
it could also be heard breathing before it went down again to the feeding
grounds below. They had experienced it many times before, and “the familiar
sights and sounds loosened our tongues in recollection” (ibid. 25).

  “Men and the beasts are much alike,” said Aua sagely. “And so it was our
  fathers believed that men could be animals for a time, then men again.”
  So he told the story of a bear he had once observed, hunting walrus like a
  human being, creeping up and taking cover, till it got within range, when
  it flung a huge block of ice that struck its victim senseless.
     Then suddenly Aua himself gave a start –​he had been keeping a good
  look out all the time –​and pointed to where Miteq was standing with his
  harpoon raised. Just ahead of him was a tiny gap in the ice, the merest
  puddle, with barely room for the broad back of a walrus that now appeared.
  Miteq waited till the head came up, and then, before the creature had time
  to breathe, drove his harpoon deep into the blubber of its flank. There was
  a gurgle of salt water, a fountain of spray flung out over the ice, and the
  walrus disappeared. But Miteq had already thrust his ice-​axe trough the
  loop at the end of the harpoon line, and the walrus was held.
                                              (Rasmussen [1927] 1999: 25–​6)

When they came back to the family, there was a celebration. A full-​grown
walrus meant meat and blubber for many people over many days. Blubber
was an essential resource –​not least for heating. At some points in time, it
became more important than the caribou, even when these migrated north
during spring, and could easily be hunted. Stefansson noted about the Copper
Eskimo tribes that during April and May they had their camps on the ice,
across which the caribou migrated in thousands on their way north; but they
did not hunt it. Stefansson suggested:

  there seems no taboo on caribou killing or caribou meat at this season, but
  we were merely told “nobody ever hunts caribou on the ice” –​they live in
  abundance of seal meat at this time, and blubber gathering is their most
  important object. It would really be foolish of them to spend their time
  pursuing the skinny migrating cows.
                                                      (Stefansson 1914: 54)

People would disregard caribou, when seals were plenty on the ice; this meant
blubber, on which they would depend next winter, as Stefansson reported
from the Puiplirmiut on Victoria Island:

  They had plenty of blubber, however. Each family lays by at this season
  as much blubber as it can. This is cut in small pieces and placed in bags
                                                    Animal Companions 109

  made by skinning a seal through the mouth. Each of these bags when
  filled with oil will weigh one hundred seventy-​five to two hundred fifty
  pounds and one good hunter will fill two or three such bags during
  March, April, and May. When it comes time to go inland these are placed
  en cache, preferably on a small island. Be the caches on an island, or not,
  they are covered with large stones so as to be safe from bears; people, it
  is said, never steal from such caches. At the same place are also cached
  usually the winter lamps, the heavier stone pots, the winter clothing and
  all good clothes, and in fact everything that is not absolutely needed for
  the summer. On account of taboo restrictions, no blubber is taken along
  inland.
                                                       (Stefansson 1914: 54)

Seals were a vital resource among the Inuit along the entire Arctic coast,
as well known. The way of hunting them might differ a little from one
place to the next, depending on surroundings and preferences. Among the
Netsilingmiut the seal was only caught from the ice; open-​water hunting was
not practiced. There would be differing ways of hunting according to season,
but it would invariably be a breathing-​hole hunting. “Breathing-​hole hunting
is a wonderful method and can be developed into a great art through years
of practice and experience” (Rasmussen 1931: 151). Rasmussen describes
how training begins early, at the age of 12 or 14, and he gives a wonderfully
detailed description of the gear and of the many ways in which the hunter
may attack the seal, depending on season and ice cover. To have success,
the hunter must not only have the necessary gear, including his clothing and
possibly a piece of skin to stand on (for warmth and for limiting noise), but
also the necessary knowledge and patience. Rasmussen relates a seal-​hunt
involving 15 hunters –​one of whom suddenly had some luck. Everyone took
part in the feast and had a small taste of the seal on the ice –​except the dogs.
There was a taboo on feeding dogs at the place where a seal had been killed
(Rasmussen 1931: 159). He sums up:

  A single seal was thus the sole result of fifteen men’s hunting for eleven
  hours, and yet my companions were pleased that they had not gone out
  in vain as so often before. Of course there are days when they get any-
  thing from three to five seals; but there is by no means an abundance of
  these animals in the region –​as the hunting scores shown in the above
  witness to the full. Even the best of the hunters think themselves very
  lucky if they get as high as thirty seals in a whole winter; compare this
  with the fact that a good hunter in North Greenland will take two hun-
  dred seals in the same time, without too much trouble, and it will give
  you an idea of the difference in the conditions and standard of living –​
  here where a whole camp often have to be content with much lower
  figures.
                                                     (Rasmussen 1931: 159)
110 Animal Companions

Seal hunting by breathing holes demands a lot of patience, not least
because the seal has several breathing holes, visible by the domes of snow
that are gradually created above them. When the dome melts, the hole
is exposed, and it is no longer difficult to find them. Woman and half-​
grown children take part in this kind of hunt, taking their position at
each of their breathing-​hole, harpooning the animals when they emerge.
Another modus was found in the crawling towards a seal, lying on the ice
by the breathing whole –​the so-​called ‘otoq’ hunting; it took a long time
to get close enough to harpoon it –​up to an hour according to Rasmussen
(ibid.: 160). He adds:

  The clothing of the Netsilingmuit is not at all suitable for “otoq” hunting,
  as caribou skin does not slide easily on the snow and in spring quickly
  becomes soaked in moist or wet snow. For this reason they have either
  a sealskin or a piece of bearskin under their left elbow, and on this they
  glide. In the right hand they hold the harpoon by the line.
                                                       (Rasmussen 1931: 160)

   The interspecies relationship, and the mutual constitution of hunters and
their prey in such world as the Arctic, where animals were the only resource
that could be relied upon, the skin clothes further underlined their deep
entanglement. In the Friendly Arctic, Stefansson notes:

  These men dress in skins, commonly eat their meat raw, and have external
  characteristics which we correctly enough ascribe to the “cave man” stage
  of our forefathers. But instead of half-​ferocious beasts, prowling around
  with clubs, fearful and vicious, we have the kindliest, friendliest, gent-
  lest people, whose equals are difficult to find in any grade of our own
  civilization.
                                                       (Stefansson 1921: 89)

   The significant point of this quote is how by dressing in skins, and eating
raw meat, the Inuit were often presumed to be savage. Clothing is not inno-
cent; it marks the people’s standing in the world. Let us take a step to the
South American fringe, where Penelope Dransart (2013) has analysed the
fur clothing of the (ancient) Fuegians in terms of an interspecies engage-
ment, and suggested that the early accounts of their dress “convey a sense
of completeness, not only in the garments made from the skins of animals
and birds that the people hunted, but also with the seal, whale or fish oil
with which they anointed their own skin” (Dransart 2013: 195). She further
suggests that the European clothing forced upon them by the missionaries
to cover their alleged nakedness, broke the intimate connection between the
people and the animal world upon which they had depended. This could not
happen in the Arctic, where lives would have been at stake, had fur clothes
                                                    Animal Companions 111

been banned. Lévi-​Strauss has an interesting observation on Inuit clothing –​
within in a larger case made for indigenous wisdom.

  The enquiries of archaeology and anthropology show that certain
  civilizations –​some of them now vanished, others still with us –​have
  known quite well how best to solve problems with which we are still
  struggling. To take one instance only: it is now only a few years since we
  discovered the physical and physiological principles on which are based the
  Eskimoes’ costume and manner of life. If they can exist in these conditions,
  it is not from long conditioning, or from an exceptional physical constitu-
  tion, but from their discovery of scientific principles of which we had until
  lately no ideas at all. So true was this that it also exploded the pretensions
  of those explorers who had claimed to have “improved upon” Eskimo cos-
  tume: the results were the opposite of those that had been hoped for. The
  Eskimoes had already arrived at the perfect solution: all that we needed, to
  be convinced of this, was to grasp the theory which lay beneath it.
                                                        (Lévi-​Strauss 1963: 383)

It is unclear what kind of ‘theory’, Lévi-​Strauss is actually referring to, but
Roald Amundsen may help us out here, as he praises the appropriateness
of the Eskimo costume, which he had soon been forced to adopt already
during the first winter in Gjöa Haven on the North West Passage Expedition
(1903–​1907).

  My experience is that the Eskimo dress in winter in these regions is far
  superior to our European clothes. But one must either wear it all or not
  at all; any mixture is bad. Woollen underclothing absorbs all the perspir-
  ation and soon becomes wet through and through. Dressed in nothing
  but reindeer skin, like the Eskimo, and with garments so loose and roomy
  on the body that the air can circulate between them, one can generally
  keep his things dry. Even if you are working so hard that you can’t help
  getting wet, the skin dries again much easier than wool. Besides, woollen
  things soon become dirty and then they do not impart much warmth. Skin
  clothing keeps nearly as well without washing. A further great advantage
  of skin is that you feel warm and comfortable the moment you put it
  on. In woollen things you have to jump and dance about like a madman
  before you get warm. Finally, skins are absolutely wind-​proof, which of
  course, is a very important point.
                                               (Amundsen 1908, I: 149–​50)

In an interesting way, the skin clothes intensify the contact zone where people
and animals live together. One could argue that the Arctic region, with which
we are dealing here, was always a meeting ground between humans and their
companion species, forming a frontier of its own.
112 Animal Companions

Arctic Precarity
The settlements in the Arctic region have been tiny in all periods and have
been at the mercy of access to game –​and to human ingenuity. The relations
between people and animals have been built upon practical and differentiated
knowledge of the available animal species –​seal, polar bear, whale, walrus,
fish, birds, and hares, as well as reindeer and muskox. The animals have vastly
differing values in a hunting community, not only in their supply of different
necessities, food, clothes, and heat, but also as non-​human agents shaping
social relations within and beyond certain landscapes. In earlier times, people
