were often on the brink of (possible) starvation and depended entirely on
hunting. Their life was precarious, in the sense suggested by Anna Tsing:

  Precarity is the condition of being vulnerable to others. Unpredictable
  encounters transform us; we are not in control, even of ourselves. Unable
  to rely on a stable structure of community, we are thrown into shifting
  assemblages, which remake us as well as our others. We can’t rely on
  the status quo; everything is in flux, including our ability to survive.
  Thinking through precarity changes social analysis. A precarious world is
  a world without teleology. Indeterminacy, the unplanned nature of time,
  is frightening, but thinking trough precarity makes it evident that indeter-
  minacy also makes life possible.
                                                            (Tsing 2015: 20)

Precarity was obvious to the early ethnographers, who shared the lives of the
Inuit and were very observant of their struggles, as well as their strengths. The
position of animals in the larger history as well as in the immediate needs of
people is not to be questioned. More details could have been added, but the
main objective has been to show how the animal companions link together
the large environmental processes and the minutiae of lived life. The whole
and the parts –​the taxonomy and the species –​are co-​produced, as incompat-
ible observations turn into compatible categories through tiny convergences
and an agreement upon natural objects. We shall close this chapter with a
song from a hunter, written down by Knud Rasmussen in 1932:

  Hunger
  I was about me . . .
  In my little house
  Remaining was intolerable.
  Hungry and starving
  I staggered in over land,
  For ever stumbling forwards.
  At “the little musk-​ox lake”
  The trout made fun of me.
  I got no bite.
                                                        Animal Companions 113

   Onward then I toiled
   To “the young man’s broad” –​
   I had caught salmon there once.
   I did so wish to see
   Swimming caribou or fish in a lake.
   That joy was my one wish.
   My thought ended in nothing.
   It was like a line
   That runs all out.
   Would I ever, I wondered
   Have firm ground to stand on?
   Magic words I mumbled all the way.

   Kingmerut
   Man. Ellis River, Queen Maud Sea
           (In Rasmussen 1932: 132–​3)
