5	Vital Materials
       The Agency of Things




The present chapter does not offer an inventory of accessible materials in the
Arctic; by focussing on a few conspicuous ones, the intention is to show the
vitality of matter in the vein of Jane Bennet, making a case for the “cap-
acity of things –​edibles, commodities, storms, metals –​not only to impede
or block the will and designs of humans but also to act as quasi agents or
forces with trajectories, propensities, or tendencies of their own” (Bennet
2010: viii).
    The materials by which the Inuit constructed their lives infiltrated the
ethnographers’ descriptions, marvelling Inuit ingenuity and their ability to
live full lives in the deep-​frozen lands. Artefacts left on the ground from
past times or studied in action by the early ethnographers give an idea of
both the materiality and the dexterity by which people lived. On the Fifth
Thule Expedition there was a distinction made between the ‘intellectual cul-
ture’ and the ‘material culture’, in both the groundwork and in the reports
resulting from the observations. Knud Rasmussen was to concentrate on the
first, while Kaj Birket-​Smith and Therkel Mathiassen published mainly on
the latter. Naturally, the boundary was not watertight, but it was upheld to
a remarkable degree. Today, anthropologists know that ‘thinking through
things’ offers an important element in ethnographic understanding (Henare
et al. 2007).
    In fact, it was built into anthropology from its very beginning; thus we
may note the early Torres-​Strait expedition in late 19th century (1898–​1899),
instigated by the biologist Alfred C. Haddon and embracing other naturalist
members. Haddon and his crew paved the way for a distinct approach to
people, based on a transference of instruments, theories, and points of view
from biology; among the instruments were cameras, literally framing the
field (see Roldán 1992: 23; Grimshaw 2001: 20). Thinking through materials
is still an important part of anthropological practice.
    For the ethnographers, moving into and living in the Arctic called for cer-
tain techniques, necessarily implicating local materials such as skin, wood,
snow, stone, and turf –​materials that were part of the landscape, allowing


                                            DOI: 10.4324/9781003374145-5
118 Vital Materials

people to either settle or move according to season and hunting possibility.
Craftsmanship was essential, if not always up to the mark. Mathiassen writes:

  The Eskimo in his primitive state is his own craftsman; with tools which
  he has made himself he fashions the weapons and other implements he
  needs. But this in several places –​and among the Iglulik Eskimo too –​
  is now a thing of the past; the European workshop technique has made
  wide inroads into the life of the Eskimo. Factory-​made implements and
  weapons to some extent supplant their old forms of implements, and this
  destroys their good old technique too. The present-​day Igluliks are on the
  whole very poor craftsmen; what they make is mostly badly and carelessly
  finished, without much thought of beauty of form and decoration. In this
  respect they are far inferior to the people who formerly inhabited their
  land (the carriers of the Thule culture) and also to many Eskimo tribes
  alive today, especially in Greenland and Alaska. Even a poor Greenlander,
  for example, would not be seen driving a sledge such as those of the
  average Iglulik, quite apart from the difference in form: cross-​pieces of
  unequal length and breadth and at unequal intervals, lashings roughly
  made, some with seal thong, others with rope, some replaced by nails; the
  runner noses unequal in their upturn, one mounted with iron, the other
  not at all, and so on.
                                                    (Mathiassen 1928: 103)

There is not much mercy concerning the pitiful craft of the hunters. The
women, however, are praised being “quite artists with their needle and, as
regards clothing, they are equal to, and in some respects superior to most
other Eskimo tribes” (ibid.). The women’s techniques were up to the mark
and confirm Bennett’s view of vitality as intrinsic to materiality –​found
wanting in Mathiassen’s judgement of the Iglulik craftsmen.


Snow and Ice
Snow and ice co-​    defined Inuit culture when such was first studied and
classified. The Inuit were singled out by their mastering of the ice, and their
ingenious architecture of the igloo (among other things), so highly praised
by Boas in his early diary from Baffin Land (Boas 1998: 157), and no less by
Rasmussen when he first met the Iglulik Inuit. Coming to America by way
of the Inughuit in High Arctic Northwest Greenland, with whom he had
worked since 1903, he had his own yardstick for assessing the American way
of life. He met his ‘first’ American Inuit in 1921, when seeking out the nearest
Hudson’s Bay Company Station –​that is, to the place they had accidentally
landed when crossing over Baffin’s Bay from Greenland (cf. Chapter 2).
   Rasmussen, to his great relief, found that he and the people they first
met understood each other, and that they had the same need to celebrate
the meeting. They immediately started to build an igloo for the celebration,
                                                         Vital Materials 119

and Rasmussen had his first experience of their skills; his view was distinctly
comparative. Noting that the “faculty of observation is of course most alert
at the first meeting” he states that even the everyday business of building
a snow hut, as he and his companions from Thule had done hundreds of
times before, suddenly became quite extraordinary, as we can see from his
observations of the use of the one material that only the Arctic offered in the
right form and quantity: snow.

  Never had we seen a house spring up so rapidly out of the snow as under
  the snow-​knives of our new friends here. Among the Polar Eskimos of
  North Greenland, the building of a fair-​sized snow hut is reckoned a good
  hour’s work for two men. One cuts the blocks from a snowdrift lying out-
  side of the ground plan of the house, and hands them, unless there happens
  to be a third man on the job, to the one who is building the hut. Here how-
  ever, one man cut the blocks and built the hut at the same time. Selecting a
  portion of a snowdrift where the snow was of the right degree of firmness
  for the purpose, he marked out a circle in it, the snow within the circle
  being reckoned to suffice for the entire hut. To make a calculation of that
  sort in a moment calls for a great deal of experience and practice. Actually,
  then, our native architect here builds his wall and the self-​supporting roof
  up over the space left by the blocks cut out of the drift as he works. He
  must therefore cut down to the full depth of the drift, working his way to
  the bottom, whereas the Greenlanders cut the blocks they need from the
  surface. It was a simplification of the process amounting to genius, and
  labour-​saving to such a degree that one man here could cut the blocks, set
  them in place and trim them off with his knife all in about the same time
  that a Greenlander would take to cut the blocks alone. As the hut grew
  up out of the drift, one of the women, taking a big flat wooden shovel,
  spread loose snow over the wall from the outside. This layer of loose snow
  fills in any cracks and crevices, making the house thoroughly sound and
  warm inside, however hard it may be blowing without. The remarkable
  skill here displayed was evidently the result of many generations’ technical
  experience, and we at once realised that we had come upon a system of
  winter housing, and a capacity for utilising available material, superior to
  that which we knew from Greenland.
                                                        (Rasmussen 1929: 15)

This small comparative analysis of the building of snow huts not only
exhibits Rasmussen’s impressionable character, but also demonstrates how
the Inughuit of Northwest Greenland provided an explicit comparative base-
line for his approach to the North American Inuit.
   Vilhjalmur Stefansson, whose long-​term work in the Arctic had preceded
Rasmussen’s, was not nearly as impressed by their skills. After all, it is a
rather simple process, he said, because ice is sticky, and the blocks keep each
other in place.
120 Vital Materials

  Building with snow blocks is far simpler than building with masonry, for
  stone is an intractable substance and has to be shaped according to math-
  ematical calculations or moulded in an exact form before it is put in its
  intended position; but snow being a most tractable substance such fore-
  thought becomes unnecessary.
                                                     (Stefansson 1921: 175)

He claims that building snow houses could be taught by correspondence to
boys in any place on earth with enough snow, perhaps adding a diagram
“for surety’s sake”; clearly, for such boys, their lives would not depend
upon it.
   He adds that: “It is therefore curious that the building of snow-​houses
has until just lately been considered a mystery” (ibid.) –​and even more
strange that earlier expeditions did not try it out, all while praising Inuit
ingenuity. Boasting a little, he adds: “So it was curiously reserved for us
to be the first explorers to build our own snowhouses for field use. We
have found by experience that an ordinarily adaptable man can learn
snowhouse-​building in a day” (ibid.: 176). After all, he implies, it nearly
builds itself, and the blocks immediately freeze onto each other, and the
structure becomes solid.

  When the snow dome has been otherwise finished, a tunnel is dug through
  the drift into the house, giving a sort of trap door entrance through the floor.
  Most Eskimos, failing to understand certain principles of thermodynamics,
  use a door in the side of the house. But it is obvious that if a door in the
  wall is open and if the interior of the house is being artificially heated,
  then warm air being lighter than cold, there will be a continual current
  of heated air going out through the upper half of the doorway, and a
  cold current from outside entering along the floor. If the door is on a level
  with the floor or a little below it, the warm air from the house cannot go
  out through the door, even when the door is open, because the warm air
  has no inclination except that of rising. Similarly cold air cannot come
  in through the door in the floor so long as the house above is filled with
  warmer air, for two bodies cannot occupy the same space at the same time.
                                                       (Stefansson 1921: 176–​7)

Interestingly, in this passage Stefansson implicitly seeks to get the upper hand
over the Inuit, downplaying their practical knowledge (and they knew very
well about the low entrances), and seeing his own theoretical, thermodynamic
knowledge as far superior. Compared to Rasmussen, he is not exactly an
enthusiast. This reminds us that personalities impinge on the descriptions
that have been handed down.
   The igloo could be a shifty partner, as Stefansson had to realize, when he
and his travelling companions were looking for people in the Coronation
Gulf area but had no idea of where to find them; they had seen some traces
                                                        Vital Materials 121

and eventually found an abandoned snow hut: to save themselves some
trouble they decided to camp there.

  A new camp is warmer than an old one, for a new snow-​house is a snow-​
  house, but an old one is an ice-​house. This particular hut had evidently
  been kept pretty warm from its former occupants, for the walls were solid,
  glistening ice. We were all warm from fast travel, and in our hurry to get
  the camp heated up we closed the door tightly.
                                                     (Stefansson 1924: 222)

It was Stefansson’s job to cook the evening meal on that day and he was on
his knees to cut up snow for the kettle, listening to the conversation between
the two Inuit, when suddenly one of them fell backwards with a gurgling
noise; the others, including zoologist Rudolph M. Anderson who sat next to
him, thought it was part of a pantomime. When Stefansson asked Anderson
to see what Tannaumirk was up to, as he did not get up again, Anderson
himself fell down forward on top of Tannaumirk.

  I knew then in a moment what the matter was, and immediately extinguished
  the Primus stove, for it was clear that we were being poisoned by carbon
  monoxide, which is so insidious that under normal circumstances one
  does not notice its presence. Natkusiak saw nothing to be alarmed at, and
  when I told him immediately to break a hole in the snow-​wall behind him,
  he went about it with deliberation. Fortunately, in order to make the hole
  he had to get up to reach for his knife, which he had stuck into the wall.
  When he tried to rise he found himself powerless to do so, and that scared
  him, so that with his last strength he threw himself back against the wall
  and broke away the loose block of snow by which we had a few minutes
  before closed up the door. He then crawled outside on all fours, but was
  too weak to stand up. I followed him out and had strength enough to
  stand up after getting out, but that was only for a moment and I fell down
  beside Natkusiak.
                                                      (Stefansson 1924: 223)

According to Stefansson it was a calm, starlit night, with a tempera-
ture about 45o below zero, and they were lightly dressed. He wanted to
get the others out of the house but could not find the strength to do it.
Anderson’s face appeared 15 minutes later, and he crawled out only to
lay down and rest. Tannaumirk followed shortly after, but neither he nor
Anderson had any recollection of what had happened. It took an hour
before they were sufficiently restored to be able to drink some hot milk in
the sleeping bags Stefansson had managed to drag out. It seemed to them,
and it continued to seem so for Stefansson “that this not very romantic
adventure was the narrowest escape from death we had on our entire
expedition” (ibid.: 225).
122 Vital Materials

   Diamond Jenness, who was part of Stefansson’s wide ranging Canadian
Arctic Expedition, if mostly travelling on his own with the Inuit, had another
approach to the snow huts; he noted the need for exact measures of blocks
and had an eye for the spiral that must be very precisely built up, lest the
whole thing would crumble (Jenness 1922: 60). He also underscored the skills
needed to make a durable and tight structure that would keep out the cold,
and paid attention to the interior of the house once the man’s work on the
structure is finished.

  Meanwhile his wife is arranging the house from within. Usually a gap is
  made in the middle of the sleeping-​platform where the clothes and other
  articles can be conveniently stowed away. Then she arranges the platform
  itself, covering it over with mats of willow twigs tied together with raw-
  hide thongs, and bridging the gap with three or four planks. Muskox skins
  are laid on top of the matting, and on these the deerskins in two layers,
  the lower ones having the fur side downwards while the upper skins have
  the fur on top …
     In front of the platform the lamp and the table with their appurtenances
  occupy half the floor space, while the door comes in the middle of the arc
  at the very bottom of the wall.
                                                           (Jenness 1922: 61)

There would have been differences from place to place and from group to
group, but the risk of asphyxiation within ice walls gradually becoming too
tight, especially when inhabited for more than one or two nights, was gen-
erally known. Jenness had experienced it himself, if in a less dramatic way
than Stefansson.
   For Europeans, seeing entire villages of snow huts for the first time made
a major impression and left a lasting mark on the Inuit as singularly close to
nature. John Ross, on his second expedition to find the Northwest Passage in
the first part of the 1830s, was in quite some trouble finding his way out of
a frozen impasse, and was more than happy to meet some Inuit, who knew
where they were, and who invited the ‘lost’ party to follow them towards
their own settlement, where they had wives, children, and lots of provisions.
They could not, however, move there until the next day. Ross notes:

  This was a most satisfactory day; for we had given up all expectations of
  meeting inhabitants in this place; while we knew that it was to the natives
  that we must look for such geographical information as would assist us
  in extricating ourselves from our difficulties and in pursuing our course.
  It was for philosophers to interest themselves in speculating on a horde
  so small, and so secluded, occupying so apparently hopeless a country, so
  barren, so wild, and so repulsive; and yet enjoying the most perfect vigour,
  the most well-​fed health, and all else that here constitutes, not merely
                                                        Vital Materials 123

  wealth, but the opulence of luxury; since they were as amply furnished
  with provisions, as with every other thing that could be necessary to their
  wants. And if the moralist is inclined to speculate on the nature and dis-
  tribution of happiness in this world, on the admirable adaptation found,
  here as elsewhere, between the desires and the means of gratification,
  the pious one will not forget the Hand, which, under the most appar-
  ently hopeless circumstances, thus spreads for His creatures, a table in the
  wilderness.
     After divine service, which, for that purpose, we held unusually early,
  we proceeded to perform our promise, though the thermometer had
  fallen to minus 37o. We found the natives at the appointed place, and, on
  approaching, one, who appeared to be a leader or chief, came a hundred
  yards in advance, holding up his arms to show that he had no weapons.
  We therefore threw away our guns; on which all the rest, in the rear,
  threw their warlike instruments into the air, as they had done before, and,
  with the usual exclamations, waited our approach. The number was now
  increased by about twenty children, and we went through the usual forms
  of salutation.
     The village soon appeared, consisting of twelve snow huts, erected at
  the bottom of a little bight on the shore, about two miles and a half from
  the ship. They had the appearance of inverted basins, and were placed
  without any order; each of them having a long crooked appendage, in
  which was the passage, at the entrance of which were the women, with
  female children and infants. We were soon invited to visit these, for
  whom we had prepared presents of glass beads and needles; a distribu-
  tion which soon drove away the timidity which they had displayed at our
  first appearance.
                                                          (Ross 1835: 248–​9)

Then comes a long description of snow huts –​the passage, the principal
apartment, the skin-​covered sleeping platform, by the end of which the mis-
tress of the house sat opposite the lamp,

  which, being of moss and oil, as is the universal custom in these regions,
  gave a sufficient flame to supply both light and heat; so that the apartment
  was perfectly comfortable. Over the lamp was the cooking dish of stone,
  containing the flesh of deer and of seals, with oil; and of such provision
  there seemed no want.
                                                         (Ross 1935: 249–​50)

The long quote above shows how, at Ross’ time, the people of the Arctic
were still little known; and, when eventually met, they were described in
terms by which people and their material surroundings were presented as
one whole.
124 Vital Materials

   While the emerging Inuit were not entirely unexpected by Ross, their
technological sophistication allowing them to survive against all odds in this
almost inaccessible and truly trying region, never ceased to cause wonder.
The lamp, keeping the house warm and boiling the meat, is a wondrous piece
of technology made of local materials and known throughout the region.
The almost impossible alignment of ice and fire invariably captured the
newcomers.
   Snow and ice were –​and remains for a little while yet (hopefully) –​local
materials, that not only allowed people to build (and rebuild) their abodes,
but also made fast travel across vast stretches possible. The sea ice was a
highway for sledge-​driving and moving about, and the principal link across
fjords and sounds. It connected both animals and humans with islands of
promise, at least seasonally. If the sea ice faltered, by freezing up too late or
breaking up too early, it would threaten access to vital resources, for both
humans and animals.


Wood and Skin
The early Thule Inuit made rapid advances from West to East along the
northern coast, from c. 1000 to c. 1200 (C E ), not least owed to their
superior transport technologies. The emblematic skin boat, the umiak, used
for whaling and transport in the Western Arctic enabled the Thule Inuit
to move east and further into Northwest Greenland, rather swiftly. Skin
boats were still in evidence when the Fifth Thule Expedition made it to the
Mackenzie Inuit, reminding Rasmussen of the Greenland that he knew so
well (Rasmussen [1927] 1999: 29–​94). Apart from that he was not entirely
happy with the Mackenzie Inuit, their being far too modernized to his taste
and mostly interested in money. While he praised their eagerness to learn, to
read, and write, he was also a little sorrowful to note how the Western Inuit
had changed their ways in most essential respects:

  Skin boats had given place to schooners, sealing to trapping and fur
  trading on modern lines; earth-​and-​stone huts lined with driftwood were
  now replaced by something approaching modern bungalows or villas; and
  in addition to all these external changes, their ancient faith had given
  place to Christianity.
                                             (Rasmussen [1927] 1999: 302)

He did find, however, that “traditions lived on unimpaired in their stories
and legends. I wrote down over a hundred such, and found a surprising
number of old acquaintances among them, both from eastern Canada and
from Greenland,” and he was glad to “have had the opportunity of studying
these people before they had quite given up their ancient life” (ibid.).
   Everywhere in the Far North, people needed access to wood, both for
dog sledges, and for the skin-​covered umiaks by which they travelled on
                                                           Vital Materials 125

sea. It was essential also for the bows that could be used against humans
and animals alike, and “could be deadly at a much greater range than the
spears and harpoons of Dorset hunters” (McGhee 1997: 223). The quick
and well-​equipped Thule Inuit crossed Smith Sound in the Eastern Arctic
and entered Northwest Greenland c. 1200–​1250 C E , while the last Dorset
people soon disappeared, either by dying out or through assimilation. The
ensemble of the Thule technologies was first identified in a midden on the
headland, where the Thule Trading Station was located (hence the name). In
the summer of 2016, the Crockerland Expedition sojourned here, waiting
for further passage, and among the men was the archaeologist George
Comer, who had started digging in the midden. When Rasmussen and his
crew arrived, to start the Second Thule Expedition to North Greenland,
they, too, had to wait, and were soon engaged with the digging at Thule
(Rasmussen 1919: 72–​3). One of the surprise finds was a layer of debris
and artefacts that showed how baleen had played a prominent role in the
making of tools and artefacts, to be forgotten later. The dependence on
whale singled out the ‘Thule Culture’, as it was named, later to be traced
backwards, so to speak, to America, where Mathiassen, the archaeologist
of the Fifth Thule Expedition, excavated a site that showed similar features
(Mathiassen 1927a, b). He also contributed to contemporary ethnographic
description of the material culture of the Iglulik (Mathiassen 1928). Later,
more nuances would be added to the ‘original’ Thule Culture (see Holtved
1944), but we shall leave it here.
   When Stefansson arrived on the northern coast of America on the
Canadian Arctic Expedition, he was concerned about choosing the right men
to organize his travels. He chose a certain Hadley, who had long-​term experi-
ence with living in the Arctic both as trapper and trader, as whaler on board
foreign ships, and with the Inuit in skin boats. Stefansson notes about the
latter:

  This last was an important consideration, for I look upon the Eskimo
  skin boat, as do all those in Alaska who have had experience with it, as
  the one boat suited for use among ice. Such a skin boat, or umiak, when
  thirty feet long, which is a common size, will carry a cargo much larger
  than a 28-​feet whale-​boat, although the whale-​boat is three or four times
  as heavy. And the whale-​boat besides is very fragile. When the ordinary
  clinker-​built whale-​boat is moving at a speed of six miles an hour it is
  easily stove by contact with even a small fragment of floating ice, while
  an Eskimo skin-​boat going at the same speed can bump into ice of almost
  any shape or size without injury. With a whale-​boat it is as if the ice were
  struck by an egg-​shell; with a skin-​boat it is as if it were struck by a foot-
  ball. In one case there is a crash and a dead stop; in the other a thump and
  a rebound. And if the umiak suffers injury, it is merely a cracked rib that
  can be replaced, or a hole in the skin which can be patched with needle
  and thread. An umiak capable of carrying more than a ton of freights can
126 Vital Materials

  be carried over land or solid ice by two men, and if placed on a low sled of
  the type used for such boats it can be pulled along by three or four dogs,
  or two or three men.
                                                        (Stefansson 1921: 37)

These original Thule Inuit vessels were created from whale skin and wood;
of the first there was generally enough, while wood was very unevenly
distributed and mainly available as driftwood at the time. Its scarcity can be
gathered from Peary’s remark about the Inughuit in Northwest Greenland,
whom he supplied with various materials in the late 19th century during his
northward campaigns:

  With our surroundings and bringing up, drawing as we do upon the entire
  world for our daily wants, we can have no conception of the earlier condi-
  tion of this people and their almost inconceivable destitution and restric-
  tion as to materials, dependent for everything upon a few miles of Arctic
  coast-​line. To them such an ordinary thing as a piece of wood was just
  as unattainable as is the moon to the petulant child that cries for it. Is it
  to be wondered at that under these circumstances a man offered me his
  dogs and sledge and all his furs for a bit of board as long as himself; that
  another offered me his wife and two children for a shining knife; and that
  a woman offered me anything she had for a needle?
                                                      (Peary 1898, I: 481–​3)

Wood was an essential, if scarce resource in the Arctic when it came to travel
and transport. The region is mostly featured as always treeless, yet there is a
growing recognition that thousands of years of human habitation may have
left significant marks of the northern landscape, not least the forest-​tundra
regions. This has been shown for the region of the Labrador Inuit, where
major changes in the ecosystem can be documented for the last 200 years,
and where wood seems to have been woven into Inuit imageries (Lemus-​
Lauzon et al. 2012: 114). This reminds us, that the landscape found in the
early years of Arctic ethnography was neither the original nor the last one;
it was what the ethnographers experienced. Stefansson, always an interested
observer when it came to ecology in a broader sense, saw how the northern
coast of Alaska offered vital materials to the Inuit, shaping their history.

  The villages were strung along this coast and were built in locations
  determined by the food-​gathering habits of the people. Between Point
  Barrow and Point Hope, the bowhead whale was of paramount import-
  ance. A village might therefore be located almost anywhere where the ice
  conditions in spring allowed the whales to approach within five miles or
  less from shore in an ordinary year. Next to the whale, the seal was the
  most important item at Point Barrow and even farther south, although the
  walrus increased in importance west of Icy Cape.
                                                          Vital Materials 127

  Everywhere along this coast were strewn huge quantities of driftwood,
  derived probably in the main from the Yukon River, at least on the coast
  section west of Point Barrow. East of Point Barrow, I am inclined to think
  the Mackenzie River is to be credited with the larger amount of driftwood.
                                               (Stefansson [1914b] 1919: 8)

Here whales and wood mingle in the description, leading to further thought
on the vitality of these materials. Stefansson notes how in former times,
villages were not located with reference to driftwood,

  for wood was not used to any extent in winter for fuel, but only seal oil,
  which furnishes a much more satisfactory method of heating houses of the
  Eskimo style than any that could have been devised in the days antedating
  the importation of white men’s stoves.
                                             (Stefansson [1914b] 1919: 8)

When these stoves came in, and when people began to live in ‘flimsy houses’,
driftwood had to be used for fuel, and thus the deposits of driftwood,
gathered by winds and tides for centuries disappeared in a few years (ibid.).
    Wood was indeed a vital material all over the Arctic, if unevenly distributed
and used differently. In the works of the pioneering ethnographers, there are
many indications of the importance of wood scattered through their reports.
It takes a specific interest to take note of it, as there seems nothing much to
fuss about –​save if there is none of it. One small example is provided by
Jenness, who in his diary would write like this: “A cold south-​west wind
blew all day so that it was not very pleasant out-​of doors. Nevertheless, we
turned out and brought in driftwood. Some was cut up for firewood, some
sawn down to make a sleeping platform for Aluk’s family. This occupied us
until dark“ (Jenness 1991: 81).
    Shrubs and woody plants have also been important elements in Inuit
economy, certainly in Labrador, being the only Inuit landscape reaching
south of the treeline and taking advantage of this in various ways. Recent
archaeo-​botanical studies from 18th-​century Inuit sites in northern Labrador
show that woody plants were consumed as food and used as medicines, as
well as tools, beams, and possibly ritualistic practices (Zutter 2012). Further
north it is less obvious, yet generally, wood in some form was needed every-
where for utensils, weaponry, house construction, tent-​poles, sledges, boats,
and kayaks, and knowledge of driftwood locations was as highly regarded as
was knowledge of good fishing grounds (Alix 2005: 84). This goes to stress
that however treeless the Arctic was, it was not without wood, even if its
presence was rather shifty in many regions, as exemplified on King Island,
Alaska (Alix 2012b).
    Driftwood in the Arctic was a trans-​local resource; the driftwood deposits
on the Arctic coastlines originated from circumpolar boreal forest zones,
Siberian or Canadian, effectively limiting the species to just a handful of
128 Vital Materials

conifers. In Greenland the driftwood would predominantly hail from the
Siberian forests –​being closer to the coast of the Arctic Sea than the Canadian
forest systems –​and being transported by the East-​        Greenlandic current
(Hellmann et al. 2013). Different species of trees would have different buoy-
ancy and different lifespans in the water, normally between 10 and 17 months
(ibid.: 7). If quickly encapsulated in ice the wood would keep longer. This
was essential, as wood procurement and woodworking “were inextricably
linked to hunting practices and food processing, thus being integral to the
subsistence cycle of people in the North” (Alix 2012a: 16). It is reported
from some regions in the Arctic how people would sing and dance for the
return of driftwood in the spring as they did for salmon (Fienup-​Riordan
1996: 153). Driftwood knowledge depended upon a degree of predictability,
which again was a function of impressive driftwood cycles.
   In the Coronation Gulf area, visited by the Stefansson-​             Anderson
Expedition, access to driftwood was also a matter of prevailing winds, adding
local weather conditions to continental driftwood cycles.

  Coming east from Cape Lyon we found the driftwood gradually
  diminishing as we went east, but not nearly so fast as we had expected.
  Logs of considerable size clearly derived from the Mackenzie River are
  numerous well beyond the Inman River, but when we come as far east as
  Cape Bexley, mostly broken sticks are found and it would be difficult to
  get together on a small stretch of beach large logs of a considerable size
  such as would be needed for instance, for the construction of a log house.
  The beaches, however, are mostly composed of broken rock and for that
  reason all the wood there is dry and has been preserved from rotting.
  There is no doubt that if the Eskimo of this country are ever taught the
  use of sheet iron stoves as the Western Eskimo have been, the supplies of
  wood which now are sufficient so that a traveling party can camp almost
  anywhere and find enough for fuel, will disappear in a few years, and
  it will be found then that a replenishing will be a slow process for the
  piles of driftwood that make such a brave showing now have clearly been
  gathered here for centuries.
                                           (Stefansson [1914b] 1919: 42–​3)

Quite apart from testifying to the importance of driftwood, Stefansson’s
point about the longevity of drift logs in this dry climate, compared to other
coastal areas, is important. He also implicitly points to the impossibility of
‘placing’ driftwood, save in the analytical mode of pointing to its importance
in ‘barren tracts’.
   The driftwood that had reached the early Thule Inuit in Northwest
Greenland (arriving between 1200 and 1250 C E and remaining there) –​
became inaccessible with the Little Ice Age, when ice prevented the sea currents
from the south to deliver (Hastrup 2016). After two or three hundred years,
old technologies and ‘wood-​knowledge’ had receded, as noted by an old
                                                           Vital Materials 129

man, having crossed over from Baffin Land in the 1860s. He told Rasmussen
how the locals “had only hunted on the ice, and had been obliged during
the spring to catch as many seals, walruses, and narwhals as they would
want for the summer, when the ice had gone” (Merqussaq, in Rasmussen
1908: 32). Thanks to him and other Baffin Landers and to a more stable
provision of wood (also from foreign vessels, now able to reach the Kane
Basin) forgotten technological skills could be restored. Yet, Marcel Mauss
still blamed Inughuit poverty in 1906 on the lack of driftwood: “The bow,
the kayak, the umiak and most of the sleds disappeared because of a lack of
wood. These unfortunate Eskimo were reduced to such circumstances that
they retained merely the memory of their former technology” (Mauss [1906]
1979: 42–​3)
    The small contingent of Baffin Landers arriving in Northwest Greenland
in the early 1860s contributed vitally to a boosting of Inughuit life. At their
arrival, the population was down to some 140 persons, according to Elisha
Kent Kane who spent some years in the region in 1853–​1855 (see Kane 1856,
II: 211). The Baffin Landers taught the Inughuit to use the available wood
for bows and arrows, fishing spears, and not least kayaks. As an old man
proudly told Rasmussen: “we taught them to build kayaks, and to hunt from
kayaks” (Merqussaq in Rasmussen 1908: 32).
    Kayaks were certainly important, in other Arctic regions as well. Amundsen
gives us a vivid picture of the care that would be invested in the making of a
kayak among the Inuit near the Magnetic North pole, where he wintered in
1903–​1904:

  While Errera is repairing his fishing spear, Nalungia is making preparations
  for getting Atikleura’s “kayak” ready. This is now lying outside the tent
  on two small piles of stone, sufficiently high to prevent the dogs from
  getting at it. The woodwork is nearly finished, and all that is required is
  some final lashing and gluing. It has been a work of patience to construct
  the kayak, as it consists throughout of quite small pieces of wood lashed
  together. Reindeer sinews are used for lashing; they are excellent when
  dry, but when they get wet they stretch and the lashing slackens. It is only
  where a superior mode of joining is required that glue is applied. This is
  made from reindeer blood by a very peculiar process. Having filled a small
  bag with blood, the Eskimo takes it in his mouth and gently sucks at it
  for a long time. By this treatment the blood coagulates and forms a thick
  liquid, which is equal to our best glue.
     Atikleura is now anxious to have his boat finished and in full working
  trim by the time the ice breaks up on the river, as the reindeer have already
  begun to arrive in herds, and the great summer hunt will soon begin. A
  little fresh reindeer flesh will be very acceptable; it is so long since they had
  the last. Besides, the housewife’s stock of thread is nearly exhausted; there
  will be none left when she has finished covering the kayak; and seal sinews
  are no good. It is quite a pleasure to watch her, as she is now hurrying to
130 Vital Materials

  finish. The single thread she has is too thin for fastening the kayak cover
  on, so she is busy plaiting a stouter one. Her little hands –​indeed it is rare
  to see finer and more shapely hands and feet that those of the Eskimo
  women –​work so fast that the eye cannot follow the movements of her
  fingers.
                                                   (Amundsen 1908, I: 305–​6)

Amundsen’s observations are related to the making of a kayak, but also
transcend this, by telling a comprehensive story of depending on animal
materials, as well as wood, and not least of human ingenuity –​requiring a
degree of patience that sometimes runs counter to the wish to act. The Inuit
depended on a multiplicity of local resources, and, as Jenness noted, they
need to be treated with utmost care:

  During our march south, Ikpuck and The Runner visited the coast to
  search for driftwood, returning with a fine plank that had probably been
  washed overboard from some vessel. So unusual a prize led to consider-
  able discussion. At first they proposed to convert it into two sled-​runners,
  since it required less shaping than the rude logs they generally trimmed
  down for that purpose; but as both their sleds were in good condition they
  finally agreed to make fish-​spears, and Ikpuck split the plank carefully
  from end to end by means of small wedges of antler driven home with a
  heavy stone. He then split each section, obtaining four stout poles which
  he planed and smoothed with his hunting-​knife. The care he expended on
  the task, and his manifest joy at its successful completion, made me realize
  the handicap under which these people laboured through the scarcity of
  wood in their country.
                                                    (Jenness [1928] 1959: 170)

Before we leave the place of wood in Inuit ways of life it bears remembering
that it was always a seasonal commodity. From the Copper Inuit Stefansson
observes that it

  makes little difference whether driftwood is abundant or scarce in any dis-
  trict, it is never used during the part of the year when people live in snow
  houses. It would manifestly be unsuited for the heating of a snowhouse
  and as a matter of fact, as I know from experience, the seal oil lamp is
  better suited for the heating of any kind of a substantially built Eskimo
  house than wood is, even when burnt in sheet iron stoves.
                                                 (Stefansson [1914b] 1919: 45)

It is a distinctly contemporary observation, but as wood came mostly in
the shape of driftwood, picked up for free, one would never see much of it
during winter. When and where proper trading stations were established on
the coast, access to timber became a different matter; it became a commodity
                                                         Vital Materials 131

that could be acquired for money. This facilitated the transition to wooden
houses of which we heard above. Meanwhile the skin boats were likewise on
the wane, as were the whales along the Arctic coast.


Stone and Bone
Stone and bone were no less important than wood and skin. This struck some
of the newcomers very forcefully, for instance Roald Amundsen, wintering
among the Netsilik Inuit during his long Gjöa expedition through the North
West Passage (1903–​1907). In his general description of the coastal Inuit,
he observed that apart from their being very enterprising, nothing suggested
that they had intercourse with the outer world, except for a few iron bars
and knives they had obtained from Inuit groups dwelling further south. He
goes on:

  We were suddenly brought face to face here with a people from the Stone
  Age: we were abruptly carried back several thousand years in the advance
  of human progress, to people who as yet knew no other method of pro-
  curing fire than by rubbing two pieces of wood together, and who with
  great difficulty managed to get their food just lukewarm, over the seal-​oil
  flame, on a stone slab, while we cooked our food in a moment with our
  modern cooking apparatus. We came here, with our most ingenious and
  most recent inventions in the way of firearms, to people who still used
  lances, bows and arrows of reindeer horn. Their fishing implements were
  long spears, fashioned out of reindeer horn; hour after hour they had to
  stand, heedless of fatigue, and waiting their chance to spear each single
  fish as it came along, while we put out our net and caught as many as we
  liked. However, we should be wrong, if from the weapons, implements,
  and domestic appliances of these people, we were to argue that they were
  of low intelligence. Their implements apparently so very primitive, proved
  to be as well adapted to their existing requirements and conditions as
  experience and the skilful tests of many centuries could have made them.
                                                 (Amundsen 1908, I: 296–​7)

The readiness by which foreigners classified the Inuit as stone-​age people is
remarkable, not least given their observations of a plethora of other materials.
Yet, stone was everywhere, and it was readily used, also for the making of
houses in the Arctic, past and present. Jenness provides a fine image of how
dwellings reflected the hunting landscape in a wider sense. Travelling around
Banks Island to possibly locate some Inuit dwellings or traces, he found sev-
eral sites with tent-​rings and muskox bones, but no living settlements. There
was one peculiar settlement, however, that sent him thinking.

  At the head of the sand spit which forms Cape Kellett, is a large deserted
  village of thirty or more houses. They seem to have been built of sod
132 Vital Materials

  around a frame work of whale bones. Some of the houses are now crum-
  bling away owing to the inroad of the sea, but the majority of them are
  still intact, but very much overgrown with moss and tundra. The earth
  nearby is very much stained with whale or seal oil, and in the heat of
  summer there is still an odor of decaying animal matter to be noticed in
  the vicinity. In winter this attracts a number of foxes that dig in the snow
  and throw up yellow stained turf, but one cannot see that they get any-
  thing to eat for their pains.
                                                            (Jenness 1922: 46)

Jenness further notes that while this was the sole village of the kind that they
found on Banks Island, they could “scarcely go in any direction without
seeing a tent ring or some chips of drift wood, showing that at some time or
other it had been a hunting ground of some human race” (ibid.). The tent-​
rings suggested Copper Inuit to him, having seen many such places before
on Victoria Island and on the mainland around Coronation Gulf; these were
mainly caribou hunters –​even if also with some sealing and fishing. On the
other hand, the ancient whale-​village indicated a people who would have had
umiaks and probably had been travelling from the mainland and settled for
some time at the island, without necessarily meeting any other groups at the
time. As whalers they were distinctly Western Arctic Inuit.
   Jenness noted how materials to construct a dwelling are limited, as there is
no timber anywhere and little sod, and this applies to the entire region. While
challenging, it did make wonderful engineers out of the locals. In some areas
there seems to be neither wood nor stones of note, in others just a trickle of
wood and plenty of stone. The limitations are manifest, yet although there is
no timber, neither in the shape of standing trees or driftwood, there are traces
of huts of wood and sod (Jenness 1922: 56). Remnants of old stone houses
have also been found, yet at Jenness’ time, the only

  types of dwellings known to the Copper Eskimos are the snow-​hut and the
  tent. Sometimes a traveller finds them with neither, camping instead under
  the open sky; only rarely, however, for it is seldom that a man can sleep
  with comfort out of doors in the region.
                                                                   (Ibid.: 58)

We note, how history and prehistory merge in Jenness’ observations.
   Tents were a widespread dwelling technology across the Inuit landscape
in the Arctic –​and across centuries. While depending on a measure of wood
for poles, and stones for keeping the tent grounded, the main material was
animal skin. In his report from his sojourn in the Eastern Arctic in 1865,
Charles Hall noted how among all the things that he experienced, “not the
least interesting to me was the native habits and customs as displayed in their
beautiful villages. I was never tired viewing them, and at every opportunity
was on shore among the tupics –​summer tents” (Hall 1865: 188). Again,
                                                       Vital Materials 133

there is a sense of wonder that such simple living quarters are not only per-
fectly liveable but also beautiful.
   Stone was a vital resource, offering itself up as a solid building material
as we have already seen. It was also a vital element in the making of meat
caches that have served as necessary deposits of meat for later use, at least
in the Eastern Arctic. More surprisingly, perhaps, stone has also been used
directly in the hunt. Before guns became a commonplace, small terrestrial
mammals were often caught in snares or traps, made of available local
materials. There are remains of both to be seen in the landscape –​at least
in Northwest Greenland, from where the following description from the
1930s stem. So-​called hare fences consisted of stones of equal height, about
20–​30 centimetres, placed at regular intervals and often stretching far into
the terrain, between which strings were stretched (Holtved 1967: 111). The
method had the advantage of keeping the skin intact; another positive feature
was that the only technology of movement needed was walking, making it
possible even for children to take responsibility. In my experience, present-​
day seniors in the region are only too happy to share their memories of
gaining such skill when they were children, and thus being able to contribute
to the welfare of their family.
   They are also keen to talk about the fox, which in the Thule Station
period introduced a new kind of economy. Hunting fox for trading the skins
could lead to emerging inequalities between people. Some very successful
foxhunters would suddenly become rich; where earlier on, sharing the hunt
had been tacit in the social economy, sharing money gained on foxhunting
was not practiced. On fox hunting Erik Holtved notes:

  Before steel traps became common after the Establishment of the Thule
  station most foxes were caught in stone traps (putLatit) of the usual
  Eskimo construction, consisting chiefly in a narrow stone chamber, open
  only in front where some stones (tooruxxaa) were placed, which guided
  and held the flat stone (miligaq) which formed the trap door. This was kept
  in position by a string (qimmiaq) carried down to a stick (pisiun) which
  was fastened to the back wall of the trap where also the bait was stuck in
  in such a way that the string was released when the fox snatched the bait.
                                                        (Holtved 1967: 108)

Something akin to the hare fences of the Inughuit, mentioned above, is found
in the inuksuit, human-​like stone monuments made for caribou hunting, as
described by Stefansson from Victoria Island –​a rocky island with relatively
sparse vegetation –​where caribou was hunted. Stefansson noted numerous
inuksuit from earlier seasons, showing that both people and animals had
been numerous in previous years. He continued:

  Inuksuit, or “likenesses of men” are used in caribou hunting by most or all
  Eskimos who hunt with bow and arrow. When a band of caribou is seen
134 Vital Materials

  grazing quietly a council of all present is held and an ambush determined
  upon towards which the caribou shall be driven. This ambush is made at
  the angle of two long lines of monuments, the monuments being set up
  from fifty to a hundred and fifty yards apart, according to the character
  of the topography. In rocky country these are made by putting two or
  three stones one on top of the other to a height of one or two feet. If the
  heard is large and the drive is looked upon as important the two lines of
  monuments may be run out each a distance of five or six or even ten miles,
  although lines of two or three miles are more common. The angle between
  them may be anything from fifteen to forty-​five degrees.
     At intervals of perhaps half a mile men, women, or even children of
  six or seven years are stationed and there must be at least one person
  at each extreme of the lines. The hunters with bows and arrows lie in
  ambush at the angle of the “V” while the rest of the men and women
  form a crescent curve beyond the caribou so as to drive them towards
  the ambush. In my experience the driving is started by the men giving
  long howls in imitation of wolves. This generally makes the caribou
  restless and starts them moving slowly and uncertainly away from the
  direction from which the howls come. Sometimes, instead of the imita-
  tion wolf-​howling, dogs which are held in leash are induced to bark. It
  may happen either deliberately or through accident that the caribou get
  the wind of these drivers, which usually has the same effect of starting
  them to leeward. The drivers gradually close in and the caribou enter the
  V-​shaped area.
                                                    (Stefansson 1921: 401–​2)

The caribou soon detect the people standing in the line of the monuments,
after which they apparently take all the inuksuit for men, a fact upon which
Stefansson muses, thinking it “absurd that two stones, one on top of the
other, reaching an elevation of only a foot, should be feared as much by
the caribou as actual persons but that appears to be a fact” (Stefansson
1921: 402).
   Beyond (or between) everyday life and seasonal migration of families and
larger groups in pursuit of game, there was also some trade of stone lamps
between regions, as Stefansson observed, even if it had already dwindled
due to access to more ‘modern’ materials in the Western region. “The Cape
Bathurst people still definitely remember that pots and lamps were the chief
objects of the trips across from the mainland at Parry to Banks Island”
(Stefansson 1914a: 18). Generally, Stefansson had a lot to report on the trade
of wood and stoneware among different groups along the Arctic coast, as
remembered or executed, and recounted the trading items all the way to
Siberia, and down the Dease River. He suggested that “the people who fre-
quent Great Bear Lake are not so much manufacturers of wooden ware as
the gatherers and distributors of wood” (ibid.: 28). His observations on the
                                                         Vital Materials 135

trade of lamps and pots among people for whom it was a proper livelihood
are interesting. To make a large pot, he says, takes all a man’s spare time for
a year, if not two years, and he continues:

  Certain individuals are considered expert pot makers, and many others
  attain old age without ever having made a large pot, though all have owned
  one or more. A man who spends the summer making a pot must live that
  summer on fish and must, therefore, to clothe himself and his family, buy
  caribou for the winter from those who have been at the caribou grounds
  while he was stonecutting. No man of these tribes probably ever devoted
  even half the summer of his active life to stonework, yet we have here the
  beginning of division of labour, the germ of a “trade.” These pot and lamp
  makers furnish the best example known to me both of specialization of
  industries by tribes and of the division of labour among individuals.
                                                      (Stefansson 1914a: 28)

Stone and bone were very important materials in the life of the Inuit; they
had different roles to play, individually and together. They were, indeed, vital
materials, of which we shall now introduce another set –​remembering that
they are all part of the larger assemblage of things that made human life pos-
sible in the Arctic.


Iron and Copper
For a long time, it was generally assumed that until European contact, the
natives of Arctic America had no idea of metals. This is not the case; there
were metals in their region, and they were known and traded. But let us start
with the assumption of ignorance, and a spectacular find of iron.
   One of the things that had astonished John Ross in 1818 when he first
met the Inughuit in Northwest Greenland, was that these isolated, and so-​
far unknown and apparently uncivilized people were in possession of iron;
they had rude knives and harpoon points with cutting edges of iron (Ross
1819: 95). At first, he believed that it was owed to barrel hoops or flattened
nails from foreign ships that had somehow been washed ashore with empty
barrels. Yet, the locals insisted that they had obtained the metal from a so-​
named Iron Mountain on the northern shores of Melville Bay. At closer
inspection and having been given some specimens that he could bring home
for chemical testing, Ross could ascertain that the iron was of meteoric
origin. He was very pleased by his discovery –​even if he had not been able to
get to the Iron Mountain himself because of the pack ice.
   Robert Peary, who was to spend many years in the region in the period
from 1891 to 1909 (on and off) –​in pursuit of his dream to reach the North
Pole –​had read Ross and was intrigued by the presence of iron. In 1894, he
sat out to solve the riddle of the Iron Mountain. He writes:
136 Vital Materials

  After I had gained the confidence of the entire little tribe of Smith Sound
  Eskimos, Tellikotinah, one of the hunters, in May of 1894, guided me to
  the “Iron Mountain,” where I found not a mountain or a vein of iron, but
  three large masses of homogenous metal, the peculiar and unmistakable
  characteristics of which, and especially the nature of the surroundings,
  proved them to be, beyond the possibility of doubt, true meteoric irons.
                                                          (Peary 1898 II: 556)

   Peary immediately wanted to take these huge lumps of iron back to
New York but he did not succeed until the following year. His was a modern
quest for enabling scientific investigation of the metal, and for securing it for
the Museum of Natural History, or rather for selling it to finance his dream
to reach the North Pole. Given his close acquaintance with the people among
whom he had dwelled for some years, he learned about their view of the
iron. According to the Inughuit, the irons had been there since times imme-
morial, having come directly out of the heaven; they had been given names
like Woman and Dog. Once, when some people from the northern part of
the region had attempted to take a larger piece with them on the dog-​sledge –​
to avoid another long journey in a near future –​they, their sledges, dogs
and iron had disappeared through the ice, after which nobody would ever
attempt to move large quantities again. This Peary was told, but he did not
allow himself to be affected by the story; he could not leave the irons alone,
and intimated that the natives did not need them anymore.

  For several generations, probably from the time of the wintering of the
  North Star or possibly earlier, no use has been made of the iron of these
  meteorites by the natives; they obtaining their scant supply of knives from
  the whalers and expedition ships visiting their coast or beset in the ice off
  Cape York.
                                                         (Peary 1898 II: 561)

The North Star had wintered in the period 1849–​1859, being stuck in the
ice. It was a long way back, yet it would be wrong to believe that the stones
were forgotten; resources rarely were, even when other possibilities turned
up. The ironstones held a deeply rooted vitality, apparently also for Peary,
setting out to sequester them. It was a heavy job, bordering on the foolhardy,
and the help from the Inughuit was essential. At certain points, there were
near misses and loss of control with the matter, and Peary spoke about the
demon of the stone –​indicating that he had not completely overheard the
tale he had been told. When finally leaving Meteorite Island with the celes-
tial prisoner on board, Peary acknowledged the immense efforts of the local
labour. “Six hours later we were at Cape York, where I sent my faithful
Eskimos at shore, accompanied by several barrels of biscuit, and loaded with
guns, knives, ammunition, and numerous other articles, which I had brought
to reward them for their faithful service” (Peary 1898 II: 591).
                                                         Vital Materials 137

   The iron was seen as up for taking, as it was no longer needed locally –​
given the presence of Europeans and Americans in the region, ready to supply
more modern versions of metal. This was not untrue, yet the lightness by
which local resources were appropriated by newcomers bears noticing. Some
made up for it in their own way; Peary did supply the Inughuit with much
cherished materials from elsewhere, and implicitly contributed to establishing
a new set of long-​term material cravings. In turn, this paved the way for
the Thule Trading Station, established in 1910. New networks and trade
partnerships gradually replaced the old ones. Newcomers, generally, found
ample excuse for their inroads into the Arctic lands by pointing to the local
need for everything they chose to bring. Whatever we might think today
about the colonial impetus behind the foreign visits, there is little reason to
believe that the newcomers were unwelcome.
   Let us look at John Ross, again, who, together with his nephew James
C. Ross, made advances into the American continent during their continued
search for the Northwest Passage in several consecutive years in the early
1830s, as we have heard before. Once, having anchored on the coast, they
saw some natives –​who were well dressed in deerskin. They were soon
discovered to have knives with iron blades or points, and Ross sees this a
proof of communication between this tribe and Europeans. Even though the
encounter is described in the conventional style of a ‘first’ encounter, the two
groups knew sufficiently about each other to establish a common ground –​
thanks partly to James Ross’ knowledge of the Inuit language. The natives
returned with the Englishmen to the ships. John Ross observes:

  Having no foresight of these visitors, we had of course no presents at
  hand for them, and we therefore sent a man back to the ship for thirty-​
  one pieces of iron-​hoop, that there might be a gift for each individual.
  But, in the mean time, they consented to accompany us on board, and we
  soon arrived at our snow wall. At this they expressed no surprise; it was,
  indeed, too much like their own work to excite any; nor did they show
  any of those marks of astonishment, at either the ship itself or the quantity
  of wood and iron before them, which we had found among the northern
  savages of Baffin’s bay in 1818. It was evident that they were no strangers
  to even an abundance of these materials.
    The present of the iron excited universal delight. In return they offered
  us their spears and knives, which to their equal satisfaction, we refused.
  We could now easily see that their appearance was very superior to our
  own; being at least as well clothed, and far better fed; with plump cheeks,
  of as rosy a colour as they could be under so dark a skin.
                                                            (Ross 1835: 245)

Well before contact with the Old World, the Inuit had traded materials with
each other, be it stone-​pots or metalware. The archaeological excavations
during the Fifth Thule Expedition would enable a more precise mapping
138 Vital Materials

of early Inuit cultures than had previously been possible. It was one of the
principal tasks of the expedition to investigate the history and development
of the Inuit people until the present day. Therkel Mathiassen was the first
to identify traces of the ancient Thule Culture at Naujan at Repulse Bay (cf.
Chapter 2) –​‘traces’ being material here. Rasmussen relates:

  The Eskimos of the present day in these regions use only snow huts in
  winter; it was the more surprising therefore to come upon remains of a
  quite different type of house. We found at Naujan a whole little township
  of these houses, constructed of stone, turf, and the bones of whales. They
  were built so as to be partly underground and must have been far more
  substantial and warm, though less hygienic perhaps, than the light, cool,
  healthy snow huts of today …
     The houses themselves had fallen to pieces long since, and the remains
  were scattered, weatherworn and overgrown with grass and moss to
  such an extent that our excavations gave but a poor idea of their ori-
  ginal appearance. The implements and objects found among the ruins,
  however, gave an excellent view of the culture of the period from which
  they were derived. The materials comprised bone, walrus tusk and caribou
  antler, flint, slate and soapstone, whalebone, some wood, and occasionally
  metal, this last in the form of cold hammered copper (probably obtained
  by barter from the Inuit of the west), with a single fragment of meteoric
  iron forming the point of a harpoon.
     It is of course impossible to mention more than a few of the finds here;
  often, too, the most insignificant objects to all outward seeming prove
  the most important from the scientific point of view. Among our most
  valuable finds, for instance, were three odd broken fragments of rough
  earthenware vessels. These are only known to exist among the Alaskan
  Eskimos, and the finding of them here was of importance; few, however,
  would have attached any value to those three dirty scraps of pottery.
                                            (Rasmussen [1927] 1999: 113–​4)

What emerges in Rasmussen’s light-​handed description is an early testi-
mony to the extensive trade networks of the Thule Inuit, challenging any
view of distinct tribes –​even if periodical isolation would have occurred
due to weather and wind, famine, or death. For the newcomers, including
the early ethnographers of the Inuit, ‘origins’ were always a major issue.
Jenness wrote:

  There is a peculiar attraction about a people which is in the transition
  stage between the stone and the iron ages. In such a people we seem to
  be studying not so much the development of an outward civilization as
  the mental processes of man before the dawn of history. For this reason,
  as well as for the intrinsic beauty of its relics, the bronze age of Europe
  has always attracted a host of enthusiastic investigators. America, too,
                                                           Vital Materials 139

   has had its transitional period, in some places a true metal age marked
   by the smelting of copper and of tin, in others a pseudo-​metal period,
   copper being treated simply as a malleable stone. The Eskimos inhabiting
   the regions around Coronation Gulf were in the pseudo-​           metal stage.
   Pure native copper is found there in three places; at the head of prince
   Albert Sound on Victoria Island, in the Copper Mountains west of the
   Coppermine River near its mouth, and on the islands in Bathurst Inlet.
   Copper obtained from all of these sources was hammered into tools and
   weapons by the Eskimos, but most of their supply seems to have come
   from the Copper Mountains. Among the Eskimos of northern Greenland
   there were also a pseudo-​metal period marked by the use of meteoric (and
   possibly telluric) iron instead of copper; but this use of iron never extended
   outside of Greenland and so does not come within the scope of this paper,
   which is concerned only with the Central Eskimos and their copper.
                                                             (Jenness 1923: 540)

Having reviewed the evidence of copper, Jenness concluded that a full copper
culture –​a culture where copper replaces stone in all cutting implements –​is
found only in the Coronation Gulf region, where there were large deposits
of accessible native copper; the Inuit on the shores of Hudson Bay also knew
of copper and obtained it from Coronation Gulf; the lack of further spread
westwards indicates that the use of copper was relatively recent.
   A report on hostilities between Native Americans and Inuit at Bloody Fall
(near the Coppermine) in 1771, shows that the weapons in Inuit possession
did not vary much from what was found in the early 20th century, when
Jenness was there, except in one respect. This one exception is important, as
Jenness quotes from the report by Samuel Hearne (1797) whom we have met
before (Chapter 4): “their arrows are either shod with a triangular piece of
black stone, like slate, or a piece of copper; but most commonly the former”
(in Jenness 1923: 547). In Jenness’ day, there were no longer any slate arrows
in use, and:

   It would seem therefore that the Copper Eskimos has passed out of the
   Stone Age not so very long before Hearne’s day, since they still retained
   the use of slate in at least one of their weapons, and that a very important
   one.
                                                                    (Ibid.: 548)

   Once again, we meet the implicit yardstick for progress in the European
classification scheme. What is no less interesting is the reference to a malevo-
lent Indian–​Eskimo encounter, which seems to have been a rare occurrence –​
although it is difficult to assess precisely how rare it would have been through
the ages. In any case, it seems that a kind of trade network was (re-​) established
when Stefansson brought together Copper Inuit with Indians of Great Bear
Lake in 1910.
140 Vital Materials

   During his long travels, Stefansson became very knowledgeable of the
trade network in the areas between north-​western Alaska and Coronation
Gulf (Stefansson 1914a). Among the chief items of this trade were soap-
stone lamps and pots (see also Morrison 1991: 239). In complement
to the network in the eastern Arctic, through which even meteoric iron
from Cape York in Greenland was distributed in some small quantity, the
western Arctic was connected through soapstone –​with copper in between
the two.

  The 19th century soapstone trade as described by Stefansson and others
  can be seen as one of the outer ripples in a series of concentric trading
  circles. At the hub was Bering Strait and the Russian-​Siberian fur trade
  …As early as 1742, Steller, of the Bering Expedition, described a well-​
  organized trade across Bering Strait, already several generations old and
  mainly in the hands of the Chukchi, who were trading Alaskan furs for
  Russian metal goods (Golder 1968: 98–​99). By the late 1780s, some of
  this material was reaching the Mackenzie Delta area, along with strange
  white men in large canoes.
                                                     (Morrison 1991: 242)

It is important to bear the Siberian connection in mind, as the Bering Strait
trade was to increase further by the peace treaty between the Russians and
the Chukchi in 1788, leading to the establishment of the Anyui fort and trade
fair on the Kolyma River the following year.

  By this establishment, Anyui greatly increased the volume of Russian
  goods entering the New World through the offices of both Chukchi and
  Alaskan Inupiat middlemen. Russian trade goods now included tobacco
  as well as the stable beads and metal goods (knives, spear points, etc.),
  which were traded along with native Siberian articles such as spotted rein-
  deer skins. In return, the Asian shore received local Alaskan products such
  as oil, ivory and carved wooden implements, but it was above all, the furs
  in which the Russians were interested.
     The Copper Inuit soapstone trade must be seen in the context of this
  Bering Strait–​Alaskan trade network, since it was Russian iron that the
  Copper Inuit received for their goods. Moreover, their soapstone vessels
  were not merely useful domestic utensils, but valuable exchange items
  within the Alaskan network, and even across the Bering Strait.
                                                        (Morrison 1991: 242)

In this connection it is worth recalling Stefansson’s observation, that while
the Alaskan people were able to make their own stone lamps,

  they were considered poor compared with the “lamps from the east.”
  The eastern lamps were supposed to “save oil” –​apparently in a (to our
                                                         Vital Materials 141

  minds) miraculous way. It was said that though a home-​made lamp were
  a duplicate in shape or size of the imported article, it would use twice as
  much oil and give no more light or heat.
                                                      (Stefansson 1914a: 27)

Stefansson found that the “pot and lamp makers furnish the best example
known to me both of specialization of industries and by tribes and of the
division of labour among individuals” (ibid.: 28). The lamps were important
to the many and may have been long distance, but trading materials was a
widespread practice among people. Jenness offers an important observation
of this.

  There is always much trade, of course, between neighbouring groups, and
  even between members of the same group. In the spring of 1915 a woman
  in Dolphin and Union strait went round the whole settlement trying to
  buy a tent-​pole. Brisk barter took place when the west Coronation gulf
  Eskimos visited this settlement in March. One woman bought musk-​ox
  skins with some cartridges she had received from the expedition, another
  sold a large wooden table. Dolphin and Union strait natives purchase
  stone lamps and pots, muskox ladles, blubber pounders, sled-​toggles, and
  skins both of deer and of muskoxen from the Eskimos of Coronation Gulf,
  besides of course copper and implements made of it. As their own region,
  however, furnishes them with few commodities for bartering, many, per-
  haps the majority, of these strait natives at some time in their lives make
  special journeys eastward, in order to collect their own copper and wood
  in the valleys of the Coppermine and Dease rivers, and to hew out their
  own lamps and pots near Tree river.
                                                          (Jenness 1922: 53)

In short, long-​distance collecting, trading, and bartering of raw materials
was an integral part of life and dreams in the Arctic, where people had to
look in many directions, not only to catch the moment of a promising hunt,
but also to get access to raw materials from other places –​whether iron,
copper, soap stone, or skin.
   Probably, the meteoric iron trade that was definitively put to a stop by
Robert Peary, was no longer sustainable, and truly, there were other and
easier ways of getting metal for knives and harpoon heads, given the many
new forms of contact and trade relations; but to remove a local resource is
to make the field of potentiality shrink. The agency of materials in the region
was misinterpreted by the newcomers, who never understood the place as
co-​constituted by matters of different temporalities and spatial connections,
which might have been reinvigorated –​in the manner of the incoming Baffin
Landers in Northwest Greenland in the 1860s. Instead, the iron was seen as
an entity to be removed, rather than as a vital force; the meteorite became
purely matter, rather than energy. When Hearne sat out to find a copper mine
142 Vital Materials

for the Hudson’s Bay Company (cf. Chapter 4), he did see copper utensils
and found the Inuit using copper in knives and arrowheads, but they hardly
knew where it came from. The “mine, if it deserves this appellation, is no
more than an entire jumble of rocks and gravel, which has been rent many
ways by an earthquake” (Hearne 1797: 194). He returned, while copper
changed hands, and the New World became older.


How Things Matter
To conclude this chapter, we note that people in Arctic America have used
almost all available materials for some purpose or other: stone and turf for
winter houses, skin and wooden poles for summer tents, and stones to keep
them in place. They also used animal skins and intestines for clothing, har-
poon lines, dogs’ whips, and string; wood was used for weapons, means
of transport, etc. Bone material was used for harpoon heads, for needles,
and amulets. Soapstone was used for lamps, working on melting blubber
and moss, serving as wick. Meteor iron was used for knives, and for arrow
points –​mainly in the eastern Arctic, though. We have not dwelled with all of
it, and whatever one singles out, it is deceptive not only by the alignment of
different things but also by omitting their individual historicity. Each ‘thing’
can be further unpacked, and put in perspective, showing the vibrancy of
matter (Bennett 2010: 20).
    Materials have great impact in volatile socialities. Bennett’s vision is to
acknowledge the non-​human multitudes as something more than context,
constraint, or tool –​and seeing them instead as participants in the ecology
of which humans are also part. This seems substantiated when we close in
on some of the essential materials in Inuit life as presented above, where we
have seen how certain materials made both tribes and trade happen, as it
were. They also made up the larger constituency of Inuit, always connected
through trade routes and moving materials (Stefansson 1914a: 3ff). Things
are agents in social life.
    Some early 20th-​century people living on the fringe of new trading com-
munities in the North were still almost self-​sufficient at the time of the Fifth
Thule Expedition. This seems true for the Arviligjuarmiut, whom Rasmussen
met, and who proudly said that they did not know of any famine because
they had such a variety of game at their disposal throughout the year. They
seem to have everything they needed, as we shall see in Rasmussen’s tele-
scopic view on their vital materials:

  The Arviligjuarmiut, whose country lies right off the routes followed by
  white men through these regions, have from the first learned to rely on
  such material as their own territory afforded for the making of weapons
  and implements generally. Knives are made from a kind of yellowish flint,
  brought from a considerable distance, in the neighborhood of Back’s
  River. Fire was obtained from “Ingnerit,” i.e., firestone, iron pyrites,
                                                             Vital Materials 143

   found near the sea west of Lord Mayor’s Bay. Sparks were struck so as
   to fall on specially prepared tinder made from moss soaked in blubber.
   Soapstone for lamps and cooking pots was procured from the interior
   south of Pelly Bay.
                                             (Rasmussen [1927] 1999: 168)

Rasmussen noted the scarcity of wood, but also how the Inuit knew to cir-
cumvent it. Tent poles were turned into sledge runners, and harpoon shafts
were made of horn, the pieces being straightened out laboriously in warm
water and joined length to length. Tent poles were fashioned in the same
way, only one being used for each tent. Owing to the scarcity of iron and
flint, harpoons were made from the shinbone of the bear. In the spring, “when
warmer weather set in and the sledges thawed and fell to pieces, the tent skin
runners did final service as food for the dogs, and the meat “stuffing” as food
for their masters” (Rasmussen [1927] 1999: 168–​9). What we have here are
snippets of a ‘biography of things’, showing their importance in social life
and in the larger historical development (Kopytoff 1990). ‘Thinking through
things’ (Henare et al. 2007, was not an outside modality of understanding, it
was a precondition for life.
    This view is as good as it gets in terms of a portrait of vital materials that
connect humans intimately with their landscapes and animal companions;
we are bound to acknowledge their agentive power –​without disregarding
the human ingenuity that it takes to act upon it. Things matter deeply to
human lives. In fact, the two can hardly be separated.
