6	Human Terrains
      A Mutable Landscape




One of the earliest ethnographic reports from the Arctic is The Central
Eskimo by Franz Boas (1888), noting how a lively intercourse would spring
up between the settlements once the ice has consolidated in winter; friends
would go visiting and undertake trading expeditions by sledge. The visits
were often short, but not always.

  Longer journeys are postponed until spring, when food can be procured
  more easily. These journeys are planned a long time before they are made.
  While the families generally leave what they can spare of their household
  goods in winter at their summer settlement, they bring away everything
  they possess to the winter village if they intend to visit a neighboring tribe
  in the spring. In April or May they leave their snow houses; the tent poles
  and the whole of their goods are loaded upon the sledge, only the boats
  being left behind in charge of some friend, and then they start upon their
  long, lonely journey. On the first day they do not travel far, but make the
  first halt after about a twelve mile journey. As the load is heavy the men
  and women sit on the top of the sledges only to rest. The driver walks
  alongside and the women lead the way, the dogs pulling more willingly if
  they see somebody ahead of the sledge. At night it is not unloaded, only
  those things being taken out which are necessary for building a small tent
  and cooking. [… ...] After having traveled about three days a longer halt
  is made; the sledge is unloaded, the dogs are unharnessed, and the men go
  out hunting in order to procure food for the dogs and for themselves. Thus
  they slowly proceed until they at last reach the end of their journey. Here
  they settle down with the friends they have come to visit, establish a hut of
  their own, and spend a whole year with them. In the following spring they
  retrace their journey to their own homes. Journeys of four to five hundred
  miles in one spring are not a rare occurrence; longer journeys, however,
  frequently last for years.
                                                      (Boas [1888] 1964: 167)



DOI: 10.4324/9781003374145-6
                                                       Human Terrains 147

Boas offers details of the many technologies that were of necessity part of
travelling, and he shows how the Central Inuit were implicated in a vast and
shifty landscape with which they had to converse. Their geographical know-
ledge stretched far, and the lived terrain extended to the open waters along
and between the coasts of islands and peninsulas. People knew where they
were, and had a well-​developed sense of geographical coordinates and navi-
gation (Boas [1888] 1964: 235f). The human terrain was created through
connections.
   Scholars and others arriving from more southerly regions of the American
continent or from Europe met with people knowing their landscape from
within, and the newcomers actively sought out so-​called native maps. While
there is no indication of a local mapping tradition in the form of geograph-
ical representations of the landscape, 19th-​century explorers from Europe
were impressed by the ease with which the locals were able to draw and dis-
cuss the nature of coastlines and landscapes. An early case is that of Captain
William Edward Parry, who was commander of a British expedition setting
out to search for the Northwest Passage in 1822, and coming very close to
success. He did not find the passage, however, and eventually he did not
believe in one, but he was on the right track until stuck in the ice. In his
book he related how people on the Winter Island, and most prominently a
woman informant among them, were able to describe the landscape “with
astonishing precision” (Parry 1824: 277). Shared cartographic reasoning, as
described also by other travellers, testify to a remarkable success in cross-​
cultural communication.
   Terrain is a deliberately evocative concept. In contrast to territory, which
immediately invokes boundaries, terrain has neither boundaries nor a fixed
centre; it is material as well as imaginative and may include many places or
none. Given the mobile life of the Arctic hunters, their terrain seems to fit
the idea of a nomadic landscape, suggested by Deleuze and Guattari, seeing
how the nomadic trajectory ‘distributes people (or animals) in an open space,
one that is indefinite’ (Deleuze and Guattari 2004: 420, original emphasis).
Calling up the human terrain allows us to sense both the specificities of
varying landscapes and their impingement on social life. In the Arctic, events
of hunting and moving house, of storing blubber and leaving the igloo for
a tent, or the tent for sleeping under the sky, make seasons and sentiments
crystallize and contribute to a particular sense of subjectivity within a shared
emotional topography (Hastrup 2010).


Landscape Literacy
It follows from the above that anthropological fieldwork is always more
than observation and conversation with people who happen to live at a cer-
tain place at a particular moment, when the anthropologist arrives. The field
is not just a place but also a set of dispositions and moods, affecting the
ethnographers. To illustrate this, we shall start with another excursion to the
148 Human Terrains

Amazonian region, made by Lévi-​Strauss in the early1930s, having felt the
fascination of high mountains taking leave of him (cf. Chapter 1). The moun-
tains had enchanted him at first, while he climbed up and down them, finding
new vistas on the other side; they offered a conversation, in which both man
and mountain had to give their best. But once in the rainforest, an unprece-
dented world of closure and surprise engulfed him.

  Seen from outside, the Amazonian Forest looks like a heap of stationary
  bubbles, a vertical accumulation of green blisters. It is as if the river-​marge
  had been visited, everywhere and at the same time, by some pathological
  affliction. But once the film is broken, and the traveller penetrates into
  the interior, all is changed; seen from within, the confused mass becomes
  a monumental universe. The forest is no longer a scene of terrestrial dis-
  order and could rather be taken for a new planetary world, which is as
  rich as our own and has taken its place.
     Once the eye has adjusted itself to the nearness of one plane to another,
  and the mind has overcome its first sensation of being overwhelmed, a
  complicated system presents itself. Storeys superimposed one on the other
  may be discerned, and for all the abrupt changes of level and intermittent
  mulching, which interfere with their alignment, these are all constructed
  in the same way.
                                                     (Lévi-​Strauss 1963: 336)

Lévi-​
     Strauss continues the presentation of the forest upwards towards
the top, where new colours and flowers emerge. To this ‘aerial’ story of
uplift towards the light and multiple colours, he adds that one might find
corresponding stories beneath the traveller’s feet. It

  would be an illusion to suppose that he is walking “on the ground.”
  The ground is buried deep beneath tangle upon tangle of roots, suckers,
  mosses, and tufts of grass. Let him tread too heavily on unsteady ground,
  and he may find himself falling –​disconcertingly far, at times.
                                                                   (Ibid.: 336)

The reason to call upon Lévi-​Strauss once again is his reminder of the mut-
ability of landscape, composed of numerous levels and life-​         forms, both
making and unmaking it as a place, where certain habits take root.
    In the Arctic, so very different from the tropical forest at first sight, com-
parable observations may eventually take hold, even if the vertical scenery is
replaced by a horizontal one. The seasonal changes and their imposition of
dramatic shifts between open and closed spaces, so peculiar to human life in
the Arctic, equally challenge the observer with its intangibility, as a scene on
which people act. Expansiveness and free movement over vast stretches, trav-
elling from one node in an extensive network of resources and relatives to
                                                        Human Terrains 149

another, is constantly cut short by forced stillness within the confined space
of the dwelling or the snow-​blinded field of vision. The agentive forces of the
landscape are part of the human terrain, as are the social responses both to
the limitless world and to the intensive moments of enclosure by snow or ice.
A case is offered by Diamond Jenness speaking about the arrival of spring in
the Coronation Gulf region, where he and his Inuit companions had lived on
the coast or directly on the ice by Victoria Island, subsisting almost exclu-
sively on seal. Finally, time came to leave the camp on the sea ice, and to
store the vast amounts of blubber that had been amassed on a small island
some three miles off the coast, and immediately covered with solid stones to
deter the seagulls. To protect the blubber deposits from foxes in the longer
run required more solid caches, however. When this was seen to, the shifting
terrain could be acknowledged in detail.

  Continual sealing began to pall on the Eskimos when the safe disposition
  of their blubber removed the last excuse for lingering on the sea-​ice. The
  long winter had come to an end, and the midnight sun was shining over
  their heads. They forgot the monotonous hours of confinement within the
  dimly lighted huts, when the blizzards raged outdoors and the twilight of
  noon resembled night rather than day. The earth was awakening to life
  after its long sleep. Ducks and loons flew overhead, the ptarmigan in the
  valleys were seeking out their mates, and a faint tinge of green had crept
  into the brown tips of the low dwarf willows that protruded in the snow.
  In the hills lay countless lakes teeming with trout and salmon, and herds
  of caribou grazed on the slopes and plains. Soon large pools would form
  around the margins of the lakes, and the unlocked waters race in foaming
  torrents to the sea. Everything beckoned us landward, to a joyous life in
  open air. There would still be a few more snow-​storms, succeeded by fogs
  and rain; later, dark clouds of mosquitoes would assail us night and day
  without rest. But there would be many days, too, of mild, clear weather
  when we could sleep beneath the open sky; and food was abundant every-
  where, so that we could roam where we pleased, as free and unfettered as
  the caribou we hunted.
    On May 9 our sleds climbed up the shore near the mouth of a large
  creek called Okowyarvik. The new life had begun.
                                               (Jenness [1928] 1959: 106–​7)

Clearly, the human terrain includes its affordances and Jenness had
internalized its riches. Presently, in the (now) rapidly warming Arctic there
is an incongruence between the unlimited nomadic landscape –​including
the sea ice –​within which the hunters lived until recently, and the increas-
ingly circumscribed possibilities for making a living on the thinning ice.
The seasonal rhythms that made the human terrain felt so intensely to
the early ethnographers are disjointed, and people are cut off from their
150 Human Terrains

history, as new global interests in sub-​surface energies, rare metals, and
deep-​sea boundaries move in. Relations between humans and their land-
scape change.
   Vilhjalmur Stefansson made a survey of Prehistoric and Present Commerce
among the Arctic Coast Eskimo, in which he suggested that there

  are three things that chiefly determine the character of Eskimo commerce:
  the geographic conditions that make certain routes of travel more feasible
  than others; the character of the natural resources of the different districts;
  and the distribution of the peopled areas (as well as the degree of friendli-
  ness of their inhabitants).
                                                     (Stefansson 1914a: 1–​2)

This aptly triangulates the ‘terrain’, we explore here.
   The retraction of the ice at the end of the last glacial era has continued
until today at different paces. The Arctic was never still, even when most
extensively covered by ice. The Earth has been in a permanent state of
changing, slowly, or violently, both before and after the arrival of humans;
this points to a terrain in permanent flux, and to the short life of eth-
nographies. The early chroniclers may have been overwhelmed by the
entanglement of landscapes, animals, and people –​and by their own lack
of knowledge –​but they could not foresee how little time there was left for
the kind of life that met them a century ago. Stefansson approached the
Arctic terrain thus:

  In the Eskimo country the great highway of travel is the sea. This is gen-
  erally known and frequently reiterated by students of the subject, but so
  habituated are many of us to mentally defining a sea route as a water
  route, that in making the above statement we speak a fact while we think
  a fiction. The sea is indeed the commercial highway, not, however, as
  water but as ice; not as a medium for boat travel so much as (in many
  districts) the sine qua non of rapid sled travel and the hauling of heavy
  loads by dog teams. Nowhere between Baffinland and Smith sound on the
  east and Cap Bathurst on the west, did boats probably ever play a consid-
  erable part in trade; certain portions of the Greenland coast were about
  the only localities where the boat completely supplanted the sled. From
  Cape Bathurst west to Mackenzie delta the use of boats was not interfered
  with so much by ice conditions as by the fact that the summer season
  here was the harvest season more absolutely than in most districts, not
  only because of the annual coming of the caribou, but chiefly because the
  various sorts of whales, upon which the Eskimo depended for food, fuel,
  and light, frequented the coast during most of the summer and engrossed
  the people’s attention, while in winter and spring they had plenty of leisure
  for travel and trading.
                                                        (Stefansson 1914a: 2)
                                                        Human Terrains 151

This is an apt illustration of a human terrain, within which nature’s rhythm
formats both life and dreams, as habits are established. The communities are
not simply put on top of the visible landscapes, they are formatted by them,
and contribute to their shaping. The landscape is always more than nature.
What meets the eye when looking on mountain slopes, riverbeds, tundra, ice
deserts, fishing waters, or glacier bursts, is already half-​interpreted; this is
where landscape literacy takes off.
   The affective ties to the landscape, negative or positive, are related to the
fact that people live by spatial habits that have gradually been installed in
their bodies and affected their perception of the environment. Social habits
are also spatial habits; and, as Marcel Mauss had it, these may be reflected in
the shifting modalities of winter-​and summer-​living. Moreover,

  a mystic efficacy is attributed to these exchanges and to this redistribution:
  they are necessary for success in hunting; for without generosity there can
  be no luck. This economic communism of winter is strikingly parallel to
  the sexual communism during this same season; it shows, once more, the
  degree of moral unity that the Eskimo community attains at this time.
                                                     (Mauss [1906] 1967: 73)

   There is no way a hunter can determine his catch beforehand, but when
setting out he naturally presumes that under the given circumstances of
season, weather, and wind, the travel will be worth his effort. The imagin-
ation of the future always balances between knowledge and desire in a
community where the horizon of knowing expands and contracts in both
expected and unexpected ways, making the seasons less certain. From his
experience among Nunavut reindeer hunters, Hugh Brody has suggested:

  Hunting itself …must defy the habit as well as follow it: no two seasons
  are identical, animal migrations are never wholly predictable. Hunters,
  following well-​worn trails, must seize new opportunities, adjust the pace
  and direction of their movements to follow, intercept or find the animals
  upon which life depends. At each point along the seasonal round, indi-
  viduals must assess and process a vast amount of information. The habit
  and pattern of mobility set the scene; action within the scene keeps
  changing.
                                                          (Brody 1987: 89)

The non-​ human prey co-​   constitutes the human trails in the landscape;
the sense of terrain depends upon both experience and hunting luck. The
implications are general and apply to all places of importance to humans,
even though they may not be hunters. Places become singled out as important
for many different reasons; implicitly they are events. Hence the general elu-
siveness of places, and the need for landscape literacy when depending on a
particular terrain. Massey says:
152 Human Terrains

  What is special about place is not some romance of a pre-​given collective
  identity or of the eternity of the hills. Rather, what is special about place
  is precisely that throwntogetherness, the unavoidable challenge of nego-
  tiating a here-​and-​now (itself drawing on a history and a geography of
  thens and theres); and a negotiation which must take place within and
  between both human and non-​human.
                                                           (Massey 2005: 140)

In the process of historical and scientific development, new places gain prom-
inence and old places lose their importance. In such processes, unknown
landscapes emerge in ancient regions. The drive for understanding the rela-
tion between humans and landscapes in the Arctic was premised by the early
ethnographers’ own emplacement in an unknown terrain. This called for
a commitment to achieve some degree of landscape literacy, meaning the
ability to read the vernacular of the lived land, beyond geography. It takes
time and effort to experience it as a socially formatted landscape, and as a
site of hidden resources. It is not always improved by a study of place names
that might actually be quite misleading, as noted by Rasmussen:

  Arviligjuaq, “The Land of Great Whales,” is a term used to denote the
  whole of the Pelly Bay district, and it is derived not from any actual
  presence of whales in those waters –​as far as I could learn, there are
  none –​but from some hill formations on land, which viewed from a dis-
  tance present the appearance of whales.
                                            (Rasmussen [1927] 1999: 167)

   Ethnographers moving into the Inuit terrain noted the diversities between
the groups, not only in terms of their relative prosperity, but also in differing
materials, new tales, variations in openness to strangers, and in originality
etc. The much-​travelled Stefansson was more than delighted when he reached
the Dolphin and Union Strait Inuit. It

  was the day of all my life to which I had looked forward with the most
  vivid anticipations, and to which I now look back with equally vivid mem-
  ories, for it introduced me, a student of mankind and of primitive men
  especially, to a people of a bygone age.
                                                    (Stefansson 1924: 149)

Stefansson goes on to compare this with Mark Twain’s Connecticut Yankee
who went to sleep in the 19th century and woke up in King Arthur’s time
among knights. The Inuit belonged to an even older past; they were an
anachronism. He adds:

  They gathered their food with the weapons of the men of the Stone Age,
  they thought their simple, primitive thoughts, and lived their insecure and
                                                       Human Terrains 153

  tense lives –​lives that were to me the lives of our far ancestors, whose
  bones and crude handiwork we now and then discover in river-​gravels or
  in prehistoric caves. Such archaeological remains found in various parts
  of the world who antedated the knowledge of the smelting of metals, tell a
  fascinating story to him whose scientific imagination can piece it together
  and fill in the wide gaps; but far better than such dreaming was my present
  opportunity. I had nothing to imagine; I had merely to look and listen; for
  here were not remains of the Stone Age, but the Stone Age itself, men and
  women, very human, entirely friendly, who welcomed us to their homes
  and bade us stay.
                                                    (Stefansson 1924: 149–​50)

The mutual perception is interesting; while Stefansson saw a stone-​        age
people, the Inuit saw fellow humans. They were, of course, contemporary,
but had each their sense of the terrain; this points to the throwntogertherness
of place, as suggested by Massey (and as quoted above). Space and time
are implicated in each other in the lived world. “An adequate recognition
of coevalness demands acceptance that one is being observed/​        theorised/​
evaluated in return and potentially in different terms” (Massey 2005: 89). At
Stefansson’s time, it was not so easy (yet) to see oneself as part of the same
landscape, let alone as ‘contemporary’ with the Inuit.


Emerging Maps
Geographical knowledge of the Arctic outside of the region was gradually
established from the 17th century onwards, and by early 20th century, the
native populations were known and named by traders, scholars, and officials,
who were driven by their own territorial practices. Together, geographers,
archaeologists, and anthropologists now worked hard to find the core
features of people who seemed supremely well suited to the Arctic landscape
after a long history of adaptation that began when North America was first
populated towards the end of the last Ice Age. Clearly, trappers and whalers,
missionaries and traders, not to speak of neighbouring people slightly fur-
ther south, knew the Inuit –​by various names –​all along. But it was with the
dawning ethnographic interest in the region that they became transfixed as a
distinct culture. Mauss contributed to this by arguing that they were basic-
ally one coherent group from Greenland to Alaska, all of them being coastal
people. He adds:

  The Eskimo aren’t simply a coastal people. They are people of the water’s
  fringe –​if we may use this term to designate all the relatively abrupt
  terminations of the sea coasts. This explains the marked differences
  between the Eskimo and other arctic peoples. Except for the deltas and
  the little known rivers of King William Land, all the coasts that the
  Eskimo occupy have the same character: a more or less narrow strip of
154 Human Terrains

   land skirting the edges of a plateau that gives way, more or less abruptly
   to the sea.
                                                    (Mauss [1906] 1967: 24)

The coast is neither a passage nor a point of transition, but a habitat
(ibid: 25). Along with sharing this habitat, the Inuit also had a remarkable
linguistic unity, yet according to Mauss they could not be seen as a tribe,
distinguished by a shared name. Names are as vague as territorial bound-
aries are invisible. There were settlements with relatively stable connections,
but the territorial practices challenged the vision of distinct tribes. While the
expeditioners noted down what they met with during their long and often
cumbersome movements in the terrain, they had difficulties when seeking to
fix and name the inhabited space on a map.
   The Fifth Thule Expedition, covering all Arctic America, also had a hard
time mapping the lived landscape. Maps remained tentative, because the Inuit
were always moving, even if connected by old tales from coast to coast. Past
and present converged in the landscape, where mapping was never a simple
matter of surface description. It was also sensing the time depth of human
habitation in the American Arctic. This was underwritten by the archaeo-
logical work of the expedition, mainly in the hands of Therkel Mathiassen,
who was to dig out the ‘Thule Culture’ of the permafrost (cf. Chapter 2).
Mathiassen also contributed to contemporary ethnography and published a
report on the material culture of the Iglulik Inuit (cf. Chapter 5). In his report
he not only listed available game, artefacts, and clothing, but also discussed
their relation to the original Inuit. The Iglulik had a distinct Central Inuit cul-
ture and resembled their neighbours in different ways, he suggested.

   The groups of other Central Eskimos whom they most closely resemble
   are the Netsilik and Caribou, that is to say the tribes on the west and east
   of their own area. The Iglulik Eskimos’ “summer culture” –​everything
   concerned with hunting and fishing inland –​is almost identical with that
   of the Caribou Eskimos; the latter’s culture has no real “marine” side, and
   the little they have is that of the Aiviliks. The same resemblance is even
   more conspicuous in the Netsiliks, whose culture is closer to that of the
   Igluliks than all others, although the “marine” side among the Igluliks has
   developed rather more than among the Netsiliks, presumably as a result
   of geographical conditions: the abundant supplies of walrus and narwhal.
   That the Iglulik Eskimos must, in spite of these resemblances, be looked
   upon as a separate tribe appears from the fact that they themselves do so,
   and that they have a certain cultural individuality which separates them
   from their neighbours.
                                                    (Mathiassen 1928: 238–​9)

Classifications are important, and among the Iglulik there are features that
must be regarded as relics of the Thule culture, while there are also some
                                                        Human Terrains 155

‘provincialisms’, that is culture elements that are peculiar to them. There
is, in other words, a ranking in relation to the prehistoric Thule Culture.
Mapping the Inuit terrain invariably seems to mix up time and space. Thanks
to the ‘discovery’ of the ancient Thule Culture, the prototype of Arctic
hunters became associated with people relying on sea mammals. It may not
have been the oldest among the Inuit cultures, but it was the one most befit-
ting the ancient image of a people living in a terrain on the edge of the world.
    The caribou hunters whom the Expedition met with were no real match
for this image, and Mathiassen speaks rather disparagingly about them.
The claim to authenticity carries a heavy weight. Even today, Inuit of nor-
thern Canada are attributed with an ancestry that goes back to the Thule
Culture (e.g., Wenzel 2008); this is what backs their claim to continuity and
indigeneity. This culture was distinguished by the close relationship between
humans and marine mammals on the brink of the Polar Sea. No cultural
history can cut itself loose from its moorings in a particular terrain. The
whales that were retrospectively defined as the primary game for the Inuit
hunters were also the animals that eventually attracted the Europeans to the
Arctic region; whales became an almost emblematic resource, underscoring
the entanglement of economic and explorative interests in the Arctic.
    Where people are defined by their relations to marine mammals, these
animals become stellar companion species, to use the term suggested
by Haraway for species that co-​      shape each other (cf. Chapter 4) and
acknowledged as a permanently undecidable category (Haraway 2008: 165).
The concept of species itself is “full of their own others, full of messmates,
of companions” despite its being framed by a vision of taxonomic order, of
logical types, of watertight conceptual boundaries (ibid.). We are reminded
of the hybridity of categories, subverting the enlightenment vision of a uni-
versal natural order.
    Mapping is most often thought of in terms of a flat geometrical image,
totalizing observations, and fixing relationships (de Certeau 1984: 119ff).
The practice of mapping, however, is never simply a representational exer-
cise; it affords its own cartographic illusion, not least by the assumption that
the map can be “fixed without regard to the movement of its inhabitants”
(Ingold 2000: 234). Given that all spaces are in some sense social, the
question becomes how many maps we may need to deal exhaustively with a
given space. Henri Lefebvre (1991: 85) provides an answer in suggesting that
we are confronted with “a sort of instant infinity.” He explains:

  It is not only the codes –​the map’s legend, the conventional signs of map-​
  making and map-​reading –​that are liable to change, but also the objects
  represented, the lens through which they are viewed, and the scale used.
  The idea that a small number of maps or even a single (and singular) map
  may be sufficient can only apply in a specialized area of study whose own
  self-​affirmation depends on isolation from its context.
                                                       (Lefebvre 1991: 85–​6)
156 Human Terrains

Lefebvre here implicitly acknowledges the indefinite nature of scale and
reminds us that any claim to exhaustive mapping rests on artificial closure
(Hastrup 2014). In the polar regions of the Earth, the instant infinity comes
with the territory, so to speak. Once the Earth had been acknowledged as
spherical, a mapping crisis was immanent; Mercator provided a represen-
tational solution in the 16th century, which was singularly well adapted to
navigation on the great oceans on mid-​Earth while extremely distorting to the
image of the Polar regions that could barely be known within the grid, and
to most were probably not worth knowing anyway (see Bravo 2019). This
made room for a great imaginative freedom regarding the Arctic until the
19th century, when actual exploration of the northern seas intensified, and
not least when the first ethnographers would describe social topographies
from within a concrete human terrain –​as visited in the early 20th century.
   The Arctic was mapped rather late in the European history of explor-
ation, partly owing to the bad press inherent in both ancient and more recent
images, and partly to sensational reports about vanished expeditions and
shipwrecks in the ice. Meanwhile, European exploits outside of the Arctic
were fuelled by a new sense of the unity of the Earth, not only through visible
landscapes but also through hidden sub-​surface connections. This was voiced
by the naturalist Alexander von Humboldt, whom we have met in earlier
chapters, travelling in South America, and fuelling an interest in earthquakes
and volcanoes, deeply affecting the human terrains. He described the violent
earthquake of Lisbon in 1755 within a broad geographical context; at the
same time as the earthquake, the Swiss lakes and the Swedish coasts were
violently disturbed, as were even the “eastern Antilles around Martinique,
Antigua, and Barbados, where the tide never reaches above 28 inches, it sud-
denly rose to twenty feet” (Humboldt [1849] 2014: 248). This revealed the
power of subterranean forces and showed that they

  do not exert their influence superficially, coming only from the thin crust
  of the earth, but instead deeply, coming from the interior of our planet
  through clefts and unfilled passages, rising simultaneously to the most dis-
  tant points on the Earth’s surface.
                                                                       (Ibid.)

Indirectly, this points us towards knew knowledge about the globe correl-
ating “with an expanding search for commercially exploitable resources,
markets, and lands to colonize, just as navigational mapping is linked with
the search for trade routes” (Pratt 1992: 30).
   This development also brought the Arctic edge of the world into the geo-
graphic picture in the 19th century, and when new trade routes were in sight
they infested the European mercantile mind. Visions of both a Northeast,
and a Northwest Passage, that would shorten the route to Asia consider-
ably, were strong inducements to Arctic exploration, having started well
before the 19th century. In the first half of the 19th century, the British
                                                       Human Terrains 157

dominated a new effort, due in part to a huge fleet that had become super-
fluous after the termination of the Napoleonic wars. Later the Scandinavians
and the Americans entered the scene. Of course, trappers and whalers had
been around for much longer, and not for entirely different reasons. For all
parties, the presumed gains related to trade, either directly through the catch
of whales and the selling of blubber, or indirectly by the search for new
trade routes to Asian markets. In their search for the Northwest Passage,
European navigators entered the Davis Strait, between Baffin Island and
Greenland, named after the explorer John Davis, leader of three expeditions
organized by London Merchants in 1585–​1587, hoping for returns in whale
oil. William Baffin himself had reached an ‘open sea’ North of the Melville
Bay, as early as 1616 (Baffin [1616] 1881: 144–​5; Markham 1881); here he
named two sounds, the Whale Sound and Smith Sound, still known by these
names today. Despite his success, the open sea was soon closed off again by
the advent of the Little Ice Age –​and forgotten for some two hundred years
(or more), as were the Inughuit living by ‘the open sea’.
   Mapping is a prominent territorial practice. Explorers of the Arctic regions
were keen mappers, seeking out genuinely new knowledge that might point
them towards their goal –​basically to European advantage –​and they often
actively sought out so-​called native maps. While there is no indication of a
longstanding, local mapping tradition in the form of a systematic graphical
representation of the landscape, 19th-​century explorers from Europe were
impressed by the ease with which the Inuit were able to draw and discuss
the nature of coastlines and places. An early case is that of Captain William
Edward Parry, who was commander of a British expedition sent out to search
for the Northwest Passage in 1822; Parry generally bowed to “the geograph-
ical information obtained from the Eskimaux …on which I conceived the
greatest reliance may be placed” (Parry 1824: 489). There was conversation
and learning on both sides.
   While evidently, the Inuit knew where they were and could map their
routes, the lasting impact on the regional maps was owed to explorers,
whalers, and reporters –​who had been guided by the Inuit, however. In the
emerging contact zone, both parties were affected by the meetings and the
ensuing relations. Just like the frontiersmen and (later) the treaty-​makers in
North America affected the Native Americans, becoming part of a larger
vision of a New World, so were also the tiny Arctic populations gradually
implicated in a larger world. They were met with some bewilderment, but
also with a sense of ‘discovery’ having unfolded ever since. “In fact, realiza-
tion of the momentous importance of the discovery of the Americas came
only with a whole series of later discoveries –​and these may not yet have
come to an end” (Brody 2002: 71). Clearly, discovery is never completed.
Territorial practices of both indigenous and foreign travellers are never just
a matter of depiction of geometry but also a question of deep seas, historical
turns, points of view, and not least the envisioned goals –​all of which are
unsteady.
158 Human Terrains

Moods of Terrain
Moving into the human terrain as lived by the Inuit until a century ago, we
shall start by a distinct territorial practice, that of extensive movement in
response to seasonal migrations of animals. What emerges is a lived space
with its own moods and provocations; seasonality is part of the terrain, as
are the companion animals. Let us hear a snippet from Stefansson’s imme-
diate report to the American Museum of Natural History after his early
expedition with Rudolf Anderson (1908–​1912).

  The Kãnhiryuarmiut have the widest range of seasonal migrations of any
  of the Copper Eskimo tribes and probably of any Eskimo tribe in the
  world. In winter most of them are found on south Banks Island just
  east of Nelson Head. They leave here late in March, cross the straits to
  Prince Albert Sound, and here the tribe scatters in all directions. Some
  go thirty or forty miles south; some sixty or seventy miles southeast;
  some forty of fifty miles northeast; and occasionally all the way across
  the island to Collinson Inlet, while the larger number go about one hun-
  dred miles east up the Kagloryuak to where it heads near the head of
  the Ekalluktok which flows from the center of Victoria Island east into
  Albert Edward Bay. At this point four or five families separate themselves
  from the rest, descend the Ekalluktok and cross the straits to the main-
  land in the vicinity of Ogden Bay. It seems they reach this point annually
  the early part of June, for it is here they have to abandon their sleds and
  proceed south carrying back loads and their dogs also carrying packs, for
  they are bound overland to the Akilinik River. Usually, on the way they
  are joined by a few families of the Ekalluktogmiut in Albert Edward Bay
  and later by some families of the Ahiagmiut at Ogden Bay. The united
  parties march overland and some time in July they come to Back River
  which they call the Haningayok. Their visit is expected by a party of the
  Haningayogmiut, who are ready for them with their kayaks to ferry them
  over.
                                                (Stefansson [1914b] 1919: 36)

The detached observational stance cannot hide the fact that the terrain
is alive with movement, promise, and obstacle. Significantly, although
Stefansson names tribes and seeks to map their movements, he also impli-
citly dismantles them, seeing how they move, disperse, and regroup –​often
with others. The human terrain is dynamic and packed with meaning and
living resources that must be pursued wherever they move. In the early days
of ethnographic interest in the Arctic, the terrain was perceived next to unin-
habitable, all while the travellers were overwhelmed by ancient traces, recent
habitations, signposts put up by traders, trading stations, houses, and not
least an awareness of ships –​sailing, frozen in the ice, or crushed –​also
having left their visible traces.
                                                          Human Terrains 159

   There were also the ‘half-​castes’, testifying to recent encounters of a more
intimate kind, and adding further to the humanization of the undecidable
terrain, where European explorers sometimes lost both their sense of direc-
tion and their sense of themselves. They were often despondent and could
not fathom how the natives could live so contentedly in this “most miserable
region and most abominable climate” even at summer solstice, as John Ross
noted for the Arctic Highlanders –​the Inughuit in Northwest Greenland –​
that he ‘discovered’ in 1818. He expanded on his general view of the Inuit
terrain after his expedition into the Northwest Passage, in company with his
nephew, James Clark Ross, and now in milder terms.

  Such, nevertheless, is the climate in which man contrives to live and, as we
  had no right to dispute, happily. He cannot drink water at Midsummer, it
  is true, till he has boiled his snow; and had he not wit enough to produce
  fire, he would have nothing to drink for nine months of the year. He smells
  no flowers, for there are none to smell at; but he prefers the odour of train
  oil. He has no carrots or “small herbs,” for his soup or his seasoning; but
  his soup and his seasoning are, alike, oil, and he can find a sallad, when
  his luck is particularly great, in the stomach of a reindeer; and that sallad
  too, cooked in a heat of which the advantages have never been disputed.
  If he never saw that utterly inconceivable thing called a tree, what matters
  it, when he can construct coaches of fish, and splinter bars of bones? and
  if he can make his lodging, not merely “on the cold ground,” but on the
  cold snow, his fare at least is not “hard,” and why, if he thinks so, is he
  not as well lodged as the princes of the earth, the marble of whose palaces
  does not approach in purity to the materials of his architecture, while his
  own marble house is erected in an hour, and can be renewed, like that of
  Aladdin, at every hour of the day, in any place that he wills? Man must
  be a noble animal, that is certain, be he even under the figure and bearing
  of a Boothian Esquimaux: is there another beast on earth that could do
  all of this, endure all this, contrive all this, conform to all this, to all this
  and more, and still be happy; happy as if he is in Naples, happy too in
  Boothia Felix?
                                                            (Ross 1835: 573–​4)

On the Arctic frontier, the explorers could not save themselves unless they
gave in to the terrain and to the people who lived there –​including their ways
of living. The terrain was humanized, and the perception of the Inuit subject
in anthropology was heavily influenced by the early encounters. Newcomers
like John Ross entered a twilight zone, formatted by an inevitable conjunc-
tion of sensual and intellectual appropriation of the landscape, where people
and places emerged simultaneously (Hastrup 2014). This offers a renewed
interpretation of Merleau-​  Ponty’s suggestion that “space and perception
generally represent, at the core of the subject, the fact of his birth, the per-
petual contribution of his bodily being, a communication with the world
160 Human Terrains

more ancient than thought” (Merleau-​Ponty [1962] 2001: 254). In the Arctic
the communication between people and landscape took a particular turn. Yet
it was not reserved for the Arctic, but also found in the deep silence of the
dense tropical forest, of which Lévi-​Strauss said:

  For one of the most striking characteristics of the forest is its way of
  seeming to merge into an element heavier than air: such light as gets
  through is greenish and enfeebled, and the human voice does not carry.
  The extraordinary silence, which reigns –​as a consequence, perhaps, of
  this condition –​would communicate its example to the traveller if he were
  not already disinclined to speak, so intent is he upon not losing his way.
  His moral situation combines with the physical state to create an almost
  intolerable feeling of oppression.
                                                    (Lévi-​Strauss 1963: 339)

Anthropology, wherever it goes, offers a truly travelling theory; it travels
with people, whether they inhabit a particular place or just pay it a fleeting
visit. It is different from geography, even though this is always in some sense
imaginative, as Edward Said reminded us; there is always ‘something more’
than merely positive knowledge (Said 1979: 55). There is also something
less, I would argue; when it comes to the Arctic, the ‘less’ has to do with
the actual, locally perceived remoteness and vulnerability, and this again is
related to the changing seasons between the polar night and the midnight
sun, which are often difficult to understand, if one has not had a chance to
experience it. Peary enlightens us:

  The phenomena of night and day in the Arctic regions are clear to very
  few. I think I should be not far from the truth if I said that they are fully
  understood only by those who have spent a year there.
     There is a vague appreciation of the fact, that in very high latitudes there
  is a long period of summer during which the sun never sets, and it is con-
  stant day; and another long period in winter during which it never rises,
  and it is constant night.
                                                        (Peary 1898, II: 317–​8)

Peary goes on to explain the astronomical facts of winter darkness, where
both nightfall and dawn may drag out when the sun is just below the
horizon; additionally, moon-​and star-​sparkling days may offer light even
at winter solstice –​if there are no clouds. In other words, winter darkness
is never as absolute as the term indicates, and travel is not precluded during
‘the long night’, even if surrounded by gloomy foreboding by many visitors.
While perception, movement, and locality are closely inter-​woven, it still
befalls human agents to describe them based on their own knowledge and
sensations. Descriptions took shape also in the explorers’ naming of places,
with particular events or benefactors in mind; thus Otto Sverdrup, who
                                                       Human Terrains 161

travelled the High Arctic Islands (1898–​1902), when still an unknown and
unnamed territory, took licence to name places that stood out –​by beauty
or by bounty (Sverdrup 1904, I & II). Many of these names still figure on
the maps.
    The above suggests that terrains are lived and practiced, while territories
point to geographical (and political) coordinates. In 1880 the High Arctic
Islands were transferred from Great Britain to Canada without much admin-
istrative concern; it could easily be done on paper –​nobody had to go there.
Yet, Norwegian Sverdrup soon found himself in the High Arctic Sea around
the Islands, successfully completing the Second Fram Expedition in 1898–​
1902 (the First being Nansen’s 1893–​1896 expedition in the Polar Sea, north
of Siberia). Sverdrup found no living people in the Canadian High Arctic
Archipelago at all, but was deeply affected by ruins, from the ‘time of the
Eskimos’, suggesting that these people belonged to a distant past, and asking
what had become of them.

  Have they migrated southward to milder regions? Or did they fight out a
  hopeless struggle against the cold and the crushing darkness of the winter
  night, until they all succumbed to the great enemy who knows no mercy?
     It is a strange feeling of forlornness and barrenness which seizes upon
  one as one looks at all these ruins, ruins which tell of the human beings
  who lived here, and had their joys and sorrows like ourselves. Out here
  on the spits of land the air of the still, clear, summer evenings echoed to
  the careless laughter of these children of nature, and to the happy shouts
  of childhood. When autumn came, and the darkness was upon them, they
  mayhap withdrew with their well-​husbanded summer catches to some
  sheltered spot, where were their winter houses, and there most likely lived
  out a languishing existence. In their miserable huts they waited patiently
  for the sun again to shed its light on the lofty peaks. Then they reavoke to
  life and action in the gladness of summer –​though, who can tell, in many
  a hut, perhaps, where the winter store fell short, every human voice was
  dumb!
     Week after week, month after month, we drove about up here, and
  never met with a single living thing, except wild animals. But anyone
  travelling here in former times might have found people who lived and
  had their homes for always in this wilderness. It seemed to us almost
  incredible.
                                                       (Sverdrup 1904, II: 212)

Ellesmere Island, on the western side of Smith Sound and Kane Basin was
often seen inhospitable to humans, yet it was part of Inughuit perception
of their terrain, even if not necessarily visited very often. Jean Malaurie, a
French geographer exploring the Thule region in 1951–​1952 (Northwest
Greenland), relates how his wish to cross over Smith Sound was met with a
lot of scepticism. At the defection of a young hunter, an old man said:
162 Human Terrains

  We must have somebody young with a little blood in him. It’s not going to
  be easy if we’re going right to Canada. We shall certainly not have enough
  food. And I do want to come back. You know the story of the Americans?
  There with Grileysouak (Greely), they all died of hunger and devoured
  one another. And then if one has to pass the winter in the desert island of
  Ellesmere the cold is terrible. Ouloulik said that the petrol freezes there. If
  the sea-​ice breaks between Canada and Greenland –​and it will break per-
  haps –​what will you do? We shall have to live by hunting. But you can’t
  carry a year of cartridges. So we shall have to use harpoons.
                                                         (Malaurie 1956: 124)

The old man certainly had little faith in an expedition likely to throw him
back to old technologies like harpoons. Greely’s story had survived, yet his
crew had not. Setting out towards the Polar Seas in 1881 the group stranded
on Pim Island (in the southern part of Kane Basin, opposite Inglefield Land,
where the Inughuit had some of their hunting grounds). Of Greely’s men 19
out of 25 died from starvation (Dick 2001: 188ff). Such stories linger for a
long time, and Malaurie had a hard time assembling a group of hunters to
go with him. The moods of terrain are not incidental, even if they may be
experienced differently by different people, at diverse times and within dis-
tinct terms of knowledge.


Territorial Claims
Places are formatted by more than feelings; of course. In the early 20th cen-
tury, national territorial claims would undermine the explorers’ prerogatives,
often borne out of accidental findings. Sverdrup’s original plan was to map
the northern coast of Greenland, but the ice in the Kane Basin prevented this
and he redirected his interests towards the High Arctic Islands that were to
all intents and purposes unclaimed, or at least unmapped. He made exten-
sive surveys, scientific studies, and maps of the entire region, and eventu-
ally claimed it for Norway (on his own account). He and the expedition
geologist, Ivar Fosheim, planted the Norwegian flag in a small cairn on Axel
Heiberg Land (so named by them) and proclaimed possession for Norway of
all the territory they had visited. It was large; Sverdrup and his crew charted
260,000 square kilometres over the four years of the expedition, and sur-
viving Norwegian place names still testify to their achievements. Having
discovered and mapped also the islands to the west of Ellesmere Island,
including Axel Heiberg Island, that had until then been unknown, Sverdrup
had what seemed a legitimate claim to the territory. This spurred a reac-
tion from Canada, claiming sovereignty over all land, whether known or
unknown within the Canadian Arctic. Sverdrup urged Norway to pursue the
matter, to little effect until much later, when Norwegian politicians could use
it to make a deal with the British about Jan Mayen Island in the High Arctic,
and the Bouvet Islands in the Antarctic.
                                                       Human Terrains 163

  While the Norwegian government expressed little official interest in the
  matter, the Dominion of Canada government took the claim far more ser-
  iously. For centuries, American and European whalers had frequented
  Arctic waters at will, living off the land and the seas as they saw fit,
  even operating land-​based stations when it was convenient. Occasional
  Hudson’s Bay Company protests had been ignored.
     Adding urgency to dealing with Sverdrup and possible Norwegian ter-
  ritorial interests were reports that another Norwegian explorer, Roald
  Amundsen, without acknowledging Canadian territoriality, was setting
  out on what would prove to be the first successful navigation by Europeans
  of the Northwest Passage.
                                                  (Schledermann 2003: 101)

That the islands of north-​eastern Canada were uninhabited was not entirely
true. Sverdrup and his team found traces of Inuit occupation, as we heard
above, and on Ellesmere Island itself, he met with North Greenlandic
Inughuit who had crossed Smith Sound for muskox hunting. It was part of
their hunting grounds, if sometimes accessible only with difficulty due to the
currents and volatile ice cover on the Sound. Even on the western islands
Sverdrup found unmistakable traces of people, having lived there in stone and
turf huts or in tents, whose stone-​rings were left on the ground for another
time, as were possibly the remnants of stone fox-​traps. The crew often had a
sensation of other people being nearby and almost among them –​unable as
they were to date the settlement. Sverdrup recalls: “This unexpected finding
of ourselves amid the marks of human habitation gave us a sudden chilling
realization of the loneliness and barrenness of the country” (Sverdrup 1904,
I: 64). The deep sense of a lived terrain for a moment outmanoeuvred politics
of territory.
    Sverdrup’s and Amundsen’s successful expeditions and their claims to
Norwegian discovery eventually made the Canadian government aware of
the weakness of historical paper-​claims in the Far North (Judd 1969: 594).
So far, the interest in governing the Northern regions had been bleak; while
the fur trade was important to the Hudson’s Bay Company and to many
Inuit, national concerns centred on immigrants and settlers. Meanwhile
the great era of exploration waned: The Northwest Passage had not only
been ‘discovered’ it had also (already) become irrelevant in a world that was
now planning a Panama Canal (ibid.). And, as has been discussed above
(in Chapter 3), the whalers would also soon withdraw from the northern
waters, leaving the forts and stations to themselves. Yet, with Amundsen’s
and Sverdrup’s successful travels, naming, and even claiming stretches of
land, things were stirred up.
    Britain had transferred the Arctic Islands to Canadian jurisdiction in 1880,
but nothing much had happened there. With Sverdrup’s work in the islands
(1898–​1902), Amundsen’s exploration of the Northwest Passage (1903–​
1907), and then Robert Peary’s explorations towards the ulterior goal, the
164 Human Terrains

North Pole, from late 19th century until his success in 1909, and then in
1914, the American Crocker Land Expedition with Donald B. Macmillan
in charge –​all of them moving into what they saw as un-​claimed lands –​
Canadian officials had to act and establish their presence and their sover-
eignty (Dick 2001: 267ff). In 1903, Canadian police were posted on Hershel
Island in the western Arctic to be followed by similar initiatives in the
central and eastern Arctic. Thus in 1903, the Neptune expedition sailed
North with a six-​man detachment of the Royal Canadian Mounted Police
headed by Major Moody (Schledermann 2003: 101). The whaling industry
was in sharp decline, being replaced by a new interest in trading muskox
skins. Much to the dismay of the traders, Moody issued a prohibition on
the muskox skin trade. In 1904, the Neptune sailed into Lancaster Sound
and Pond Inlet, and planted another Canadian flag, this time in the eastern
Arctic. This was a first. In 1904 the Arctic “travelled throughout the Arctic
Islands, placing official Canadian declarations of possession in large stone
cairns. Occasionally they replaced documents left by Sverdrup” (ibid.: 102).
What we have here is a manifest change from a lived, mutable terrain to a
claimed, absolute territory –​a game changer for explorers and not least for
local inhabitants.
    Another token of this was the work of Vilhjalmur Stefansson, being busy
on behalf of the Canadian government in the Arctic, where traders of motley
nationalities began to move in by the Beaufort Sea. Apart from the ethno-
graphic programme, his task was to map, and hence claim the yet unknown
lands and isles for Canada and investigating possibilities for life –​meaning
if there would be food –​even further north than the known islands beyond
the presumed Northwest Passage. The Prime Minister, Sir Robert Laird
Borden, who endorsed the expedition, writes in his preface to Stefansson’s
The Friendly Arctic about his achievement:

  Against the belief and traditions of the Eskimo, against the universal
  experience and strong opinion of the most eminent Arctic explorers,
  against the advice of the whalers, Stefansson maintained his thesis and,
  risking not only his reputation but his life, committed himself to the ice
  of the Beaufort Sea. Two companions accompanied him, and there would
  have been more if necessary, although no Eskimo could be induced to
  embark upon a venture that he regarded as suicidal. For ninety-​six days
  the leader and his comrades journeyed and drifted. There were a few
  days of discouragement when the anticipated signs of seal life were not
  observable, but then came the sure and triumphant vindication of a theory
  founded on accurate knowledge, keen observation and sure deduction.
  Another secret had been wrested from the northern ocean. Stefansson had
  proved that in the farthest Arctic the sea supplied food even more abun-
  dantly than the land. For more than a year the world knew nothing of his
  success, and it was generally believed (not by those who knew him best),
  that he had expiated failure by death.
                                                          Human Terrains 165

   As a result of the Expedition many thousands of square miles have been
   added to the territory of Canada, much interesting material of great sci-
   entific value has been secured, unknown areas of vast extent have been
   explored and many illusions with respect to Arctic conditions have been
   dissipated.
                                    (Borden 1921; in Stefansson 1921: xxiv)

Thousands of square miles added to the territory of Canada. Stefansson
himself wrote a small article explaining how food was the limiting factor
of movement in the Arctic, not the sea ice in general, nor, indeed, the lati-
tude; the problem was the ‘ice-​deserts’ –​“regions of ‘Sargasso Sea’ char-
acter. In them pressure due to winds or currents operating from a distance
heaps the ice up, and it may even remain in an eddy for years” (Stefansson
1920: 735). In such deserts, travellers would have to depend on their own
resources, as well as those offered by the surrounding nature; and we know
from Stefansson’s own work that he had been very ill on the trip.
   When living and moving in the Arctic landscape, one succumbs to a
feeling of perpetual emergence; nothing is fixed. People are manifestly insig-
nificant and readily renounce any comprehensive language of signification.
In modern society and in urban settings we have come to privilege sight and
vision, and to rely on objects, boundaries, and perspectives for orientation.
In the Arctic, however, the visual field potentially extends so far, and the air is
so clear that it effectively prevents space from being boxed in and organized
into different distances and perspectives (Tuan 1990: 11). If the landscape is
simply ‘seen’, it looks empty, and relative size and distance evades any scale.
This challenges any fixed notion of territory.
   Nature itself is a slippery category, and a deceitful partner in communities
that live with it, and who sometimes go hungry. Yet, even in times of hunger,
people in the Arctic would find time to think about something else. Edward
W. Nelson, who made extensive ethnographic observations in Alaska in
the late 19th century, was impressed when he met with the hospitality of a
people, notoriously starving, on Sledge Island, in March 1880:

   We found the people on the verge of starvation, and nearly all of their
   dogs had died from lack of food. Our own dogs were dying from the
   same cause, so we decided to return at once to the mainland. When the
   headman of the villagers learned this he came to beg us to remain at
   least one night with them so that they might show their appreciation
   of our visit, promising that the inhabitants would find something with
   which to feed our dogs and that they would give a dance in the kashim
   in our honor.
      About two hours after dark we were invited into the kashim and given
   the place of honor at the rear end of the room. Half a dozen men, including
   the headman of the village, stepped out in time to singing and drumming,
   and went through the movements of an ordinary dance such as had been
166 Human Terrains

  seen at the beginnings of dances at Cape Darby, Unalaklit, and elsewhere
  southward to the mouth of Kuskokwim river….
     Then a man and a woman took over the floor, performing some of the
  figures of movements such as I had seen used by a couple at Unalaklit.
  Next was a dance of similar character by a half-​grown boy and a girl; their
  motions were greatly varied but in perfect unison; they postured with the
  body inclined first to one side and then to the other, alternating with an
  inclination forward until their heads nearly touched the floor. Many of
  their movements were characterized by the swaying grace that is notable
  in many of the dances of these people.
                                              (Nelson [1899] 1983: 354–​5)

The dances went on, and Nelson was impressed. He had witnessed dances and
festivals in many places, seeing them serve “to enliven the long, depressing
evenings of Arctic winter, and at intervals render the cold, stormy season
a period of enjoyment and feasting” (Nelson [1889] 1983: 357). There is
more to it, however, they also serve to promote friendly intercourse, and the
interchange of products and manufactures of different localities (ibid.). The
dancing was a token of life and merriment that for a time lifted people out
of their dire situation.
   Poetry and narrative were also integral parts of life in the American Arctic
at the time of early ethnographic interest. There were differences between the
groups but there seems to have been a set of stories and poetical forms that
were shared across the Arctic. Rasmussen saw his main calling on the Fifth
Thule Expedition as a collector of narratives and poems, which he certainly
fulfilled. The different groups had each their primary storytellers and themes,
and the art of narration had different standards. In The Intellectual Culture
of the Iglulik Eskimos, he says of their stories that they show “the powers
which rule the world and the life of mankind on earth.” They are part of the
precepts laid down. Rasmussen adds:

  they are described in stories which may often, it is true, –​thanks to a
  poorly developed art of narration –​appear insignificant, but which have
  nevertheless, to the natives themselves, their great power and import-
  ance in the fact that they are regarded as historical documents concerning
  events which once took place, and which are now the source of all infor-
  mation regarding past ages, ancient times when there was hardly any diffe-
  rence between men and animals, and when both men and animals could
  in some inexplicable way be transformed into mighty and terrible spirits.
  For they were all, at one time, the Sea Spirit and her father, the Spirit
  of the Air and the Moon Spirit and the Sun and the Entrail Eater, quite
  ordinary human beings living on the earth like everyone else, without any
  uncommon attributes whatever.
                                                        (Rasmussen 1929: 91)
                                                        Human Terrains 167

The stories did not really move him; they were rather predictable, and
did not change the world. By contrast to the poor narratives (as he saw
them, and of which he heard many alike each other across the continent),
Rasmussen gave in to the poetry of shamans, whose interest in spiritual
matters impressed him. The spiritual expressions and the songs made a
world emerge, confirming –​it seems –​the unity of life and thought. The
perceived extensional indeterminacy of the open Arctic landscape moment-
arily transformed into a strong sense of invisible powers –​in the shape of
poetry. In 1888 Boas wrote:

  Among the arts of the Eskimo poetry and music are by far the most prom-
  inent …Besides the contents their form also is very interesting, as most
  of them have been handed down in unchanged form and their narration
  demands a great deal of art. Many traditions are told in very abridged
  form, the substance being supposed to be known.
                                                 (Boas [1888] 1964: 240)

   Whether old or new, the poetic language seems to enable a comprehensive
view of the times. The abridgement may be seen as a form of poetical com-
pression; a sense of cultural density is created from half-​told ancient stories.
Rasmussen echoed this in his own way, asserting that Inuit knowledge was
based in experience rather than theories, and suggested:

  I am thinking here not only of their songs and poems, their festivals when
  strangers come to their place, but also of the manner in which they regard
  religious questions, wherein they evince great adaptability and versa-
  tility. This it is which always gives their accounts that delightful origin-
  ality which is the peculiar property of those whose theories are based on
  experience of life itself. Their naturalness makes of them philosophers and
  poets unawares, and their simple and primitive orthodoxy gives to their
  presentment of a subject the childlike charm, which makes even the mystic
  element seem credible.
                                                        (Rasmussen 1929: 33)

The poets unawares give voice to a particular sense of place. In narrative,
the voices are represented by characters, while in poetry the voices are
represented by “changing registers of diction, contrastive rhythms, and var-
ieties of tone” (Vendler 1995: 6). This fits the Arctic landscape, being of such
greatness that it cannot be told, only experienced as a whole –​compressing
vastness into intensity. This likens it to poetry rather than epic.
    This again resonates with the maps that people live by, when on their own,
so to speak, and takes us to a more recent analysis made by Hugh Brody in
north-​western Canada, asking people to draw the maps of their various hunts,
reflecting different species. He assumed that the native mappers, especially
168 Human Terrains

older men, and women, would draw different maps from different periods of
their hunting lives; yet despite noticeable changes in the socio-​political envir-
onment and in access to certain hunting grounds, all the mappers insisted that
the lands they had used five years ago were the same as the ones they used
30 years ago.

  Their hunting system, which is based above all on the skillful tracking
  of animals that live all year round within a general area, requires a com-
  paratively large territory. Hunters may use part of this territory infre-
  quently; some locations they may not have seen for twenty years. But
  no part is therefore dispensable: dependence is upon the territory as a
  whole. Successful harvesting of its resources requires knowledge of animal
  movements over the whole area, including places that are rarely, if ever,
  visited. This aspect of the system goes someway to explaining why the
  people of northeast British Columbia did not see the usefulness of separate
  maps for different periods of time. The land-​use maps show a pattern of
  harvesting that is flexible in details but surprisingly constant and exten-
  sive. The Indians say, with their maps, that they continue to use or need
  all of their territories.
                                                           (Brody 2002: 174)

In the Arctic, such view makes sense; the landscape must be seen in its
totality of possibilities. The anthropologist of the present still finds herself
submerged into the sensation of a land somehow beyond immediate reading;
the magnitude of the landscape itself suppresses any linear story –​when seen
from a human perspective. We shall have such a perspective from one of
our travelling companions, Diamond Jenness, on board Mary Sachs, slowly
progressing through the ice and reporting his sensations in a diary from the
early 20th century, published by his son in 1991.

  The night is calm and beautiful. The colouring in the Arctic is wonderfully
  chaste and delicate. Tonight the sky, flecked with cirri and strato-​cumuli,
  is mirrored in the calm water of the sea, with strange effects of depth and
  distance; it requires no effort to imagine no sea at all, only twin skies with
  oneself in between …I watched the Eskimo camp ashore through the bin-
  oculars. There are 15 people living there, five tents, all of calico, three rect-
  angular gable tents and two oval, like the old Eskimo skin tents, stretched
  over curving willow sticks. On the beach a man was sitting watching a
  fish-​net stretched in the water on the edge. Presently a boy went down to
  him and they gathered the fish –​salmon trout they looked like –​eight in
  all I think, and flung them up on the beach; then they sat down side by
  side to chat for a time. Higher up on the bank in front of one of the tents
  four girls or women were sitting. A young fellow approached, apparently
  said something to them, then began to run. One of the girls or women
  sprang up and chased him for a short distance then picked up a clod of
                                                        Human Terrains 169

  earth and threw it after him. Some distance away a man was approaching
  with a gun –​clearly he had been out hunting –​probably for ducks. A little
  while afterwards a little girl in bright red calico dress wandered down to
  the beach. The same girl ran after her and threw her up the bank then
  scrambled up herself and stretched out on the grass beside the others.
  Outside another tent a man was gazing placidly out to sea, very likely
  watching our schooner. The sky was mirrored in the placid water and a
  little way out from the beach a line of small, grounded ice floes stretched
  along for miles in each direction. Now and again, an eider duck or a
  regiment of old squaws flew by. Faint and blurred in the distance, hardly
  perceptible to the naked eye, were the Endicott Mountains. Blue smoke
  curled up from one of the tents and made a dark line across the sky. The
  whole scene was pervaded with the peace and charm of home, with the
  melancholy comfort of a sunset.
                                                        (Jenness 1991: 254–​5)

This is a fine case of the ethnographer being gripped by the place and emer-
ging as a poet, far from the turmoil of the practicalities of travel. Jenness’
praise reflects the fact that from the ship he has enough distance to see the
settlement as floating in a landscape of extreme power, and inhabited by
people, who were young and old, playful, and serious –​and above all, alive
right there. His vision supports the view of terrain as implicitly poetic –​how-
ever deceitful its moods and outside claims.


Times of Wonder
Above, we have visited the human terrain as it struck the early travellers,
including ethnographers, when they first visited and mapped the Arctic
region. Suggesting a certain poetics of place does not, of course, obliterate
more obvious views of the lived landscape, and the comprehensive know-
ledge of the Inuit, as found in Boas’ work:

  The Eskimo have a sort of calendar. They divide the year into thirteen
  months, the names of which vary a great deal, according to the tribes and
  according to latitude of the place. The surplus is balanced by leaving out
  a month every few years, to wit, the month of siringilang (without sun),
  which is of indefinite duration, that name covering the whole time of the
  year when the sun does not rise and there is scarcely any dawn. Thus
  every few years this month is totally omitted, when the new moon and
  the winter solstice coincide. The name qaumartenga is applied only to
  the days without sun but with dawn, while the rest of the same moon is
  called siriniktenga. The days of the month are very exactly designated by
  the age of the moon. Years are not reckoned for a longer space than two,
  backward and forward.
                                                (Boas [1888] 1964: 236–​40)
170 Human Terrains

Such calendar presupposes a volatile mind, as do the sense of larger powers of
the universe, including celestial bodies. The Inuit awareness of a larger envir-
onment, including celestial bodies, often amazed the European explorers,
when they arrived in the Arctic and found learned people. A case was made
by the explorer Charles Francis Hall, who noted about the Inuit:

  They have a tradition of a deluge, which they attribute to an unusually high
  tide. On one occasion, when I was speaking with Tookoolito concerning
  her people, she said, “Innuits all think this earth once covered with water.”
  I asked her why they thought so. She answered, “Did you never see little
  stones, like clams and such things as live in the sea, away up the mountains?”
                                                                (Hall 1865: 572)

One wonders about Hall’s own view of this. He notes it as a tradition but
does not seem to recognize its truth. It may not be as surprising as it seems.
It was not until 1837 that a ‘glacial theory’ was voiced by the Swiss scientist
Louis Agassiz. He suggested that the entire northern hemisphere had once
been covered in ice, of which the Alpine glaciers were remnants. The gla-
cial theory was not easily accepted, as it conflicted with the diluvial theory,
explaining the conspicuous presence of huge boulders from elsewhere by
a major flooding episode, possibly the Biblical flood in the Mosaic trad-
ition (Rudwick 1969). The travelling boulders were of a size that precluded
human interference, and as they could be traced back to faraway sources in
Scandinavia and elsewhere, a grand theory was needed. The diluvial theory
made some sense, and it was supported by prominent naturalists like Charles
Lyell (1797–​ 1875) who was a renowned geologist (Hansen 1970). This
reminds us how recent the understanding of the powerful presence of the ice
in the northern hemisphere is, even though inhabitants of the Arctic did have
a sense of the long, untold story of the Earth.
    The glacial theory was to gain momentum from studies of fossils and
their living exemplars carried out by Swedish expeditions to Svalbard; the
first was undertaken in 1861 under the leadership of Otto Torell, often seen
as the father of Swedish glaciology (Liljeqvist 1993). Through such work,
the Ice Age was identified retrospectively, and reluctantly, from empirical
observations of the ice and of ancient fossils of species that were still living
in the Arctic. This happened in the 19th century, at the pinnacle of polar
exploration. The ice was recognized as a repository of climate histories in
the depth of time. Fossils and other traces in the landscape from earlier ages
emerged as strong mementos about the agency of the ice in the creation of
the entire history of the North (Sörlin 2002: 91–​3). The mutable landscape
expanded in both time and space.
    Knud Rasmussen’s Second Thule Expedition (1916–​1918), being a broad
cartographic and naturalist expedition along the northernmost coast of
Greenland, took place when the Ice Age had become an established truth. The
geologist Lauge Koch was a member of the expedition, and he made important
                                                          Human Terrains 171

geo-​morphological observations, which he passed on to Rasmussen who was
thrilled to learn about the traces from a past time, when there had been no
ice in region (Koch 1919). The current inhabitants of Northwest Greenland
still find fossils from before the Ice Age, collecting them and exhibiting them
on their windowsills. Generally, people in the Arctic live with the remnants
of the Ice Age, as well as the time before it –​as we all do, if rarely voiced. The
terrain is constituted as much by the long-​term development of the Earth, as
by the shifty seasons of contemporary landscapes; it only gradually becomes
human. The times of wonder continue.
