7	Close Quarters
       Intimacy and Travelling Theory




The rich literature left behind by the travellers from the later part of the 19th
century and from the great ethnographic period in early 20th century not
only portray the ‘others’ –​the ethnographic subjects –​but also expose the
relations between the ethnographers and the people studied (Hastrup 2013).
Ethnography requires a degree of closeness. In Knud Rasmussen’s case, the
relations are often foregrounded. He writes that to gain the necessary con-
fidence of these people, “it was absolutely necessary to place myself in their
position. I was not concerned to guide or correct them in any way, but had
come to their country expressly for the purpose of learning what they could
teach” (Rasmussen 1929: 20). To achieve this, he had to work in a way that
parted company from the collector’s method:

  It was necessary for me first of all to gain the complete confidence of my
  Eskimo collaborators before I could begin the work at all. And this was
  only to be achieved by sharing their daily life, living with them under
  precisely the same conditions as they themselves. That I was able to do
  so with complete success was due to the fact that I could speak their
  language, and, from my knowledge of their kinsfolk in Greenland, was
  already familiar with their ideas and habit of mind!
                                                      (Rasmussen 1929: 11)

Clearly, friendship was instrumental to gain access to knowledge; the sharing
of social spaces, was part of this in the Arctic. It still is, where fieldwork
remains a key point. Rasmussen collected, charted, and represented Inuit
life –​publishing new ethnographic knowledge about the Inuit and their
perceptions of their land.
    The relation between the Inuit and himself was based on two equally sig-
nificant if different kinds of intimacy. There was his own intimate knowledge
of Inuit life, which he had shared since childhood, and there was the direct,
often bodily, intimacy, that the study of the roaming Inuit in Arctic America
entailed. Here, ethnographic work often implied a sharing of tight spaces and
available provisions –​and of personal stories. The latter could be difficult

DOI: 10.4324/9781003374145-7
                                                        Close Quarters 175

among the new acquaintances; to win people over, one might have to tell
one’s own stories first, as Rasmussen described in his work with the Caribou
Inuit (Rasmussen 1930: 46).
    Travelling along with the Inuit, whether individuals or groups, and living
with them along the way, was the only way to know the people within their
own landscapes. There was no way of knowing from without, hence the trad-
ition of ethnographic fieldwork in general, and the need for mobility in the
Arctic, where movement defined the hunters’ lives. Approaching unknown
people in their own space, so to speak, implied living with them at their
terms also when it came to housing. Ethnographers would hardly have been
able to understand the people without offering themselves up for a degree of
intimacy, however challenging it might seem for some; keeping distance was
not an option. While early explorers of the region might not have expected
much contact with the natives, they would often meet them and learn from
the encounter when it came to navigate their landscape.


Meeting Others Halfway
Ethnographic practice was a matter of giving in to the local forms of life
at many levels; it was not always easy to find the right approach, however.
Sometimes a basic lack of trust in arriving explorers emerging out of nowhere
would thwart the trust and practical operation of the ‘first meeting’. Let us
have a glimpse of an actual encounter that seemed fraught with uncertainty
and surprise and turned into a rather peculiar zone of contact. It relates to
Roald Amundsen and his crew during their first wintering at Gjøa Haven
(1903–​1904), where some day they spotted a handful of Inuit in the distance.
The word ‘Eskimo’ reverberated among the sailors:

  Now, we had been discussing Eskimo frequently and at great length, but
  for some reason or other we had all but considered it most improbable
  that we should encounter any here. We were now near the end of October,
  and we thought that the Eskimo were extinct, and had been relegated to
  oblivion. And here they were before us.
    All the information we had gathered concerning these Arctic barbarians
  rushed back into our memories. We knew from old books of travel in
  these regions that the North American Eskimo were not always amicably
  disposed. But we had learnt from Ross and Klutschak that the Eskimo
  word “Teima” was the best greeting with which to approach them. It
  meant something like a right hearty “good-​day,” and we had rehearsed
  this word “Teima” in the most varied styles of pronunciation. However,
  we did not dream of being so foolish as to put our whole trust in one feeble
  word. The only right course was to consider the newcomers in the light of
  enemies, and our plan of campaign was laid. I was going with two men to
  meet the enemy; Hansen and Lund volunteered. The rifles were carefully
  examined and loaded to the utmost capacity of the magazine. Down on
176 Close Quarters

  the ice I drew up my troops and inspected them, and even the most crit-
  ical general could not have found fault with their appearance and bearing.
  I myself threw out my chest as well as I could, drew myself up, made a
  regulation right-​about turn, and gave the command “Forward –​march!”
  With my brave men close behind me I advanced, casting a sidelong glance
  up to the deck where the Lieutenant and the cook stood side by side. It
  seemed to me that their expression, at the sight of our little host, was not
  exactly one of admiration, not even of seriousness. Well, I thought, it is
  easy enough to be gay when standing well-​sheltered on board, while we
  were going forth to meet the uncertain, possibly death, here on the open
  field.
                                                 (Amundsen 1908, I: 113–​5)

Meanwhile the Inuit began to descend from their hillside and to approach
them. Amundsen went through several different scenarios and strategic pos-
sibilities, all while the Inuit pointed excitedly in the direction of the formid-
able military contingency of three and obviously decided to move forward
in their skirmishing order. “Well, I thought, rather death with honour than
saving our lives by craven flight. ‘Forward –​march!’ ”. On they marched,
expecting the enemy to take to their bows at any moment.

  Suddenly, there flashed through my mind, heated with the excitement of
  warfare, the word “Teima!” and “Teima” I shout with all the power of my
  lusty lungs. The Eskimo stop short. But now our excitement can no longer
  be restrained, we must bring matters to a crisis, and we rush forward,
  ready for action. Then I hear the call: “Manik-​tu-​mi! Manik-​tu-​mi!” And
  this has a quite familiar sound –​I well remember it from M’Clintock –​it is
  the Eskimo’s friendliest greeting. In a moment we fling away our rifles and
  hasten toward our friends, and with the universal shout of “Manik-​tu-​mi!
  Manik-​tu-​mi!” we embrace and pat each other, and it would be hard to
  say on which side the joy is greater.
                                                   (Amundsen 1908, I: 116)

Amundsen’s meeting with new people in this peculiar way was premised on
his reading of available literature, on his own military training, and some
exaggeration of his own role as commander. The reader is not altogether
sure, whether there is a hint of self-​irony in Amundsen’s description, yet
having read his autobiography as well as a major biography, irony and self-​
deprecation does not seem to have been part of his character (Amundsen
1927; Bomann-​Larsen 2006).
   A more recognizable description of a meeting is found in Diamond
Jenness’ diary from his work with the Copper Inuit, where he travelled along
with a family, as he could not travel on his own, and who adopted him as a
‘son’. He describes the meagre reserves of food in mid-​winter, the sharing of
a squirrel and some rice for a meal, and how hard even the children would
                                                        Close Quarters 177

work to help fetching snow for water among other things; they are hoping
for the man of the house to return with flour and blubber from a place some
days away –​travelling also for ‘native’ provisions stored somewhere else.
Jenness muses on his own position.

  Meanwhile here am I, the ethnologist of the Canadian Arctic Expedition,
  living in a tiny one-​roomed house of driftwood, turf, and snow (that I
  myself helped to construct), with an elderly lady of perhaps 35 to 40
  years and her two children, a girl of about 8 and a boy of 2 years. For
  food we have a little rice, which we boil, frozen fish, which we eat raw,
  skinning and slicing them, I with my sheath-​knife, the other two with
  their curious-​shaped ulos. Tea and sugar supplement this diet, while the
  two or three biscuits are reserved for the children. We have a stove to
  burn wood, a primus stove, and a very little coal oil (or kerosene), skins
  and sleeping clothes, three or four pots, a kettle and a frying pan, with a
  couple of plates, three spoons, and four cups. A string across the ceiling
  near the stove enables us to hang up our boots and mits etc. to dry. For
  light we have four candles besides the one which is now burning. Itaqluq
  and Brick have taken the lantern. For clothes I have an inner and an
  outer deerskin shirt (the latter I seldom need) with a snow shirt of blue
  denham [denim] to wear on top, a pair of deerskin mits made for me by
  Otoyuk (they are bad, for the fur is coming out; the skin from which
  they were made was very poor), a pair of deerskin trousers with burberry
  kneebreeks over them, an inner and an outer pair of deerskin boots. The
  boots are badly made and very cumbersome, so when the weather is not
  very cold and I am not likely to be standing about in the snow I wear two
  pairs of woollen socks, a pair of sheepskin socks, and light deerskin boots
  over them. Then there is my sleeping bag, also of deerskin; its fur too
  comes out much more than it should. My home is for the time being on
  the west side of a large bay which forms the western portion of Harrison
  Bay –​about 80–​10 miles east of Point Barrow. The temperature is appar-
  ently the ordinary mid-​winter temperature here; what it is in degrees I do
  not know, having no thermometer, but it must be uniformly considerably
  below zero [degrees Fahrenheit]. The sun has been invisible for 6 weeks,
  and the twilight lasts about 5 hours. Today the weather was much the
  same as yesterday. No aurora in early part of evening but brilliant one
  after moon had set.
                      (Diary entry for 1 January 1914; Jenness 1991: 104–​5)

The New Year seems to have inspired Jenness to take stock of his dwelling
and clothing; although it is not unusual for him to report on practical
matters, whether cutting firewood or fishing, there is a sense here that he is
making a larger statement about the work conditions of an anthropologist
in the Arctic. One notices the ‘we’ –​having only meagre reserves of food
and restricted means for cooking. He is part of the family for better and for
178 Close Quarters

worse, while also keenly aware of his duty to meticulously report everything,
a duty enforced upon him by his being a member of the Canadian Arctic
Expedition. The closeness between the ethnographer and the people he has
come to study was central to their success. Whether they were moving or
staying over in some region, the ethnographers had to live with the Inuit in
their style and share the available provisions according to season and cir-
cumstance. In Jenness’ case, his life with the adoptive family often implied
hungry periods and demanded his own active participation in hunting and
fishing. He reports conscientiously about successes as well as failures on his
own part.
    This is not due to the diary being ‘private’; it was not. The expedition
members were under duty to hand over their diaries to the patrons of the
expedition after their return. His book on the Copper Inuit gives a similar
feeling of rendering travels and dwellings and thoughts along the road (Jenness
1928). There is little in the way of analysis, as we now understand it, reading
more like a set of reminiscences at some distance, yet in no way detached
from the environment. While providing a valuable historical snapshot, its
anecdotal nature weakens it as ‘knowledge’ –​to be sorted and enfolded into
a scholarly domain, as we would now see it. It belongs to its own time, and it
is a marvellous source for later generations of anthropologists to peruse for
its baring the facts of early Arctic anthropology and the creation of know-
ledge in a virginal terrain. Anthropological work in the Arctic region, among
people who moved and shifted around according to season and game, pri-
marily was to observe and to map. Life is, indeed, anecdotal, and ethnog-
raphy is built upon details even today, whatever else we want to do with it.
While it holds as documentation, it does not always live on as an intellec-
tual feat.
    It is worth to look towards Lévi-​Strauss again; at some distance from his
actual travels in Brazil he asked himself why one becomes an anthropolo-
gist in the first place. He muses on his own experience and suggests more
generally:

  He has, in fact, become an anthropologist for one of two reasons: either
  he finds it a practical method of reconciling his membership of a group
  with his severely qualified acceptance of it –​or, more simply, he wishes
  to turn to advantage an initial attitude of detachment which has already
  brought him, as we say, “half-​way-​to-​meet” societies unlike his own.
                                                    (Lévi-​Strauss 1963: 381)

    Whether or not this rings true for today’s anthropologists, it would
make sense for the early ethnographers, who faced long-​term fieldwork in
unknown places, often with a very tenuous communication with the outer
world. To leave behind the known world of peers and family, required a
strategic detachment from ‘home’. The paradox, according to Lévi-​Strauss,
is that while the detachment relative to the known easily turns into a critical
                                                         Close Quarters 179

stance, anthropologists often immerse themselves uncritically with the new
people. In other words, a revolutionary spirit at home may turn conservative
in the new world.
   Some early ethnographic works in the Arctic are replete with implicit or
explicit disapproval of the Inuit taking on the airs of modernity. Nearing
the end of his long expedition across Arctic America, and having met with
the Western Inuit in Alaska, so deeply influenced by the modern world,
Rasmussen was still able to collect authentic legends and says:

  The journey through the sparsely populated wastes was now at an end,
  and our route henceforward lay through richer and more civilized regions.
  I was glad, however, to have had the opportunity of studying these people
  before they had quite given up their ancient ways of life. As it was, I had
  an abundance of material, and was now more than ever filled with admir-
  ation for the Eskimos themselves.
     I cannot leave this part of the country without saying that I got a strong
  impression of the way in which the Canadian government evidences its
  feeling of responsibility toward the Eskimos. Admittedly the supervision
  is difficult, because the people are scattered far along inaccessible shores.
  Nothing can be done without great expense.
     The plan of allotting reservations to the Eskimos is undoubtedly the
  only right one, for it shields them somewhat in those first meetings with
  civilization which are always the most dangerous for a primitive people.
                                             (Rasmussen [1927] 1999: 302–​3)

This is one way of showing how the ethnographer meets the others halfway,
and it reminds us of the larger historical context and of the zone ‘in between’
that anthropologists enter while working. The idea of reservations, endorsed
by Rasmussen, can be seen as an attempt at leaving some space for a people
who were facing a new world; but ‘reservations’ are rarely helpful.
    Early ethnographers in Arctic America were (partly) travelling in
unknown terrains, if less so in Alaska. To find and to map Inuit movements
and dwellings, invariably implied moving along more or less inhospitable
coasts, setting out from harbours that were known from traders or whalers,
testifying to the long-​time presence of outsiders in the region at least on its
fringes. They had gathered at certain points along the coast from the Bering
Strait eastwards, where whales were abundant, and where they were still out-
side of political restraints; there was no colonization of the inner landscapes
of the region, where ethnographers in search of people were sometimes the
first to describe both the place and its people. Stefansson introduces his first
journey with naturalist Rudolf M. Anderson as follows:

  The district traversed by either Dr. Anderson or myself, singly or both
  of us together, between our reaching the mouth of the Mackenzie River
  in July, 1908, to our leaving the Arctic in September, 1912, is chiefly a
180 Close Quarters

  stretch of the north shore of the North American continent, although we
  penetrated some distance inland on the Colville River in Alaska, in the
  Endicott Mountains on the Horton River, and on the Coppermine. We also
  visited Victoria Island and a score or more of the islands of Coronation
  Gulf; Banks Island we saw only from shipboard. In Alaska the western-
  most point visited by sled in winter by members of our expedition was Icy
  Cape although by water we also visited Cape Lisburne and Point Hope.
     The northern portion of Alaska is in general a low alluvial plain, rolling
  in some places, as level as a Dakota prairie in others, and everywhere
  covered in grass and moss in summer. There are many rivers, mostly
  sluggish, and therefore of an apparent size greater than justified by the
  volume of water they discharge. None of them, with the exception of a
  portion of the Colville, are represented on published maps with even an
  approximation of correctness, and some of the largest, such as the one
  falling into the foot of Smith Bay are unindicated on any chart known
  to me.
                                                        (Stefansson 1914: 7)

The sense of discovery and of pioneering proper mapping of the region
is noticeable; at the time, scholars still worked very much within the
Enlightenment quest for accurate charting of places and people that had so
far been beyond the horizon of scientific interest. Anthropology became part
of the quest for topographical knowledge; being ready to detach themselves
from civilization they would penetrate new and unknown regions. In the pro-
cess, new people emerged as part of the landscape. Having described rivers
and mountains, Stefansson continues:

  The country itself being a low plain, it follows that the coast line is low,
  although there are in some places sea cliffs to the height of thirty or
  forty feet. The villages were strung out along this coast and were built in
  locations determined by the food-​gathering habits of the people. Between
  Point Hope and Point Barrow, the bowhead whale was of paramount
  importance. A village might therefore be located almost anywhere where
  the ice conditions in spring allowed the whales to approach within five
  miles or less from the shore in an ordinary year. Next to the whale, the
  seal was the most important item at Point Barrow and even farther south,
  although the walrus increased in importance west of Icy Cape.
                                                        (Stefansson 1914: 8)

By travelling, Stefansson maps the coast and evaluates it for its affordances
relative to human life; in the process he arrives at a synoptic picture of life
in the Arctic, surprisingly ‘still’ for all the implicit movement of rivers, ice,
animals, and humans. Stefansson literally arrived at his knowledge by going
there, meeting people in person, asking for their names, counting them, and
listing all the small tribes along the coast –​often tagging along with one or
                                                         Close Quarters 181

more of them to the next group. Through this practice, Stefansson made a
string of contact points, summing up to a growing contact zone between
natives and newcomers, including trading stations on the coast.
   Newcomers generally depended on the locals and had to adopt to their
ways of living to even survive. Even the Factories of the Hudson’s Bay
Company, relied on the hunters and knew how the exchange mattered (cf.
Chapter 3). There was no viable relation of domination and subordination,
even if at some points the explorers and ethnographers call up an asymmet-
rical relation. Let us hear Jenness in such a situation among his adoptive
kinsmen:

  A burning passion was inflaming the heart of The Runner, Icehouse’s
  son. He craved for a Winchester rifle, like the .44 he had used so suc-
  cessfully against the polar bear; and he offered me anything he owned
  in exchange for one. If he could not earn it by teaching me the native
  traditions, of which he knew too little, he could at least guide me to the
  eastern natives, introduce me to them as his adoptive brother, and in that
  way help my researches. His wife, Leaf, pleaded in his favour; but she
  made me promise, quite unnecessarily as it happened, that I would protect
  him from the wiles of the women in the Musk-​ox land and restore him
  without delay, whole in body and untouched in heart. “Men are so foolish
  with women,” she added, noting his tolerant smile.
                                              (Jenness 1928 [1959]: 213–​4)

A sense of mutual interest certainly affected the relations between Inuit and
ethnographers, which could not be read as simply colonial relations. The
meeting related above reminds us that in ‘first’ encounters there was always
more to the contact zone than a simple cultural encounter and asymmetrical
social relations. As James Clifford suggests: “contact approaches presuppose
not sociocultural wholes subsequently brought into relationship, but rather
systems already constituted relationally, entering new relations through his-
torical displacement” (Clifford 1997: 7). People in the Arctic stayed within
the bounds of known coordinates, even while living as itinerants; their
placement also changed with each new encounter with outsiders, pushing
their horizon in new directions.
   In the Arctic a hundred years ago, at the time of the ‘great ethnographic
period’, natives and explorers increasingly depended on each other; in the
contact zone this fused the subjects and objects of interest. With Stefansson,
we shall unpack some of the implications of this, as he does unwittingly in his
report from the Anderson/​Stefansson-​expedition. He suggests that the view
of life as either good or bad is largely a function of comfort and security, the
latter in terms of a steady food supply:

  Most travellers (e.g., the English explorers from Parry to M’Clintock) are
  a unit in characterizing the Eskimo’s conditions of life as “wretched.”
182 Close Quarters

  What most of these writers say is that the Eskimo are wretched; what they
  really mean is that they suppose an Englishman would be wretched if he
  had to live as the Eskimo live. In this latter they may be right, though my
  own experience goes against it no less than that of the well-​known English
  travellers David T. Hanbury and Alfred H. Harrison, men who really have
  lived as Eskimo which Parry and the other ships’ commanders, of course,
  never did.
     But whether or not an Englishman could live comfortably in a
  snowhouse on seal meat is beside the question. That their houses and
  clothes are comfortable in winter is sufficiently shown by the experience
  of such men as Peary, who have given them a severer test than the Eskimo
  themselves are called upon to do under ordinary conditions of life. That
  their native foods meet all their wants and wishes, if only they have plenty
  of them, is best shown by the pronounced distaste they invariably have at
  first for any of our foods when invited to try them. After half a century of
  abundance on white men’s goods at Point Barrow, Alaska, caribou meat,
  seal meat, and whale “blackskin” are still considered the three things
  without which no one can reasonably be expected to do, even for a week,
  though tea, sugar, flour, and ship’s biscuits have secured a place on their
  bill of fare.
                                                       (Stefansson 1914: 129)

With the gradual ethnographical mapping of the American Arctic, the images
of the Inuit –​based on half-​way-​meetings –​easily became facts, which again
impinged on the self-​perceptions of both selves and others.


Closeness by Implication
A feature of Arctic exploration around 1900 was an increasing reliance on
local resources, even when shipborne –​as was the case of Robert Peary’s
work in Northwest Greenland between 1891 and 1910. Franklin’s deadly
conserved, canned food (cf. Chapter 2) was replaced by fresh game; on
land, the explorers travelled considerably lighter, and healthier than before.
Importantly, it also meant that travel was neither restricted to the short
summer without pack ice, nor to the coastal regions, in the manner of earlier
expeditions arriving by ship. The way was paved for proper ethnographic
work, as can be seen from a memorandum from the American Museum of
Natural History (of 1908) concerning Stefansson’s ethnographic mission to
the Mackenzie Eskimos:

  The present expedition …differs essentially from ordinary Arctic ventures
  in this, that where it is usual to take along with the party everything
  that the party is expected to need during its stay in the field, in this case
  there will be taken neither food, clothing nor house materials, and there
  will be complete dependence on local resources. Seeing the object of the
                                                          Close Quarters 183

  expedition as chiefly ethnological, it is in a sense desirable that this should
  be so; for the only way in which one can become familiar with the real life
  of a primitive people is to live with them in their houses, and as they do,
  rather than to live near them in one’s own “civilized” way.
                                               (Quoted in Pálsson 2005: 90–​1)

The idea of ethnographers having to live with the natives in their houses or
tents was widely shared among the Arctic ethnographers; it was also a matter
of sheer survival in the icy landscape. By comparison we note that fieldwork
in warmer regions was often conducted at some personal distance from the
natives, and more directly enmeshed in colonial relations. A case in point is
Arthur Radcliffe-​Brown’s early fieldwork on the Andaman Islands, where he
chiefly conducted his fieldwork around the penal colony, where the islanders
came to trade; this method was reasonable under the circumstances, and here
it serves mainly to underscore how different landscapes –​physical and pol-
itical –​make different degrees of closeness possible (Radcliffe-​Brown 1922
[1964]; Hastrup 2013).
    The ethnographers in the barren arctic landscape had to live like, and with,
the natives not only to observe but also to survive. To adopt the ‘native point
of view’ was a matter of survival, while for Bronislaw Malinowski (coining
the notion), it was more of an ethnographic strategy, where the “goal is,
briefly, to grasp the native’s point of view, his relation to life, to realise his
vision of his world” (Malinowski 1922: 25). Nevertheless, he remained in
his own tent and spent a lot of time at a neighbouring mission (Young 2004;
Hviding 2020). In the Arctic we note that for the contemporary Stefansson,
the native point of view was implicit in his first Arctic Expedition (1908–​
1912). In his book from the expedition, tellingly called My Life with the
Eskimo, he writes:

  They took me into their houses and treated me hospitably and courte-
  ously, but exactly as if I were one of them. They gave me clothes to wear
  and food to eat, I helped them in their work and joined their games, until
  they finally forgot that I was not one of them, and began to live their lives
  before my eyes as if I were not there. This gave me a rare opportunity to
  know them as they are.
                                                        (Stefansson 1924: 2)

This life included a native common-​law-​wife, by whom he had a son. He
met her on his second expedition (with Rudolf Andersen, 1913–​         1918)
and simply writes in his autobiography, that “I boarded the Karluk with
Natkusiak and a widowed Eskimo woman, Fanny Pannigabluk, who came
from Natkusiak’s part of Alaska. She was an excellent seamstress, something
no party wintering on the Arctic coast can afford to be without” (Stefansson
1964: 45; Pálsson 2001: 42; 2002: 292). As Pálsson has shown, Pannigabluk
was much more than a seamstress, she was also a mistress, and not least
184 Close Quarters

a collaborator (Pálsson 2005). As collaborator and a provider of informa-
tion on sundry ethnographic issues, she was decidedly part of his success.
Significantly, around the time of her giving birth to her son by Stefansson, he
took many notes about reproduction, childbirth, motherhood, and related
matters, such as: “Pannigabluk tells that among the western Eskimos (gener-
ally, she thinks) a woman who wishes her (unborn) child to be healthy and
strong should eat the cartilage between the bones of the knee-​joint of the
caribou” (29 January 1911; in Pálsson 2001: 240–​1)
   Sexuality had a distinct, shameless flavour in the lands of the new people;
this infected some of the newcomers to the region, who might have a hard
time tempering their lust, as we have seen it for Father Petito and a number
of captains and sailors (cf. Chapter 2). The increasing presence of foreigners,
be they traders or ethnographers, opened up a new mode of intimacy that
contrasted with similar experiences elsewhere under colonial rule (e.g., Stoler
2008). The crowded dwelling facilities in the Arctic made it impossible for
the male explorers to stand aloof from the natives, male or female. They
had no choice but to shun the notion of privacy. Under such conditions and
given their dependence on women for making and repairing their fur dresses,
intimacy was a fact whether sexual or not.
   Amundsen who was not beyond appraising female charms also had a keen
eye for sexual liberty among the natives he met, describing some cases with
an ill-​disguised curiosity. The following passage from his sojourn with the
Nechilli (as he called them), where both men and women are readily assessed
for their favours, is a case in point:

  The third brother, Oyara, was the best of the lot, and one of the most
  attractive types in the tribe. He was about twenty-​seven, tall, dark, and
  with a friendly winning smile. He and his bosom friend, Ahiva, were
  among the swells of the tribe. They both had young sweet wives, Alo-​Alo
  and Alerpa. When I first met these two couples, Oyara was married to
  Alo-​Alo, and I do not remember having ever seen a more loving couple.
  When I met them again, however, a little later, the conditions had changed.
  Alo-​Alo was now occupying the housewife’s place in Ahiva’s tent. When
  I intimated that I must either have been, or was now under, some misap-
  prehension, the little coquette lent towards her husband, took his head
  between his hands and rubbed noses with him, as much as to say: “look
  here, there can be no mistake about this.” “Rubbing noses” among the
  Eskimo is equivalent to “kissing” among Europeans. Of course I took in
  the situation at once. This sort of thing, in fact, is not quite unknown in
  “Kablunaland” either, but it was the only case of exchange of wives that I
  had actually witnessed. Both of these men were smart huntsmen, and their
  wives, young as they were, were capable housewives. Their tents were
  situated near each other, in a charming little spot, where, I should say,
  love could thrive.
                                                    (Amundsen 1908: 308–​9)
                                                       Close Quarters 185

The case was unusual because the couples remained in the same spot.
Sometimes men would simply share a wife, but it was not as easy as it
may sound. Jealousy and violence were not uncommon in such situ-
ations (Rasmussen [1927] 1999: 232). Most ethnographers seek rational
explanations, often demographic, such as the lack of women due to female
infanticide. But there was probably also a measure of desire and a masculine
exertion of freedom; a woman could not invite another man to share her
husband’s rights on her, but her husband could.

  “Changing wives” for a short time is of common occurrence. The man’s
  position is altogether one of considerable freedom, and it is regarded as
  perfectly natural that he should have intercourse with other women as
  often as an opportunity occurs. Consequently, a woman left alone while
  her husband is out hunting is exposed to some risk from the advance of
  other men; should she give way to any such, she will as a rule be punished
  by her husband. On occasion, however, it is the co-​correspondent who is
  called to account, the matter being settled by a bout of fisticuffs. All the
  men are practised boxers.
     The freedom thus claimed by the man in marital relations is by no
  means extended to the woman, who in this respect is considered her
  husband’s property. Changing wives is effected without the least regard
  to the feelings of the respective wives, who are not consulted in the matter
  at all. Even where a woman definitely wishes to remain “faithful” to her
  own spouse, her constancy would not only be unappreciated, but would
  be regarded as disobedience, and punishable as such. It is indeed regarded
  as a sin: “the spirits do not like it.”
     Natural desire and economical necessity, combined with the fact that
  there are not enough women to go round, give rise inevitably to keen
  competitions among the men, as well as to quarrels, not infrequently with
  fatal termination.
                                            (Rasmussen [1927] 1999: 232–​3)

Natural desire and economic necessity also sometimes landed ethnographers
in intimate relations with native women, as it did explorers and traders all
along the coast. Thus Amundsen, who often commented on the relative
beauty of Eskimo women, seems to have had a local ‘wife’ in the region of
Gjøa Haven; he found some of them truly attractive, according to his diaries
(Bomann-​Larsen 2006: 41–​2). Allegedly, the crew on board Gjøa had found
him to be absent rather often and suspected that there was a woman some-
where, but Amundsen chose to keep it secret. When, during the Fifth Thule
Expedition, Rasmussen was in the neighbourhood of Gjøa Haven, he asked a
local man if he had known and remembered Amundsen, to which he proudly
answered: ‘Indeed –​we shared a wife!’ (Knud Rasmussen’s diary from the
Fifth Thule Expedition 20 May 1923, Royal Library of Copenhagen; Hastrup
2010: 355).
186 Close Quarters

   Women had a marked position among the Inuit; they were vital to the
survival of the community, including temporary guests from elsewhere. The
division of labour was such that women were needed not only to prepare
food but also to process the skin and make the clothes; they were essential
as seamstresses. But there was more to it, according to the old bear hunter
Sorqaq, whom Rasmussen met among the Inughuit in 1903. Sorqaq urged
Rasmussen to take a wife and explained himself like this:

  You should understand that it is not the right thing for a man to travel
  all over the country, as you are doing, young and unmarried. You will get
  a bad reputation, and expose yourself to be made game of. Seest thou,
  a bachelor is a man who is rejected because he is a poor provider. For a
  woman is one of the things that a man should have. Here, a woman is the
  first thing a man takes; after that come dogs, kayak, and last and most
  difficult of all, a gun. All this thou hast already. But who is to look after
  thy things, who will warm thy bed, and caress thee? Up here a young man
  always travels with a wife; sometimes, if necessary, with a borrowed one.
                                                        (Rasmussen 1908: 47)

Rasmussen was not immune to female charms –​and vice versa. In Denmark
women readily fell for the young ‘Aladdin’ (Fenger 1979), who by all accounts
was irresistible. When on expedition, though, his work took centre stage; but
it always would in his writing, of course.
    Among the established traders and whalers on the Canadian North Coast,
there seems to have been the ‘usual’ sexual encounters between Inuit and
settlers or traders from elsewhere. Early explorers and ethnographers in
the Arctic frequently mention mixed-​race when they meet people of uncer-
tain origin. It seems to be of more concern to the newcomers than to the
locals, who are, after all, used to intermingle with other groups. Many
are the remarks of wives or husbands from ‘elsewhere’ that pop up in the
descriptions. Given the fewness of people and their itinerant practices, it is
hardly surprising that mixed marriages occurred, and that children might
happen across classificatory boundaries; these are after all an abstraction.
    There were many mixed marriages along the northern coast, some of them
long-​standing, and being mentioned in several reports. One of them relates
to the allegedly rather shady Dane at Barrow, Captain Klengenberg, who
married an Inupiat woman and had six children by her (Pálsson 2005: 87ff).
Klengenberg directed Stefansson’s attention towards the Copper Inuit –​this
was his contribution to anthropology. His oldest daughter was married to
the Norwegian Storker Storkerson, who helped Stefansson on the last part of
the Canadian Arctic Expedition, when Stefansson was rather ill (ibid.: 150–​
3). When, after his sojourn with Stefansson, Storkerson went to Norway to
investigate whether reindeer husbandry could be transplanted to the American
Arctic, he never returned, and Storkerson’s family moved to a mission for
support (ibid.: 154–​6). There were many cases of intimate relations between
                                                        Close Quarters 187

natives and settlers; some were long-​standing and life-​changing, as shown by
Jenness (1965) for Labrador, but there were also more passing encounters
that we know from most contact zones, sometimes resulting in offspring.
   A well-​known case is that of Peary, who during his long and repeated
sojourns in the region of the Polar Inuit sired three children by two different
women; also his mate, Mathew Henson, and Captain Bartlett both had sons
by local women. It was well-​known, and the descendants have in some cases
taken the names, even if it was not until the 1970s that the American Peary
family publicly (and hesitantly) acknowledged the relations (Counter 1991).
Today, new generations hardly speculate on their name, the use of which
becomes rarer. When, in 1885, Peary “articulated his vision of the ideal
exploration party,” likening it to “the physical structure of a tough, hardy
man,” he wrote:

  Following this analogy, one intelligent white man would represent the
  head, two other white men selected solely for their courage, determin-
  ations, physical strength, and devotion to the leader would represent the
  arms, and the driver and the natives the body and legs. The presence of
  women an absolute necessity to keep the men contended.
                          (Robert E. Peary Papers, cited by Dick 2001: 382)

Peary further remarked: “Feminine companionship not only causes greater
contentment but as a matter of both physical and mental health and the reten-
tion of the top notch of manhood it is a necessity” (cited by Dick 2001: 382).
Ad hoc intimacy between (male) newcomers and local women was not only
seen as an implication of local practices; for Peary it also seemed to be a
precondition for proper work in the Arctic. Much more could be said about
his and others’ relations with the Inughuit women, yet this is not the place.
Some relations had other consequences than offspring, such as new kinds
of disease among the natives, of which Jenness writes in his diary from the
Mackenzie area:

  Dr. Anderson told me that tuberculosis had been very common around
  the Mackenzie and along the coast to the east and west and that its action
  seemed to be very rapid. A large proportion of the population in the same
  region had suffered from syphilis and many from gonorrhoea, but in many
  cases they appeared to have recovered even without treatment from the
  whaling captains. Now there appear to be very few cases of the primary
  stage. He knew of several instances of insanity where the victims had for-
  merly been syphilitic. The gonorrhoea frequently resulted in stricture, but
  the patients had recovered without aid.
                                                 (Jenness [1914] 1991: 288)

The close connections had their price. Yet, there were also gifts; the birth
of children by women who had been visited by passing travellers was not
188 Close Quarters

necessarily a disaster. Rasmussen relates a case of this, when he –​along
with Qâvigarsuaq (Miteq) and Arnarulúnguaq –​had left Repulse Bay and
reached Depot Island. First, they noted ancient ruins and other traces of
past life.

  All these ruins were the work of the “Tunit,” and from all they could see,
  this highly developed coastal race with their kayaks and umiaks, must
  have been identical with the Eskimos that came into Greenland from these
  regions a thousand years ago. Both Miteq and Arnaruluk felt thoroughly
  at home in these surroundings. Much of what they had met with among
  the living natives of the present day was strange to them, but these relics
  of the dead from a bygone age were such as they knew from their own
  everyday life at home.
                                            (Rasmussen [1927] 1999: 44–​5)

It is striking that the Thule they had left just two years ago, was now seen as
contemporary with the ancient Thule Culture moving east from Alaska (c.
1000–​1200 C E ), while the ‘modern’ conditions in the American Arctic felt
foreign –​except for the language and stories. Once, on their way westwards
guided by an American Inuit, Inujaq, they were told about the wars between
people in Repulse Bay and the people they were nearing, now concentrated
on Depot Island –​wars that had almost exterminated the inhabitants. Now,
warfare was over, and they could safely proceed towards their destination.
When arriving they saw a village with several snow huts and wooden frames
for skins and inner garments. Miteq called out, in jest:

  “Here we are: here we are at last,” a piece of mischievous fun that brought
  out the inmates at once. There was a confusion of cries and shouting, as
  of women in a flutter, a sound of rapid steps along the passage way, and
  out among us tumbled –​a black girl. A little negro lady as black as one
  could wish to see.
     This was perhaps the most surprising encounter we have experienced to
  date. I noticed also, that the sight was almost too much for Miteq, who
  started back and stood with wide eyes fixed in wonder on the unexpected
  figure. Here we were come all the way from Greenland to seek out other
  peoples of the farthest northern lands; and all of a sudden we found our-
  selves face to face with a child of the tropic South; a creature of the sun
  leaping up out of the snow!
                                             (Rasmussen [1927] 1999: 46–​7)

The girl went back in –​and came out again with three older women ‘of
normal Eskimo type’; one of them took charge, asking who the guests were,
and explaining that the men were out hunting. She also introduced her
companions and when it came to the dark young lady,
                                                         Close Quarters 189

  informed us that this was her daughter by a stranger, a man who had
  come from a land where it was always summer. A remarkable man, she
  explained, one who never went out hunting himself, but devoted his life
  to the task of preparing rare feasts and luscious dishes for his fellows. He
  had come to their country on a great ship and had spent the winter in
  their huts.
                                             (Rasmussen [1927] 1999: 47–​8)

For Rasmussen everything now seemed simple; the girl’s father had been a
“negro cook” on an American whaler. Global travel and intimate relations
were ingrained into the Inuit world.


Freedom and Friendship
Among the early images of the Inughuit in Northwest Greenland was
their living in perfect liberty, fuelling a dream of total freedom among the
newcomers. Eivind Astrup, the Norwegian companion of Peary on his first
two expeditions (1891–​    1892 and 1893–​    1894), wrote a book about the
neighbours of the North Pole, as he called them, and stated:

  Among the Esquimaux at Smith’s Sound there are no chiefs in authority
  over the others. Every one is his own master, and no one interferes with
  his neighbour’s business; but the elder man who once has had, or still
  possesses, more than ordinary skill, seems to exercise a certain unassuming
  authority over his comrades in a settlement. It may be safely said that lib-
  erty is the guiding principle among these happy citizens; not that liberty
  which is bounded by the strict letter of the law, but liberty as complete as
  one could hope for in this world, the liberty of mutual confidence.
                                                           (Astrup 1898: 290)

Apart from their liberty, Astrup also praised the intelligence of these people,
including their inventive powers and mechanical gifts. In his, as well as in
Peary’s eyes they were not lacking in essential respects. For Peary, the liberty
also included the easy ways (to his eye) in which the Eskimos married. It was
totally without ceremony and was not ‘for life’. Wives could be changed, and
exchanged, and they could remarry, if widowed. He gives the following gen-
eral verdict on their character:

  In disposition and temperament these people are a race of children,
  simple, kindly, cheerful, and hospitable. In powers of endurance, in cer-
  tain directions, they probably are not surpassed by any other known race,
  and in their ingenuity and the intelligence displayed in making use, to the
  fullest extent, of every one of the few possibilities of their country which
  can assist them to live and be comfortable, they are, in my opinion, ahead
190 Close Quarters

  of any other aboriginal race. Of arts, sciences, culture, manufactures, and
  such other adjuncts to civilization, they know nothing.
     There is no form of government among them, no chief, each man being
  supreme in his own family, and literally and absolutely his own master.
  Such a thing as real-​estate interest is unknown to them. Every man owns
  the whole country and can locate his house and hunt where his fancy
  dictates. The products of the hunts are common property with slight
  limitations.
                                                       (Peary 1898, I: 492–​3)

Anthropologists generally insisted that the natives of the Arctic were far from
lost or depressed, unless the hunt failed, or epidemics swept. As Stefansson
noted, on the basis of conventional images of the barren and lifeless Arctic,
one would assume that the Eskimos were wretched, but on closer inspection
they lead happy lives with ample leisure for “games, story-​telling, singing
dancing and the enjoyment of life in general, and most explorers will agree
that an Eskimo laughs as much in a month as the average white man in a
year” (Stefansson 1921: 24). This is partly owed to the healthy diet of their
uncivilized state.

  When we realize that the Eskimos secure their living with little labor as
  compared with the rest of us, and that they are healthy and happy, it
  dawns on us that they are really inhabiting a desirable country. Nearly
  every close observer from Sir John Richardson down has pointed out that
  on the continent of North America the relation of the Eskimos to the
  Indians south of them has always been aggressive, and though there is fear
  on both sides, still the Indians are more frightened of the Eskimos than
  the Eskimos are of the Indians. It follows, then, that the Eskimos have
  not been crowded by a more powerful people into an undesirable place
  which they now inhabit. There is no more evidence that the Eskimos have
  been crowded north by the Indians than there is evidence that the present
  population of England are living there because of being crowded north by
  the French.
                                                      (Stefansson 1921: 25)

In other words, the Eskimos live there by their own choice, are happy enough
with their life, and do not want to move into other regions. This content-
edness is problematic for Stefansson, who finds that one “attribute of high
civilization is a development of the spirit of adventure, of the will to experi-
ment” (ibid.). Thus, despite his apparent admiration for the natives of the
Arctic, they fall short in Stefansson’s judgement, due to the lack of a forward
push –​the will to risk what they have for something unknown. This did not
tally with his own modern outlook.
   The friendships that Rasmussen struck with the Inuit were genuine, while
framed by an epoch in European history, when peripheral ‘others’ were seen
                                                         Close Quarters 191

to be doomed. Rasmussen’s quest for knowledge about the Inuit was driven
by a wish to get there before it was too late. Like Stefansson, he mused over
the relationship between the Inuit and the whites:

  One may often hear people who know nothing of the life of “savage”
  tribes suggest that these should be left to live their own way and not have
  a civilization enforced upon them. My own experiences in these particular
  regions have convinced me that the white man, though bringing certain
  perils in his train does nevertheless introduce a gentler code, and in many
  ways lightens the struggle for existence.
     On the other hand, one must not judge these children of nature too
  harshly. They are in fact still in but an early stage of evolution as human
  beings. And we should bear in mind that life in these inhospitable regions,
  exposed to the cruellest conditions and ever on the verge of extermination
  is not conducive to excessive gentleness.
                                                (Rasmussen [1927] 1999: 236)

Around the same time, Diamond Jenness had addressed the fateful encounter
between the different claimants to the territory. In 1928, on the final pages of
his book on the People of the Twilight, Jenness writes what he calls L’Envoi,
in which he laments the fate of the people with whom he had worked some
12 years earlier:

  The country has changed since 1916. Even while we said our farewells
  the traders were all heading eastward to the new land where beautiful
  white fox skins were valueless and a fortune could be gained in a night.
  White men have invaded it from every quarter, and the twilight of ignor-
  ance and superstition is yielding to the dawn of greater knowledge. Bows
  and arrows have passed with other weapons into the darkness of the past,
  and a new mechanical age has brought magazine rifles, steel traps, and
  even gasoline engines. The caribou are passing with the bows and arrows;
  of all the herds that once crossed the narrow strait to Victoria Island
  hardly one now reaches the Arctic shore. Strange diseases are making
  their appearance, diseases that the old-​time Eskimo never heard of and for
  which he has no name. The stern laws of civilization have descended on
  the land; no longer is infanticide tolerated or the blood-​feud allowed to
  run its course. “Furs, furs, more furs,” is the white man’s cry. “Without
  furs there is no salvation, no ammunition to shoot the scattered game and
  satisfy your hungry children.” The tribal bands where each man toiled
  for all and shared his food in common are resolving into their constituent
  families, and every family vies with the rest in the race for wealth and
  worldly prosperity.
     Whither will it all lead? Fifty years ago the cyclone swept over the
  Eskimos of the Mackenzie River Delta, and of its two thousand inhabitants
  a scant two hundred survive. Fifty years earlier it struck Baffin Island with
192 Close Quarters

  a similar result. Will history, fifty years hence, record the same fate for this
  twilight land where for two years we carried out our mission? Were we the
  harbingers of a brighter dawn, or only messengers of ill-​omen, portending
  disaster?
                                                  (Jenness [1928] 1959: 246–​8)

In the second edition (of 1959), Jenness added an Epilogue to L’Envoi of the
first edition (1928). In the Epilogue he refers to the time of his fieldwork and
asks whether the current situation of death and disease was inevitable. “Had
we been more conscious of our responsibilities could we have spared them
so rude an awakening, so painful an entry into what we call the civilized
world?” These are questions that cannot be answered, and Jenness seeks to
qualify his concern with reference to his long-​gone adoptive sister among the
Copper Eskimo, Jennie.

  The children of Jennie’s generation who escaped the new diseases under-
  went a social and economic revolution. Even as we sailed away traders
  entered their country seeking fox-​furs, always fox-​furs; and for those
  pelts, so useless for real clothing, they offered rifles, shot-​guns, steel
  tools, and other goods that promised to make life much easier. So the
  Eskimos abandoned their communal seal hunts and scattered in isolated
  families along the coast in order to trap white foxes during the winter
  months when the fur of that animal reaches its prime. Their disposal
  loosened the old communal ties that had held families together. The men
  no longer labored for the entire group, but hunted and trapped each
  one for his family alone. Their diet and costume changed: more and
  more it became the costume of the white man. For they had to purchase
  their winter’s supply of food from the trading stores, since trapping left
  them no time to hunt seals; and they had to buy clothing too from the
  same stores when the rifles that the traders supplied them exterminated
  the herds of caribou or drove them into districts far remote. So the new
  barter economy –​furs in exchange for the goods of civilization –​made
  life harder instead of easier, more complicated instead of simple. The
  commercial world of the white man had caught the Eskimos in its mesh,
  destroyed their self-​sufficiency and independence, and made them eco-
  nomically its slaves. Only in one respect did it benefit them: it lessened
  the danger of those unpredictable famines which had overtaken them
  every ten or fifteen years, bringing suffering and death to young and old
  without distinction.
                                              (Jenness [1928] 1959: 249–​50)

It is significant how the abandonment of the old life was seen to destruct
Inuit lives. It was hardly just a sense of nostalgia; it would have been a real
sorrow. Jenness was serious, when he said that the white man had made their
lives harder.
                                                        Close Quarters 193

Ethnographic Ambiguity
The first major ethnographic expeditions to the Arctic took place at a time
when expeditions were mounted all over the globe, seeking to understand
the origins of and inter-​relations between people. ‘Anthropology’ was borne
between hard science and soft ethnographic sensation. An early example is
Alfred C. Haddon’s Torres Straits Expedition in 1898–​1899, opening the
scholarly world to a new kind of anthropology:

  Of particular note are the advances developed by the Expedition
  members, largely through the application of natural science models to
  anthropological study. A central concern was how to collect ethnographic
  “facts” and they pioneered the integration of field research with scholarly
  interpretation.
                                                (Herle and Rouse 1998: 15)

    The Torres Strait Expedition has been acclaimed as a watershed in British
anthropology, paving the way for systematic ethnographic enquiry (Stocking
1983: 83–​4). It may have been ‘systematic’ at some level, but it was also
incidental. Haddon himself was trained as a zoologist and has been credited
with importing the term “fieldwork” into anthropology from zoology (Herle
and Rouse 1998: 17). In the preface to the first publication from the exped-
ition, Haddon wrote how all while investigating the marine zoology of the
region in 1888–​1889, he had devoted his spare time to recording the cus-
toms and beliefs of the (human) natives. He wanted to publish a Memoir
on the Ethnography of the Islands of Torres Straits, but when going over
the material he found “that it was too deficient to make into a satisfac-
tory monograph” (Haddon 1901: v). He therefore decided to return to
the Torres Straits “to collect more data, with a view to making, with the
aid of colleagues, as complete a study of the people as was practicable”
(ibid.). Haddon assembled a team that was to set the tone of anthropology at
Cambridge for generations to come. Between them they covered psychology
and physiology, in addition to Haddon’s own zoological field –​all of them
merging into an incipient anthropology, embracing both sedentary and trav-
elling fieldwork. In contrast to the close, physical, and emotional relations
emerging among the Arctic ethnographers, Haddon and his crew kept the
subjects at arms’ length, aided by foreign instruments and measures; they
wanted data.
    The course taken by anthropology in the Arctic was different when it came
to collective efforts. Boas had made his impact on the east coast and was
training the younger generations in fieldwork, where language and customs
were to take centre stage, as was the material culture, such as reported from
the Baffin Land Inuit. It later befell Boas to lead The Jesup North Pacific
Expedition (1897–​1902); it had some Arctic flavour and was designed to
trace the relations between groups that were otherwise seen as quite distinct.
194 Close Quarters

It embraced a vast geographical area, integrating diverse people into a com-
prehensive comparative project.

  The Jesup North Pacific Expedition organized early in 1897 to study
  the relationship between the peoples of Asia and northwestern North
  America, was a research project of such scientific importance and geo-
  graphical scope that it still ranks as the foremost expedition in the history
  of American anthropology. It was funded by Morris K. Jesup (1830–​
  1908), a rich American banker and philanthropist, then president of the
  American Museum of Natural History, the sponsoring institution.
                                                         (Freed et al. 1988: 7)

Jesup had made his fortune in banking and railroad finance, but it was Boas,
who at the time was assistant curator of the Museum, who directed the
expedition. After his early studies of the Baffin Land Inuit, he had become
interested in the Northwest Coast culture and art and, together with the
director of the Museum, he proposed a six-​year long expedition. This was
granted, a team was assembled on both sides of the Bering Strait, and then
the struggles began –​not only between the scientists who were caught up in
difficult situations, politically and institutionally, but also in relation to the
‘natives’ –​possibly feeling unduly objectified and overpowered. Each of the
participants had the task of writing an ethnographic report and collect cul-
tural artefacts from a select community. ‘The fields’ were seen as singular and
located –​but proved rather more complex than presumed.
   Boas worked intensively with his own material, while also seeking to keep
the collective ambition alive. He had to provide a truly comparative ethno-
graphic material, but after six years the sponsor had grown impatient with
Boas for not yet having produced his concluding volume. The sponsor with-
drew his financial support to further work and publication, and by 1905 the
atmosphere had become so poisonous that Boas resigned. The pressure on
him to write a summary volume was too much, given that not all the ethno-
graphic material had been published and that the Russian colleagues had
been caught up in political turmoil, but also –​apparently –​because there was
an inherent contradiction between general and specific ambitions of the com-
parative project. When writing on his Kwakiutl material, Boas says:

  It seemed to me well to make the leading point of view of my discussion,
  on the one hand an investigation of the historical relations of the tribes
  to their neighbours, on the other hand a presentation of the culture as it
  appears to the Indian himself. For this reason, I have spared no trouble to
  collect descriptions of customs and beliefs in the language of the Indians,
  because in these the points that seem important to him are emphasized,
  and the almost unavoidable distortion contained in the descriptions given
  by the casual visitor and student is eliminated.
                                  (Boas 1909: 309; also in Wike 1957: 302)
                                                         Close Quarters 195

What we sense here, is the deep-​seated ambiguity inherent in ethnographic
practice. This goes part of the way to explain why a ‘conclusion’ was diffi-
cult; Boas’ view of proper ethnographic records did not fit the larger design
to trace the relations between the groups. These days one could add that
stationary fieldwork and the ‘native point of view’ (which did not sit well
with the mandate of the expedition) was always a moot point. ‘Native lives’
never stand still, and ‘ethnography’ is always a point of view. How can they
be part of the same picture, and how can they not? In the Arctic, living so
closely with ‘others’, as the Arctic ethnographers did, in some ways made
their ethnographies rather thin, being lived rather than thought, and docu-
mentation before analysis. Yet, they were instrumental to the making of new,
vital knowledge about the elusive hunters in the north.
   The long-​time inhabitants of the American Arctic were not outside of the
world and its many histories, and they were often eager to share their know-
ledge and views. The ‘discoverers’ from elsewhere were impressed, all while
their own position shifted. Nansen suggested:

  Of all the races of the earth, that of the Eskimo is one that has established
  itself farthest north. His world is that of sea-​ice and cold, for which nature
  has not intended human beings. In his slow, stubborn fight against the
  powers of winter he has learnt better than any other how to turn these to
  account, and in these regions, along the ice-​bound shores, he developed his
  peculiar culture, with its ingenious appliances, long before the beginning
  of history. As men of the white race pushed northward to the “highest
  latitudes” they found traces of this remarkable people, who had already
  been there in times long past; and it is only in the last few decades that
  anyone has succeeded in penetrating further north than the Eskimo, partly
  by learning from him or enlisting his help. In these regions, which are his
  own, his culture was superior to that of the white race, and from no other
  people has the arctic navigator learnt so much.
                                                              (Nansen 1911: 66)

At close quarters, Europeans acknowledged the superior knowledge of
the Inuit, when it came to survival in the Arctic. In Nansen we meet an
impressed traveller, but also a call for acknowledging the vitality and tech-
nical diligence required to survive in the Arctic. People learn from each
other and enlist knowledge from many sources; ethnographic work itself
implies giving in to the multiplicity of views and experiences within a par-
ticular landscape.
   With reference to Stefansson, Pálsson has suggested that a particular
arcticality emerged in the early years of ethnographic work in the Arctic.
“While that notion is not Stefansson’s, it seems implicit in his work, much
of which characterizes the Arctic as both the home of howling, exotic wil-
derness (the source of ‘strange’ knowledge and ancient wisdom) and a semi-​
domestic, ‘friendly’ space” (Pálsson 2002: 277). The ambiguity of studying
196 Close Quarters

people’s lives as they unfold, demands a particular way of thinking –​both
within and beyond ethnographic documentation.
   This applies also to Rasmussen’s two-​year long travel towards Western
Arctic with Qâvigarsuaq and Arnarulúnguaq –​once the other members of
the Fifth Thule Expedition had returned to Denmark. The three of them
were friends and partners en route, but most of all –​they were together. Co-​
travelling sharpened their observations, including Rasmussen’s view of the
surroundings and its affordances; where others would have seen strangers,
they saw fellow humans. There was no need for mystification here; people
had solved the problems of living in the Arctic all by themselves. Lévi-​Strauss
has an observation on the surprise held by certain travellers that ‘other’
people could survive the way they lived; of course, people have known to
solve problems, ‘with which we are still struggling’. He continues:

  To take one instance only: it is now only a few years since we discovered
  the physical and physiological principles on which are based the Eskimoes’
  costume and manner of life. If they can exist in these conditions, it is not
  from long conditioning, or from an exceptional physical constitution,
  but from their discovery of scientific principles of which we had until
  lately no idea at all. So true was this that it also exploded the pretentions
  of those explorers who had claimed to have “improved upon” Eskimo
  costume; the results were the opposite of those that had been hoped
  for. The Eskimoes had already arrived at the perfect solution: all that
  we needed, to be convinced of this, was to grasp the theory which lay
  beneath it.
                                                       (Lévi-​Strauss 1963: 383)

Acknowledging this is to acknowledge that anthropology always implies
meeting people halfway. Lévi-​Strauss reminds ethnographers of the fact that
the Inuit were the true masters of the region, unambiguously.


Shared Lives
Above, we have touched on some of the relations between Inuit and
ethnographers that were established in the Arctic. They were of various
kinds, as were the people. It has been part of anthropological self-​criticism
to speak of imperialism and unequal relations between the ethnographers
and their ‘objects’ of study, but we should make room for nuances, from
region to region, and from ethnographer to ethnographer. Sometimes it does
not seem right to speak of imperial manners, even when it comes to ‘trav-
elling passions’. Gísli Pálsson sensitively describes the relationship between
Stefansson and his seamstress and common law wife, Fanny Pannigabluk,
and clearly wants to tread carefully (Pálsson 2005). Stefansson acknow-
ledges his son, teaches him to read and write and supports both him and his
mother for years. Yet there is something missing, it seems, maybe a louder
                                                         Close Quarters 197

claim to fatherhood and a more direct acknowledgement. There seems to be
a lingering uncertainty about what one should think about the relation:

  Judging from Stefansson’s field diaries Pannigabluk has a much more
  important role than Stefansson’s publications indicate. Her name appears
  quite often in Stefansson’s diaries (Pàlsson 1998). Pannigabluk was not
  just a seamstress who made fine clothes from animal skins, she would act
  as interpreter and narrator. In effect, she was Stefansson’s closest ethno-
  graphic collaborator –​his key informant.
                                                        (Pálsson 2004: 373)

So, it certainly seams; the conjugal relation was publicly known and written
down, and as testified by the diaries, Pannigabluk’s position is obvious.
Stefansson’s fatherhood to her son was acknowledged and noted in the birth
register. Yet, there were differences between them, as sometimes mentioned
by Stefansson, about matters of dishwashing and cleanliness, for instance,
having a tinge of a general conjugal matter. Stefansson notes in his diary:

  On my telling Pannigabluk one day that certain water was dirty she said
  that was no matter, she was just going to wash the teapot inside with it.
  I am considered a sort of renegade because I insist on the teapots and my
  own cup remaining unwashed. I have often heard my natives tell strangers
  that with many good qualities I have the failing, differing thus from most
  white men, of caring little for cleanliness in my food utensils. That I prefer
  manifestly dirty dishes to apparently clean ones in an Eskimo house, is
  considered a curious eccentricity; some seem to think it is “put on.”
                                             (3 May 1910; Pálsson 2001: 193)

It is difficult to see this as an instance of a “collision between two radic-
ally different cultural worlds” as suggested by Pálsson (2004: 374), let alone
a major racial rift in the household, or even an imperialist attitude. It
seems more of a well-​known marital battle over details, (obviously) erased
from Stefansson’s major publications. What may come closer to reality, is
Pálsson’s point that “Like most of his contemporaries, Stefansson was unable
to acknowledge the intellectual role of his indigenous collaborators, in par-
ticular women” (ibid.). This may hold in the public domain of anthropo-
logical writing, but not necessarily in daily life.
   On a general note, the case reminds us that the professional travellers
roaming the Arctic in the great ethnographic period were ordinary humans;
they made friends and engaged in intimate relations; they sired children,
and they may or may not have hidden them or even known about them.
The travellers were not easily shamed –​and neither were the women.
Anarulúnguaq apparently left a child behind in Nome after the Fifth Thule
Expedition had landed there; this does not figure in any report but it is posted
in the Museum in Qaanaaq (Northwest Greenland), from where she and
198 Close Quarters

the other Inughuit participants hailed. If it was not a big issue at the time,
why would it matter now, one might ask. Intimacy was absorbed into the
travelling theory that worked in the Arctic. Physical distance and separate
dwellings were not always an option, when moving along with or between
native groups or camps.
   The intimate practices in the Arctic were correlated with the general
patterns of movement and shared quarters, at a time when Arctic America
was still without borders. In some sense the region was ‘claimed’ by the
ethnographers, but their claims were based on what they had learnt from the
Inuit. The ethnographers wanted to know rather than to conquer –​except
perhaps to follow their hearts –​while living and moving along with the Inuit
in a landscape that had to be shared.
   It is appropriate to close this chapter by a poem written by a young
Stefansson and rendered by Pálsson. Here, Stefansson emerges as gentler,
than one would have thought.

  A feeling comes to my heart tonight
  That has filled, since the world began,
  The centuries; and been the light
  Of the life of the common man.
  For love is the law, the master force,
  That makes the world akin;
  That throws a glow over all without
  And mellows the soul within.
  ‘Tis glorious, on a world-​wide stage,
  To wear a hero’s crown
  That shines with the gems of mighty deeds,
  With the gold of a fair renown.
  But every prize this earth holds out,
  Or has held since the world began,
  I would renounce, and live, for a woman’s love
  The life of the common man.
                   (Stefansson, in Pálsson 2005: 7)

One message of this chapter is the need for keeping an open mind about
social relations –​of all kinds, and in all fields where ethnographers stop over
to listen and learn about other ways of being in the world, a world that is
always more than ‘local’. It is made through contacts and connections. ‘The
great ethnographic period’ in the Arctic stands out not only for its mapping
of places and people, but also for admitting that ethnography depended on a
readiness to share each other’s lives.
