8	Emergent Subjects
       Bodies in the Cold




In previous chapters we have seen how in the Arctic, ethnographic work
implied living closely with the Inuit, sharing their huts, tents, or igloos, and
moving along with itinerant groups or families. The ethnographers depended
on local hospitality; even the most general pictures of the Inuit took off
from personal experiences and chance meetings in the vast landscape. The
individuality of people did not pass unnoticed in the close quarters that the
ethnographers shared with the Inuit, or in their meetings with other people,
when on the move. A case in point is Knud Rasmussen’s meeting with
the Aivilingmiut in the Repulse Bay area –​where he first experienced the
ingenious building of a snow hut (cf. Chapter 5). He also met Ivaluardjuk, a
man of great geographical learning.

  I discovered at once, in the course of our first talk, that Ivaluardjuk,
  though very careful about what he said, was remarkably well acquainted
  with the ancient traditions of his tribe. In order to draw him out a little,
  I narrated a few of the stories common in Greenland. They proved to
  be well-​known here, and the surprise of the natives at finding a stranger
  from unknown lands able to relate old tales they fancied were exclusively
  their own, was such that in a short time the house was filled with inquisi-
  tive listeners. Thus I gained the old man’s confidence, and we were soon
  discussing the folk-​lore of his people and experts, the reserve he had shown
  at first being gradually discarded. He is the oldest member of the house-
  hold I am visiting, and is indeed one of the oldest members of his tribe. He
  himself may have stepped out of some old, weird story, with his strange,
  worn look, and the quiet, steady manner of his own narration makes deep
  impression on those around. After a while he tells me something of his
  own family affairs. He ought long since to have been a widower, he says, a
  poor old fellow with no authority among his neighbours; for his first wife
  had died many years before, and all his children of that marriage grown
  up. But he dreaded the loneliness and helplessness that are always the lot
  of a wifeless man in his country, and had therefore married an adoptive


                                            DOI: 10.4324/9781003374145-8
202 Emergent Subjects

  daughter of his own, and bought her a child, paying for it by one of his
  dogs. He laughs in his quiet, kindly fashion as he tells the story, and adds:
  “Thus men can better their existence and soften the harshness of fate.
  Now I no longer feel alone, and my old age is a restful time. But when I
  chance to think of my childhood and recall the old memories from those
  days, then youth seems a time when all meat was juicy and tender, and no
  game too swift for a hunter. When I was young, every day was the begin-
  ning of some new thing, and every evening ended with the glow of the
  next day’s dawn. Now, I have only the stories and songs to fall back upon,
  the songs that I sang myself in the days when I delighted to challenge my
  comrades to a song-​contest in the feasting house.”
                                                       (Rasmussen 1929: 17)

This is an amazing first encounter, spurring mutual feelings of goodwill,
offering social and historical observations, and not least a vivid portrait of
an old man –​at one with his history. Through multitudes of such encounters,
the Inuit emerged as subjects, as did the Arctic ethnographers; their co-​
constitution challenges the image of the ethnographer as the discoverer of the
‘others’. As Lévi-​Strauss emphasized (1963: 383; cf. Chapter 7), the Inuit cer-
tainly mastered the cold in their own, brilliant style. Such statement sharpens
our vision of human inventiveness and the equal powers of reflection when
it comes to survival. The ethnographic subjects in the Arctic –​studied or
studying –​were equally ingenuous and relied on the vitality of their body.


Classifying Others
Since the European Enlightenment, travellers and proto-​ethnographers had
wanted to classify people, to group them into societies, tribes, classes, bands,
or just families, also in the Arctic, where fewness was a fact of social life.
In the vast expanses of the northern fringe of America, the ethnographers
met with the Inuit and found them both related to and distinct from each
other. The groups were most often small, migrating families or bands that
would meet and disperse as seasons changed. They were connected by lan-
guage and intermarriage, by hunting grounds and enmities, yet reading back
into the extensive literature on the gradual mapping of the Inuit terrain, we
sense how early anthropologists still worked under an implicit obligation to
classify.
   In the Arctic the balance between description and classification was dif-
ficult. Anthropologists worked closely with the Inuit as we have seen in
previous chapters; their knowledge was indispensable for the progress and
even survival of the ethnographers. When on their own, it was up to the
ethnographers to find provision for themselves, to even survive. We note how
Vilhjalmur Stefansson, for instance, was incessantly considering the prob-
abilities for getting food on his route to the unknown eastern archipelagos
and islands, mostly devoid of people (Stefansson 1921: passim). With time,
                                                     Emergent Subjects 203

most could master a dog sledge and shoot a seal, but with time, one had also
learnt from the local experts and had hired a seamstress to look after the
clothes and other matters, and not least found persons of knowledge that
might guide one’s way. There was a distance to keep, however, at least in
writing. ‘Going native’ had to be stalled, or at least temporarily denied when
seeking refuge at trading posts and whaling stations –​for other reasons than
to collect letters and provisions.
   There was balance to be maintained between the place-​bound ethno-
graphic self, and the larger scholarly ambition. This speaks to a remark made
by Lévi-​Strauss, on the fact that anthropology had been able to expand its
object of interest progressively, tendentially to include all human societies.
This had made him realize how humanity had gradually been alienating itself
from itself; and “not least, how the fraction of humanity that has produced
anthropology, is the same one that made so many other peoples an object
of contempt and disgust. An aftermath of colonialism, our investigations are
sometimes called” (Lévi-​Strauss 1973: 43; transl. by Hastrup) Anthropologists
must still balance on a tightrope in their writing, never innocent of time and
personal inclination (Clifford and Marcus 1986).
   Writing from his Baffin Land field, Boas claimed that he was soon to
become like an Eskimo: “I live like them, hunt with them and count myself
among the men of Anaritung” (Boas 1998: 186). His fieldwork certainly
affected him as a thinking person (ibid.: 159). Not least did it affect the
way he saw the Inuit, as kindred persons. He had become a different person
himself.

  After all the many little adventures, and after a long and intimate inter-
  course with the Eskimos, it was with feelings of sorrow and regret that
  I parted from my Arctic friends. I had seen that they enjoyed life, and a
  hard life, as we do; that nature is also beautiful to them; that feelings of
  friendship also root in the Eskimo heart; that, although, the character of
  their life is so rude as compared to civilized life, the Eskimo is a man as we
  are; that his feelings, his virtues and his shortcomings are based in human
  nature, like ours.
                                                    (Boas in Stocking 1974: 55)

This oft cited passage testifies to an attempt at balancing objectification and
feeling. Objectification was an integral, and obvious part of physical anthro-
pology, often carried out along with cultural anthropology (as it came to be
known in the United States). In the early days there was a growing demand
from museums and anatomical institutes, for anthropologists to supply
“human material” for comparative purposes (Pöhl 2008: 40). While travel-
ling with Inuit, Boas noted human graves, but decided to leave them be, so
as not to offend his companions. In his diary (for 30 September 1883), he
wrote that he had found a grave on Arilik Island with a corpse wrapped in
blue cloth in a box beneath a rock; he also stumbled upon old skulls (Boas
204 Emergent Subjects

1998: 104). On October 10, landing on a flat terrace north of Alikun, he
noted the following:

   I went ashore and found traces of rabbits and three graves which were
   clearly very old. In two there were skulls overgrown with moss and lichens.
   Rectangular rock structures, 4´ long and 2´wide, much too small for an
   extended corpse, onto which rocks had been rolled. The entire structure
   was an oval heap of rocks. Here there are high terraces, which have been
   cut through by the descending streams. Unfortunately, I cannot take away
   the skulls that were in the two graves, because of my Eskimos.
                                                             (Boas 1998: 116)

In a letter to his father on the same evening, Boas once more regretted that he
could not take the skulls along, but “I did not dare do it, because that would
have offended the Eskimos seriously. So I had to paddle on without saying
anything about my find” (in Pöhl 2008: 40–​1). As Pöhl stresses, there can be
no doubt about Boas’ knowledge about the Inuit attitude to grave robbery,
and he did not want to spoil the relationship upon which he depended. For
strategic reasons, if nothing else, he had to take their sensibilities into account,
if adverse to anthropological interest. There is a major schism embedded in
this, and we sense Boas’ wavering loyalty –​when the Inuit change from per-
sons to remains, emerging out of the earth. Both are bodies –​if unequal.
    Later, on the Northwest Coast of America, Boas was to take many liberties
with skull-​digging in the interest of science, wanting to ascertain origins and
inter-​group relations. Here, it seemed easier to meet contemporary (schol-
arly) demands for skeletal material that could help solve in the question of
race, a question to which Boas seems to have been committed from early on.
He was interested in the malleability of humans, rather classification. Most
peoples of the world were, at his time, affected by agriculture, cattle breeding
or fishing, yet there were exceptions, as he suggests:

   The only modern tribes among which the effects of civilization on bodily
   activities are slight are those who, like the Bushmen of South Africa, the
   Australians, the Eskimo of Arctic America, the Veddahs of Ceylon, obtain
   their livelihood by the constant, daily repeated pursuit of animals, or by
   the gathering of plants or small invertebrates which grow scattered over
   a wide area.
      Connected with these conditions are also the characteristic selections
   of food-​stuffs by different tribes, such as the exclusive meat diet of some
   tribes (perhaps most pronounced among the Eskimo) and the exclusive
   vegetable diet of others, well developed, for instance, in southern Asia.
   Both of these have, in all probability, a far-​reaching effect upon the bodily
   form of these races.
                                                               (Boas 1911: 72–​3)
                                                     Emergent Subjects 205

Anthropology had taken off in the temperate zone, among agriculturalists,
and this had affected anthropological thinking. Boas draws the conclusion
that the “environment has an important effect upon the anatomical struc-
ture and physiological functions of man; and that for this reason differences
of type and action between primitive and civilized groups of the same race
must be expected” (Boas 1911: 74–​5). The few people, who had not yet
been totally domesticated would, given time, become more like the rest of
the world. Such conclusions were reached partly based on measurements
and comparisons of skeletal material. It still does not explain why Boas was
(almost) prepared to break the bond of trust to obtain ‘evidence’ from skulls
and other bone material. On the Northwest Coast, Boas felt less restricted,
and “sacrificed his ethical imperative for science,” even if he also found it
“very unpleasant work to steal bones from a grave” (Pöhl 2008: 41).
   The growing science of physical anthropology was closely related to the
expanding practice of ethnography, as we know from museum collections
of skulls and skeletons that continue to challenge our feelings for the early
anthropological quest. For Boas, it implied taking active part in the building
up of the collections at the American Museum of Natural History in New York
that became (in-​) famous through the case of Minik, a young Inughuit who
had come to New York with Robert Peary in 1897 from Northwest Greenland
together with five kinsmen, including his father Qissuk. Peary acted on the
request of Boas, wanting to study and interview them. The latter befell the
young Alfred Kroeber, who was a student of Boas’ and sharing many of
his views. Kroeber made the most of it, publishing three substantial art-
icles on the Smith Sound Eskimos, based on his interviews through an Inuit
interpreter (Kroeber 1899a, b, c). These were Kroeber’s first professional
ethnographic publications, and they are still acclaimed for their richness and
their review of existing literature (Jacknis 2002). Kroeber later took over the
Museum in San Francisco, where he was to ‘exhibit’ Ishi, the last Yahi –​an
indigenous group of California –​who had walked out of the forest in 1911
and was ‘adopted’ by the museum, where he was engaged in showing his
artisanal skills (T. Kroeber 1961).
   Of the Inughuit in New York, we know that one of them returned almost
immediately with another ship bound for Greenland, while four of them died
from pneumonia during the first winter, leaving seven-​year-​old Minik in the
care of the Museum attendant William Wallace. When Minik’s father died,
a mock burial was staged, while the museum proceeded to exhibit his skel-
eton as a generic ‘Eskimo Body’. The story has been told many times (e.g.,
Gilberg 1994; Harper 2000), and the sense of wrongdoing will not abate. At
the time, Boas saw himself and his museum working in the name of science,
as did other skull-​diggers. Among them was young Vilhjalmur Stefansson,
who made an excursion to Iceland –​the homeland of his parents –​in 1905,
where he collected numbers of skulls from consecrated graveyards, allegedly
on the verge of falling into the sea; he appropriated them surreptitiously and
206 Emergent Subjects

shipped them home to the Peabody Museum at Harvard University, before
the angry Icelanders could react (Pálsson 2005: 51ff).
   Objectification need not entail actual skeletonization, of course; there
were other ways of marking people as distinct, and of suggesting that some
might possibly be (almost) Icelandic. Stefansson again provides a case from
his discovery of the ‘Blond Eskimos’ at Victoria Island in the company of
Natkusiak, a guide from the other side of the Dolphin and Union Straits. They
met a strange tribe on the island, who baffled them by their appearance –​but
with whom they were perfectly able to speak. After having had a snow house
built and been offered food, Stefansson and Natkusiak turned in and spent
hours in their tent talking about the extraordinary people among whom they
found themselves.

  We had been told by our guide that we should find the Victoria Islanders
  of a light complexion, with fair beards, but still we were not prepared for
  what we saw –​we had believed what we had been told, but we had not
  realized it. Natkusiak kept saying, “These are not Eskimo; they merely
  dress and talk and act like Eskimo.” And so it seemed to me.
                                                     (Stefansson 1913: 191–​2)

In other words, the natives did not fit the category they were supposed to –​
given their isolation. Stefansson goes on:

  It is hard, looking back over a gap of years, to call to memory even the
  intense feelings with which we meet a crisis in life. That morning, when
  the nine men and boys in the village stood before me in line on the ice
  in front of their huts of snow and skins, I knew I was standing face to
  face with an important scientific discovery. From childhood I had been
  familiar with the literature of the North; I knew that here a thousand and
  there a hundred men of Scandinavia and England had disappeared into
  the Northern mists, to be hid by them forever from the eyes of Europe;
  and when I saw before me these men who looked like Europeans in spite
  of their garb of furs, I knew that I had come upon either the last chapter
  and solution of one of the historical tragedies of the past, or else I had
  added a new mystery for the future to solve: the mystery of why these
  men are like Europeans if they be not of European descent. But although
  the situation appealed to whatever there was in me of the poet and the
  theorist, I had to remember that my supply of writing-​paper was limited,
  and that the definite recording of my first impressions of facts was more
  important than filling the pages of my note-​book with speculations.
                                                      (Stefansson 1913: 192)

Actual descriptions follow, convincing the reader that Stefansson was truly
baffled by the European and Scandinavian features of the Eskimo. It is not
simply the blondness and the colour of their eyes that call for his attention,
                                                      Emergent Subjects 207

but also the form of their heads, as shown by his measurements of adult males
(of whom he lists nine). These he compared with the facial index published
by the American Museum of Natural History, where Boas had listed the
indices for a ‘typical Eskimo’, and for ‘Eskimos of mixed European and
Eskimo descent’. The Victorian Islanders matched the index for this last cat-
egory (Stefansson 1913: 194–​5). Stefansson went on to speculate on the dis-
appearance of the Norse settlers in North America and recalls observations
of atypical Inuit by John Franklin. Today, one would not be surprised if
some ‘atypical’ Inuit were descendants of passing whalers and trappers,
hailing from elsewhere. In small populations odd things may happen to
the gene pool, even by one intimate encounter. In fact, non-​conforming
individuals crop up in many early anthropological travelogues intent on
documenting the meetings with individuals, yet to become assembled into
‘tribes’. Stefansson’s deliberations seem a lot of speculation –​based on nine
individuals.
    While Stefansson was intrigued by the Blond Inuit and spun major the-
ories around them, others took them simply at face value, so to speak, while
the name lingered on. Thus, when Rasmussen once found himself in the
vicinity of Point Barrow on his long expedition from east to west along the
North American coast and received an invitation to a spirit séance in one
of the huts, he noted that the “invitation was issued by one Kingiuna, a
typical blond Eskimo, with a bald head, reddish beard, and a touch of blue
about the eyes” (Rasmussen [1927] 1999: 271). Later he mentions others
that could be described in similar terms among the Copper Inuit, but he
does not buy into Stefansson’s hypothesis of a Nordic descent. The paler
variants of the Eskimo might be found in many places, and there was no
tradition among any groups about foreign blood. Rasmussen concludes: “I
am convinced that these peculiar types are the result of purely biological
conditions, which are altogether accidental, and for which no rule can be
established” (Rasmussen [1927] 1999: 287). Among the accidents were chil-
dren sired by passing strangers –​like Stefansson himself, who fathered a son
by the seamstress who kept his house and clothes in order as we heard in the
previous chapter.
    The above illustrates how in the early days of anthropology there was a
keen interest in bodies and races. This was part of what needed mapping in the
Arctic, a region to be conquered by science rather late in the Enlightenment
quest for discovery and classification of all species. The Inuit were implicitly
objectified by their guests, whenever they met, and often called to serve the
ethnographers’ needs in more ways than telling stories. Robert Peary, who
became ethnographer by default, provides a rather extreme case of object-
ification, at least in the writings referring to his own achievements. He had
lived in close companionship with the Inughuit over many years, but his
principal ambition had always been to reach the North Pole, not to describe
local life. When writing about his conquest of the North Pole, Peary presents
the main features of his programme very early on. First, he would use the
208 Emergent Subjects

Smith Sound Route, being the safest; second, he should select a winter base,
commanding a wider range of the Polar Sea than other places; and third, he
would use sledges and Inuit dogs. The reason was obvious:

  Man and the Eskimo dog are the only two machines capable of such
  adjustments as to meet the wide demands and contingencies of Arctic
  travel. Airships, motor cars, trained polar bears, etc. are all premature,
  except as a means of attracting public attention.
     Fourth, the use of the hyperborean aborigine (the Whale Sound Eskimo)
  for the rank and file of the sledge party. It seems unnecessary to enlarge
  upon the fact that the men whose heritage and work in that very region
  must present the best obtainable material for the personnel of a serious
  Arctic party.
                                                          (Peary 1910: 5–​6)

Peary had lived with these people for a long time, having first arrived in
1891, and stayed for several year-​long periods since then. He had truly come
to appreciate their human qualities, as we have heard before, describing them
as “at first sight to be very near the bottom of the scale of civilization; yet
closer acquaintance shows them to be quick, intelligent, ingenious, and thor-
oughly human” (Peary 1898, I: 481). We know that Peary engaged in intimate
relations with more than one woman and fathered at least three children.
Even so, when it came to presenting his own major feat –​the conquering of
the North Pole –​he crosses a line between respect and objectification. Having
praised the contribution of ‘this interesting little race’ to his tour de force
towards the North Pole, Peary reiterates a prophecy that he had voiced some
years before:

  I have often been asked: Of what use are the Eskimos to the world? They
  are too far removed to be of value for commercial enterprises and, further-
  more, they lack ambition. They have no literature nor, properly speaking,
  any art. They value life only as does a fox, or a bear, purely by instinct.
  But let us not forget that these people, trustworthy and hardy, will yet
  prove their value to mankind. With their help, the world shall discover
  the Pole …
     Since 1891 I had been living and working with these people, gaining
  their absolute confidence, making them my debtors for things given them,
  earning their gratitude by saving, time after time, the lives of their wives
  and children by supplying them with food when they were on the verge of
  starvation. For eighteen years I had been training them in my methods, or,
  to put it another way, teaching them how to modify and concentrate their
  wonderful ice technic and endurance, so as to make them useful for my
  purposes. I had studied their individual characters, as any man studies the
  human tools with which he expects to accomplish results, until I knew just
  which ones to select for a quick courageous dash, and just which dogged,
                                                    Emergent Subjects 209

  unswerving ones would, if necessary, walk straight through hell for the
  object I had placed before them.
                                                      (Peary 1910: 43–​4)

This is an extreme instance of instrumentalizing the Inuit, reducing them to
bodies. We note that there is also a marked distinction made between ‘these
people’, obviously males, and ‘their wives’; Peary could not have overlooked
the importance of women in the community, also for his own well-​being, but
forgets it in writing. Generally, in the high latitudes to which the newcomers
only adjusted with time, their dependence on the natives was often cast as a
skill of ‘taming’ them and making them do whatever they themselves needed
to be done. In the process of objectifying and instrumentalizing the vital
collaborators in the field, the early anthropologists made themselves heroes
of the High North. In other words, when the Inuit were written into the
texts, their subjectivity and ingeniousness were frozen and recycled as the
outsider’s accomplishment.


Collecting Tales
Boas was one of the first anthropologists making a strong point of collecting
tales and traditions. His German background would have provided the
impetus, given the growing interest in popular tales and traditions in 19th-​
century northern Europe, where folklore, songs, and stories were collected
and studied as the bottom line of popular history. Eventually it became a main
theme also in overseas investigations, as in Rasmussen’s quest for collecting
tales and poetry in the Arctic. The question of what we should mean by ‘trad-
ition’, and how one may ‘collect’ verbal forms and freeze them as if they were
artefacts, remains. Collectors like Stefansson had their own view of this.

  The beliefs of men of our own country often lack freshness to us because
  we have been familiar with them from childhood, and lack interest
  because we have outgrown most of them. But here were people in whose
  daily conversation unheard-​of superstitions kept cropping out continu-
  ally. When they were telling about their sealing experiences I could enjoy
  the intellectual gymnastic of trying to separate the biological knowledge
  from the superstition, the facts from the theories. Very few Eskimos are
  really liars, and still there is scarcely an Eskimo who can describe to
  you a day’s seal hunt without mixing in a great many things that never
  happened (although, of course, he believes they have happened). Their
  delight in seeing you when you come, the hospitality and friendliness of
  their treatment no matter how long you stay, and the continual novelty
  of their misknowledge and the frankness with which they lay their entire
  minds open to you –​all these are not only fascinating at the time but prof-
  itable for record and reflection.
                                                       (Stefansson 1921: 89)
210 Emergent Subjects

Rasmussen made interesting observations on a similar method in relation to
his work on Inuit intellectual culture, noting how he “had to place myself
in their position” (Rasmussen 1929: 20), echoing Malinowski, as noted in
Chapter 7 –​and by then a well-​known anthropological trope. In the book
from his first expedition to Northwest Greenland in 1902–​1904 (under the
leadership of Ludvig Mylius-​Erichsen), he wrote about collecting tales, as a
task of definite folkloristic bent.

  These fables and legends were written down during the Polar Night. They
  are told in the houses at this season, when the Eskimos, after great banquets
  of raw, frozen meat late in the evening, are digesting their food and are
  heavy and tired. Then it is the task of the story-​teller to talk his hearers to
  sleep. The best story-​tellers boast of never having told any story to the end.
     The legends are known to all; it is the grandmother’s business to teach
  them to her grandchildren.
     This is the first time that the legends of the Polar Eskimos have been put
  on record, and my principle, during my task, has been never to repeat any
  story until I myself had learnt it and told it. In this way I made the whole
  manner of the story-​telling my own, and I have endeavoured, as far as
  possible, to give literal translations.
                                            (Rasmussen 1905: 175; 1908: 159)

Today’s opinions about the authenticity of the tales that Rasmussen
collected in this fashion vary. He was always directly engaged with the
material, moving, gesturing, dancing, singing, reciting, and making his own
poetic interventions along the road. When the Inuit were pleased with his
performance, so was he. His body became an instrument of knowledge.
While collecting the tales he always had to be directly engaged and not
simply attentive. An example is provided from the Netsilingmiut, where he
first met the esteemed shaman Orpingalik, who was also a ‘deadly archer’,
and the ‘quickest kayakman’. Speed and a certain economy of time was
essential:

  Before settling down in earnest among the Netsilingmiut it was of great
  importance for me to know how many they knew of the tales I had already
  written down among the Iglulingmiut. Thanks to Orpingalik’s rapidity of
  apprehension I was able to go through almost a hundred different tales
  in the course of a week. In addition he gave me the words of several
  magic songs, I paying for them with some of those I had got from the
  Iglulingmiut. It was considered that these transactions were quite legit-
  imate, for as they were made through the agency of a white man they could
  not, it was thought, offend the spirits. Translating magic words is a most
  difficult matter, because they often consist of untranslatable compounds of
  words, or fragments that are supposed to have their strength in their mys-
  teriousness or in the very manner in which the words are coupled together.
                                                     Emergent Subjects 211

  But as every magic word has its particular mission, it is considered quite
  immaterial whether they are understood by humans or not –​as long as the
  spirits know what it is one wants, a caribou, a seal, or maybe a cure for
  a sickness. Nevertheless, Orpingalik’s magic words were easier to under-
  stand than they usually are, and therefore I am including them here under
  the description of the man himself, regardless of the fact that later on in
  the original texts they will be rendered in literal translation.
     In communicating them to me Orpingalik uttered them in a whisper, but
  most distinctly and with emphasis on every word. His speech was slow,
  often with short pauses between the words. I have endeavoured to show
  the pauses by means of a new line of verse. Here are some magic words
  used for the purpose of ensuring a successful hunt to men who are in a
  strange country. When I had received them Orpingalik declared that these
  secret words which we owned in fellowship almost made us brothers. The
  spirits of life would regard us as one, as it were, and treat us the same if
  only we closely observed all the taboo that life required.
                                                      (Rasmussen 1931: 13–​4)

This long quote illustrates how Rasmussen’s practice gradually parted com-
pany from the folklore collector’s detached method as known from Europe.
In his view, it was only possible even to begin ‘collecting’ if one shared the
life of the people and moved along with them (e.g., Rasmussen 1929: 11).
In this case, we know that he also had to barter for information (see Remie
1988: 108), but if he could barter with words, he was satisfied. The magic
songs were duly reported, and we cannot but trust his skill in understanding
and relating the tales, songs, and magic words he noted down all the way
from Greenland to Alaska. While any collection of skulls had to be done sur-
reptitiously, the verbal collection of tales and songs could not but be public.
They, too, would soon belong to a frozen past, however.


Inuit Characters
In objectifying their hosts, explorers and ethnographers implicitly passed
judgement on their character; the issue of gender and intimate relations
with natives was part of this, if rarely acknowledged openly. What was also
rarely discussed was a certain envy for the Inuit kind of life –​not least
their freedom of movement. Anthropologists are travellers by default, even
if mostly in their minds when truth be told, and for some the movement
across continents of wide expanses of difficult terrain became a cause for
permanent longing.
   Rasmussen’s first encounter with the ‘Polar Eskimo’ –​the Inughuit in
Northwest Greenland –​and his observation of the freedom of movement
indicates that travelling suited him extremely well. If lawlessness was seen
as a general feature of the American Arctic at the time, in Rasmussen’s work
this was part of his deep respect, even envy, for Inughuit life. One example is
212 Emergent Subjects

his description of the old bear hunter Sorqaq in Northwest Greenland, who
declared one morning that a man who idled about the house when spring
had arrived was wasting his life. Consequently, he called his dogs, harnessed
them, and prepared his sledge to take off travelling and hunting.

  “It has come to pass that a man starts on his travels!” he called abruptly in
  at a window: that is the Polar Eskimo’s farewell. “Great Sorqaq is going!”
  echoed from within, and the people rushed to accompany him as far as
  the ice.
     “Without a wife –​old man’s fashion!” one called jestingly after him.
     “Quite right! Old men are always satisfied with the women they happen
  upon. And where men are gathered together, there is pretty well always
  a woman as well,” retorted old Sorqaq, with a laugh. Then he swung his
  whip high over his head. The dogs sprang yapping across the ice and tore
  off in a playful gallopade. Soon the sledge was out of sight.
     Happy Sorqaq! Thou wast born with an energy that will never let thee
  rest. Thou must live traveling because thou canst not stand always and
  every day to return to the pen. The domestic animal nature has never
  formed part of thy disposition.
     The world is large that men may take it in possession. And so, when the
  traveling fever comes over thee, then do thou fling thyself on thy sledge,
  lord of the day, master of thy dogs!
                                                     (Rasmussen 1908: 42–​3)

Here, Rasmussen posits an opposition between the free-​roaming hunter and
the domesticated animal, and implicitly showing his own sense of restlessness.
On the Fifth Thule Expedition, where the aim was to trace the connections
between all the Inuit groups in Northern America, his wish to always press
on made sense. He rarely travelled along with the families or groups he was
studying as did Diamond Jenness, for instance. Rasmussen moved at his own
pace between groups, Inuit camps, and the trading posts that were often
longed for, when en route.
   When Rasmussen first published his popular account of the Fifth Thule
Expedition, he said in the introduction, “from my heart I bless the fate that
allowed me to be born at a time when Arctic exploration by dog sledge
was not yet a thing of the past” (Rasmussen 1925: 4; [1927] 1999: xxxi).
The sledge was not only an instrument to get from one place to another
in the Arctic landscape but also a means to inscribe oneself into the top-
ography. From this perspective, Rasmussen could not keep an external
view on the people living there, which was to have major impact upon
his work. By travelling like the Inuit, Rasmussen shared their social space.
A by-​product of this was his insistence on the individuality of the Inuit. He
never casts people as types or races, but treats and portrays his interlocutors
as named individuals, each with their own character. New meetings were
always precipitated by means of the sledge, with the bottom line provided
                                                      Emergent Subjects 213

by the likes of Sorqaq and other Inughuit travellers, since his first exped-
ition to Northwest Greenland. The following note is from the early days
among the Caribou Inuit, when after a meal they see a group of new people
approaching:

  This feast was barely over when we caught sight of a whole train of
  sledges coming to our village from a large island lying out in the middle
  of Hikoligjuaq. It was a remarkable procession in the eyes of one who is
  accustomed to seeing the Coast Eskimos come rushing up with their dogs.
  Here were in all six heavily laden sledges, three and three tied together;
  each sledge group was drawn by two dogs, but together with them the
  men pulled too; the women walked in front. The only one allowed to sit
  on the load was a mummy-​like old woman who, on account of her know-
  ledge of all kinds of taboo and the rules of life of the old, was said to
  enjoy great respect among the Pâdlermiut. That she also was Igjugârjuk’s
  mother-​in-​law did not diminish the respect either.
     In Greenland, among the real dog-​     drivers, it is regarded as almost
  degrading to have such a poor team that one cannot sit on the sledge and
  let the dogs do all the work. Here, however, all journeys are synonymous
  with walking and, on the spring journeys when everyone must be taken
  along when camp is broken in order to move to a new spot where possibly
  the stay will last the whole of the next year, it is looked upon as a natural
  thing that one must help to drag the sledges. The small number of dogs is
  not, however, due to any lack of desire to have good teams, but simply the
  difficulty of procuring dog-​feed.
                                                       (Rasmussen 1930: 35–​6)

This passage testifies both to his implicit comparative baseline set in Greenland
(“the real dog-​drivers”) and to his own uneasiness in contemplating the slow
move on foot. At another level, it also partly explains his failure to recognize
the fact that the Caribou Inuit, as he called them, were then on the verge of
extinction (Burch 1988: 91). Neither Rasmussen nor Birket-​Smith, his com-
panion in the study, understood that due to their dependence on one wildlife
resource, caribou, the people were extremely vulnerable. The observers were
simply “not very good historians,” as Burch noted (ibid.). Rasmussen did
not overlook the challenge of being able to feed the dogs, however, and its
impingement on travel and speed. Yet, there is an important truth in Burch’s
statement about their not being good historians; this was partly related to
Rasmussen’s professed goal of getting new tales and information at every
turn of the route, and partly to his own deeply ingrained restlessness. A week
without some sort of a discovery was almost too much for him to bear; just
being there, sitting still, was not his major forte.

  After a week’s life of idleness, most of which passed in this way, that
  I answered all the questions put to me and really was the storyteller of the
214 Emergent Subjects

  village, I had at last gained the confidence to such an extent that I could get
  the information concerning their intellectual life that I so much desired.
     But finally, Igjugârjuk came to me one day and confided to me that I
  was the only man they had ever met who was both a white man and an
  Eskimo. They no longer felt shy before me, nor had they any secrets if I
  would ask.
                                                         (Rasmussen 1930: 46)

Much as Rasmussen liked to tell stories, he did want something in return;
in the case just related, his ‘long wait’ of a full week did pay off immensely.
He was invited to a song-​feast and became party to a remarkable form of
devotion; he

  was asked to go outside the tent and stand at a spot in the snow where
  there were no footprints. There I was to remain until I was summoned
  again. On this clean an untrodden place I was to exhibit myself to Hila,
  stand silent and humble with downcast eyes and merely desire that the
  sky, the weather and all forces of nature should notice me and have pity
  on me.
                                                     (Rasmussen 1930: 46)

   Within the Canadian Inuit population there were still some uncertainties
about unknown people, living elsewhere, and whose characters were ques-
tionable. A case is provided by Stefansson, who in 1910 started a trip from
Langton Bay eastwards to Coronation Gulf and to an unknown people.

  We were now fairly started for the unknown, but no one but myself was
  very enthusiastic over the enterprise. The reluctance of my people was
  due in part only (and in less part) to their fear of finding the unknown
  country gameless –​they feared to find it inhabited by a barbarous and
  bloodthirsty race of which the Baillie Inlands Eskimo had been telling us
  grotesque tales whenever our party and they came together. These dreaded
  people were the Nagyuktogmiut, the people of the caribou antler, who
  lived far to the east, and who used to come in semi-​hostile contact with
  their ancestors a long time ago.
     “These people bear the name of the caribou antler,” they had told us,
  “because of a peculiar custom they have. When a woman becomes of
  marriageable age, her coming-​out is announced several days in advance.
  At the appointed time she is made to take her place in an open space
  out-​of-​doors, and all the men who want wives form around her a circle,
  each armed with the antler of a large bull caribou. The word is given, and
  they all rush to her, each trying to hook her towards him with the antler.
  Often the woman is killed in the scrimmage, but if someone succeeds in
  getting her alive from the others he takes her for a wife. As strength and
  the skill which experience gives are the main requirements for success,
                                                      Emergent Subjects 215

  some of the Nagyuktogmiut have great many wives, while most of them
  have none. Because so many women are killed in this way there are twice
  as many men as women among them. We know many stories, of which
  this is one, to show what queer people these Easterners are. They also
  kill strangers.” That was the way all stories of the Easterners ended. Like
  Cato’s delenda est Cartago, “they kill all strangers” were the unvarying
  words that finished every discussion of the Nagyuktogmiut by the Baillie
  Islanders.
                                                  (Stefansson 1913: 159–​60)

This belligerent tale is an interesting case of little known ‘others’, being seen
as less civilized than oneself; what Stefansson actually found was a people
who were not at all violent, but where

  the kernel of truth consisted in the fewness of women as compared with
  men, but the reason for this fact had nothing to do with caribou antlers,
  but was instead connected with the fact that they practice the Spartan
  custom of exposing new-​born children, and especially female children,
  with the result that women among them are much fewer than men.
                                                               (Ibid.: 160)

‘Character’ seems as much a matter of environment and hardship as an
inborn quality.
   Infanticide and leaving old people to die when the supplies were small,
were practiced almost universally among the Inuit, where it was silently
acknowledged. Jenness gives an eye-​witness account from the Copper Inuit,
with whom he travelled for a long winter season in care of Ikpuck’s family.

  A woman who entered Ikpuck’s hut about 4. A.M. one morning
  announced that his half-​niece had just given birth to a daughter. Icehouse
  dressed quickly and went over to visit the mother; but Ikpuck, after the
  first enquiries paid no attention. A boy would have augmented the rank
  of hunters; but a girl, well, she was only one more to support. I, too, gave
  the matter no thought, leaving at daybreak to return to the station. But the
  day following the newly delivered wife and her husband marched across
  the strait in the teeth of a blizzard and built a snow-​hut beside us. The
  baby remained behind. It had come at an inopportune season when the
  parents were migrating eastward; so without a pang, they had crushed out
  its little life and left the body on the wind-​swept snow.
     Four other mothers destroyed their babies this winter within an hour of
  delivery. Terrible as their crime may seem, they should not be condemned
  too hastily. Every society has devised some method, conscious or uncon-
  scious, for checking the full and unrestricted growth of their popula-
  tion; and infanticide, the simplest of them all, was in past ages the most
  widely spread. Greece and Rome condoned it, and even Christianity with
216 Emergent Subjects

  its emphasis on the value of the individual soul has failed to eradicate it
  entirely in civilized countries.
                                              (Jenness [1928] 1959: 207–​8)

Jenness elaborates on the hardships of a small child and its mother, who
must carry it, protect it and feed it, also at times of few resources. A major
issue was food: “Cow’s milk and cereals did not exist for these Eskimos, and
no babe could survive on fish and flesh, the only foods the country yielded”
(ibid.: 208). When the child began to walk it could have pre-​masticated
morsels of seal, but

  for three years and even longer the mother furnished its nourishment
  from her own breast. Can we blame her if sometimes she shrank from
  the burden? Often she had no choice. It was physically impossible to raise
  twins, or children born within two years of another.
                                                             (Ibid. 208–​9)

All in all, Jenness suggests that it was kinder to stifle the infant in its first
hour “before it knew pain or hunger” (ibid.: 209). He noted that the life-​
expectations in general were very low, the only pleasures being here and now
for everybody; yet, he also emphasizes that “the natives did not desire the
suicide of their race” (ibid.: 210), and that they generally wanted children –​if
they could feed them. He accepted the Inuit point of view and understood it
as part of their survival, but also felt that he had to redeem them.
   This was not always the case, as we know from earlier judgements passed
on the Inuit, as James Hall attests in his account from 1865 –​before pro-
fessional ethnographers arrived. While generally meeting the people with
forbearing, certain situations provoked a strong response of condemnation;
his was not an ethnographic point of view. Once he heard that an old woman
had been left behind in a snow-​hut village, from where the inhabitants had
moved out –​their being on the brink of starvation and having to seek new
possibilities. Hall was determined to find the village and possibly rescue the
woman; the former was difficult, and the latter proved impossible. When he
finally found her hut, covered with new masses of snow, and made his way
into it, he found her dead –​her forehead as cold as frosted marble.

  Her whom we thought to rescue, God himself had rescued. He found
  her here, lonely and helpless, imprisoned in a clay-​tabernacle, and this
  entombed in ice walls and snow –​deserted, abandoned by her people,
  when at His bidding an angel with a white wing –​whiter than the pure,
  radiant snows around –​took a jewel from its broken casket, and bore it
  aloft to its home.
    “Is she not better off now than when in this sinful world?” asked my
  weeping heart, as I looked upon the ice-​fixed features before me.
                                                          (Hall 1865: 491–​2)
                                                       Emergent Subjects 217

Hall relinquishes further contemplation along those lines and decides to stick
to the facts, describing the scene, where the Inuit had “so cruelly abandoned
her to her fate.” The scenery is presented in detail, including her having
been left with some water to drink and a pipe to smoke. She was mere skin
and bones and scantily clad, but when looking closer at her, Hall saw her
tattooed face as “youthful and fair; even a smile was there, as if the King of
Cold had fastened upon her at the very moment when her spirit welcomed
the white-​winged angel from heaven” (ibid.: 493). Hall is torn here, between
bearing witness to cruelty and his understanding of it; his own religious spirit
enables both. While Hall did not have a professional ethnographic agenda,
his work is a rich repository of observations and ponderings about the Inuit.
   The general challenge for anthropologists –​then and now –​is the need to
find patterns, to generalize from details, and to present something more dur-
able than a personal travelogue. Jenness is explicit about the tension between
the personal circumstances and general traits of the people he studied, at
least in his diary from living with the Copper Inuit (2013–​2016):

  Eskimos are kind to each other in some ways. Aiyakuk, for example says
  he has given Oyaraq about two bags of flour this year, and he probably
  spoke the truth. Yet if Oyaraq’s dog were tangled up and liked to strangle
  itself it is quite likely that he would let it do so unhindered –​“I don’t care,
  it’s not mine,” he would say. If he saw Oyaraq he would tell him, but he
  would not stir a finger himself or go a step out of his way. I have noticed
  this repeatedly among all Eskimos I have been with –​it seems to be typical
  of the people as a whole. They are unable, I suppose, to project themselves
  out of themselves –​to love their neighbours as themselves. This perhaps
  explains their cruelty –​or so it seems to us –​to birds and animals –​a
  child-​like thoughtlessness which permits them to torment an injured bird
  or thrash unmercifully a dog which has provoked them –​although in the
  latter case the dog’s great value makes them refrain from doing a lasting
  injury.
                                                           (Jenness 1991: 244–​5)

Jenness earnestly attempted to straddle his regard for the Inuit companions
on the one hand, and his sharp eye for their shortcomings, individually and
collectively, on the other. Being an ethnographer in a vast landscape, totally
dependent on a very small group or one family, is extremely demanding.
    Rasmussen, ‘the butterfly’, never settled down for long, as we know. He
moved from one group of people to another, fulfilling his ambition to visit
all the Inuit groups from east to west, and to document their oral traditions,
poetry, and stories. He was not always entirely pleased with the ethno-
graphic subjects that were his prime interest. Moving towards the Mackenzie
delta, and settling in comfortably with Henrik Henriksen, a Dane in charge
of the Hudson’s Bay Company Station on Baillie Island, he vents a feeling of
homecoming.
218 Emergent Subjects

  It was like coming into a new country altogether. We had been accustomed
  to living among people who lived chiefly on land game, and only hunted
  seal from the ice. Here we found ourselves among folk who won their food
  from the open sea, and spoke a language which was almost exactly like
  that of the Eskimos in Greenland; they talked of whale and white whale,
  seal and ribbon seal, which were hunted in kayaks or umiaks. And these
  umiaks were exactly like those used in Greenland; it was a pleasure to us
  to see all the well-​known lines, coming as we did from among people to
  whom the very name of Greenland was unkown.
                                             (Rasmussen [1927] 1999: 292)

These people seem right; they are almost like their compatriots in Thule.
Qâvigarsuaq and Arnarulúnguaq almost felt at home, given also that the
snow huts in which they had so far lived were here replaced by log huts,
almost as they knew them from Thule. The first impression was very wel-
coming, but closer inspection revealed that things were actually very different
from what they knew. Rasmussen saw that the Mackenzie River “had been
the great source of culture, and just as its mighty currents had torn up whole
forests by the root and spread the timber far along its shores, so also it had
torn up the Eskimo Culture from its old surroundings” (Rasmussen [1927]
1999: 293).
   Rasmussen continued to lament that these people had no consideration
for the future; as a result, he and his travel companions now found them-
selves among people who were ‘highly paid’ and independent.

  The price of a white fox was $30, and many could be caught between
  November and April, in addition to the other sorts of fox, and other fur-​
  bearing animals. The Eskimo hunters were no poor savages in kayaks;
  they owned schooners and called one another “Captain.”
                                                               (Ibid.: 294)

Rasmussen elaborated further on the changed lives of people, who could
read and write, who would use safety razors, and whose blubber lamps were
sold to tourists as curiosities.

  I felt, indeed, something of an old fossil myself at first, among all these
  smart business folk; legend and myth and ancient traditions were things
  they had left far behind. Many a time during those first few weeks did I
  think wistfully of the eastern tribes, where men and women still had some
  respect for the wisdom of their forbears. Here, if I wanted folk tales, I
  found myself confronted with salesmanship; demand created the supply,
  and a self-​styled specialist in folklore, mythology and local information
  offered his services confidently at $25 per day.
                                             (Rasmussen [1927] 1999: 294–​5)
                                                    Emergent Subjects 219

There was much to lament as prices went up, and Rasmussen felt hope-
lessly out of his element; yet, he eventually found that this ‘outward’ civil-
ization was but skin-​deep and after some days –​when he had talked about
their eastern kinsfolk and had offered up a lot of stories –​people gradually
opened. He could soon leave with a great amount of valuable information;
yet, still with a deep regret that people had changed so much and so rap-
idly. It was to get worse further west, where the ethnographic subjects were
equally disappointing –​at least outwardly (cf. Chapter 5).


Vital Bodies
In his early work in Greenland, when Rasmussen was less self-​conscious,
he repeatedly described how friendship and intimacy were instrumental to
accessing knowledge. He collected, charted, and interpreted –​and added
new knowledge of Inuit life from within. Rasmussen was downright “in
love with Eskimo imagination and intelligence” (Sonne 1988), and easily
surrendered to the friends and freedoms of the North. In the icy world, it
was impossible to look at the natives from an external position: One had to
sing along, so to speak, and Rasmussen did so happily. He was therefore well
prepared to listen to the shaman Orpingalik (whom we met above) when
among the Netsilingmiut:

  Orpingalik was not alone a famous shaman; he was also a poet. His
  imagination was a luxuriant one, and he had a very sensitive mind; he was
  always singing when he had nothing else to do, and he called his songs
  “comrades in solitude,” or he would say that his songs were his breath, so
  necessary were they to him, to such an extent were they part and parcel
  of himself…
     One day I asked Orpingalik how many songs he had actually composed.
  He replied:
     “How many songs I have I cannot tell you. I keep no count of such
  things. There are so many occasions in one’s life when a joy or a sorrow is
  felt in such a way that the desire comes to sing; and so I only know that I
  have many songs. All my being is song, and I sing as I draw breath.”
     Orpingalik was not the only singer at this village. On the whole, song
  seemed indispensable to these people. They sing at all times of the day.
                                                     (Rasmussen 1931: 15–​6)

This resonated with Rasmussen’s personal bent; he was a great singer when
camping, according to Peter Freuchen. This tapped into a strong North-​
European trend at the time, the ‘vitalist’ movement in philosophy, art, music,
and literature to which he inadvertently contributed (Hastrup 2010: 661–​
88). The body was a source of strength, and the vitality of society depended
upon freeing the embodied energies and life-​sources of people. In some parts
220 Emergent Subjects

of Europe, it developed into rather unpleasant political views of the lesser
gifted people, be they poorer or of ‘other’ races.
    In Norway, Nansen was a prime mover in turning attention towards
the Arctic as well as to sportsmanship and he became a leading figure in
popularising skiing as healthy for city youth; until then it had been associated
only with rural needs and Sámi life. Nansen scorned the physical weakness
of the city youth and went a long way to inspire new interest in exercise and
strength –​not to be mistaken for ‘sport’ that was simply competitive and
would not take the nation anywhere. When he was elected Honorary Rector
of the University of St. Andrews, Nansen made a strong call for ‘adven-
ture’ and for throwing one’s energy into the pursuit of the unknown (cf.
Chapter 2). Strength and the passion for action, should carry the students to
new places and new heights (Nansen 1927).
    In Europe, the Olympics took off in 1900, and sports became acknowledged
as an antidote to modern degeneration, in some ways against Nansen’s more
naturalist view. Yet, it was part of the same movement towards building up
new strength, given the passive and sickly lives of the growing urban major-
ities. In Denmark, Rasmussen was seen as the epitome of inner vitality; he
was adopted by the literati of the time as an image of natural strength and
a healthy will to invest his own body in pursuit of a calling that refused
to be hemmed in by modern bourgeois aspirations (Hastrup 2010: 276ff.).
Physical strength and bodily health were, of course, major practical assets
for conquering the Arctic; but the notable concern with the body transpiring
in many auto-​biographical (and other) accounts of polar exploration in late
19th and early 20th century, cannot be explained in purely functional terms.
It must also be understood within a larger interest in a strong (male) body
that was very much part of the horizon at the time.
    Polar explorers took great pride in their fitness and, conversely, they
were ashamed over any sign of weakness. An example is Stefansson, who
edited his diaries in order to make the right impressions, with later readers
in mind (Pálsson 2005: 10). Among the items suppressed were information
of his bouts of illness, some of which were rather serious and grounded him
for a long period during his expedition in the northern Archipelago. His
first biographer attempted to write on his health, but Stefansson insisted
that “such things are never mentioned in biographies”; consequently,
the book was withdrawn from publication. A friend later remarked that
Stefansson’s unwillingness to publicly reveal matters of health was under-
standable because “it was unromantic and out of harmony with the picture
of a hardy explorer and hunter on the march” (Pálsson 2005: 11; see also
Levere 1993: 388ff). Vitality was integral to the new era of Arctic explor-
ation and ethnography.
    This compelled explorers to emphasize their strength, and their perfect
bodies often stretched to the limit. Implicitly it also affected ethnographers,
who often found themselves just a step behind the more adventurous
explorers. An example of the latter is Roald Amundsen, who made much
                                                    Emergent Subjects 221

out of his own strength, and of his altogether stronger nature than the one
sported by for instance an Italian crew, who wanted to conquer the North
Pole by airship (in 1926). They were rescued by Amundsen and his team
when their airship crashed on the ice. Amundsen had only slighting remarks
on the small men, who could hardly walk on the ice; one of them stumbled
and could not even get back on his feet on his own.

  It is only ridiculous to believe that men of this half-​tropical race, who
  did not have the faintest idea of how to behave in the cold, would have
  thought of engaging in an expedition on their own account, when such an
  expedition first and foremost requires knowledge and capacity for living
  on the ice in case of mishap.
                                                  (Amundsen 1927: 152–​3)

    In his autobiography he offers an antidote to the weak southern Europeans
by referring to his own body, which he finds an opportunity to praise by a
slightly circuitous route. Looking back at his youth and reporting an event
from his draft board, where he stood naked in front of the military doctor,
who inspected him carefully and suddenly burst into praising his appearance:

  apparently the training I had undertaken incessantly for eight years had
  not been without effect. He said: ”Young man, how on earth have you
  succeeded in acquiring such muscles?” I explained to him that I was keen
  on physical exercise and had pursued this vigorously.
                                                     (Amundsen 1927: 12)

The doctor even called in an officer working in the neighbouring room to
see the wonder, and although Amundsen professes to some timidity, he says
about the doctor that he “was an elderly doctor, and, as I discovered to
my great pleasure and astonishment, an interested researcher of the human
body” (ibid.).
   Amundsen gives a hint of the general pursuit of bodily strength and
human vitality of the time. This trend led to a new interest in gymnastics
and physical education on the one hand, and –​academically –​to a renewed
vigour of social Darwinism, ultimately morphing into eugenics and even
genocide in the Second World War. For the polar explorers the praise of phys-
ical strength served other purposes as well, if not beyond the concepts of the
times. In a diary entry from 1908, two months after the death of his wife Eva,
who left him alone with five children one of whom was rather sickly, Fridtjof
Nansen writes about the socialist movement as a ‘feminine’ movement, and
he continues:

  It is extremely reactionary, grotesquely taking to the extremes the desire
  to protect the weak at the expense of the strong …It is an inconceivable
  contravention of the fundamental principles of the living world. It is the
222 Emergent Subjects

  religion of all incompetence. Nature and society require that incompe-
  tence must sink to the bottom …Nature has only given the strong the right
  to live.
                                         (Quoted in Huntford 1997: 457)

These are hard and unexpected words from the man who was later to receive
the Nobel Prize of Peace (in 1922) for his untiring aid to eastern refugees.
   The general view of the strong body was a token of the times in early
20th-​century Europe; small wonder that similar attitudes prevailed among
other Arctic explorers. Thus, when the painter Harald Moltke, being part of
the Danish Literary Expedition to Northwest Greenland (1902–​1904), fell
sick, he was mortified. For almost a year, Moltke was virtually immobile, and
in his diary, he vents the view that Rasmussen was ashamed of him. In fact,
Rasmussen was deeply sorry and affected by his illness, as Moltke notes in
his diary (Moltke 1936: 214). While it was of course a problem for the whole
group, why should anyone be ashamed?
   If Stefansson did also not want to dwell on his own physical shortcomings,
as we heard, he was relatively candid when it came to admitting to having
felt low and uncertain of whether he would ever return home, and if he did,
whether he would ever want to go back north. Gísli Pálsson has singled out
some entries in Stefansson’s diary from 1909 and 1910.

  A confession: Lest sometime in a warmer clime the less agreeable mem-
  ories of the north may blend with the pleasanter into a uniform agreeable
  haze –​lest I come to believe I “enjoyed every day of the year” up here –​
  I will set down now the fact that I don’t enjoy today and that I sincerely
  wish myself in a warmer, more civilized land. Every few minutes I sub-
  consciously make and renew various resolutions (which I shall doubtless
  break if the opportunity offers) of never going north again if I ever get
  south from this venture. (11 November 1909) …
     My work up here, I have been fond of asserting, entails few hardships.
  But just now it is pretty hard work, and has often been so these three
  years. To be five or six miles from camp every morning at daybreak and
  about that far from home at the last daylight, to carry home heavy loads
  over rocky ground when successful and still heavier loads of disappoint-
  ment when unsuccessful –​this makes a monotonous –​and trying life. The
  continual nervous strain of a hand-​to-​mouth existence, where there is not
  even the shelter of a poorhouse in case of failure, has a telling and cumula-
  tive effect. Without in the least relinquishing my hope of many more years
  of arctic work, I continually feel more strongly to be so well equipped in
  future that I shall have at least a year’s supply of food somewhere awaiting
  to tide me over a season of failure. Just knowing of such a reserve if one
  never had the comfort of a month feel of it to vary one’s diet would lessen
  by half the strain of winter. This is a hard country for a hungry man.
                                    (26 November 1910; Pálsson 2005: 111–​2)
                                                     Emergent Subjects 223

The issue of physical and mental strength is prominent when it comes to
human life in the Arctic, as it became gradually mapped by travellers from
outside, including ethnographers in search of the aboriginal, authentic Inuit.
Meanwhile we note that the lack of vigour and trust in one’s body was rarely
written down.


Heroes of the North
In America from where many of the early ethnographers hailed, the wilderness
had become a veritable ‘cult’ in early 20th century. It fostered such powerful
organizations as the Peary Arctic Club and the National Geographic Society,
whose support was instrumental to Peary’s success and Cook’s (alleged)
failure to reach the North Pole. “Part and parcel of that discourse was a gen-
dered concept of heroism associated with the new importance given to the
wilderness as a source of national virility and toughness” (Bloom 1993: 32).
This may be seen is an American parallel to the European vitalist movement,
if now tinged with a strong nationalist bent.

  By the early twentieth century appreciation of the wilderness spread to
  become a national cult. An increasing number of Americans thought that
  the survival of white masculinity depended upon contact with the wil-
  derness and strenuous physical work. Following the closing of the U.S.
  frontier announced by Frederick Jackson Turner in 1893, new open spaces
  such as the Arctic provided a male testing ground where adventure and
  hardship could still be faced, outside of the weary routine of business and
  social life at home. Theodore Roosevelt observes in an 1899 men’s club
  speech that confronting the wilderness revitalizes “that vigorous manli-
  ness for the lack of which in a nation, as in an individual, the possession
  of no other qualities can possibly atone.”
                                                           (Bloom 1993: 32)

Along with many others, Roosevelt felt that urban life did not sufficiently
support the ability to pursue both one’s own and the country’s need of healthy
and purposeful lives. This was part of the inducement to starting the scouting
movement, the purpose of which, according to one of the co-​founders of
that movement, was “to combat the system that has turned such a large pro-
portion of our robust, manly, self-​reliant boyhood into a lot of flat-​chested
cigarette smokers and doubtful vitality” (quoted in Bloom 1993: 34). Peary
seems to have embodied the positive side of all this, at least if we follow
Roosevelt, who wrote an introduction to Peary’s book on the conquering of
the North Pole in which he states:

  Probably few outsiders realize the well-​nigh incredible toil and hardship
  entailed in such an achievement as Peary’s; and fewer still understand how
  many years of careful training and preparation there must be before the
224 Emergent Subjects

  feat can even be attempted with any chance of success. A “dash for the
  pole” can be successful only if there have been many years of painstaking,
  patient toil. Great physical hardihood and endurance, an iron will and
  unflinching courage, the power of command, the thirst for adventure, and
  a keen and farsighted intelligence –​all of these must go to the make-​up of
  the chief of successful arctic explorers, of the man who succeeded where
  hitherto even the best and the bravest had failed.
    Commander Peary has made all dwellers in the civilized world his
  debtors; but, above all, we, his fellow Americans, are his debtors. He had
  performed one of the great feats of our time; he has won high honor for
  himself and for his country; and we welcome his own story of the triumph
  which he won in the immense solitudes of the wintry North.
                                            (Roosevelt in Peary 1910: vii–​viii)

While heroics glued to the achievements of the explorers, the ethnographers
left more space for the Inuit in the process of discovery and description,
and they emerged as subjects in their own right. Let me once again call
upon Lévi-​Strauss for opening our eyes for the place of humans among
other beings in the larger natural space. First, we must realize “that it is
not within our power to emancipate ourselves completely from either its
structure or its natural rhythms. Space has values peculiar to itself, just as
sounds and scents have their colour and feelings their weight” (Lévi-​Strauss
1963: 126). This also affected the ethnographic subjects of the Arctic who
could not disentangle themselves from their surroundings –​offering the
scholars an entirely new terrain to ‘think through’. This is one of the gifts
hidden in fieldwork, as is the access to a distinct kind of knowledge –​as
celebrated by Lévi-​Strauss:

  If fish can make an aesthetic distinction between smells in terms of light
  and dark, and bees classify the strength of light in terms of weight –​
  darkness is heavy, to them, and bright light light –​just so should the work
  of the painter, the poet, and the composer and the myths and symbols
  of primitive Man seem to us: if not as a superior form of knowledge, at
  any rate as the most fundamental form of knowledge, and the only one
  that we all have in common; knowledge in the scientific sense is merely
  the sharpened edge of this other knowledge. More penetrating it may be,
  because its edge has been sharpened on the hard stone of fact, but this
  penetration has been acquired at the price of a great loss of substance.
                                                     (Lévi-​Strauss 1963: 127)

Lévi-​Strauss adds that the sociologist, and by implication the anthropologist,
has a role to play in the elaboration of such humanism, for societies are col-
lective and individual at the same time. This certainly marks the results of
the travelling ethnographers in the American Arctic, setting out to find the
Inuit and seeing the new people emerge as distinct subjects, like themselves.
                                                       Emergent Subjects 225

A fine lesson from anthropology is, that however much one longs to describe
the subjects in general cultural or social terms, they always stand out as very
much their own, and as individuals. There is no way around the fact that
when meeting on common ground –​as in the Arctic landscape –​all subjects
emerge as just that: subjects.

References
Amundsen, Roald E.G. 1927. Mitt liv som polarforsker. Oslo: Gyldendal Norsk
  Forlag.
