9	Elusive Arctic
       Ancient Steps and Future Traces




The ethnographic material presented in the previous thematic chapters is not
organized historically but has been left to itself as part of the world dealt
with. The literature is rich and captivating; the ethnographies and travelogues
transcend the individual points of view and strengthen the sense of a time of
discovery and of making a new history. In more recent years, archaeological
investigations have added more knowledge about the gradual population of
the American North by the Inuit and their predecessors (e.g., Friesen 2022).
We also find a rich source in modern local interest in ancestral knowledge
and movement, supplementing the extensive readings found in the archives
of early ethnographers (see Stern 2022). The latter have been in focus in this
book, unpacking the interest in Arctic America now on the brink of new
futures (see Nuttall et al. 2018).
   Ethnographic work places the anthropologist within a larger assemblage
of peoples, animals, landscapes, and materials that have had a lasting impact
on the view of Inuit life in the Arctic. Depending on where and when, the
elements of the assemblage could play different parts in the larger whole, as
we have seen in the previous chapters. This book has focused on the making
of ethnographic subjects in the Arctic, through a close reading of the rich
collection of works on human life and movement in the region, where Inuit
and ethnographers moved and met.
   These years, ‘elusiveness’ has a strong tenor in the High North, given
global climate changes that are so manifest in the melting ice and the chan-
ging waters. The inhabitants of the Arctic always were enrolled in larger
global processes, even if the early ethnographers saw them as if living in a
secluded territory. The ethnographic pioneers, whom we have met in the pre-
ceding chapters, were the first to ‘write’ the Inuit, even if their claim to have
discovered them was only partly true. The Inuit were a mobile people, con-
tinuing an eastward movement from the Bering Strait, and gradually popu-
lating the American Arctic from west to east, as the ice retreated.
   In early 21st century, where the Holocene gives way to the Anthropocene,
there is no doubt that both landscape and habitation in the Arctic will


                                             DOI: 10.4324/9781003374145-9
228 Elusive Arctic

continue to change. As the ice continues receding at an increasing pace,
the elusive nature of the Arctic is not simply a fact, but also a major game
changer in the North, deeply affecting people, landscapes, and animals.
Much has happened since the first ethnographic interest in the region. A per-
sistent challenge is that the landscape refuted any attempt at grasping it as a
totality, always surprising the newcomers, however well-​read they might be.
With new media and modern travelling technologies –​and a receding ice –​
the landscape may have less secrets than in the early 20th century, but it still
evades absolute definition.
   The Arctic seems suspended in time rather than space –​between now and
then, rather than here and there. Meanwhile a New Arctic is in the making,
to which we shall return below. Newness is a slippery term and often a matter
of degree rather than of kind. Even as we reflect upon the current changes in
the Arctic, we are caught up in persistent imageries and old fantasies, a per-
sistence owed to the undecidable nature of the Arctic –​including the latitude,
the cold, the treelessness, the animal world, the sounds of ice and wind, and
the challenge of living on the fringe of the world.


Writing the Arctic
The unfathomable Arctic has been underwritten by anthropologists since
their arrival, and their seeking to fall into step with the Inuit. Franz Boas
pioneered Arctic fieldwork in the 1880s, and later scholars contributed to
the ‘great ethnographic period’ at the centre of this book. Along with the
ethnographic writings, we have touched on some of the impressive reports
written by traders and explorers, paving their own way into the region
from the late 18th century onwards; these provided another bottom line,
another view upon the Arctic. Long ago, Lévi-​Strauss made the following
observation:

  There was a time when travel confronted the traveller with civilizations
  radically different from his own. It was their strangeness, above all, which
  impressed him. But these opportunities have been getting rarer and rarer
  for a very long time. Be it in India or in America, the traveller of our
  day finds things more familiar than he will admit. The aims and itiner-
  aries which we devise for ourselves are, above all, ways of being free to
  choose at what date we shall penetrate a given society. Our mechanistic
  civilization is overcoming all others, but we can at least choose the speed
  at which it will be affecting its conquests. The search for the exotic will
  always bring us back to the same conclusion, but we can choose between
  an early or a late stage of its development. The traveller becomes a kind
  of dealer in antiques –​one who, having given up his gallery of primitive
  art for lack of stock, falls back on fusty souvenirs brought back from the
  flee-​markets of the inhabited world.
                                             (Lévi-​Strauss [1955] 1963: 90–​1)
                                                             Elusive Arctic 229

Among the souvenirs from the Arctic wilderness were writings, proofs of a
long pedigree of approaches to the evasive terrain in the North. Reading them,
we understand how the anthropologist easily became a dealer in antiquities,
be they physical, religious, or poetical. Lévi-​Strauss, musing over the Brazil
that he was soon to leave, qualifies this sense of dealing in remains: “The
Tropics are not so much exotic as out of date” (Lévi-​Strauss [1955] 1963: 9;
cf. Chapter 1). The Arctic that we find in the early chroniclers’ work, as cited
in the preceding chapters, seems equally out of date; yet the old writings are
important milestones in a discipline that is fluctuating by default.
    Through the ‘first encounters’, the distinction between the Old and the
New World collapsed, as did the distinction between past and present.
Both anthropologists and Inuit still live with the imprint made by the early
ethnographers. There is never a clean baseline. Each discovery, and each eth-
nography carries its own preconceptions not only from earlier writings and
new experiences, but also from those dreams and ideas that made explorers
and ethnographers crave for the Far North, when still seen as (largely) uncon-
taminated by the modern world. Anthropology was never a summation of
facts or a report of culture; nor is it disinterested. It is a way of looking at the
world with a double gaze. Henry David Thoreau, deeply interested in Native
Americans, once wrote: “Consider what stuff history is made of, –​that for
the most part it is merely a story agreed on by posteriority” Thoreau 1906;
4: 259; Willson 1959: 280).
    Books are written, as are travelogues, exploration reports, logs, and field-​
notes. They may be illustrated, but writing is the key. We have met many
genres in the preceding chapters of this book –​itself a piece of writing.
Writing sets a track, formats a picture, and shapes a narrative with its own
drive; it fashions a particular time, a certain history, framing a past in both
time and space –​reminding us about the temporal character of human
experience (Ricoeur 1984: 3, 91ff). Whenever we set to writing about others,
we format a story. This book is heavily indebted to the works left by early
ethnographers and explorers in Arctic America –​challenging the exposition.
It has become a meta-​memoir, a testimony to the ever-​changing world, often
so inconsiderate about the care and precision with which so many individual
writers have sought to render its constitution and its inhabitants. It is not
only the ever-​changing Arctic that we are faced with, but also the implicit
challenge of writing about a time past after a century of comments and
anthropological interest. Writing is an afterthought; it cannot deliver facts
about the past, only suggest a pattern. “Ethnographic truths are thus inher-
ently partial –​committed and incomplete” (Clifford 1986: 7). James Clifford
cites a Cree hunter who had come to testify in court regarding the fate of his
hunting lands in view of a new hydroelectric scheme; when he administered
the oath he hesitated: “I’m not sure I can tell the truth …I can only tell what
I know” (ibid.: 8).
    Writing sustains a particular kind of knowledge, as we have found it in
reports on the Arctic that have been transmitted, read, improved, or rejected
230 Elusive Arctic

through several generations of anthropologists, telling what they knew –​in
each their way. Writing has taken the upper hand as a medium of knowledge
and power. Once again, we shall revert to Lévi-​Strauss for a précis. In Tristes
Tropiques there is a chapter on ‘A writing lesson’ unfolding among the
Nambikwara who could neither read nor write, but soon were busy drawing
wavy horizontal lines with the pencils offered.

  With most of them that was as far as they got; but their leader saw fur-
  ther into the problem. Doubtless he was the only one among them to have
  understood what writing was for. So he asked me for one of my notepads;
  and when we were working together he did not give me his answers in
  words, but traced a wavy line or two on the paper and gave it to me, as
  if I could read what he had to say. He himself was all but deceived by his
  own play-​acting. Each time he drew a line he would examine it with great
  care, as if its meaning must suddenly leap to the eye; and every time a look
  of disappointment came over his face. But he would never give up trying,
  and there was an unspoken agreement between us that his scribblings had
  a meaning that I did my best to decipher; his own verbal commentary was
  so prompt in coming that I had no need to ask him to explain what he
  had written.
                                          (Lévi-​Strauss [1955] 1963: 288–​9)

Later, the scribbler would produce a long list of required gifts, if he and his
fellows were to oblige Lévi-​Strauss with further help in the impenetrable
rainforest. Through the lines of putative writing, he had made himself equal
to Lévi-​Strauss, and positioned himself apart from his group. The problem
was that it took two precious hours to ‘read out’ his claims, and they were
pressed for time to get out of the spot. Lévi-​Strauss wanted to move on, but
then his mule disappeared. He sat out to find it, only to immediately dis-
appear in the dense forest; when he eventually located the animal, he was
completely lost in the tangle. Recalling what other people had done in a
similar situation, he fired his gun. Nothing happened, but he was eventually
‘found’; in fact, two of the Nambikwara crew had been following his traces
all afternoon. During the sleepless night, Lévi-​Strauss pondered the incident
spurred by the writing.

  So the Nambikwara had learnt what it meant to write! But not at all,
  as one might have supposed, as the result of laborious apprenticeship.
  The symbol had been borrowed, but the reality remained quite foreign
  to them. Even the borrowing had had a sociological, rather than an intel-
  lectual object: for it was not a question of knowing specific things, or
  understanding them, or keeping them in mind, but merely of enhancing
  the prestige and authority of one individual –​or one function –​at the
  expense of the rest of the party. A native, still in the period of the stone
                                                          Elusive Arctic 231

  age, had realised that even if he could not himself understand the great
  instrument of understanding he could at least make it serve other ends.
                                           (Lévi-​Strauss [1955] 1963: 290)

Writing lost its power in the dense forest, where the natives regained the
upper hand, knowing how to find their way and recapture a mule, without
written notes and drawn maps. The Nambikwara may have been a stone-​
age people in some classification scheme, but they could read the landscape,
where European reading skills was of no use. While early ethnographers and
explorers in the American Arctic might likewise have seen the Inuit as a
stone-​age people (e.g., Amundsen, 1908: I; cf. Chapter 5), they never saw
them as illiterate. Many had an amazing grip of words, their tales were extra-
ordinary, and their poets were praised both by ethnographers and by their
own kinsmen –​if rarely written. When the whites moved in, many Inuit
rapidly learnt to read and write and were soon to adopt English along with
the old languages, as happened in Alaska where a concerted effort of edu-
cation was made in the mid-​1920s (Jenness 1962: 18ff). This came with a
price; schooling refashioned ways of thinking and changed social relations.
Reading and writing change the power of words.
    The writings of the early ethnographers established a kind of authority
over the elusive Arctic. The genre was documentary rather than analytical,
and part of what was documented was the relationship between landscape
and language, on the verge of poetry. Writing itself is rarely a driver of his-
tory, yet it works upon people, who are ‘caught up’ in conversation. The
oral traditions, and the general poetic aptitude of the Inuit, forced their
chroniclers to engage endlessly with condensed language forms that were not
immediately accessible –​but soon became so.
    We heard how Rasmussen had to deliver his own lines, before Igjugârjuk
was ready to share his own stories; he wanted to make sure that they were
equal, and of course they were (cf. Chapter 8). Rasmussen liked to tell stories,
but he also wanted to hear theirs; in this case, his long wait of a full week
(!) did pay off immensely. Apart from Igjugârjuk’s knowledge of tradition,
Rasmussen also noted the wide range of his travelling. Rasmussen himself
was an impatient traveller, always working with writing in mind. He saw
the Inuit eye-​to-​eye from the minute he met them, all while musing about
how to present them; in fact, he began ‘writing’ long before he sat down to
it. The literary momentum in Europe swept him along with his own verbal
vitality, further enhanced by the stories he was granted in return; in fact, all
‘ethnographic moments’ are imbued with narrative framing from the start
(Strathern 1999).
    Back in Denmark, Rasmussen worked hard on his literary style, if not
entirely on his own. He was helped by Tom Kristensen, a Danish author and
poet of renown, on the demand of the publisher of the (Danish) Report of
the Fifth Thule Expedition, eventually published in two volumes (Rasmussen
232 Elusive Arctic

1925, 1926). Kristensen relates how he moved into Rasmussen’s house
and became party to the way he worked (Kristensen 1943). Rasmussen
rarely wrote himself, except in the long-​hand notes in the diaries. For the
publications he dictated to a secretary (or two), while walking up and down
the room, reliving his travel in body and sound, moving, whispering, joking,
and making incredible noises to illustrate the sounds of people, winds, dogs,
and birds. The manner of dictation mirrored the speed and restlessness of his
travel and gives weight to the point that his movements in the Arctic land-
scape were directly transferred to the paper (Hastrup 2010). Kristensen’s
task (on behalf of the publisher) was to edit the raw texts and ensure that
there were no repetition of tales and a maximum exposure of the heroic feats
of the author.
   Rasmussen’s literary standing in Denmark reached a high point when
in 1929, the renowned scholar of the culture and language of the East
Greenlanders, William Thalbitzer, nominated Rasmussen for the Nobel Prize
in literature on behalf of the Royal Danish Academy of Sciences and Letters.
Rasmussen deserved it not only on his own merits, Thalbitzer claimed, but
also on behalf of the many Inuit poets whose stories he had made accessible
to the wider world. He was portrayed as singularly well placed to receive
the Nobel Prize on behalf of ‘all Eskimo storytellers’, because their voice
was also his own native voice. The Nobel Committee was not convinced,
however, and Thalbitzer’s suggestion was in vain (Jørgensen 2002; Hastrup
2010: 694–​8).
   Outside of ethnography proper, the famous and often ill-​fated expeditions
across ice-​packed seas kept the sense of danger and exceptional achievement
alive in the public (Lambert et al. 2006). The traversing of often violent seas
that the first European settlers and explorers had to manage, inscribed itself
into the feat of conquering the Arctic. Meeting unknown people in the wil-
derness was equally clad in a sense of achievement, even if rarely as unknown
as suggested in the writings and in the portraits of the Inuit, who superfi-
cially seemed rather wild with their furs and strange abodes. In ethnographic
writing, as well as in literature, “the word in language is half someone else’s”
(Bakhtin 1981: 293). Bakhtin further qualifies this, suggesting that: “Each
word tastes of the context and contexts in which it has lived its socially
charged life; all words and forms are populated by intentions. Contextual
overtones (generic, tendentious, individualistic) are inevitable in the word”
(ibid.). This is also the case for such words as ‘Muskox People’, ‘Polar
Eskimo’, and ‘Victoria Islanders’, bearing the marks of early ethnographies
and the placing of the ethnographic subjects.
   When first encountered by people from temperate and largely agricultural
regions, the Inuit stood out as a particular kind of human; this was not least
owed to the nature of the collective, of which they were part –​far more than
to a distinct kind of humanity. The animal resources and eating habits are
some of “the many agencies operative in the moods, cognitive dispositions,
and moral sensibilities that we bring to bear as we engage the questions of
                                                             Elusive Arctic 233

what to eat, how to get, and when to stop” (Bennett 2010: 51). In fieldwork
the anthropologist gathers knowledge by way of social relations, having a
bearing on the processes by which facts are established as (relevant) facts in
the first place. The relation between the ‘knower’ and the ‘object’ of neces-
sity bends back into the perception of the object itself and its cementation in
writing (Jenkins 1994: 443). Knowledge, therefore, is no simple ‘object’, as it
bears all the marks of its institution, including a particular ‘style of reasoning’
that by itself becomes a standard of objectivity (Hacking 1992: 13).
    This displaces the problem of ethnographic evidence; while the actualities
of the world (or the field) give substance to our thoughts, they cannot at the
same time be external evidence for the connections we make. The positivity
of evidence is shattered; there is simply not enough distance between facts
and evidence for the latter to prove the former. To get it right we have to
shift our attention from the collapsed relationship between knowledge and
evidence to the processes by which we know, i.e., how we make connections
in a fragmented world (Hastrup 2004). Carlo Ginzburg (1994) has suggested
that the discussion of evidence in history is closely linked to a notion of
proof that draws on an implicit parallel between the historian and the judge.
While their objectives are vastly different, both must look for clues, leads,
distortions, and discrepancies in the material collected to support their case.
If justice is the concern of the judge, accurate reconstruction of past events
is the ambition of the historian, or so it was held. What easily slips by the
historian’s notion of reconstruction is the impossibility to ever depict an event
in its totality of implications; it is the limitation of ‘evidence’ that makes the
illusion of wholeness possible.
    We are back to the source of ethnographic authority: the ability to incorp-
orate two modes of thinking in one narrative style. Anthropological writing
can never ‘tell the world, as it is’ without implicit reference to a frame that
always transcends the story itself. Through their engagement with both
people and words, the early ethnographers kept a dual perspective alive
in their reports, if often unacknowledged by themselves. A hundred years
hence, anthropologists are possibly more alert to their own interpretations,
yet we are still bound to keep the tension between different points of view
within one frame. This is part of the anthropological vocation, as designated
by Lévi-​Strauss ([1955] 1963: 58), being so much more than a description
of societies ‘other’ than one’s own; it was proving that these societies were
important, and that we might learn from them –​as in the case of adopting
the clothing of Arctic people, who had already found the best solution
(ibid.: 383; cf. Chapter 4).
    Anthropologists who took their time, in more than one sense, were rarely
subject to enmity, and in addition to their being chroniclers, mastering pen
and paper, they also became distinguished as hunters, co-​          travellers, and
companions. To fulfil their ambitions, they had to master other technolo-
gies than the pen. They had to weather a particular world, for which they
depended very much on the Inuit. They might have come to study them, but
234 Elusive Arctic

they would have been lost in the wilderness without their guidance. This
practical infiltration of ethnographers’ and natives’ points of view emerges
in a particular kind of writing –​sometimes of a cubist bent, as suggested by
Lévi-​Strauss with reference to Max Ernst –​implying that the ethnographer
had a relatively passive role in the creation of the picture or the text. Lévi-​
Strauss elaborates:

  Like his paintings and collages, my work on mythology has been elaborated
  by means of samples from without –​the myths themselves. I have cut them
  out like so many pictures in the old books where I found them, and then
  arranged them on the pages as they arranged themselves in my mind, but
  in no conscious or deliberate fashion.
                                                   (Lévi-​Strauss 1985: 243)

This again speaks to the larger point about the language of ethnographic
writing being always half someone else’s, as suggested above.


Ways of Seeing
Ethnographic writing is always entrenched in a language, belonging equally
to a particular historical moment and to the ethnographer’s observations
and personal sensations, as well as the current register of knowledge. Each
scholar has his or her own way of seeing and mutating the observations into
texts. In North America, the early ethnographers moved between small,
often temporary settlements and larger, permanent trading stations; these
practices formatted a particular way of seeing at the level of lived life.
Ethnographers are always in dialogue with other people. There may be more
than one conversation going on, however, as we shall see from an 18th-​
century encounter between the Greenlanders and the Danish missionary
(and naturalist) Otto Fabricius, working in West Greenland in 1768–​1773
(Holtved 1962).
    Fabricius worked hard on a complete portrait of Greenland, of which
only parts were known until 1946, when a general Description of Greenland
emerged (Fabricius [ca. 1780] 1946). It was clearly designed as the first part
of a larger work, and his general ambition to contribute to the knowledge of
Greenland (Ostermann 1946). He produced a Greenlandic grammar and a
dictionary, based on empirical work among the people, and being the earliest
attempt at understanding the make-​up of Greenlandic.
    When moving to Greenland he brought with him a copy of Linnaeus’
Systema Naturae as his only reading; this sent him thinking about the
‘Greenlandic man’. In his Fauna Groenlandica (1780), he placed ‘man’
first among the living creatures in Greenland, immediately followed by the
walrus; he thus disregarded Linnaeus’ taxonomic scheme but confirmed the
Christian idea of man’s primary place among the living. He linked Homo
groenlandicus to the Homo americanus, the latter referring to the Esquimaux
                                                         Elusive Arctic 235

of the Hudson Bay area, known through other missionaries. About the
Greenlandic man he says:

  A human being, who moves by day, dirty red in colour, with black,
  straight, thick head hair, almost beardless …They call themselves Innuit
  (singular Innuk), which is a common term for them and other peoples,
  and Kalalik (singular Kalalek), a term specially for them.
                       (Fabricius [ca. 1780] 1946, in Thalbitzer 1962: 11ff)

   Fabricius went on to describe their physical appearance in detail, as he
did their habitat and their food, and concluded that they lived in a nat-
ural state: “Without god and without ruler they are governed by rules”
(ibid.: 16). By living according to rules that are truly their own, they might
be placed between the law-​     abiding Europeans and the custom-​      bound
Americans of Linnaeus. His close association with the Greenlanders also
allowed him to make a detailed descriptions of all the implements used for
catching seal –​their foremost occupation, without which they could not
exist. He notes:

  [I]‌n the native language a man is called: Angut, which actually signi-
  fies : “A means of catching seals,” just as if he really were created for
  the purpose, which indicates that from the very beginning this has been
  regarded as the most important accomplishment, and to which belonged
  great masculine intelligence and manly strength; for in truth, other kinds
  of hunting do not require nearly so much insight and dexterity as sealing,
  in which one has to do with a most wary and cunning animal; and only
  when man becomes its superior can he provide for himself and his family,
  dispense with something for trade, barter his surplus for other necessary
  or at least pleasing things, and then he is respected and honoured among
  his fellow-​Greenlanders. For this reason, a foreigner who can become pro-
  ficient enough to catch a seal becomes highly estimable in the eyes of the
  Greenlanders; and I, who could accompany them on this hunt, am told,
  that, even after so long a time, my memory is held in esteem by them.
                                          (Fabricius in Holtved 1962: 28–​30)

Fabricius was committed to the task of converting the Greenlanders, but
to succeed he found that he had to know them better –​on their own terms.
Their gathering around the emerging colonies with their tempting stores
and ill-​fitting white manners, was not the right way. He moved out of the
colony for some time and settled in a small hunting community, where he
lived in Greenlandic style and observed a marked difference in people’s
condition. As Thalbitzer says, “it has always been detrimental to the free-​
living Greenlanders to have to be bound to stay at one place for the sake
of trading or schooling. The seals do not stop migrating for that reason,
of course” (Thalbitzer 1962: 9). Fabricius’ move gave him the opportunity
236 Elusive Arctic

to apprehend the ingenuity of the Greenlandic hunters, if measuring it up
against another trait:

  all who, like myself, in Greenland are able to accompany them on the sea
  must also credit them with great dexterity in the use of these implements
  and in correcting possible defects in them, which is all the more strange, as
  the same people’s apathy in other matters is so deplorably evident.
                                           (Fabricius in Thalbitzer 1962: 30)

The dual perspective on the hunters may have been unique, if foreshadowing
later ethnographic investigations in the Arctic –​seeing both people and
landscapes as ‘other’ in the very process of learning to understand it.
    Fabricius learns from the Inuit –​their language, their hunting practices,
and their kayaking skills –​yet they are reluctant to learn from him. The
asymmetry amplifies the need for unpacking actual encounters. Outside the
colony, people remain their own masters; seeing them for what they are,
Fabricius cannot but admire their skills. He acts the ethnographer rather
than the missionary here. The praise of the Greenlanders’ hunting skills
differs radically from his assessment of their Christian morals –​where he
cannot but see them from the point of view of the Church and his own
wish for their conversion. This underwrites Clifford’s point (cited above)
that the contact zone is relationally constituted before people even encounter
each other. Fabricius knew that the Greenlanders were ‘heathens’ before
he arrived, while he knew nothing about their extremely refined hunting
practices and technologies. He made it his duty to note and write down the
unknown, and stressed his particularly fortunate position, both by speaking
the language, and “because I also accustomed myself to rowing in their
kayaks and thus was able to accompany them on hunts of all kinds and
there see, indeed learn for myself, the usefulness of every single contrivance”
(Fabricius, in Holtved 1962: 28). It is difficult to see him as merely a coloni-
alist, even if ‘colonial moments’ may be identified within a larger scheme of
analysis (cf. Hastrup 2019).
    When the colonies were established, the Inuit began to gather around
them; the Danish administration had to remind them about their old hunting
places. In 1782, an elaborate Instruction was devised, where the proper
relations between Danes and Greenlanders were spelled out, urging the dir-
ectors of the trading stations where people gathered, craving modern amen-
ities, to encourage them to disperse and continue their lucrative fishing and
sealing (Bobé 1936: 364–​90). Fabricius already understood this; his way of
seeing embraced both the stations and the life beyond.
    At a larger Arctic American scale, we note how the long process of local
displacement in the wake of new encounters, new missionaries, new winds
on the Atlantic, new European realities of war or peace, and changing expan-
sionist visions during industrial development, could neither have been fore-
seen, nor necessarily curtailed. The world, again, was basically non-​scalable.
In South America, conquest balanced “on the edge of the void” –​large
                                                            Elusive Arctic 237

territories having been ruined “by conquest and made invisible by the state”
(Gordillo 2013: 228–​9). In the Arctic, there may have been trappers from
outside in some tracts, and whalers who would land their catch at certain
points. Gradually, permanent trading stations were constructed at key points
along rivers and coastal fringes, often attracting missionaries. Yet, around
1900 there was still enough space for the Inuit to evade encounters and
live on their own if they so wished –​from time to time. The ethnographers
worked on the middle ground between the stations and the elusive, often
invisible camps beyond the colonial gaze, not unlike Fabricius. From there,
they constructed an Inuit ethnography, always more than just noticing; it was
also always a way of seeing –​and writing.
   We may think of Rasmussen’s Fifth Thule Expedition whose aim was to
study all Inuit groups of Arctic America. His westward move –​over next to
four years –​offered a view of larger social and historical processes, increas-
ingly affecting the natives. Moving from east to west he compiled lateral
knowledge from group to group that deeply marked his entire vision of the
Inuit. He was a child of Greenland, and he met the American Inuit as an
extension of his past, if often surprising him, and increasingly so as he moved
west. Relating how he and his two companions from Thule, Arnarulúnguaq
and Qâvigarsuaq, arrived at Nome on the Alaskan coast towards the end of
the Fifth Thule Expedition (in late 1924), he appears baffled.

  Thirty years ago, the population consisted of a few Eskimo families,
  winning a bare existence from the sea. Then, in 1900, gold was found, and
  as if by magic a town sprang up, with room for ten thousand souls. The
  haste with which it was constructed shows even now in the lack of regard
  for beauty or comfort. Gold was the one idea. It is said that in Nome,
  there is gold underfoot wherever you tread; and during the last twenty
  years, the district has produced over eighty million dollars. Methods at
  first were of the most primitive sort; men dug with spades in the sand
  wherever they could get at it, or stood in lines along the shore trying to
  wash out the gold dust from the sand. Mighty machines have superseded
  all this, and men now prefer the certainty of a high wage regularly paid to
  the chance of a fortune that may never come.
     The season at Nome is but short; in the first half of June the ice disappears,
  and navigation begins; by the end of October, or early in November, the
  last vessel has left for the south. The Summer population now is about
  2000; in winter barely 900, chiefly whites; of the permanent residents
  hardly more than a hundred are Eskimos. The town is a sort of capital for
  North-​west Alaska, a centre for equipment of trading expeditions, and the
  constant stream of people passing through in summer provides a means of
  existence for stores, agencies and trades of various kinds.
                                             (Rasmussen [1927] 1999: 340–​1)

Rasmussen was disappointed by the modern Arctic; his sense of the
Inuit was shattered by the focus on money. He would rather have seen a
238 Elusive Arctic

collective of people and matter, more in the vein of the Central Inuit than of
white traders and gold-​diggers. The ‘future’ Arctic had begun long before
he reached Nome, however. Rasmussen had described how already at Cape
Lyon they had encountered “the first Eskimo immigrants from Alaska, who,
like the white trappers, were now seeking their fortune in the country of
their ‘wild’ tribal kinsmen” (Rasmussen [1927] 1999: 289). In consequence
he now had to pay the Inuit for their services, and they were not cheap.

  We thought perhaps, for a moment, with regret of the kindly folk we had
  left, who would have helped us on our way for a week and been only
  too pleased, without any question of payment. But the principle here was
  unquestionably right; the Eskimos had now to compete with the white
  men, and if they were to make ends meet, it was necessary to ask a fair
  payment for services rendered. We were strangers, merely passing through
  the country and had to pay our way.
                                            (Rasmussen [1927] 1999: 289)

It is interesting to note the shift in tonality, and not least the use of the
term ‘strangers’ for themselves. Until then, Rasmussen had seen himself as of
their kind, on a par with Qâvigarsuaq and Arnarulúnguaq. He was genuinely
impressed by the modern Inuit –​among whom he could still find traditional
lore –​but he was beginning to realize that changes were inevitable. This
applied to Arctic America as well as Greenland, where he always had had a
double citizenship. In Alaska, suddenly, he was faced with an inverse process
of (internal) colonization.
    Anthropological work always implies analysis, sorting through
impressions and conversations in a field that bends and stretches according
to pressing concerns at the time and to individual interest. To deal with het-
erogeneous landscapes, multiple agents, and shifting points of view, anthro-
pology cannot resort to flat taxonomic terms. Recent inventions of terms
like biodiversity, multi-​species ethnography, and post-​human anthropology
direct ethnographers towards significant relationalities, but they do not make
it up for analysis. Transcending the dualism between nature and culture does
not free us –​scholars of the humankind –​from the duty to ‘discipline’ the
world in which humans live. We constantly need new words to frame emer-
gent realities in a world under siege by the success of human ingenuity; like
other sciences, anthropology can be seen as a kind of translation (Tsing
2015: 217ff). As translators of other lives, we cannot but maintain a human
perspective on the wild diversity of life and take responsibility for thinking
afresh about its potential.


Mapping People
In the chapters of this book, we have met and travelled along with individual
ethnographers, seeking to understand life in the Arctic, and the position of
                                                          Elusive Arctic 239

the Inuit in particular. In early 20th century, ethnographers were struggling
on the border between colonial and scholarly interests, and in practice had
to straddle the two. This applied in the Arctic as well as in the Indian Ocean.
While their writings were certainly of a scholarly kind, they were also impli-
citly framed by colonial relations that bore their own national or imperial
dreams. A case in point is Arthur Radcliffe-​Brown’s earliest work on the
Andaman islanders (1922), where one gets a clear view of his position on
the fringe of both the colony and the Islanders, describing anthropology “as
a study which has an immediate practical value in connection with admin-
istration and education of back-​ward peoples” (Radcliffe-​Brown 1958: 39;
Hastrup 2013: 9). In the High North, Rasmussen sat out without any colo-
nial interest, but he met with it and endorsed it along the way –​seeing the
Inuit in need for education and security against hunger and exploitation.
    Amidst the expansive European interests in other continents,
anthropologists following in the wake of colonial enterprises had to struggle
against the vision of the discipline as mainly documentary and instrumental.
Radcliffe-​Brown stressed that anthropology should keep itself above mere
mapping and insist on its own strict scientific methods and standing, now
being “in a position to give results that may be of immense practical value,
more particularly to those who are concerned with the government or the
betterment of backward people” (Radcliffe-​Brown 1958: 37).

  Meanwhile, just as the science is, as it were, coming of age, its subject
  matter is disappearing with great rapidity. The spread of the white race
  and of European civilization over the world has produced in a century
  or two immense changes. Native peoples in many regions have been
  extinguished, as the Tasmanians, or are approaching extinction, as the
  Australians and our own bushmen. Elsewhere, though the people survive,
  their customs and their mode of life are changed. They no longer make
  the things they formerly made, they learn a new language, their customs
  fall into disuse, and many of their former beliefs become forgotten. The
  very material on which the ethnologist and the social anthropologist rely
  for their studies is disappearing before our eyes. There is, I think, no other
  science in this position. There is no other in which work which is not done
  at once will never again be possible.
                                                  (Radcliffe-​Brown 1958: 37)

This is a strong view of anthropologists as chroniclers of a disappearing
world. The world map was changing, and the colonial expansion made the
‘disappearance’ of the natives more visible, and lamentable –​seen from the
point of view of the ethnographers, who wanted to know and to map the dis-
tinctive tribes that they knew. Their demise threatened the value of anthro-
pology; the unknown withered away.
   At the occasion of the Columbus quincentenary, Robert Paine drew
attention to “the most intriguing question for anthropology coming out
240 Elusive Arctic

of the scholarly literature of that occasion, namely how, in the West, the
unknown is apprehended, then and now” (Paine 1995: 47; italics added).
This is still a pertinent question. Encountering the New World, the observers
first classified the unfamiliar by their resemblance to something known –​
pumas as lions, jaguars as tigers, and so on (Pagden 1982: 11). In the long
run, it was impossible not to acknowledge difference, however, and gradually
not only new species but also new words entered the European vocabulary.
The natural categories expanded, if within an already established classifi-
catory scheme, with which the world (surprisingly) still lives. Classifying
humans proved more difficult.

  Classifying men is not, after all, like classifying plants. For when regarding
  his own species, the observer not only has to decide what he is seeing, he
  also has to find some place for it in his own world. This task is made all
  the more urgent, and the more difficult, if the observer is possessed, as all
  Europeans in the sixteenth century were, by a belief in the uniformity of
  human nature, a belief that require every race to conform, within certain
  broad limits, to the same “natural” patterns of behaviour.
                                                               (Pagden 1982: 13)

Even if there is, at some level, a certain conformity of human nature, there
are also differences both within and between groups. These differences can
now be measured and reconstructed all the way back to the early Holocene
thanks to modern DNA analysis. This means that the ancients can be placed
in a comprehensive scheme of tribes and connections. There are also newer
versions of such technology, affecting the descendants of the Inuit with
whom we have travelled. Mapping individuals out-​manoeuvre earlier maps
of tribes, but where does it take us?
   Recently, some Canadian Inuit have been subject to an emerging ‘genomic
anthropology’ (Pálsson 2008), adding to the older method of DNA analysis
of ancient bones –​of which there are still many in museum repositories, if no
longer exhibited. We recall the ethnographers’ thirst for recovering skeletons
and bones in the early 20th century (cf. Chapter 8) in the interest of science
and precise classification of the Inuit in the scheme of humans. The more
recent genomic investigation was carried out with the approval of the Inuit
(of course) and took place in the Kitikmeot region of Nunavut, “quite pos-
sibly a genomic as well as an archaeological hot spot, the Silk Road of the
Arctic” (Pálsson 2008: 550–​1). It was the centre of the Copper Inuit land-
scape, as described by the early ethnographers. Comparative tests were made
in Greenland, and between these sites the project collected swab samples
from 299 people. A modest sample, it seems, but important and telling.
   The ‘Inuit Genetic History Project’ (IGHP) focused on the origin of the
population, and on long-​term connections to the land and to other groups.
The findings did seem to solve (part of) the Dorset-​Thule Inuit riddle about
the relation between the two prehistoric ‘cultures’, until recently defined
                                                           Elusive Arctic 241

mainly by distinct material remains. Newer archaeological excavations have
suggested a degree of coexistence between the two groups (or cultures), and
part of the genome project was to address this. Indeed, the genetic analysis
indicated,

  a complicated pattern of regional stratification in the distribution of
  individual haplotypes that is not easily accounted for by the prevailing
  hypothesis that all Inuit populations in Canada and Greenland are exclu-
  sively descended from Thule ancestors that expanded from Alaska only
  800–​1000 years ago (Helgason et al. 2006, 132). The alternative scenario
  suggesting that expanding Inuit groups encountered Dorset populations in
  Canada and Greenland and interbred with them would, however, account
  for contemporary patterns of mtDNA variation, that is, the discrepancy
  between the ages of the A2a and A2b haplotype clusters in Canada and
  Greenland.
                                                    (Pálsson 2008: 551–​2)

With recent archaeological excavations in mind, Pálsson states “that it is
likely that the Late Dorset and the Thule people not only coexisted for a
time but also interbred” (ibid.: 552). Indeed, it does seem likely; and it is
not entirely new thinking, if now more firmly established. A new method of
mapping people has emerged, even if it is still a bit early to say how anthro-
pology may relate to the new genetic possibilities, as Pálsson rightly points
out (ibid.: 547). It is tempting to rethink the entire notion of ‘peoples’ –​past
and present; can they truly be defined by genetics –​or artefacts for that
matter?
   Whether definable or not, the past is still with us in unfathomable ways,
and gives impetus to a new, detailed interest also in mapping –​of which
anthropology may be seen as a distinct kind. We are bound to admit the
“creative interpretation of the space it claims to represent” –​as Jerry Brotton
suggests for any map, itself an ingenious argument (Brotton 2013: 14). The
creation of The Geographical Society of London in 1831 was to have a
major impact on 19th-​century mapping, not only of the extensive overseas
possessions but also “all the new, interesting, and useful facts and discov-
eries” (ibid.: 337). ‘Cartography’ quickly became professionalized; the name
was first suggested by a Portuguese historian in 1839, to be adopted by the
Englishman Sir Henry Burton in 1859, when he sat out for an expedition to
central Africa:

  The rise of cartography gave the subjective act of mapmaking a degree of
  scientific expertise which enabled both its practitioners and political bene-
  ficiaries to represent a coherent discipline from which all geographical
  knowledge developed. It was increasingly regarded as an objective, empir-
  ical and scientifically verifiable field of study, separate from the extra-
  neous disciplines of cosmography, navigation, surveying and astronomy
242 Elusive Arctic

  with which it had been associated (and often subsumed by) for so many
  centuries.
                                                     (Brotton 2013: 340)

From the 19th century onwards, cartography played an important role
in exploration of unknown terrains, from pole to pole and from coast to
coast. The emerging professional anthropology took advantage of the recent
mappings of the Arctic landscape, often received by ear rather than on print,
moving on foot from settlement to settlement, or on sledge, another local
means of transport –​and adding to the map as they moved through the terrain.
    The most spectacular feats were of another kind, such as the reaching
of the Poles. The North Pole had been dreamt of, imagined, and coveted
for centuries before it was understood and mapped (Bravo 2019). The first
to reach it was Robert Peary, accompanied by Mathew Henson and four
Inughuit, in 1909 (cf. Chapter 2). What they found was not exactly spec-
tacular –​looking like any other heap of snow and ice in the frozen Arctic
Sea, and the coordinates were questionable. The South Pole was first reached
in late 1911 by Roald Amundson, outrunning Robert Scott, who arrived in
January 1912 (cf. Chapter 2). The polar exploration narratives –​even the
failures –​contributed crucially to a social construction of white masculinity
(Griffiths 2007: 202). To claim such was not foreign to ethnographers in the
cold –​or in the darkness of tropical forests, for that matter. The physicality
of the field always affects the view on the ethnographic subjects. ‘Being there’
is not a guarantee of objectivity; quite the opposite (ibid.: 18). Ethnographers
are mostly left on their own sensations.
    We are back to the source of ethnographic authority: the ability to incorp-
orate two modes of thinking in one narrative style, which may be literary
but not literature. A double awareness of audience and protagonists makes
people listen –​both within the igloo and on the larger European scene of
knowledge and letters. Anthropological writing can never purport to ‘tell
it, as it is’ without implicit reference to a frame that always transcends the
story. Ethnographic authority is vested in the ability to keep both the par-
ticular and the general within view. Fieldwork is carried out by persons con-
versing and living with other persons, remembering, or forgetting significant
conversations and notes. Looking back into one’s own notebooks, some of
the notes often seem incomprehensive. David Parkin suggests that long-​term
anthropological fieldwork is not only incomplete, but exponentially so, and
he goes on:

  There is always more revelation where that came from. The changing the-
  ories and intellectual styles within which we work, the altered global pol-
  itics and the increasing presence in the field of persons wishing and able to
  question our reasons for being anthropologists, are undoubtedly factors
  which shape how we observe and write.
                                                            (Parkin 2000: 103)
                                                            Elusive Arctic 243

Looking back to the early ethnographers in the American Arctic, this hits
the nail.
   Anthropology was never simply a matter of grasping ‘the native’s point
of view’, as Malinowski had suggested (1922) –​and as many ethnographers
were eager to honour. There is no single vision of the world, as this is always
embedded in modes of living that are also material in a vital sense. Mapping
people never finishes; while the poles may remain in place, people always
shift around –​as do their possibilities.


The New Arctic
In anthropology, as in some natural sciences, ‘the field’ has been a prominent
disciplining device, basing knowledge solidly in direct personal experience
of life in ‘another’ place. In the process, people living there became ‘others’
in our very attempt at understanding them. We have been advised to loosen
up the field, and to be mindful of the implicit boundary work that delineates
the disciplines in the first place (Pritchard 2013: 14). Clearly, we need to
acknowledge that our research practices impinge on the worlds we study.
The vocabularies are shifty, drawing on different fields and other knowledge
practices; in that sense, the field is always an ‘event of place’ (Massey 2005),
defined by particularities of perspective, time, and interest, as seen in the pre-
vious chapters, travelling along with the early ethnographers in the American
Arctic.
   The field is not radically different from the laboratory, even if at some
point in time it seemed like a great divide for the natural sciences taking an
interest in the Arctic (Zeller and Ries 2014). The field is a laboratory in its
own way, giving access to multiple relations within or between species at
various scales of concern. The question is how far individual scholars are
prepared to stand up for the authority vested in fieldwork, implicitly based
on an attention to relations. When working in the field, like the one I know
so well in Northwest Greenland, the challenge at first is to meet the world
head-​on in all its ‘wild diversity’. Whatever scholarly domain we subscribe
to, we know the world as humans, sensing the ‘field’ as thrown together
by place, time, and interest. While sledging along with the hunters, who
are constantly working to anticipate the movements of winds, sea currents,
walruses, or seals, both they and their passenger anthropologist sense how
the salient relations shift along the way. We know the world, the ice, the
water, the walrus, and the little auks as both part of human life and outside
of it –​and can share this knowledge with others by speaking, or indeed by
moving silently together (Hastrup 2013, 2014).
   Even when studying the wild diversity on Earth at its wildest, so to speak,
we cannot but acknowledge the power of local analysis and theorizing in
anthropology. This is where the notion of a spatial vernacular makes double
sense. It is from within this vernacular that we may understand the distribu-
tive agency and acknowledge the accountability of humans, speaking up
244 Elusive Arctic

from a particular perspective (Bennett 2010: 38). Knowledge itself scales the
world in diverse ways; certainly, the cherished notion of ethnography is not
devoid of interests or points of view. It is a relational practice that demands
multiple skills, as we have seen in the previous chapters.
   This goes to stress that the landscapes, needs, and movements we find in the
early ‘archives’ of Arctic anthropology were formatted by the ethnographers’
position within a certain assemblage of peoples, animals, landscapes, and
materials. In the chapters of this book, we have not only revisited a cer-
tain era of anthropology, we have also used the rich archive of early Arctic
ethnography to explore the emergence of modern anthropology. While the
Arctic is a different place now, compared to the era of emerging ethnographic
interest, it is still a special and taxing environment. The frontier may have
changed, but it still works as an analytical concept for the in-​betweenness of
any place, any field, which is never given, never still (Hastrup 2020).
   In the American Arctic there have been several instances of demographic
catastrophe, of dramatic loss of vital technologies, of famine and epidemics,
of colonial encroaches that turned out to be either productive or destruc-
tive, and of foreigners that either exploited or restored certain possibilities.
Currently, it is climate change and geopolitics that seem to undermine the
region, as it accelerates in the North and creates a New Arctic, “referring
to a recent era in circumpolar history set in motion by an unparalleled con-
fluence of political and natural phenomena” (Doel et al. 2014: 2). The New
Arctic also signals a new era for questioning the old narratives of Arctic
exceptionalism (ibid: 6). This era paradoxically calls for a return to early
ethnographies and reports, as has been part of this work, focussing on the
infiltration of times, texts, landscapes, and movements.
   In the Great Ethnographic Period, the Arctic landscape was seen as powerful
and often hostile; now it emerges as fragile and in need of protection. This
challenges the scientific community in new ways. Paradoxically, perhaps,
the emergence of the New Arctic calls for penetrating historical analyses.
Without understanding the lived world of the past, the long-​term processes
of colonization, resource exploitation, and military logic, we may not be
able to see the immensity of what is happening now (Doel et al. 2014: 6).
In making a historical analysis we should “refocus our historical lens on
distinctions between what is residual and tenacious, what is dominant but
hard to see, and not least what is emergent in today’s imperial formations –​
and critically resurgent in responses to them” (Stoler 2008: 211). The Arctic
is not only fraught with ancient geopolitics but also with new visions, often
reshaping local communities as heritage to be protected. Meanwhile, a silent
change of these communities takes place, as described by Klaus Dodds and
Mark Nuttall:

  So much of what we are told about the Arctic is partial, out of date, and
  simply wrong. Many children grow up imagining the Arctic as populated
  by native peoples (simplified as “Eskimos”) living in igloos. Indigenous
                                                            Elusive Arctic 245

  cultures are diverse, and many indigenous peoples live below the Arctic
  Circle. In most parts of the circumpolar North, non-​indigenous residents
  far outnumber indigenous Arctic peoples, largely because of the legacies
  of colonialism and settlement, but also because of recent trends in immi-
  gration, rural-​urban migration, and the global processes of demographic
  change. The communities living in the Arctic are not unchanging. Arctic
  regions are also cosmopolitan places.
                                               (Dodds and Nuttall 2019: 6)

This observation of the present could be pushed backwards in time. Inuit
migration and movement, the expansion of settler-​communities, and the
cosmopolitan outlook of indigenous peoples began a long time ago. Around
1900 there were fewer people and more cumbersome communication, yet
there seems to have been a certain knowledge about (strange) neighbours
on the coast, on islands, or along inland river systems, as testified to by
the ethnographers, seeking to understand the Inuit as both different and
connected.
   Today’s Inuit and other indigenous peoples “are constantly called upon
to demonstrate that they remain tied to the traditions and practices of their
ancestors. In other words, unlike non-​indigenous peoples, Inuit are expected
to prove their authenticity” (Stern 2022: 5). Once again, we sense a friction
between ‘otherness’ and ‘sameness’ –​within which both the Inuit and their
ethnographers must work. The friction is built into ethnographic practice,
but also part of the historical moment, where schooling, television, etc. are
eroding Inuit language and ways of life, as Stern notes:

  Schooling has produced perhaps the most serious challenge to the reten-
  tion of the Inuit language as an everyday vernacular. It has proved very
  difficult for Inuit to create educational institutions that are truly based on
  Inuit values and ways of sharing knowledge.
                                                                        (Ibid.: 9)

This is a global predicament now, where any history so obviously is change.
What is also noticeable is the loss of Inuit languages, as English gains weight;
the exception is Greenland where the large majority use Greenlandic as the
principal language, also in schools (Tulloch 2022). There is much more to
this than speaking in the experience of the Inuit (and others), as ‘education’
often meant moving children to residential schools, of which some have later
been proved vile.
   Another predicament are the unending changes in the Arctic region,
already noted by the ethnographers presented in the previous chapters. What
they saw was never untouched, let alone still. People from here and there,
traders, fortune seekers, explorers, ethnographers and, not to forget, the
Inuit’s own movements across and between ethnic and tribal groups, that
were never fixed, left their mark. So did the emerging money economy –​in
246 Elusive Arctic

some cases built upon riches from the earth itself that would boost the local
expectations, but not always fulfil them, as we saw from Alaska. Today, an
array of investigations into the riches, hidden underneath the ice –​at sea
or on land –​deeply affects the Arctic field (Powell 2017). Yet, the field was
always changing; industrial extraction of Arctic natural resources had taken
place since the 16th century (Avango et al. 2014). Today sub-​surface mining
materializes in many places as part of a larger dream of development (e.g.,
Nuttall 2017).
   As the Arctic fields burst and extractive mining expand, the region is no
longer reserved for the Inuit, or for ethnographers and naturalists on foot
or on sledge. Outside interest in the Arctic is burgeoning, not only because
of its (presumed) hidden riches, but also as a recreational territory for non-​
indigenous people. Meanwhile natural and environmental sciences are
fashioning their own fields, where Jeeps take over from sledges, as the ice and
snow disappear. The Arctic is new, and perceptions are changing, not only
due to an economic interest in resources, like tar sand, timber, or oil, but also
as a result of a new focus on global relations that have been sharpened during
in the recent past –​precipitated by global climate changes and politics.
   Clearly, the Arctic was always embedded in global relations of climate,
from the time of the wandering continents to the present melting of the ice.
Yet, a century ago, when the early ethnographers documented Inuit lives in
Arctic America, it seemed detached from the rest of the world. Now, the Inuit
are not only citizens of the North but also its chroniclers and the custodians
of their past, verbal, or monumental. Paul Connerton (1989) suggested that
embodied acts, rituals, and normative behaviour are possibly more important
than material objects, when it comes to understand ‘how societies remember’.
What seems obvious in the New Arctic is that such means of remembering
are not easily kept up with, as society turns round, and ecologies change.
Anthropologists need to think hard about future traces, to come to terms
with past steps.
   We may do so with a view to the ground upon which these traces are
marked, acknowledging anthropology as a particular mapping exercise,
placing human and non-​human actors in one and the same landscape –​of
which they are all part. Since their appearance, humans have always been
agents in the development of the Earth, if at different scales of time and
effect. This includes the Antarctic, where the landscape and its ecology is not
(yet) necessarily downtrodden by humans, but where their marks remain for
ever, it seems, reminding us about the peculiarly human dream to conquer
the impossible –​as suggested by Tom Griffiths (2017). While the Antarctic
faces a massive loss of icy terrain, people in Arctic America look towards
more land and open seas, giving access to new spaces and new resources for
people and animals –​and international interests. In High Arctic Greenland,
the increasingly unstable sea ice deeply affects the hunt for marine mammals,
always the basic resource; little does it help that the North Water Polynya
has been declared an Indigenous Protected Area, if the game no longer needs
                                                            Elusive Arctic 247

it, as the ice cover lessens dramatically and make wide new spaces for marine
game. The future cannot be governed by declarations.


Deep Time
Deep time is not fixed in a particular epoch or at a certain geological depth; it
points to past movements of rising seas, or folding hills, and other processes
that have shaped the terrain on which we live and depend, as suggested by
Richard Irvine, emphasizing that the horizons of time and its relative depth
is a crucial dimension of social life (2020: 2–​3). By foregrounding the sig-
nificance of deep time, a new sense of history and life opens; it is a vertical
counterpart to Gordillo’s suggestion (2014) of the territorial void created
by imperial ruination of large territories, described above. The void was
declared from a particular point of view; truly, there was much more to be
said about past lives on the plain and the traces people had left. So it is with
time; it is much more than clockwork. While it seems to move only forward,
gradually eating up individual lifetimes, Irvine unearths another dimension,
suggesting that our sense of time affects the sense of ourselves, as suspended
between the biological and the geological. Nowhere could this be more per-
tinent than in the Arctic, where the ice age has implicitly set the scene for the
Inuit and their companion species, as described by chroniclers and scholars
through the ages. It has borne witness to the “larger temporal and spatial
field of relationships” that dislocates us from the present, as suggested by
Barbara Bender in another setting (2001: 85). The moving ice has affected
the geological face of the region; in that sense, the landscape itself is a provo-
cation, to adopt Doreen Massey’s notion (2006).
    Today, global warming accelerates exponentially in the Arctic, and the
geo-​ social relations are changing. Geo-​   sociality points to the inevitable
entanglement of the geological and the social (Pálsson and Swanson 2016).
The notion serves as an important reminder about humans being grounded
in the physical world and about their contribution to its gradual reshaping.
This reshaping did not simply come with the Anthropocene, it also brought
us there. In the Arctic, it is apparent that the ‘times of ice’ and the ‘times of
life’ are intertwined. These ‘times’ are not on the same scale, even though
they infiltrate each other. This takes us back to Irvine’s notion of deep time,
and to human sensations (Hastrup et al. 2022). In both domains we note
the plasticity of anthropological work and the need to keep the field open.
Latour adds another dimension to this, suggesting that to define a particular
scale before doing a study would be a mistake in the social sciences, because

  scale is what actors achieve by scaling, spacing, and contextualizing each
  other through the transportation in some specific vehicles of some specific
  traces …they are the ones defining relative scale. It’s not the analyst’s job
  to impose an absolute one.
                                                        (Latour 2005: 183–​4)
248 Elusive Arctic

    The relevant scale is a function of the study of social actors, whose concerns
may outstretch the obviously local, without becoming less located. In lived
life, the near and the far intermingle and contribute to a particular figuring
of the world (Tsing 2010). In the process, apparently diverse scales become
part of the same picture, within which both the local and the global have
become destabilized. By following the actual encounters, actions, interpret-
ations, imaginations, relations, etc. anthropologists may eventually identify
the figures that distinguish a (temporary) world, smaller or larger, short-​or
long-​term, partial or total, but never closed off from the rest of the world,
and its complex history of emergence.
    ‘Deep time’ enters this picture, not only as a dimension of time, but
also in the constitution of the landscape within which people live –​and
anthropologists work. Irvine’s discussion of the drained peat in the fenlands
of East Anglia is an important contribution to our understanding of the infil-
tration of human lives and ancient geological processes (Irvine 2020: 57–​
77). This has a profound resonance in the American Arctic, existentially
and analytically. The early ethnographers, whose work has been presented
in the preceding chapters, were intent on understanding and documenting
everyday life on the edge of the world. Inadvertently, they were also caught
up in deep geological time; the ice still marked the environment to a degree
that it became a main theme in early exploration and ethnography. A more
ominous kind of ‘deep time’ emerges from the debris that one finds all over
the globe, in imperial ruins (Stoler 2008), or rubble from communities run
over by advancing colonizers as in South America (Gordillo 2014), but also in
sunken vessels, crumbling airstrips, or empty tar pits. The Arctic has its own
‘imperial ruins’, often recent ones. One of them is owed to the Thule Airbase
(established in 1952), excluding the Inughuit in Northwest Greenland from
the central part of their land. With new international interests in the Arctic,
including the Arctic Sea, ‘deep time’ is good to think with, for its disclosure
of the geological and historical backdrops of social life –​and the danger of
ruination.
    In a recent book, the depth of time is measured on an even larger scale,
from the creation of the Earth and the wandering tectonic plates until our
own steps in the debris of earlier eras. Hugh Raffles (2020), moving through
different landscapes and cities makes us acutely aware of the incidental nature
of human life within the larger making of the Earth, and the unmaking of
landscapes, even continents, deeply affected by the massive forces that made,
or unmade, the globe. A well-​known case is the power of huge meteorites at
a certain historical moment, accounting for the extinction of the dinosaurs.
While we have known about this for some time, Raffles takes us into the
processes of change through the lifetime of the earth as it has shaped the pre-
sent; his work is a travelogue of rare finesse. Reading his book while reaching
the closure of my own, I felt that thinking through different times and eras,
moving into the deep unknown of the Arctic and its many materials, and
not least the reflections on the making and unmaking of worlds –​was not
                                                             Elusive Arctic 249

only deeply interesting, but also added to the implicit ambition of the pre-
sent work. The ‘field’ is never given but emerges in a conversation with geo-​
histories as well as new people and ancient thoughts. Opening a space for
deep time in ethnography allows new questions, along the line of “do glaciers
listen” –​as posed by Julie Cruikshank in her important book of that name
(Cruikshank 2005) –​and what happens to the Arctic peoples if the Polar
Sea opens.
    In their attention to the everyday, anthropologists seek to articulate its
implications at many epistemological scales, and even in the absence of
‘external’ evidence, they produce solid knowledge of the social (Hastrup
2004). Most fields are elusive; it is impossible to repeat the experiment.
Yet, even as ethnographies outrun their date, they remain important –​if
only as ecological nostalgia, itself a social force (Angé and Berliner 2021;
Hastrup 2021). Nostalgia keeps a vision of another possible kind of life
alive and reminds us, we humans, that there are other ways of living. From
compilations of knowledge about an identified past, future traces may
emerge.
    The Arctic has often been portrayed as a coherent field, and certainly in
the ‘great ethnographic period’ there was a vision of the Inuit as one people,
connected through their landscape. They remained elusive, however, in a
landscape whose time was soon to run out. This is a lesson and a challenge
to the Tristes Arctiques today, where anthropologists cannot but be mar-
ginal observers in a region, where nature shifts, the ice disappears, and the
landscape changes so rapidly that even the question of seasons cannot be
answered.
    The Arctic, indeed, is as elusive as ever.
